Arithmetic Hirzebruch-Zagier divisors and central derivative values of Rankin-Selberg L L -functions
Jeanine Van Order

Abstract
. We give two distinct proofs of the Gross-Zagier formula in terms of sums of automorphic Green’s functions realized as regularized theta lifts, including one involving arithmetic Hirzebruch-Zagier divisors on the Hilbert modular surface X 0 ​ ( N ) × X 0 ​ ( N ) X_{0}(N)\times X_{0}(N) . We then describe applications to the refined conjecture of Birch and Swinnerton-Dyer. Through these calculations, we also describe known and conjectural relations of the central derivative values of Rankin-Selberg L L -functions that appear to Fourier coefficients of certain half-integral weight forms.



1.
Introduction

The theorem of Gross-Zagier
[
26
, Theorem
\lx@sectionsign
I (6.3)]
represents one of the most significant advances on the conjecture
of Birch and Swinnerton-Dyer to date, and forms the foundation for all progress made on the case of Mordell-Weil rank one
through the techniques of Kolyvagin Euler systems and Iwasawa main conjectures.

To recall it, let
E
be an elliptic curve of conductor
N
defined over the rationals
{\bf{Q}}
.
Hence,
E
is modular by fundamental work of Wiles
[
59
]
, Taylor-Wiles
[
53
]
,
and Breuil-Conrad-Diamond-Taylor
[
5
]
, and consequently parametrized by some cuspidal newform

\displaystyle\phi(\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{m\geq 1}c_{\phi}(m)e(m\tau)=\sum\limits_{m\geq 1}a_{\phi}(m)m^{\frac{1}{2}}e(m\tau)\in S^{\operatorname{new}}_{2}(\Gamma_{0}(N)),\quad\tau=u+iv\in\mathfrak{H},e(z)=\exp(2\pi iz).

In particular, the Hasse-Weil
L
-function
L(E,s)=L(E/{\bf{Q}},s)
of
E
over
{\bf{Q}}
has an analytic continuation

\Lambda(E,s)=L_{\infty}(E,s)L(E,s)
to all
s\in{\bf{C}}
given by a shift of the standard
L
-function,
\Lambda(s,\phi)=L_{\infty}(s,\phi)L(s,\phi)

\displaystyle\Lambda(E,s)=L_{\infty}(E,s)L(E,s)

\displaystyle=\Lambda(s-1/2,\phi)=L_{\infty}(s-1/2,\phi)L(s-1/2,\phi).

Here,
L_{\infty}(s,\phi)=(2\pi)^{-s}\Gamma(s)
denotes the archimedean local Euler factor,
with
L(s,\phi)=\prod_{p<\infty}L(s,\pi(\phi)_{p})
the finite Euler product whose Dirichlet series expansion for
\Re(s)>1
is given by

\displaystyle L(s,\phi)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{m\geq 1}a_{\phi}(m)m^{-s}=\sum\limits_{m\geq 1}c_{\phi}(m)m^{-(s+1/2)}.

Hence,
\Lambda(s,\phi)
satisfies a symmetric functional equation
\Lambda(s,\phi)=\pm N^{1-2s}\Lambda(1-s,\phi)

with odd sign or root number
\Lambda(s,\phi)=-N^{1-2s}\Lambda(1-s,\phi)
if and only if
\phi
is invariant under the Fricke involution
w_{N}\phi=\phi
.

Let
k
be an imaginary quadratic field of discriminant
d_{k}
prime to
N
and odd quadratic Dirichlet character
\eta_{k}(\cdot)=(\frac{d_{k}}{\cdot})
.
Write
C(\mathcal{O}_{k})=I(k)/P(k)
to denote the ideal class group of
k
, with class number
h_{k}=\#C(\mathcal{O}_{k})
.
Let
\chi\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})^{\vee}
be any class group character, with

\displaystyle\theta(\chi)(\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})}\chi(A)\theta_{A}(\tau)\in M_{1}(\Gamma_{0}(|d_{k}|),\eta_{k})

the corresponding Hecke theta series of weight
1
, level
\Gamma_{0}(|d_{k}|)
, and character
\eta_{k}
. By the theory of Rankin-Selberg
convolution, the twisted basechange Hasse-Weil
L
-function
L(E/k,\chi,s)
of
E
over
K
twisted by
\chi
has an analytic continuation

\Lambda(E/k,\chi,s)=L_{\infty}(E/k,\chi,s)L(E/k,\chi,s)
given
by a shift of the completed Rankin-Selberg
L
-function
\Lambda(s,\phi\times\theta(\chi))=L_{\infty}(s,\phi\times\theta(\chi))L(s,\phi\times\theta(\chi))
,

\displaystyle\Lambda(E/k,\chi,1)

\displaystyle=\Lambda(s-1/2,\phi\times\theta(\chi))=L_{\infty}(s-1/2,\phi\times\theta(\chi))L(s-1/2,\phi\times\theta(\chi)).

Here, the archimedean local factor is given by

\displaystyle\Lambda_{\infty}(s,\phi\times\theta(\chi))

\displaystyle=(2\pi)^{-s}\Gamma\left(s-\frac{1}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(1+\frac{1}{2}\right),

and does not depend on the choice of class group character
\chi\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})
.
As we explain in Proposition
6.1
, this
L
-function has a well-known analytic continuation to
s\in{\bf{C}}
via the symmetric functional equation

\displaystyle\Lambda(s,\phi\times\theta(\chi))

\displaystyle=\eta_{k}(-N)|d_{k}N|^{1-2s}\Lambda(1-s,\phi\times\theta(\chi)).

In particular, if
\eta_{k}(-N)=-1
so that the sign of this symmetric functional equation is odd,
then the central value
\Lambda(1/2,\phi\times\theta(\chi))=0
is forced to vanish,
and it makes sense to study central derivative values
\Lambda^{\prime}(1/2,\phi\times\theta(\chi))=\Lambda^{\prime}(E/k,\chi,1)
. This happens
for instance if the level
N
is squarefree, and the number of prime divisors
q\mid N
which remain inert in
k
(so
(\frac{d_{k}}{q})=-1
)
is even, or more stringently if
N
is squarefree and totally split, so that the “Heegner hypothesis” of Gross-Zagier
[
26
]
holds.
In this latter setting, the compactified modular curve
X_{0}(N)
comes equipped
with a family of Heegner divisors
y
of conductor
d_{k}
. In brief, there are
h_{k}
many Heegner points
z:E\rightarrow E^{\prime}
of conductor

d_{k}
on
X_{0}(N)(k[1])
, where
k[1]
denotes the Hilbert class field of
k
. More precisely, the class group

C(\mathcal{O}_{k})\cong\operatorname{Gal}(k[1]/k)
acts simply transitively on the set of these points, and we denote this natural action by
z^{A}
.
We obtain from each Heegner point
z\in X_{0}(N)(k[1])
the divisor
y=(z)-(\infty)
in the corresponding Jacobian
J_{0}(N)(k[1])
.

Theorem 1.1

(Gross-Zagier)
.

Let
E
be an elliptic curve of conductor
N
, parametrized by a cuspidal newform
\phi\in S_{2}^{\operatorname{new}}(\Gamma_{0}(N))
.
Let
k
be an imaginary quadratic field of discriminant
d_{k}
prime to
2N
and odd Dirichlet character
\eta_{k}(\cdot)=(\frac{d_{k}}{\cdot})
.
Assume the Heegner hypothesis, so that
\eta_{k}(-N)=-1
. Then for any character
\chi
of the ideal class group
C(\mathcal{O}_{k})
of
k
,
we have the central derivative value formula

\displaystyle\Lambda^{\prime}(E/k,\chi,1)=\Lambda^{\prime}(1/2,\phi\times\theta(\chi))

\displaystyle=\frac{8\pi^{2}||\phi||^{2}}{h_{k}u_{k}^{2}}\cdot\left[y_{\chi}^{\phi},y_{\chi}^{\phi}\right]_{\operatorname{NT}}.

Here,
||\phi||^{2}=\langle\phi,\phi\rangle
denoted the Petersson inner product of
\phi
,

h_{k}=\#C(\mathcal{O}_{k})
the class number,
u_{k}=w_{k}/2
half the number of roots of unity in
k
,
and
\left[y_{\chi}^{\phi},y_{\chi}^{\phi}\right]_{\operatorname{NT}}

the Néron-Tate height of the projection
y_{\chi}^{\phi}

to the
\phi
-isotypical component of
J_{0}(N)(k[1])\otimes{\bf{C}}
of the twisted Heegner divisor

\displaystyle y_{\chi}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})}\chi(A)y^{A}

We remark that this theorem has been generalized by the various works of
Zhang
[
65
]
,
[
66
]
,
[
63
]
,
[
64
]
and Yuang-Zhang-Zhang
[
60
]

to quaternionic Shimura curves over totally real fields,
developing similar ideas with the theta correspondence
and the Jacquet-Langlands correspondence
[
31
]

[
32
]
in the style of Waldspurger’s theorem
[
58
]
.
Here, we give a distinct proof using regularized theta lifts and arithmetic Hirzebruch-Zagier divisors on the
Hilbert modular surface
X_{0}(N)\times X_{0}(N)
, developing the main theorems of Bruinier-Yang
[
13
]

and Andreatta-Goren-Howard-Madapusi Pera
[
1
]
on the Kudla programme for Shimura varieties
or orthogonal type for this setting, and describing the integral presentations of the Rankin-Selberg
L
-functions
in more detail. We explain implications for the refined conjecture of Birch and Swinnerton-Dyer through
Euler characteristic calculations, after Iwasawa main conjectures. In Appendix A, we explain how the theorem
of Bruinier-Yang
[
13
, Theorem 7.3]
can be developed to recover the full Gross-Zagier formula. In Appendix B,
we describe known and conjectural links between the central derivative values and Fourier coefficients of half-integral
weight forms, using the theorem of Bruinier-Funke-Imamoglu
[
11
]
on traces and periods of modular functions.
Finally, we derive many of the analytic results more generally for real quadratic fields.

1.1.
Main results

Let us first suppose more generally that
(V,Q)
is a rational quadratic space of signature
(n,2)
for any integer
n\geq 1
.
Write
(v_{1},v_{2})=Q(v_{1}+v_{2})-Q(v_{1})-Q(v_{2})
for the corresponding inner product.
Let
\operatorname{GSpin}(V)
denote the corresponding general spin group, which fits into the short exact sequence

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle\longrightarrow{\bf{G}}_{m}\longrightarrow\operatorname{GSpin}(V)\longrightarrow\operatorname{SO}(V)\longrightarrow 1.

Let

\displaystyle D(V)=D^{\pm}(V)=\left\{z\subset V({\bf{R}}):\dim(z)=2,Q|_{z}>0\right\}

denote a fixed connected component of the Grassmannian of oriented negative definite hyperplanes in

V({\bf{R}})
. Given any integral lattice
L\subset V
, the adelization

\widehat{L}=L\otimes\widehat{\bf{Z}}
is fixed under the action of

\operatorname{GSpin}(V)({\bf{A}}_{f})
via conjugation by a uniquely-determined
compact open subgroup
K=K_{L}
of
\operatorname{GSpin}(V)({\bf{A}}_{f})
.
We consider the corresponding Shimura variety
X_{K}
with complex points

\displaystyle X_{K}({\bf{C}})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{GSpin}(V)({\bf{Q}})\backslash D(V)\times\operatorname{GSpin}(V)({\bf{A}}_{f})/K,

which determines a quasiprojective variety of dimension
n
over
{\bf{Q}}
. This Shimura variety
X_{K}
is projective if and only if the space

(V,Q)
is anisotropic, and smooth only if the corresponding level structure
K=K_{L}
is neat. We refer to the discussion below for more details.
Fixing a set of reprentatives
h
for the finite set
\operatorname{GSpin}(V)({\bf{Q}})\backslash\operatorname{GSpin}(V)({\bf{A}}_{f})/K
and writing

\Gamma_{h}=\operatorname{GSpin}(V)({\bf{Q}})\cap hKh^{-1}
for the arithmetic subgroup for each
representative, we have the decomposition into geometrically connected components

\displaystyle X_{K}({\bf{C}})

\displaystyle=\coprod\limits_{h\in\operatorname{GSpin}(V)({\bf{Q}})\backslash\operatorname{GSpin}(V)({\bf{A}}_{f})/K}\Gamma_{h}\backslash D(V).

One important feature is that any subspace
(V^{\prime},Q^{\prime})=(V^{\prime},Q|_{V^{\prime}})
of signature
(n^{\prime},2)
determines an algebraic cycle of dimension
n^{\prime}
,
given by the corresponding Shimura variety for
\operatorname{GSpin}(V^{\prime})
and
D(V^{\prime})
(see
[
40
]
). In particular, we obtain from
this construction special divisors of the following type. Let
L^{\vee}
denote the dual lattice of
L
, and
L^{\vee}/L
the corresponding
discriminant group. Given any vector
x\in V
with
Q(x)=m>0
, we consider the corresponding divisor given by the orthogonal complement

D(V)_{x}=\left\{z\in D(V):(z,x)=0\right\}
. Given any coset
\mu\in L^{\vee}/L
and positive rational
m>0
,
we consider the divisor
Z(\mu,m)\subset X_{K}
defined by

\displaystyle Z(\mu,m)

\displaystyle=\coprod\limits_{h\in\operatorname{GSpin}{V}({\bf{Q}})\backslash\operatorname{GSpin}{V}({\bf{A}}_{f})/K}\Gamma_{h}\Big\backslash\left(\coprod\limits_{x\in\mu_{h}+L_{h}\atop Q(x)=m}D(V)_{x}\right),

where
L_{h}\subset V
denotes the lattice determined by
\widehat{L}_{h}=h\cdot\widehat{L}
,
and
\mu_{h}=h\cdot\mu\in L_{h}^{\vee}/L_{h}
. Note that when
(V,Q)
has signature
(1,2)
,
these special divisors
Z(\mu,m)
recover summands of classical Heegner divisors or CM divisors on quaternionic
Shimura curves. Similarly for
(V,Q)
of signature
(2,2)
, the special divisors
Z(\mu,m)
recover summands of classical
Hirzebruch-Zagier divisors on quaternionic Hilbert modular surfaces.

Important theorems of Borcherds
[
3
]
and more generally Bruinier
[
6
]
give explicit constructions
of the Arakelov theoretic automorphic Green’s functions for these divisors
Z(\mu,m)
. As we describe in detail below,
these are given by the regularized theta lifts
\Phi(F_{\mu,m},\cdot)
of certain Maass Poincaré series
F_{\mu,m}
.
To be more precise, let
\omega_{L}:\operatorname{Mp}_{2}({\bf{Z}})\rightarrow\mathfrak{S}_{L}
denote the Weil representation associated
to the chosen lattice
L\subset V
, and let
\theta_{L}(\tau,z):\mathfrak{H}\times D(V)\rightarrow\mathfrak{S}_{L}^{\vee}
denote the
corresponding Siegel theta series defined in
(\ref{Stheta3})
below.
Let
f=f^{+}+f^{-}\in H_{l}(\omega_{L})
more generally be any harmonic weak Maass form of weight

l=1-n/2
and representation
\omega_{L}
. Here, we write the Fourier series expansion of the holomorphic part
f^{+}
as

\displaystyle f^{+}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\mu\in L^{\vee}/L}\sum\limits_{m\in{\bf{Q}}\atop m\gg-\infty}c_{f}^{+}(\mu,m)e(m\tau){\bf{1}}_{\mu}

and the Fourier series expansion of the nonholomorphic part
f^{-}
as

\displaystyle f^{-}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\mu\in L^{\vee}/L}\sum\limits_{m\in{\bf{Q}}\atop m<0}c_{f}^{-}(\mu,m)W_{l}(2\pi mv)e(m\tau){\bf{1}}_{\mu},

where
W_{l}(a):=\int_{-2a}^{\infty}e^{-t}t^{-l}dt=\Gamma(1-l,2|a|)
denotes the Whittaker function given by the partial Gamma function,
and
{\bf{1}}_{\mu}=\operatorname{char}(\mu+\widehat{L})
the characteristic function of
\mu+\widehat{L}
.
Write
M_{l}^{!}(\omega_{L})\subset H_{l}(\omega_{L})
for the space of weakly holomorphic forms whose poles are supported at the cusps,
the subspace of holomorphic forms
M_{l}(\omega_{L})\subset M_{l}^{!}(\omega_{L})
, and the suspace of holomorphic cusp forms

S_{l}(\omega_{L})\subset M_{l}(\omega_{L})\subset M_{l}^{!}(\omega_{L})\subset H_{l}(\omega_{L})
. Here, we also have the antilinear differential
operator
\xi_{l}:H_{l}(\omega_{l})\rightarrow S_{2-l}(\overline{\omega}_{L})
of Bruinier-Funke
[
10
]
defined in
(\ref{xi})
below,
which allows us to identify the weakly holomorphic forms as the kernel
\ker(\xi_{l})=M_{l}^{!}(\omega_{L})
. Given harmonic weak Maass forms

\displaystyle f(\tau)=\sum\limits_{\mu\in L^{\vee}/L}f_{\mu}(\tau){\bf{1}}_{\mu}\in H_{l}(\omega_{L})\quad\text{and}\quad g(\tau)=\sum\limits_{\mu\in L^{\vee}/L}g_{\mu}(\tau){\bf{1}}_{\mu}\in H_{-l}(\omega_{L}^{\vee}),

we consider the pairing
1
1

1

For
f,g\in H_{l}(\omega_{L})
of the same weights and representation,
we define the inner product
\langle\langle f(\tau),\overline{g}(\tau)\rangle\rangle
in the analogous way.

\displaystyle\langle\langle f(\tau),g(\tau)\rangle\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\mu\in L^{\vee}/L}f_{\mu}(\tau)g_{\mu}(\tau).

Hence,
\langle\langle f,g\rangle\rangle
determines a scalar-valued Maass form of weight
0
. We write

\displaystyle\operatorname{CT}\langle\langle f,g\rangle\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\mu\in L^{\vee}/L}\sum\limits_{m\in{\bf{Q}}}c_{f}(\mu,-m)c_{g}(\mu,m)

to denote the constant term in its Fourier series expansion.
Let
\mathcal{F}=\left\{\tau\in\mathfrak{H}:-1/2\leq\Re(\tau)\leq 1/2,\tau\overline{\tau}\geq 1\right\}

denote the standard fundamental domain for the action of
\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\bf{Z}})
on
\mathfrak{H}
.
Given any real number
T>0
, we also consider the truncated fundamental domain

\mathcal{F}_{T}=\left\{\tau\in\mathfrak{H}:-1/2\leq\Re(\tau)\leq 1/2,\tau\overline{\tau}\geq 1,\Im(\tau)\leq T\right\}
.
Let
\mu(\tau)=\frac{dudv}{v^{2}}
denote the Poincaré measure on
\mathfrak{H}
.
We define the regularized theta lift
\Phi(f,z,h)
for
f\in H_{1-n/2}(\omega_{L})
,

z\in D(V)
and
h\in\operatorname{GSpin}(V)({\bf{A}}_{f})
by the regularized integral

\displaystyle\Phi(f,z,h)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{F}}^{\star}\langle\langle f(\tau),\theta_{L}(\tau,z,h)\rangle\rangle d\mu(\tau)=\operatorname{CT}_{s=0}\left(\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\int_{\mathcal{F}_{T}}\langle\langle f(\tau),\theta_{L}(\tau,z,h)\rangle\rangle v^{-s}d\mu(\tau)\right)

given by the constant term in the Laurent series around
s=0
of the function

\displaystyle\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\int_{\mathcal{F}_{T}}\langle\langle f(\tau),\theta_{L}(\tau,z,h)\rangle\rangle v^{-s}d\mu(\tau).

As we explain for Theorem
4.2
, the main theorems of
[
3
]
and
[
6
]
show that
regularized theta lift
\Phi(f,\cdot)
determines an automorphic Green’s function in the sense of Arakelov theory for the divisor defined by

\displaystyle Z(f)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\mu\in L^{\vee}/L}\sum\limits_{m\in{\bf{Q}}\atop m>0}c_{f}^{+}(\mu,-m)Z(\mu,m)\subset X_{K}.

1.1.1.
Quadratic summation formulae

As a preliminary step for our main result, we calculate these Green’s functions
\Phi(f,\cdot)
along zero cycles corresponding to subspaces
of signature
(0,2)
of
(V,Q)
following the theorems of Bruinier-Yang
[
13
, Theorem 4.7]
and Schofer
[
48
]
,
as well as along “geodesic sets” corresponding to Lorenzian subspaces of signature
(1,1)
in the style of
[
55
]
. To be more precise, each
rational quadratic subspace
(V_{0},Q_{0})
of
(V,Q)
of signature
(0,2)
gives rise to a zero cycle
Z(V_{0})\subset X_{K}
with complex points

\displaystyle Z(V_{0})({\bf{C}})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{GSpin}(V_{0})({\bf{Q}})\backslash\{z_{0}^{\pm}\}\times\operatorname{GSpin}(V_{0})({\bf{A}}_{f})/(K\cap\operatorname{GSpin}(V_{0})({\bf{A}}_{f})),\quad z_{0}^{\pm}=V_{0}({\bf{R}})\in D(V)

as defined in
(\ref{CMcycle})
below. Such as cycle is sometimes called a CM cycle,
as it can be associated to an imaginary quadratic field
k=k(V_{0})
. We associate to the lattice
L_{0}=V_{0}\cap L
,
an Eisenstein series
E_{L_{0}}(\tau,s;1)\in H_{1}(\omega_{L_{0}})
of weight
1=2+(0-2)/2
and representation

\omega_{L_{0}}
as defined in
(\ref{E-CM})
. We also consider the derivative Eisenstein series

\displaystyle E^{\prime}_{L_{0}}(\tau,s;1)

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{ds}E_{L_{0}}(\tau,s;1)=E^{\prime+}_{L_{0}}(\tau,s;1)+E^{\prime-}_{L_{0}}(\tau,s;1)\in H_{1}(\omega_{L_{0}}),

and write

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{L_{0}}(\tau)

\displaystyle=E^{\prime+}_{L_{0}}(\tau,0;1)=\sum\limits_{\mu\in L_{0}^{\vee}/L_{0}}\sum\limits_{m\in{\bf{Q}}}\kappa_{L_{0}}(\mu,m)e(m\tau){\bf{1}}_{\mu}

to denote its holomorphic part at
s=0
.
In an analogous way, any rational quadratic space
(W,Q_{W})=(W,Q|_{W})
of signature
(1,1)
of
(V,Q)
gives rise to a geodesic set

\displaystyle\mathfrak{G}(W)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{GSpin}(W)({\bf{Q}})\backslash D(W)\times\operatorname{GSpin}(W)({\bf{A}}_{f})/(K\cap\operatorname{GSpin}(W)({\bf{A}}_{f}))

as defined in
(\ref{geodesic})
below, where
D(W)=\{z\in W({\bf{R}}):\dim(z)=1,Q_{W}|_{z}<0\}

denotes the domain of oriented hyperbolic lines in
W({\bf{R}})
.
This space can be associated to a real quadratic field
k=k(W)
.
We associate to the lattice
L_{W}=W\cap L
, an Eisenstein series
E_{L_{W}}(\tau,s;2)\in H_{2}(\omega_{L_{W}})
of weight
2=2+(1-1)/2
and representation

\omega_{L_{W}}
as defined in
(\ref{E-geo})
. We also consider the derivative Eisenstein series

\displaystyle E^{\prime}_{L_{W}}(\tau,s;2)

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{ds}E_{L_{W}}(\tau,s;2)=E^{\prime+}_{L_{W}}(\tau,s;2)+E^{\prime,-}_{L_{W}}(\tau,s;2)\in H_{2}(\omega_{L_{W}}),

and write

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{L_{W}}(\tau)

\displaystyle=E^{\prime+}_{L_{W}}(\tau,0;2)=\sum\limits_{\mu\in L_{W}^{\vee}/L_{W}}\sum\limits_{m\in{\bf{Q}}}\kappa_{L_{W}}(\mu,m)e(m\tau){\bf{1}}_{\mu}

to denote the holomorphic part at
s=0
.
Using the functional equations and behaviour under Maass lowering operators of these Eisenstein series, we compute the weighted sum

\displaystyle\Phi(f,Z(V_{0}))

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{(z_{0},h)\in Z(V_{0})}\frac{\Phi(f,z_{0},h)}{\#\operatorname{Aut}(z_{0},h)}

of the Green’s function
\Phi(f,\cdot)
along the CM cycle
Z(V_{0})\subset X_{K}
corresponding to an
imaginary quadratic field
k(V_{0})
, by a minor variation of the arguments of
[
13
, Theorem 4.7]
and
[
48
]

(cf.
[
1
, Theorem 5.7.1]
). We also use such properties to compute the weighted sum

\displaystyle\Phi(f,\mathfrak{G}(W))

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{(z_{W},h)\in\mathfrak{G}(W)}\frac{\Phi(f,z_{W},h)}{\#\operatorname{Aut}(z_{W},h)}

of the Green’s function
\Phi(f,\cdot)
along the geodesic set
\mathfrak{G}(W)
corresponding to a real quadratic field
k(W)
.
Writing
U=V_{0},W\subset V
to denote either of these subspaces of dimension
2
with
k(U)
the corresponding
quadratic field, we fix Tamagawa measures on the special orthogonal group
\operatorname{SO}(U)
as follows: We
fix the Tamagawa measure on
\operatorname{SO}(U)({\bf{A}})

for which
\operatorname{vol}(\operatorname{SO}(U)({\bf{R}}))=1
and

\operatorname{vol}(\operatorname{SO}(U)({\bf{Q}})\backslash\operatorname{SO}(U)({\bf{A}}_{f}))=2
.
We also fix the Haar measure on
{\bf{A}}_{f}^{\times}
with
\operatorname{vol}({\bf{Z}}_{p}^{\times})=1
for each prime
p
so that

\operatorname{vol}(\widehat{\bf{Z}})=1
and
\operatorname{vol}({\bf{A}}_{f}^{\times}/{\bf{Q}}^{\times})=\frac{1}{2}
.
This determines a Haar measure on
{\bf{A}}_{k(U)}^{\times}
via the exact sequence

1\rightarrow{\bf{A}}^{\times}\rightarrow{\bf{A}}_{k(U)}^{\times}\rightarrow\operatorname{SO}(U)({\bf{A}})\rightarrow 1
.

Theorem 1.2

(Theorem
5.12
and
5.14
)
.

Let
(V,Q)
be any rational quadratic space of signature
(n,2)
. Fix an integral lattice
L
with corresponding Weil representation
\omega_{L}
.
Let
f\in H_{l}(\omega_{L})
be any harmonic weak Maass form of weight
l=1-n/2
and representation
\omega_{L}
,
with
g=\xi_{l}(f)\in S_{2-l}(\overline{\omega}_{L})
its image under the antilinear differential operator

\xi_{l}:H_{l}(\omega_{l})\rightarrow S_{2-l}(\overline{\omega}_{L})
.

(i)

Let
(V_{0},Q_{0})
be a rational quadratic subspace of signature
(0,2)
with sublattices
L_{0}=V_{0}\cap L
,

L_{0}^{\perp}\subset V
, and
L_{0}\oplus L_{0}^{\perp}\subset L
.
Write
k=k(V_{0})
for the imaginary quadratic field determined by the space.
Let

\displaystyle\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}}(\tau)=\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}}(\tau,1,1)

denote the holomorphic Siegel theta series associated to the positive definite lattice
L_{0}^{\perp}
of signature
(n,0)
,
defined via restriction of
\theta_{L}(\tau,z,h)
as in Lemma
5.7
and
(\ref{IPrelations})
. Let

\displaystyle L(s,g\times\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}})=\langle g(\tau),\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}}(\tau)\otimes E_{L_{0}}(\tau,s;1)\rangle

denote the Rankin-Selberg
L
-series defined in
(\ref{RS-CM})
,
with
L^{\star}(g\times\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}})=\Lambda(s+1,\eta_{k})L(s,g\times\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}})
its completion.
Let
\operatorname{vol}(K_{0})
denote the volume
of the compact open subgroup
K_{0}=K\cap\operatorname{GSpin}(V_{0})({{A}}_{f})
. Then, we have the summation formula

\displaystyle\Phi(f,Z(V_{0}))

\displaystyle=-\frac{4}{\operatorname{vol}(K_{0})}\left(\operatorname{CT}\langle\langle f^{+}(\tau),\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}}(\tau)\otimes\mathcal{E}_{L_{0}}(\tau)\rangle\rangle+L^{\prime}(0,g\times\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}})\right)

\displaystyle=-\operatorname{deg}(Z(V_{0}))\left(\operatorname{CT}\langle\langle f^{+}(\tau),\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}}(\tau)\otimes\mathcal{E}_{L_{0}}(\tau)\rangle\rangle+L^{\prime}(0,g\times\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}})\right).

(ii)

Let
(W,Q_{W})
be a rational quadratic subspace of signature
(1,1)
with sublattices
L_{W}=W\cap L
,

L_{W}^{\perp}\subset V
, and
L_{W}\oplus L_{W}^{\perp}\subset L
. Write
k=k(W)
for the real quadratic field determined by
W
.
Let

\displaystyle\theta_{L_{W}^{\perp}}(\tau)=\theta_{L_{W}^{\perp}}(\tau,1,1)

denote the nonholorphic Siegel theta series associated
to the Lorenztian lattice
L_{W}^{\perp}
of signature
(n-1,1)
,
defined via restriction of
\theta_{L}(\tau,z,h)
as in Lemma
5.7
and
(\ref{IPrelations})
. Let

\displaystyle L(s,g\times\theta_{L_{W}^{\perp}})=\langle g(\tau),\theta_{L_{W}^{\perp}}(\tau)\otimes E_{L_{W}}(\tau,s;2)\rangle

denote the Rankin-Selberg
L
-series defined in
(\ref{RS-geo})
,
with
L^{\star}(g\times\theta_{L_{W}^{\perp}})=\Lambda(s+1,\eta_{k})L(s,g\times\theta_{L_{W}^{\perp}})
its completion.
Let
\operatorname{vol}(K_{0})
denote the volume
of the compact open subgroup
K_{W}=K\cap\operatorname{GSpin}(V_{W})({{A}}_{f})
. Then, we have the summation formula

\displaystyle\Phi(f,\mathfrak{G}(W))

\displaystyle=-\frac{4}{\operatorname{vol}(K_{W})}\left(\operatorname{CT}\langle\langle f^{+}(\tau),\theta^{+}_{L_{W}^{\perp}}(\tau)\otimes\mathcal{E}_{L_{W}}(\tau)\rangle\rangle+L^{\prime}(0,g\times\theta_{L_{W}^{\perp}})\right).

We note again that Theorem
1.2
(i) (Theorem
5.12
) a reproof of Bruinier-Yang
[
13
, Theorem 4.7]
and Schofer
[
48
]

(cf.
[
1
, Theorem 5.7.1]
). On the other hand, Theorem
1.2
(ii)
(Theorem
5.14
) appears to be new, and generalizes the main calculation of
[
55
]
.

1.1.2.
Spaces of signature
(2,2)
associated to quadratic fields and relations to Rankin-Selberg
L
-functions

We apply these calculations to the following quadratic spaces. Let
k
be any quadratic field, real or imaginary, of discriminant
d_{k}
prime to
N
.
We again write
\eta_{k}(\cdot)=(\frac{d_{k}}{\cdot})
to denote the quadratic Dirichlet character, and
C(\mathcal{O}_{k})=I(k)/P(k)
to denote the
ideal class group. We consider he following rational quadratic spaces
(V_{A},Q_{A})
attached to each class
A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{K})
.
Given a class
A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})
, fix an integer ideal representative
\mathfrak{a}\subset\mathcal{O}_{k}
, and let

\mathfrak{a}_{\bf{Q}}:=\mathfrak{a}\otimes_{\bf{Z}}{\bf{Q}}
be the corresponding fractional ideal.
We write

\displaystyle Q_{\mathfrak{a}}(z)={\bf{N}}(z)/{\bf{N}}\mathfrak{a}={\bf{N}}_{k/{\bf{Q}}}(z)/{\bf{a}}

to denote the corresponding norm form,
where
{\bf{N}}(z)={\bf{N}}_{k/{\bf{Q}}}(z)=zz^{\tau}
for
\tau\in\operatorname{Gal}(k/{\bf{Q}})
the nontrivial automorphism denotes
the norm homomorphism. Hence,
Q_{\mathfrak{a}}
has signature
(2,0)
if
k
is imaginary quadratic, and signature
(1,1)
if
k
is real quadratic.
In either case, we consider the space
(V_{A},Q_{A})
defined by

\displaystyle V_{A}=\mathfrak{a}_{\bf{Q}}\oplus\mathfrak{a}_{\bf{Q}}

with
quadratic form

\displaystyle Q_{A}(z)=Q_{A}((z_{1},z_{2}))=Q_{\mathfrak{a}}(z_{1})-Q_{\mathfrak{a}}(z_{2}).

As we explain in Proposition
2.3
below, we have an exceptional isomorphism of algebraic groups

\displaystyle\operatorname{GSpin}(V_{A})\cong\operatorname{GL}_{2}^{2}

over
{\bf{Q}}
.
As we explain in Corollary
2.4
, we can choose a lattice

L_{A}=L_{A}(N)=N^{-1}\mathfrak{a}\oplus N^{-1}\mathfrak{a}\subset V_{A}
whose
adelization
L_{A}(N)\otimes\widehat{\bf{Z}}
is fixed under the action of

\operatorname{GSpin}(V_{A})({\bf{A}}_{f})\cong\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\bf{A}}_{f})^{2}
via conjugation
by the compact open subgroup
K_{A}=K_{L_{A}(N)}=K_{0}(N)\oplus K_{0}(N)
. In this way, we find that

X_{A}\cong Y_{0}(N)\times Y_{0}(N)
is isomorphic to two copies of the noncompactified modular curve
Y_{0}(N)
,
and that the corresponding special divisors
Z_{A}(\mu,m)\subset X_{A}\cong Y_{0}(N)^{2}
corresponding to summands
of classical Hirzebruch-Zagier divisors. In this setting, we deduce from known theorems in the literature such as Strömberg

[
52
, Theorem 5.2]
or more directly via the Doi-Naganuma lifting (Theorem
6.3
) that each cuspidal holomorphic newform

\phi\in S_{l}^{\operatorname{new}}(\Gamma_{0}(N))
has a unique/canonical lifting to a vector-valued form
g_{\phi,A}\in S_{l}(\omega_{L_{A}})

(Corollary
6.4
). We use this to derive the following integral presentation of standard Rankin-Selberg
L
-functions.
Fix a character
\chi
of
C(\mathcal{O}_{k})
, and let
\theta(\chi)
denote the corresponding Hecke theta series. Hence,

\displaystyle\theta(\chi)=\sum\limits_{A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})}\chi(A)\theta_{A}\in M_{l(k)}(\Gamma(|d_{k}|),\eta_{k}),\quad l(k):=\begin{cases}1&\text{ if $k$ is imaginary quadratic}\\
0&\text{ if $k$ is real quadratic}\end{cases}.

Let
\Lambda(s,\phi\times\theta(\chi))=L_{\infty}(s,\phi\times\theta(\chi))L(s,\phi\times\theta(\chi))

denote the corresponding standard Rankin-Selberg
L
-function, whose analytic continuation and functional
equation we recall in Proposition
6.1
. We first show the following link between these completed Rankin-Selberg

L
-functions, and the Rankin-Selberg
L
-functions appearing in Theorem
1.2
for the spaces
(V,Q)=(V_{A},Q_{A})
.

Theorem 1.3

(Theorem
6.7
and Corollary
6.8
)
.

Fix a holomorphic cuspidal newform
\phi\in S^{\operatorname{new}}_{2}(\Gamma_{0}(N))
of weight
2
, level
\Gamma_{0}(N)
, and trivial character.
Let
g_{\phi,A}\in S_{2}(\overline{\omega}_{L_{A}})
denote the lifting of
\phi
to a vector-valued cusp form of weight
2

and conjugate Weil representation
\overline{\omega}_{L_{A}}
. Let
f_{0,A}\in H_{0}(\omega_{L_{A}})
be any harmonic weak
Maass form of weight zero and representation
\omega_{L_{A}}
whose image
\xi_{0}(f_{0,A})
under the antilinear differential
operator
\xi_{0}:H_{0}(\omega_{L_{A}})\rightarrow S_{2}(\overline{\omega}_{L_{A}})
equals
g_{\phi,A}
.
We have the following identifications of completed Rankin-Selberg
L
-functions.

(i)

If
k
is the imaginary quadratic field associated to the negative definite subspace
V_{A,0}\subset V_{A}

with
L_{A,0}=L_{A}\cap V_{A,0}
, then we have the identifications of completed Rankin-Selberg
L
-functions

\displaystyle L^{\star}(2s-2,g_{\phi,A}\times\theta_{L_{A,0}^{\perp}})=\Lambda(s-1/2,\phi\times\theta_{A})

for each class
A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})
, and for each class group character
\chi\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})^{\vee}
the identification

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})}\chi(A)L^{\star}(2s-2,g_{\phi,A}\times\theta_{L_{A,0}^{\perp}})=\Lambda(s-1/2,\phi\times\theta(\chi)).

Hence, if
k
is imaginary quadratic with
(d_{k},N)=1
and
\eta_{k}(-N)=-\eta_{k}(N)=-1
, we have

\displaystyle\Lambda^{\prime}(1/2,\phi\times\theta(\chi))

\displaystyle=-\frac{2\pi h_{k}}{w_{k}}\sum\limits_{A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})}\chi(A)\left[\left(\frac{\operatorname{vol}(K_{A,0})}{4}\right)\Phi(f_{0,A},Z(V_{A,0}))+\operatorname{CT}\langle\langle f_{0,A}^{+}(\tau),\theta_{L_{A,0}^{\perp}}(\tau)\otimes\mathcal{E}_{L_{A,0}}(\tau)\rangle\rangle\right].

Here,
h_{k}=\#C(\mathcal{O}_{k})
denotes the class number, and
w_{k}=\#\mathcal{O}_{k}^{\times}
the number of roots of unity in
k
.

(ii)

If
k
is the real quadratic field associated to the Lorentzian subspace
W_{A}\subset V_{A}
with
L_{A,W}\cap W_{A}\cap L_{A}
,
then we have the identifications of completed Rankin-Selberg
L
-functions

\displaystyle L^{\star}(2s-2,g_{\phi,A}\times\theta_{L_{A,W}^{\perp}})

\displaystyle=\Lambda(s-1/2,\phi\times\theta_{A})

for each class
A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})
, and for each class group character
\chi\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})^{\vee}
that

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})}\chi(A)L^{\star}(2s-2,g_{\phi,A}\times\theta_{L_{A,W}^{\perp}})

\displaystyle=\Lambda(s-1/2,\phi\times\theta(\chi)).

Hence, if
k
is real quadratic with
(d_{k},N)=1
and
\eta_{k}(-N)=\eta_{k}(N)=-1
, we have

\displaystyle\Lambda^{\prime}(1/2,\phi\times\theta(\chi))

\displaystyle=-2\ln(\varepsilon_{k})h_{k}\sum\limits_{A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})}\chi(A)\left[\left(\frac{\operatorname{vol}(K_{A,W})}{4}\right)\Phi(f_{0,A},\mathfrak{G}(W_{A}))+\operatorname{CT}\langle\langle f_{0,A}^{+}(\tau),\theta_{L_{A,W}^{\perp}}(\tau)\otimes\mathcal{E}_{L_{A,W}}(\tau)\rangle\rangle\right].

Here,
h_{k}=\#C(\mathcal{O}_{k})
denotes the class number, and
\varepsilon_{k}
the fundamental unit of

\mathcal{O}_{k}^{\times}\cong\mu(k)\times\langle\varepsilon_{k}\rangle
.

1.1.3.
Relations to arithmetic Hirzebruch-Zagier divisors, Birch-Swinnerton-Dyer constants, and periods

To derive applications to arithmetic heights in the first setting (i) with
k=k(V_{A,0})
an imaginary quadratic field,
we first recall the arithmetic height formula implied by the combined works of Bruinier-Yang
[
13
]

and Andreatta-Goren-Howard-Madapusi Pera
[
1
]
for the general setup we consider above for Theorem
1.2
.
Hence, let us again take
(V,Q)
to be any rational quadratic space of signature
(n,2)
. Since we know that the regularized theta
lift
\Phi(f,\cdot)
gives the automorphic Green’s function for the divisor
Z(f)\subset X_{K}
, we have a supply of arithmetic divisors

\widehat{Z}(f)=(Z(f),\Phi(f,\cdot))
in the corresponding arithmetic Chow group of codimension one cycles on
X_{K}
. It then makes
sense to consider the arithmetic/Faltings height
[\widehat{Z}(f),Z(V_{0})]
of such a divisor
\widehat{Z}(f)
along the a CM cycle
Z(V_{0})
.
As we explain for Theorem
7.5
below, if
d_{k}
is odd, then the combined works of Bruinier-Yang
[
13
, Theorem 4.7]

and Andreatta-Goren-Howard-Madapusi-Pera
[
1
, Theorem A]
imply that we have the arithmetic height formula

\displaystyle\left[\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}(f),\mathcal{Z}(V_{0})\right]=\left[\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}(f),\mathcal{Z}(V_{0})\right]_{\operatorname{Fal}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\deg(\mathcal{Z}(V_{0}))}{2}\cdot\left(c_{f}^{+}(0,0)\cdot\kappa_{L_{0}}(0,0)+L^{\prime}(0,\xi_{1-n/2}(f),\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}})\right).

Here, we suppress the discussion of the extensions
\mathcal{Z}(f)
of the divisors
Z(f)
and
\mathcal{Z}(V_{0})
of the
CM cycles
Z(V_{0})
to the integral model
\mathcal{X}=\mathcal{X}_{K}
of
X_{K}
,
and refer to the discussion below for more details.
Writing
Z_{A}(\mu,m)\in X_{A}\cong Y_{0}(N)^{2}
for the arithmetic special
divisors for the spaces
(V_{A},Q_{A})
we consider above,
with
Z_{A}^{c}(\mu,m)\subset X_{0}(N)^{2}
their extensions to
the compactification
X_{A}^{\star}\cong X_{0}(N)\times X_{0}(N)
, we obtain the following consequence.
Here, we write
\mathcal{Z}_{A}(\mu,m)
to denote the extension of
Z_{A}(\mu,m)
to the integral model

\mathcal{X}=\mathcal{X}_{K_{A}}=\mathcal{Y}_{0}(N)\times\mathcal{Y}_{0}(N)
,
and
\mathcal{Z}^{c}_{A}(\mu,m)
to denote the extension of
Z_{A}^{c}(\mu,m)
to the integral model

\mathcal{X}^{\star}=\mathcal{X}_{K_{A}}^{\times}\cong\mathcal{X}_{0}(N)\times\mathcal{X}_{0}(N)
.

Theorem 1.4

(Theorem
7.8
, Corollary
7.9
, Corollary
A.10
)
.

Retain the setup of Theorem
1.3
(i).
Then, for any class group character
\chi\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})^{\vee}
, we have the central derivative value formula

\displaystyle\Lambda^{\prime}(1/2,\phi\times\theta(\chi))

\displaystyle=-2\pi\sum\limits_{A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})}\chi(A)\left[\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}_{A}(f_{0,A}):\mathcal{Z}(V_{A,0})\right]

for the completed Rankin-Selberg
L
-function
\Lambda(s,\phi\times\theta(\chi))
of
\phi
times
the Hecke theta series
\theta(\chi)
, where each term on the right-hand side denotes the arithmetic height of the arithmetic special divisor

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}_{A}(f_{0,A})

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\mu\in L_{A}^{\vee}/L_{A}}\sum\limits_{m\in{\bf{Q}}\atop m>0}c_{f_{0,A}}^{+}(\mu,-m)\mathcal{Z}_{A}(\mu,m)

on the
integral model
\mathcal{X}=\mathcal{Y}_{0}(N)\times\mathcal{Y}_{0}(N)
of the Hilbert modular surface

X=Y_{0}(N)\times Y_{0}(N)
evaluated along the corresponding CM cycle

\mathcal{Z}(V_{A,0})\subset\mathcal{X}=\mathcal{Y}_{0}(N)\times\mathcal{Y}_{0}(N)
.
Here, each
\mathcal{Z}_{A}(\mu,m)
is the arithmetic Hirzebruch-Zagier divisor

\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}_{A}(\mu,m)=(\mathcal{Z}_{A}(\mu,m),\Phi^{L_{A}}_{\mu,m})

on
\mathcal{X}=\mathcal{Y}_{0}(N)\times\mathcal{Y}_{0}(N)
for
the theta lift
\Phi^{L_{A}}_{\mu,m}
of the corresponding Poincaré series
F_{\mu,m}^{L_{A}}
described below.
We can also extend arithmetic divisors to the compactification

\mathcal{X}^{\star}\cong\mathcal{X}_{0}(N)\times\mathcal{X}_{0}(N)

as described in
(\ref{Hilbertboundary})
to derive the corresponding formula

\displaystyle\Lambda^{\prime}(1/2,\phi\times\theta(\chi))

\displaystyle=-2\pi\sum\limits_{A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})}\chi(A)\left[\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}_{A}^{c}(f_{0,A}):\mathcal{Z}(V_{A,0})\right].

In particular, if the newform
\phi\in S_{2}^{\operatorname{new}}(\Gamma_{0}(N))
parametrizes an elliptic curve
E/{\bf{Q}}
,
then we have the central derivative value formula

\displaystyle\Lambda^{\prime}(E/K,\chi,1)

\displaystyle=-2\pi\sum\limits_{A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})}\chi(A)\left[\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}_{A}(f_{0,A}):\mathcal{Z}(V_{A,0})\right]

for the Hasse-Weil
L
-function
\Lambda(E/K,\chi,s)=\Lambda(s-1/2,\phi\times\theta(\chi))
of
E
over
K
twisted by
\chi

in terms of arithmetic divisors on the Hilbert modular surface
\mathcal{Y}_{0}(N)\times\mathcal{Y}_{0}(N)\longrightarrow\operatorname{Spec}({\bf{Z}})
.
Extending to the compactification
\mathcal{X}_{0}(N)\times\mathcal{X}_{0}(N)\longrightarrow\operatorname{Spec}({\bf{Z}})
,
we derive the central derivative value formula

\displaystyle\Lambda^{\prime}(E/K,\chi,1)

\displaystyle=-2\pi\sum\limits_{A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})}\chi(A)\left[\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}_{A}^{c}(f_{0,A}):\mathcal{Z}(V_{A,0})\right].

By comparison with the Gross-Zagier formula (Theorem
1.1
) for
L^{\prime}(E/K,\chi,1)
, we also derive the relation

\displaystyle 2\pi\cdot\widehat{h}_{k}(y_{\chi}^{\phi})

\displaystyle=\frac{|D|^{\frac{1}{2}}}{8\pi^{2}||\phi||^{2}}\cdot\Lambda^{\prime}(1/2,\phi\times\theta(\chi))=-2\pi\sum\limits_{A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})}\chi(A)\left[\widehat{Z}^{c}(f_{0,A}):\mathcal{Z}(V_{A,0})\right],

between heights of Heegner divisors on the modular curve
X_{0}(N)
and arithmetic heights of arithmetic Hirzeburch-Zagier
divisors on the Hilbert modular surface
X_{0}(N)\times X_{0}(N)
.

Here, in both cases on the quadratic field
k
, we also use Theorem
1.2
to derive some results towards the refined
conjecture of Birch and Swinnerton-Dyer for the principal class group character
\chi_{0}={\bf{1}}\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})^{\vee}

and Euler characteristic calculations, using known results on the Iwasawa main conjectures in the setting of Mordell-Weil rank one.
To fix ideas, let
E
be an elliptic curve of conductor
N
defined over
{\bf{Q}}
,
parametrized via modularity by a cuspidal newform
\phi\in S^{\operatorname{new}}_{2}(\Gamma_{0}(N))
as above.
Let
k
be a quadratic field with discriminant
d_{k}

and character
\eta_{k}(\cdot)=\left(\frac{d_{k}}{\cdot}\right)
. We consider the Mordell-Weil group

E(k)\cong{\bf{Z}}^{r_{E}(k)}\oplus E(k)_{\operatorname{tors}}
, along with that of the quadratic twist

E^{(d_{k})}({\bf{Q}})\cong{\bf{Z}}^{r_{E^{(d_{k})}}({\bf{Q}})}\oplus E^{(d_{k})}({\bf{Q}})_{\operatorname{tors}}

and
E({\bf{Q}})\cong{\bf{Z}}^{r_{E}({\bf{Q}})}\oplus E({\bf{Q}})_{\operatorname{tors}}
over
{\bf{Q}}
.
Recall that the conjecture of Birch and Swinnerton-Dyer predicts that the completed
L
-function

\displaystyle\Lambda(E/K,s)

\displaystyle=\Lambda(s-1/2,\phi\times\theta({\bf{1}}))=\Lambda(s-1/2,\Pi(\phi))

\displaystyle=\Lambda(s-1/2,\phi)\Lambda(s-1/2,\phi\otimes\eta_{k})=\Lambda(E,s)\Lambda(E^{(d_{k})},s)

has order of vanishing
\operatorname{ord}_{s=1}\Lambda(E/K,s)=r_{E}(k)
.
Moreover, the leading term in the Taylor series expansion around
s=1
of this function
is expected to be given by the corresponding Birch-Swinnerton-Dyer constant
\kappa_{E}(k)
,
which is defined more generally as follows. For any number field
K
, we put

(1)

\displaystyle\kappa_{E}(K)

\displaystyle:=\frac{\#{\mbox{{Sh}}}(E/K)\cdot T(E/K)\cdot R(E/K)\cdot\Omega_{\infty}(E/K)}{|d_{K}|^{\frac{1}{2}}|E(K)_{\operatorname{tors}}|^{2}}.

Here,
\#{\mbox{{Sh}}}(E/K)
denotes the cardinality of the conjecturally finite Tate-Shafarevich group

\displaystyle{\mbox{{Sh}}}(E/K)

\displaystyle=\ker\left(H^{1}(K,E)\longrightarrow\prod\limits_{w}H^{1}(K_{w},E)\right).

We write
R(E/K)
to denote the regulator, defined for any basis
\{e_{j}\}_{j}
of
E(K)/E(K)_{\operatorname{tors}}

by the determinant of the corresponding height matrix
(\left[e_{i},e_{j}\right]_{\operatorname{NT}})_{i,j}
,

\displaystyle R(E/K)=\det\left(\left[e_{i},e_{j}\right]_{\operatorname{NT}}\right)_{i,j}.

We write
T(E/K)
to denote the product over the local Tamagawa factors,

\displaystyle T(E/K)

\displaystyle=\prod\limits_{v<\infty\atop v\subset\mathcal{O}_{k}~~~\text{prime}}\left[E(K_{v}):E_{0}(K_{v})\right]\cdot\left|\frac{\omega}{\omega_{v}^{*}}\right|_{v},

where
\omega
denotes a fixed invariant differential for
E/k
, and each
\omega_{v}^{*}
denotes
the local Néron differential at
v
. We then define the corresponding archimedean local periods

\displaystyle\Omega_{\infty}(E/K)

\displaystyle=\prod\limits_{{v\mid\infty\atop v:k\hookrightarrow{\bf{R}}}\atop\text{real}}\int\limits_{E(k_{v})\cong E({\bf{R}})}|\omega|\cdot\prod\limits_{{v\mid\infty\atop v,\overline{v}:k\hookrightarrow{\bf{C}}}\atop\text{complex}}2\int\limits_{E(k_{v})\cong E({\bf{C}})}\omega\wedge\overline{\omega}.

Theorem 1.5
.

Let
E/{\bf{Q}}
be an elliptic curve parametrized by a newform
\phi\in S_{2}^{\operatorname{new}}(\Gamma_{0}(N))
.
Let
k
be a quadratic field of discriminant
d_{k}
prime to
N
and character
\eta_{k}(\cdot)=(\frac{d_{k}}{\cdot})
.
Assume
E
has semistable reduction, hence that
N
squarefree. Assume that the completed
L
-function

\displaystyle\Lambda(E/K,s)=\Lambda(E,s)\Lambda(E^{(d_{k})},s)=\Lambda(s-1/2,\phi)\Lambda(s-1/2,\phi\otimes\eta_{k})

has order of vanishing
\operatorname{ord}_{s=1}\Lambda(E/K,s)=1
, so that exactly one of the central values
\Lambda(E,1)=\Lambda(1/2,\phi)
or

\Lambda(E^{(d_{k})},1)=\Lambda(1/2,\phi\otimes\eta_{k})
vanishes. Write
\left[e,e\right]
to denote either the regulator
R(E/{\bf{Q}})

or the regulator
R(E^{(d_{k})}/{\bf{Q}})
according to which factor vanishes. Let us also assume for each prime
p\geq 5
that

•

The residual Galois representations
E[p]
and
E^{(d_{k})}[p]
are irreducible.

•

There exists a prime
l\mid N
distinct from
p
where
E[p]
is ramified, and a prime
q\mid N
distinct from
p
where
E^{(d_{k})}[p]
is ramified.

Then, up to powers of
2
and
3
, we have the following unconditional identifications for the constant(s)

\displaystyle\kappa_{E}({\bf{Q}})\cdot\kappa_{E^{(d_{k})}}({\bf{Q}})

\displaystyle=\frac{\#{\mbox{{Sh}}}(E/{\bf{Q}})\cdot\#{\mbox{{Sh}}}(E^{(d_{k})}/{\bf{Q}})\cdot[e,e]\cdot T(E/{\bf{Q}})\cdot T(E^{(d_{k})}{\bf{Q}})\cdot\Omega_{\infty}(E/{\bf{Q}})\cdot\Omega_{\infty}(E^{(d_{k})}/{\bf{Q}})}{\#E({\bf{Q}})_{\operatorname{tors}}^{2}\cdot\#E^{(d_{k})}({\bf{Q}})_{\operatorname{tors}}^{2}}.

(i)

If
k
is imaginary quadratic with
\eta_{k}(-N)=-\eta_{k}(N)=-1
, then

\displaystyle\kappa_{E}({\bf{Q}})\cdot\kappa_{E^{(d_{k})}}({\bf{Q}})\approx\Lambda^{\prime}(E/k,1)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\pi}{2}\sum\limits_{A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})}\Phi(f_{0,A},Z(V_{A,0}))=-2\pi\sum\limits_{A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})}\left[\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}_{A}^{c}(f_{0,A}):\mathcal{Z}(V_{A,0})\right].

(ii)

If
k
is real quadratic with
\eta_{k}(-N)=\eta_{k}(N)=-1
, then

\displaystyle\kappa_{E}({\bf{Q}})\cdot\kappa_{E^{(d_{k})}}({\bf{Q}})\approx\Lambda^{\prime}(E/k,1)

\displaystyle=-2\ln(\varepsilon_{k})h_{k}\sum\limits_{A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})}\left[\left(\frac{w_{k}\ln(\varepsilon_{k})}{4h_{k}}\right)\Phi(f_{0,A},\mathfrak{G}(W_{A}))+\operatorname{CT}\langle\langle f_{0,A}^{+}(\tau),\theta_{L_{A,W}^{\perp}}(\tau)\otimes\mathcal{E}_{L_{A,W}}(\tau)\rangle\rangle\right].

Here, in either case, we write
\approx
to denote equality up to powers of
2
and
3
. Moreover, in each case,
the central derivative value
\Lambda^{\prime}(E/K,1)
lies in the ring of periods
\mathcal{P}
described by Kontsevich-Zagier
[
39
]
.

1.1.4.
Relation to quadratic spaces of signature
(1,2)
and Fourier coefficients of half-integral weight forms

Finally, we explain in Appendix A how to develop similar ideas via the rational quadratic space

(2)

\displaystyle(V,Q)

\displaystyle=(\operatorname{Mat}_{2\times 2}^{\operatorname{tr}=0}({\bf{Q}}),N\det(\cdot))

of signature
(1,1)
from Bruinier-Yang
[
13
, Theorem 1.5, Theorem 7.7,
\lx@sectionsign
7]
to reprove the full Gross-Zagier formula (Theorem
1.1
).
We refer to Theorem
A.9
for details.

In both this case and the case of the signature
(2,2)
spaces
(V_{A},Q_{A})
described above, we explain in the second
Appendix B the known and conjectural links between the central derivative values and Fourier coefficients of half-integral weight Maass forms.
We refer to Theorem
B.5
for the main result derived in Appendix B, using the main theorem of Bruinier-Funke-Imamoglu
[
11
]

for quadratic spaces of the form
(\ref{(1,1)})
to interpret the sums
\Phi(f,Z(V_{0}))
and
\Phi(f,\mathfrak{G}(W))
of Theorem
1.2
as the Fourier
coefficients of some harmonic weak Maass form of weight
1/2
. To describe this in some more detail, let
k
be any quadratic field of discriminant

d_{k}=D
prime to
N
, and let
\mathcal{Q}_{d_{k}}
denote the class group of binary quadratic forms

q_{a,b,c}(x,y)=ax^{2}+bxy+cy^{2}
of discriminant
d_{k}=b^{2}-4ac
.
We write
[q_{a,b,c}]=[a,b,c]\in\mathcal{Q}_{d_{k}}
to denote the corresponding class.
We have a well-known identification of class groups

\displaystyle\varphi:\mathcal{Q}_{d_{k}}\cong C(\mathcal{O}_{k}),\quad[a,b,c]

\displaystyle\longmapsto[a,(-b+\sqrt{d_{k}})/2].

For each class
A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})
, we fix an integral ideal representative
\mathfrak{a}\subset\mathcal{O}_{k}
,
so that the norm form
[Q_{\mathfrak{a}}]\in\mathcal{Q}_{d_{k}}
represents the corresponding class,
i.e.
\varphi([Q_{\mathfrak{a}}])=A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})
. We then define
\mathcal{L}_{A}=\mathcal{L}_{A}(N)\subset V

to be the lattice of the space
(\ref{(1,1)})
given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{A}

\displaystyle=\left\{\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}b&-a/N\\
c&-b\end{array}\right):a,b,c\in{\bf{Z}},\quad N\det\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}b&-a/N\\
c&-b\end{array}\right)\Bigg|_{L_{A,U}}\equiv-q_{a,b,c}\text{ for }\varphi([a,b,c])=A\right\},

with dual lattice

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{A}^{\vee}

\displaystyle=\left\{\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}b/2N&-a/N\\
c&-b/2N\end{array}\right):a,b,c\in{\bf{Z}},\quad N\det\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}b&-a/N\\
c&-b\end{array}\right)\Bigg|_{L_{A,U}}\equiv-q_{a,b,c}\text{ for }\varphi([a,b,c])=A\right\}.

As explained in Lemma
A.2
(cf.
[
13
, Lemma 7.4]
or
[
12
, Lemma 7.3]
),
if we assume
\phi\in S_{2}^{\operatorname{new}}(\Gamma_{0}(N))
is invariant under the Fricke involution
w_{N}
,
then there exists both a vector-valued lift
g_{A}=g_{\phi,A}\in S_{3/2}^{\operatorname{old}}(\overline{\omega}_{\mathcal{L}_{A}})
of the
Shimura lift of
\phi
, as well as a harmonic weak Maass form
f_{1/2,A}\in H_{1/2}(\overline{\omega}_{\mathcal{L}_{A}})
such that

•

We have the relation
\xi_{1/2}(f_{1/2,A})=g_{A}/||g_{A}||^{2}
.

•

The Fourier coefficients
c_{f_{1/2,A}}^{+}(\mu,m)
lie in the Hecke field
{\bf{Q}}(\phi)={\bf{Q}}
of the newform
\phi
.

•

The constant Fourier coefficient
c_{f_{1/2,A}}^{+}(0,0)
vanishes.

When
k
is an imaginary quadratic field, the map
\mathcal{S}_{\mu_{0},m_{0}}

of Gross-Kohnen-Zagier
[
27
,
\lx@sectionsign
II.4]
can be used to relate the
L
-series

L(s,g_{A}\times\theta_{\mathcal{L}_{A,0}^{\perp}})
to
L(s+1/2,\mathcal{S}_{\mu,m}(g))
(see Lemma
A.1
).
We derive the following relations.

Theorem 1.6

(Theorem
B.5
)
.

We have via Theorem
5.12
and Theorem
5.14
for the quadratic space
(V,Q)
of signature
(1,2)
described above
the following identification of central derivative values of
L
-functions as Fourier coefficients of half-integral weight forms.

(i)

Let
k
be an imaginary quadratic field of discriminant
D=d_{k}<0
.
Assume as in Lemma
A.1
that
m=-D/4N
for
D=-4Nm
with
D\equiv r^{2}\bmod 4N
, and take
\mu=\mu_{r}
.
Then, for
\chi
and character of the ideal class group
C(\mathcal{O}_{k})
, we have the relation

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})}\chi(A)\cdot c_{g_{A}}(\mu,m)\cdot\operatorname{tr}_{\mu,m}\left(\Phi(f_{A,1/2})\right)

\displaystyle=-\frac{|D|^{\frac{1}{2}}}{16\pi^{2}||\phi||^{2}}\cdot L^{\prime}(1/2,\phi\times\theta(\chi)).

(ii)

Let
k
be a real quadratic field of discriminant
d_{k}>0
,
and
x\in\Omega_{A,\mu,m}({\bf{Q}})\subset\mathcal{K}_{A}
a positive norm vector
with orthogonal complement
W_{A}=W_{A}(x):=x^{\perp}\subset V
as in Proposition
B.4
(ii).
We have for each class
A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})
the relation

\displaystyle\operatorname{tr}_{\mu,m}(\Phi(f_{1/2,A}))

\displaystyle=-\frac{4h_{k}}{w_{k}\ln(\varepsilon_{k})}\cdot L^{\prime}(0,g_{A}\times\theta_{\mathcal{L}_{A,W}^{\perp}}).

As we describe in Appendix B, it should be possible to extend these calculations
in the real quadratic case (Theorem
1.2
(ii)) to derive an affirmative answer to the conjecture posed implicitly in
Bruinier-Ono
[
12
, Theorem 1.1 (2)]
, relating Fourier coefficients of the holomorphic part of the vector-valued Shimura lift to the
nonvanishing central derivative values of the real quadratic twisted
L
-function.
We speculate that such relations via Green’s functions along CM cycles or geodesics to singular moduli should be possible to establish
for Hilbert Maass cusp forms in the setup of signature
(2,2)
described in Theorem
1.2
and Theorem
1.3
above;
see Conjecture
B.8
. We plan to return to this in a subsequent work, however describe it here as it fits organically into the topic of proving
the formula of Gross-Zagier (Theorem
1.1
) via sums of automorphic Green’s functions along CM cycles of spin Shimura varieties.

2.
Quadratic spaces and spin groups

Let
k={\bf{Q}}(\sqrt{d})
be a quadratic field of discriminant

\displaystyle d_{k}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}d&\text{ if $d\equiv 1\bmod 4$}\\
4d&\text{ if $d\equiv 2,3\bmod 4$}.\end{cases}

We write
\mathcal{O}_{k}
for its ring of integers,
C(\mathfrak{O}_{k})
for its ideal class group, and
h_{k}=\#C(\mathcal{O}_{k})
its class number.
We also write
w_{k}=\#\mu(k)
to denote the number of roots of unity in
k
.

2.1.
Quadratic spaces associated to class groups of quadratic fields

Fix an ideal class
A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})
. Let
\mathfrak{a}\subset\mathcal{O}_{k}
be an integral ideal representative.
Consider the corresponding fractional ideal
\mathfrak{a}_{\bf{Q}}=\mathfrak{a}\otimes_{\bf{Z}}{\bf{Q}}
,
which when equipped with the norm form
Q_{\mathfrak{a}}(\lambda):=\lambda\mapsto{\bf{N}}_{k/{\bf{Q}}}(\lambda)/{\bf{N}}\mathfrak{a}

can be viewed as a rational quadratic space over
{\bf{Q}}
.
That is,
(\mathfrak{a}_{\bf{Q}},Q_{\mathfrak{a}})
determines a rational quadratic space of signature

\displaystyle\begin{cases}(2,0)&\text{ if $d<0$ so that $k={\bf{Q}}(\sqrt{d})$ is imaginary quadratic}\\
(1,1)&\text{ if $d>0$ so that $k={\bf{Q}}(\sqrt{d})$ is real quadratic}.\end{cases}

On the other hand, we can also consider the corresponding isomorphic quadratic space
(\mathfrak{a}_{\bf{Q}},-Q_{\mathfrak{a}})
of signature

\displaystyle\begin{cases}(0,2)&\text{ if $d<0$ so that $k={\bf{Q}}(\sqrt{d})$ is imaginary quadratic}\\
(1,1)&\text{ if $d>0$ so that $k={\bf{Q}}(\sqrt{d})$ is real quadratic}.\end{cases}

Thus, we obtain for each class
A=[\mathfrak{a}]\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})
a rational quadratic space of signature (2,2) defined by

(3)

\displaystyle(V_{A},Q_{A}),\quad V_{A}:=\mathfrak{a}_{\bf{Q}}+\mathfrak{a}_{\bf{Q}},\quad Q_{A}(z)=Q_{A}((z_{1},z_{2})):=Q_{\mathfrak{a}}(z_{1})-Q_{\mathfrak{a}}(z_{2}).

2.1.1.
Anisotopic subspaces

Henceforth, we consider the isotropic quadratic space
(V_{A},Q_{A})
of signature
(2,2)

for each class
A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})
. We consider the corresponding isotropic subspaces

(V_{A,1},Q_{A,1})=(V_{\mathfrak{a}},Q_{\mathfrak{a}})
and
(V_{A,2},Q_{A,2})=(\mathfrak{a}_{\bf{Q}},-Q_{\mathfrak{a}})
of respective
signatures
(2,0)
and
(0,2)
when
k={\bf{Q}}(\sqrt{d})
is imaginary quadratic (
d<0
), and signature
(1,1)
when
k={\bf{Q}}(\sqrt{d})
is real quadratic
(d>0)
.

2.2.
Spin groups

Let
V=(V,Q)
be a rational quadratic space of signature
(n,2)
for any integer
n\geq 0
.
We consider the reductive group
\operatorname{GSpin}(V)
over
{\bf{Q}}
, which fits into the short exact sequence

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle\longrightarrow{\bf{G}}_{m}\longrightarrow\operatorname{GSpin}(V)\longrightarrow\operatorname{SO}(V)\longrightarrow 1.

2.2.1.
General characterization

Given
R
a commutative ring with identity, we consider a quadratic space
(V,Q)
over
R
.
Hence,
V
is a projective
R
-module of finite rank equipped with a homogeneous function
Q:V\rightarrow R
of degree two
for which the corresponding symmetric pairing
(x,y)=Q(x+y)-Q(x)-Q(y)
is
R
-bilinear.
We call the space
(V,Q)

self-dual
if this pairing induces an isomorphism
V\cong\operatorname{Hom}(V,R)
.
We call the space
(V,Q)

non-degenerate
if its orthogonal complement

V^{\perp}=\left\{x\in V:(x,y)=0~~\forall y\in V\right\}
is
\{0\}
.

Let
C(V)=T(V)/I(V)
denote the Clifford algebra of
V
, given by the quotient of the tensor algebra

\displaystyle T(V)

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{m=0}^{\infty}V^{\otimes}=R\oplus V\oplus(V\otimes_{R}V)\oplus\cdots

of
V
by the two-sided ideal
I(V)
generated by elements of the form
v\otimes v-Q(v)
for
v\in V
.
Note that we have canonical embeddings of
R
and
V
into
C(V)
.
In this way, we see that
R
-algebra
C(V)
is generated by the image of the natural injection
V\rightarrow C(V)
,
and that the grading on
T(V)
induces a
{\bf{Z}}/2{\bf{Z}}
grading

\displaystyle C(V)=C^{0}(V)\oplus C^{1}(V).

Concretely,
C^{0}(V)
is the
R
-subalgebra of
C(V)
generated by products of an even number of basis vectors,
and
C^{1}(V)
is the
R
-subalgebra of
C(V)
generated by products of an odd number of basis vectors.
We call
C^{0}(V)
the
even (or second) Clifford algebra of
V
.
For simplicity, we shall write
v_{1}\cdots v_{m}
to denote the element of
C(V)
represented
by
v_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{m}
(for
v_{1},\ldots,v_{m}\in V
).
Observe that for
v_{1},v_{2}\in V\subset C(V)
, we have
v_{i}^{2}=Q(v_{i})
(for
i=1,2
) and
v_{1}v_{2}+v_{2}v_{1}=(v_{1},v_{2})
.
In particular, we have that
v_{1}v_{2}=-v_{2}v_{1}
if and only if
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
are orthogonal.

As explained in
[
7
,
\lx@sectionsign
2.2]
, multiplication by
-1
defines an isometry on
V
, which by the universal property of
C(V)

(e.g.
[
7
, Proposition 2.3]
) induces an algebra automorphism
J:C(V)\rightarrow C(V)
known as the
canonical automorphism
.
If
2
is invertible in
R
, then the even Clifford algebra can be characterized equivalently as

\displaystyle C^{0}(V)

\displaystyle=\left\{v\in C(V):J(v)=v\right\}.

We also consider the anti-automorphism defined by

{}^{t}C_{V}\longrightarrow C_{V},(x_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{m})^{t}:=x_{m}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{1}
, better known as

canonical involution
on
C(V)
. This is the identity on
R\oplus V
, and gives rise to the
Clifford norm

\displaystyle N_{C(V)}:C(V)\longrightarrow C(V),\quad N_{C(V)}(x):={}^{t}xx.

On vectors
x\in V
, this reduces to
N_{C(V)}(x)=Q(x)
, and so
N_{C(V)}
can be viewed
as an extension of the quadratic form
Q
. Note that
N_{C(V)}
is not generally multiplicative.
We have the following classical results.

Proposition 2.1
.

Let
(V,Q)
be a non-degenerate quadratic space over a field
F
of characteristic
\operatorname{char}(F)\neq 2
.
Fix an orthogonal basis
v_{1},\ldots,v_{m}
of
V
, and let
\delta(V):=v_{1}\cdots v_{m}\in C(V)
. Let
d(V)
denote the discriminant of the
space
(V,Q)
, given by the determinant of the Gram matrix
((v_{i},v_{j}))_{i,j}
(for any basis
v_{1},\cdots v_{m}
of
V
).

(i)

We have that

\displaystyle\delta(V)^{2}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}(-1)^{\frac{m}{2}}2^{-m}d(V)\in F^{\times}/(F^{\times})^{2}&\text{ if $m\equiv 0\bmod 2$}\\
(-1)^{\frac{m-1}{2}}2^{-m}d(V)\in F^{\times}/(F^{\times})^{2}&\text{ if $m\equiv 1\bmod 2$}\end{cases}.

(ii)

The centre
Z(C(V))
of
C(V)
is given by

\displaystyle Z(C(V))

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}F&\text{ if $m\equiv 0\bmod 2$}\\
F+F\delta(V)&\text{ if $m\equiv 1\bmod 2$},\end{cases}

and the centre
Z(C^{0}(V))
of
C^{0}(V)
is given by

\displaystyle Z(C^{0}(V))

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}F+\delta(V)F&\text{ if $m\equiv 0\bmod 2$}\\
F&\text{ if $m\equiv 1\bmod 2$}.\end{cases},

Proof.

For (i), see
[
7
, Remark 2.5]
. For (ii), see
[
7
, Theorem 2.6]
. ∎

Let us now consider the Clifford group of
C(V)
,

\displaystyle G_{C(V)}

\displaystyle=\left\{x\in C(V):~~\text{$x$ is invertible and $xVJ(x)^{-1}=V$}\right\}.

We can then define the general spin group
\operatorname{GSpin}(V)
as the intersection
\operatorname{GSpin}(V)=G_{C(V)}\cap C^{0}(V)
,
and the spin group as the subgroup
\operatorname{Spin}(V)=\left\{x\in\operatorname{GSpin}(V):N_{C(V)}(x)=1\right\}

of elements of Clifford norm one.

Lemma 2.2
.

If
m=\dim_{F}(V)\leq 4
, then we have identifications

\displaystyle\operatorname{GSpin}(V)\cong\left\{x\in C^{0}(V):N_{C(V)}(x)\in F^{\times}\right\}~~~~~\text{and}~~~~\operatorname{Spin}(V)\cong\left\{x\in C^{0}(V):N_{C(V)}(x)=1\right\}.

Proof.

See
[
7
, Lemma 2.14]
. ∎

2.2.2.
Exceptional isomorphisms

Let
(V_{A},Q_{A})=(\mathfrak{a}_{\bf{Q}}+\mathfrak{a}_{\bf{Q}},Q_{\mathfrak{a}}-Q_{\mathfrak{a}})
be any
of the rational quadratic spaces of signature
(2,2)
considered above. Hence,
\dim_{{\bf{Q}}}(V_{A})=4
,

\dim_{\bf{Q}}C(V_{A})=2^{4}=16
, and
\dim_{\bf{Q}}C^{0}(V_{A})=8
.

Proposition 2.3
.

Let
k
be a quadratic field with class group
C(\mathcal{O}_{k})
.
Fix any class
A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})
, together with an integer ideal representative

\mathfrak{a}\subset\mathcal{O}_{k}
,
and write
Q_{\mathfrak{a}}(z)={\bf{N}}_{k/{\bf{Q}}}(z)/{\bf{N}}\mathfrak{a}
to denote the corresponding norm form.
Consider the corresponding rational quadratic space

(V_{A},Q_{A})=(\mathfrak{a}_{\bf{Q}}+\mathfrak{a}_{\bf{Q}},Q_{\mathfrak{a}}-Q_{\mathfrak{a}})
of signature
(2,2)
,
with Clifford algebra
C(V_{A})
and even Clifford subalgebra
C^{0}(V_{A})\subset C(V_{A})
. We have the identification

C^{0}(V_{A})\cong M_{2}({\bf{Q}})^{2}
, from which we derive exceptional isomorphisms

\operatorname{Spin}(V_{A})\cong\operatorname{SL}_{2}^{2}

and
\operatorname{GSpin}(V_{A})\cong\operatorname{GL}_{2}^{2}
of algebraic groups over
{\bf{Q}}
.

Proof.

Fix a
{\bf{Z}}
-basis
[\alpha_{\mathfrak{a}},z_{\mathfrak{a}}]
of the chosen
integral ideal representative
\mathfrak{a}\subset\mathcal{O}_{k}
for each class
A
.
We also let
Q_{\mathfrak{a}}(z)=N_{k/{\bf{Q}}}(z)/{\bf{N}}\mathfrak{a}
denote the norm form.
Here, writing
\tau\in\operatorname{Gal}(k/{\bf{Q}})
to denote the nontrivial automorphism,
we define the norm
{\bf{N}}_{k/{\bf{Q}}}(z)=zz^{\tau}

and the trace
\operatorname{Tr}_{k/{\bf{Q}}}(z)=z+z^{\tau}
. Consider the basis

\displaystyle v_{1}=(\alpha_{\mathfrak{a}},0),\quad v_{2}=(z_{\mathfrak{a}},0),\quad v_{3}=(0,\alpha_{\mathfrak{a}}),\quad v_{4}=(0,z_{\mathfrak{a}}).

We compute the inner products

\displaystyle(v_{1},v_{1})_{A}

\displaystyle=Q_{\mathfrak{a}}(2\alpha_{\mathfrak{a}})-2Q_{\mathfrak{a}}(\alpha_{\mathfrak{a}})={\bf{N}}\mathfrak{a}^{-1}2{\bf{N}}_{k/{\bf{Q}}}(\alpha_{\mathfrak{a}})

\displaystyle(v_{1},v_{2})_{A}

\displaystyle=Q_{\mathfrak{a}}(\alpha_{\mathfrak{a}}+z_{\mathfrak{a}})-Q_{\mathfrak{a}}(\alpha_{\mathfrak{a}})-Q_{\mathfrak{a}}(z_{\mathfrak{a}})={\bf{N}}\mathfrak{a}^{-1}\operatorname{Tr}_{k/{\bf{Q}}}(z_{\mathfrak{a}}\alpha_{\mathfrak{a}}^{\tau})=(v_{2},v_{1})_{A}

\displaystyle(v_{1},v_{3})_{A}

\displaystyle=Q_{\mathfrak{a}}(\alpha_{\mathfrak{a}})-Q_{\mathfrak{a}}(\alpha_{\mathfrak{a}})-Q_{\mathfrak{a}}(\alpha_{\mathfrak{a}})+Q_{\mathfrak{a}}(\alpha_{\mathfrak{a}})=0=(v_{3},v_{1})_{A}

\displaystyle(v_{1},v_{4})_{A}

\displaystyle=Q_{\mathfrak{a}}(\alpha_{\mathfrak{a}})-Q_{\mathfrak{a}}(z_{\mathfrak{a}})-Q_{\mathfrak{a}}(\alpha_{\mathfrak{a}})+Q_{\mathfrak{a}}(z_{\mathfrak{a}})=0=(v_{4},v_{1})_{A}

\displaystyle(v_{2},v_{2})_{A}

\displaystyle=Q_{\mathfrak{a}}(2z_{\mathfrak{a}})-2Q_{\mathfrak{a}}(z_{\mathfrak{a}})={\bf{N}}\mathfrak{a}^{-1}(2{\bf{N}}_{k/{\bf{Q}}}(z_{\mathfrak{a}}))

\displaystyle(v_{2},v_{3})_{A}

\displaystyle=Q_{\mathfrak{a}}(z_{\mathfrak{a}})-Q_{\mathfrak{a}}(\alpha_{\mathfrak{a}})-Q_{\mathfrak{a}}(z_{\mathfrak{a}})-Q_{\mathfrak{a}}(\alpha_{\mathfrak{a}})=0=(v_{3},v_{2})_{A}

\displaystyle(v_{2},v_{4})_{A}

\displaystyle=(Q_{\mathfrak{a}}(z_{\mathfrak{a}})-Q_{\mathfrak{a}}(z_{\mathfrak{a}}))-Q_{\mathfrak{a}}(z_{\mathfrak{a}})+Q_{\mathfrak{a}}(z_{\mathfrak{a}})=0=(v_{4},v_{2})_{A}

\displaystyle(v_{3},v_{3})_{A}

\displaystyle=-Q_{\mathfrak{a}}(2\alpha_{\mathfrak{a}})+2Q_{\mathfrak{a}}(\alpha_{\mathfrak{a}})=-{\bf{N}}\mathfrak{a}^{-1}2{\bf{N}}_{k/{\bf{Q}}}(\alpha_{\mathfrak{a}})=-(v_{1},v_{1})_{A}

\displaystyle(v_{3},v_{4})_{A}

\displaystyle=-Q_{\mathfrak{a}}(\alpha_{\mathfrak{a}}+z_{\mathfrak{a}})+Q_{\mathfrak{a}}(\alpha_{\mathfrak{a}})+Q_{\mathfrak{a}}(z_{\mathfrak{a}})=-{\bf{N}}\mathfrak{a}^{-1}\operatorname{Tr}_{k/{\bf{Q}}}(z_{\mathfrak{a}}\alpha_{\mathfrak{a}}^{\tau})=(v_{4},v_{3})_{A}=-(v_{1},v_{2})_{A}

\displaystyle(v_{4},v_{4})_{A}

\displaystyle=-Q_{\mathfrak{a}}(2z_{\mathfrak{a}})+2Q_{\mathfrak{a}}(z_{\mathfrak{a}})=-{\bf{N}}\mathfrak{a}^{-1}2{\bf{N}}_{k/{\bf{Q}}}(z_{\mathfrak{a}})=-(v_{2},v_{2})_{A}.

We then compute the determinant of the corresponding Gram matrix

\displaystyle d(V_{A})=\det((v_{i},v_{j})_{A})_{i,j}=\det\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\frac{2{\bf{N}}_{k/{\bf{Q}}}(\alpha_{\mathfrak{a}})}{{\bf{N}}\mathfrak{a}}&\frac{\operatorname{Tr}_{k/{\bf{Q}}}(z_{\mathfrak{a}}\alpha_{\mathfrak{a}}^{\tau})}{{\bf{N}}\mathfrak{a}}&0&0\\
\frac{\operatorname{Tr}_{k/{\bf{Q}}}(z_{\mathfrak{a}}\alpha_{\mathfrak{a}}^{\tau})}{{\bf{N}}\mathfrak{a}}&\frac{2{\bf{N}}_{k/{\bf{Q}}}(z_{\mathfrak{a}})}{{\bf{N}}\mathfrak{a}}&0&0\\
0&0&-\frac{2{\bf{N}}_{k/{\bf{Q}}}(\alpha_{\mathfrak{a}})}{{\bf{N}}\mathfrak{a}}&-\frac{\operatorname{Tr}_{k/{\bf{Q}}}(z_{\mathfrak{a}}\alpha_{\mathfrak{a}}^{\tau})}{{\bf{N}}\mathfrak{a}}\\
0&0&-\frac{\operatorname{Tr}_{k/{\bf{Q}}}(z_{\mathfrak{a}}\alpha_{\mathfrak{a}}^{\tau})}{{\bf{N}}\mathfrak{a}}&-\frac{2{\bf{N}}_{k/{\bf{Q}}}(z_{\mathfrak{a}})}{{\bf{N}}\mathfrak{a}}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{2{\bf{N}}_{k/{\bf{Q}}}(\alpha_{\mathfrak{a}})}{{\bf{N}}\mathfrak{a}}\left|\begin{array}[]{ccc}\frac{2{\bf{N}}_{k/{\bf{Q}}}(z_{\mathfrak{a}})}{{\bf{N}}\mathfrak{a}}&0&0\\
0&-\frac{2{\bf{N}}_{k/{\bf{Q}}}(\alpha_{\mathfrak{a}})}{{\bf{N}}\mathfrak{a}}&-\frac{\operatorname{Tr}_{k/{\bf{Q}}}(z_{\mathfrak{a}}\alpha_{\mathfrak{a}}^{\tau})}{{\bf{N}}\mathfrak{a}}\\
0&-\frac{\operatorname{Tr}_{k/{\bf{Q}}}(z_{\mathfrak{a}}\alpha_{\mathfrak{a}}^{\tau})}{{\bf{N}}\mathfrak{a}}&-\frac{2{\bf{N}}_{k/{\bf{Q}}}(z_{\mathfrak{a}})}{{\bf{N}}\mathfrak{a}}\end{array}\right|

\displaystyle-\frac{\operatorname{Tr}_{k/{\bf{Q}}}(z_{\mathfrak{a}}\alpha_{\mathfrak{a}}^{\tau})}{{\bf{N}}\mathfrak{a}}\left|\begin{array}[]{ccc}\frac{\operatorname{Tr}_{k/{\bf{Q}}}(z_{\mathfrak{a}}\alpha_{\mathfrak{a}}^{\tau})}{{\bf{N}}\mathfrak{a}}&0&0\\
0&-\frac{2{\bf{N}}_{k/{\bf{Q}}}(\alpha_{\mathfrak{a}})}{{\bf{N}}\mathfrak{a}}&-\frac{\operatorname{Tr}_{k/{\bf{Q}}}(z_{\mathfrak{a}}\alpha_{\mathfrak{a}}^{\tau})}{{\bf{N}}\mathfrak{a}}\\
0&-\frac{\operatorname{Tr}_{k/{\bf{Q}}}(z_{\mathfrak{a}}\alpha_{\mathfrak{a}}^{\tau})}{{\bf{N}}\mathfrak{a}}&-\frac{2{\bf{N}}_{k/{\bf{Q}}}(z_{\mathfrak{a}})}{{\bf{N}}\mathfrak{a}}\end{array}\right|

\displaystyle=\frac{4{\bf{N}}_{k/{\bf{Q}}}(z_{\mathfrak{a}}\alpha_{\mathfrak{a}})}{{\bf{N}}\mathfrak{a}^{2}}\left(\frac{4{\bf{N}}_{k/{\bf{Q}}}(z_{\mathfrak{a}}\alpha_{\mathfrak{a}})}{{\bf{N}}\mathfrak{a}^{2}}-\frac{\operatorname{Tr}_{k/{\bf{Q}}}(z_{\mathfrak{a}}\alpha_{\mathfrak{a}}^{\tau})^{2}}{{\bf{N}}\mathfrak{a}^{2}}\right)-\frac{\operatorname{Tr}_{k/{\bf{Q}}}(z_{\mathfrak{a}}\alpha_{\mathfrak{a}}^{\tau})^{2}}{{\bf{N}}\mathfrak{a}^{2}}\left(\frac{4{\bf{N}}_{k/{\bf{Q}}}(z_{\mathfrak{a}}\alpha_{\mathfrak{a}})}{{\bf{N}}\mathfrak{a}^{2}}-\frac{\operatorname{Tr}_{k/{\bf{Q}}}(z_{\mathfrak{a}}\alpha_{\mathfrak{a}}^{\tau})^{2}}{{\bf{N}}\mathfrak{a}^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{4{\bf{N}}_{k/{\bf{Q}}}(z_{\mathfrak{a}}\alpha_{\mathfrak{a}})}{{\bf{N}}\mathfrak{a}^{2}}-\frac{\operatorname{Tr}_{k/{\bf{Q}}}(z_{\mathfrak{a}}\alpha_{\mathfrak{a}}^{\tau})^{2}}{{\bf{N}}\mathfrak{a}^{2}}\right)^{2}\equiv 1\in{\bf{Q}}^{\times}/({\bf{Q}}^{\times})^{2}.

That is, we find that
d(V_{A})\in({\bf{Q}}^{\times})^{2}
is a nonzero rational square, and hence trivial.
Using the relation
\delta(V_{A})^{2}=2^{-4}d(V_{A})
of Proposition
2.1
(i),
we deduce that the volume form
\delta(V_{A})\in{\bf{Q}}^{\times}
must be rational.
We then deduce from Proposition
2.1
(ii) that
Z(C^{0}(V_{A}))={\bf{Q}}+\delta(V_{A}){\bf{Q}}={\bf{Q}}
,
and hence that the even Clifford algebra
C^{0}(V_{A})
of
\dim_{\bf{Q}}(C^{0}(V_{A}))=8

must be a direct sum of two isomorphic copies of a quaternion algebra
B
over
{\bf{Q}}
.
Using the classifications of Clifford algebras over
{\bf{R}}
, we see that
C(V_{A}\otimes{\bf{R}})\cong C_{2,2}({\bf{R}})\cong M_{4}({\bf{R}})

and
C^{0}(V_{A}\otimes{\bf{R}})\cong C_{2,2}^{0}({\bf{R}})\cong M_{2}({\bf{R}})\oplus M_{2}({\bf{R}})
.
Hence,
B
must be indefinite. Since the discriminant
d(V_{A})=1
is trivial,
we deduce that
B
must be the matrix algebra
M_{2}({\bf{Q}})
, with the Clifford norm corresponding to the reduced norm
homomorphism
\operatorname{nrd}:B\rightarrow{\bf{Q}}
, which for
B=M_{2}({\bf{Q}})
is simply the determinant

\det=\operatorname{nrd}
. The claimed isomorphisms for the spin groups then follow from the characterization given in Lemma
2.2
. ∎

Corollary 2.4
.

Fix
N\in{\bf{Z}}_{\geq 1}
. Let
L_{A}=L_{A}(N)\subset V_{A}
denote the lattice whose adelization
L_{A}\otimes\widehat{\bf{Z}}

is stabilized under the action via conjugation by
\operatorname{GSpin}(V_{A})({\bf{A}}_{f})\cong\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\bf{A}}_{f})^{2}

by the compact open subgroup
K_{0}(N)\oplus K_{0}(N)
, where

K_{0}(N)\subset\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\widehat{\bf{Z}})\subset\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\bf{A}}_{f})
denotes the congruence subgroup defined by

\displaystyle K_{0}(N)

\displaystyle=\left\{\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}a&b\\
c&d\end{array}\right)\in\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\widehat{\bf{Z}}):c\equiv 0\bmod N\right\}.

(i)

The lattice is
L_{A}=L_{A}(N)=N^{-1}\mathfrak{a}\oplus N^{-1}\mathfrak{a}
, with dual lattice

L_{A}^{\vee}=L_{A}(n)^{\vee}=\mathfrak{d}_{k}^{-1}N^{-1}\mathfrak{a}\oplus\mathfrak{d}_{k}^{-1}\mathfrak{a}

(ii)

The level of the lattice is
N=\{\min a\in{\bf{Z}}_{\geq 1}:aQ_{A}(\lambda)\in{\bf{Z}}\forall\gamma\in L_{A}^{\vee}\}
.

Proof.

Consider
\operatorname{GSpin}(V_{A})({\bf{A}}_{f})\cong\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\bf{A}}_{f})^{2}
acting on

V_{A}=\mathfrak{a}_{\bf{Q}}\oplus\mathfrak{a}_{\bf{Q}}\cong[\alpha_{\mathfrak{a}},z_{\mathfrak{a}}]{\bf{Q}}\oplus[\alpha_{\mathfrak{a}},z_{\mathfrak{a}}]{\bf{Q}}
by conjugation.
Here, we use the canonical embedding
V_{A}\rightarrow C(V_{A})
and the identification

C^{0}(V_{A})\cong M_{2}({\bf{Q}})\oplus M_{2}({\bf{Q}})
. Writing

\displaystyle R(N)

\displaystyle=\left\{\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}a&b\\
c&d\end{array}\right):c\equiv 0\bmod N\right\}\subset M_{2}({\bf{Q}})

to denote the Eichler order of level
N
, we can characterize
L_{A}=L_{A}(N)
as the lattice stabilized under conjugation by invertible elements of
R(N)\oplus R(N)
.
We claim that the conjugation action
g\cdot v=gvg^{-1}
for
g=(g_{1},g_{2})\in\operatorname{GSpin}(V_{A})({\bf{A}}_{f})\cong\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\bf{A}}_{f})^{2}

and
v=(v_{1},v_{2})\in\mathfrak{a}_{{\bf{A}}_{f}}\oplus\mathfrak{a}_{{\bf{A}}_{f}}
takes the simpler form

(g_{1},g_{2})\cdot(v_{1},v_{2})=(g_{1}v_{1}g_{1}^{-1},g_{2}v_{2}g_{2}^{-1})
,
for
g_{i}\in\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\bf{A}}_{f})
and
v_{i}\in\mathfrak{a}_{{\bf{A}}_{f}}=[\alpha_{\mathfrak{a}},z_{\mathfrak{a}}]{\bf{A}}_{f}
for
i=1,2
.
We can then see by inspection
that
L_{A}=L_{A}(N)=N^{-1}\mathfrak{a}\oplus N^{-1}\mathfrak{a}
is the stabilized lattice,
with dual lattice
L_{A}^{\vee}=L_{A}(N)^{\vee}=\mathfrak{d}_{k}^{-1}N^{-1}\mathfrak{a}\oplus\mathfrak{d}_{k}^{-1}N^{-1}\mathfrak{a}
, and
that this lattice has level
N
. ∎

3.
\operatorname{GSpin}
Shimura varieties

We now describe the
\operatorname{GSpin}
Shimura varieties and special cycles that appear, starting with the general setting, then describing
the Hilbert modular surfaces corresponding to the rational quadratic spaces
(V_{A},Q_{A})
of signature
(2,2)
introduced above.

3.1.
Complex Shimura varieties

Let
(V,Q)
be any rational quadratic space of signature
(n,2)
with bilinear form
(x,y)=Q(x+y)-Q(x)-Q(y)
.
Write
\operatorname{GSpin}(V)
for the corresponding general spin group.
We consider the Grassmannian
D(V)=D^{\pm}(V)
of oriented
2
2

2

Although we drop it from the notation
henceforth, we write
D^{+}(V)
to denote the hyperplanes with positive orientation, and
D^{-}(V)
the hyperplanes with negative
orientation, so that
D^{\pm}(V)
denotes one of these choices – which we fix consistently.
negative two-planes in
V({\bf{R}})
,

\displaystyle D(V)=\left\{z\subset V_{{\bf{R}}}:\dim(z)=2,Q|_{z}<0\right\}.

Extending the bilinear form
(\cdot,\cdot)
to
V_{\bf{C}}
, we see that this real manifold
D(V)

is isomorphic to the complex manifold of dimension
n
defined by the quadric

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}(V)

\displaystyle=\left\{w\in V_{\bf{C}}\backslash\{0\}:(w,w)=0,(w,\overline{w})<0\right\}/{\bf{C}}^{\times}\subset{\bf{P}}(V({\bf{C}})),

from which
D(V)
acquires the structure of a complex manifold. Here, the isomorphism sends an
oriented hyperplane
z=[x,y]
with basis
[x,y]
such that
Q(x)=Q(y)
and
(x,y)=0
to
w=x+iy\in V_{\bf{C}}
.

We now explain how
(\operatorname{GSpin}(V),D(V))
determines a Shimura datum. We have a natural embeddings of
{\bf{R}}
-algebras

{\bf{C}}\rightarrow C(z)\rightarrow C(V_{\bf{R}})
for any hyperplane
z=[x,y]\subset V_{\bf{R}}
, with the first induced by the map

\displaystyle i\longmapsto\frac{xy}{\sqrt{Q(x)Q(y)}}.

The induced map
{\bf{C}}^{\times}\rightarrow C(V_{\bf{R}})^{\times}
takes values in
\operatorname{GSpin}(V)({\bf{R}})
,
and arises from a morphism of real algebraic groups

\alpha_{z}:\operatorname{Res}_{{\bf{C}}/{\bf{R}}}{\bf{G}}_{m}\rightarrow\operatorname{GSpin}(V)({\bf{R}})
.
In this way, we can identify
D(V)
with a conjugacy class in

\operatorname{Hom}(\operatorname{Res}_{{\bf{C}}/{\bf{R}}}{\bf{G}}_{m},\operatorname{GSpin}(V)({\bf{R}}))
.
Hence, we can associate a Shimura variety to
(\operatorname{GSpin}(V),D(V))
.

Any choice of integral lattice
L\subset V
determines a compact open subgroup

\displaystyle K=K_{L}:=\operatorname{GSpin}(V)({\bf{A}}_{f})\cap C(\widehat{L})\subset\operatorname{GSpin}(V)({\bf{A}}_{f}),\quad\widehat{L}=L_{\widehat{\bf{Z}}}.

We write
L^{\vee}=\{x\in V:(v,L)\subset{\bf{Z}}\}
for the dual lattice, and
L^{\vee}/L\cong\widehat{L}^{\vee}/\widehat{L}
for the discriminant group.
Note that
K=K_{L}
acts trivially on
\widehat{L}
. Fixing such a choice, we consider the corresponding Shimura variety

(4)

\displaystyle X_{K}({\bf{C}})=\operatorname{GSpin}(V)({\bf{Q}})\backslash D(V)\times\operatorname{GSpin}(V)({\bf{A}}_{f})/K\cong\coprod\limits_{h\in\operatorname{GSpin}{V}({\bf{Q}})\backslash\operatorname{GSpin}{V}({\bf{A}}_{f})/K}\Gamma_{h}\backslash D(V)

for arithmetic subgroups
\Gamma_{h}=\operatorname{GSpin}(V)({\bf{Q}})\cap hKh^{-1}
. This complex orbifold
X_{K}({\bf{C}})
has the structure of a
quasiprojective variety
X_{K}
of dimension
n
over
{\bf{Q}}
which is projective if and only if
V
is anisotropic. It is smooth if
K=K_{L}
is neat.
We refer to
[
1
,
\lx@sectionsign
2]
,
[
40
]
, and
[
41
,
\lx@sectionsign
1]
for more background.

3.2.
Special divisors

Given a vector
x\in V
with
Q(x)>0
, we define a divisor

\displaystyle D(V)_{x}

\displaystyle=\left\{z\in D(V):z\perp x\right\}.

For each
\mu\in L^{\vee}/L
and
m\in{\bf{Q}}_{>0}
, we consider the divisor
Z(\mu,m)
on
X_{K}
given by the complex orbifold

(5)

\displaystyle Z(\mu,m)({\bf{C}})

\displaystyle=\coprod\limits_{h\in\operatorname{GSpin}{V}({\bf{Q}})\backslash\operatorname{GSpin}{V}({\bf{A}}_{f})/K}\Gamma_{h}\Big\backslash\left(\coprod\limits_{x\in\mu_{h}+L_{h}\atop Q(x)=m}D(V)_{x}\right).

Here, for any element
h\in\operatorname{GSpin}{V}({\bf{A}}_{f})
, we write
L_{h}\subset V

for the lattice determined by
\widehat{L}_{h}=h\cdot\widehat{L}
, and
\mu_{h}=h\cdot\mu\in L_{h}^{\vee}/L_{h}
.
As explained in
[
1
,
\lx@sectionsign 2
]
(cf.
[
40
]
,
[
41
,
\lx@sectionsign
1]
), these
Z(\mu,m)({\bf{C}})\rightarrow X_{K}({\bf{C}})

determine effective Cartier divisors, and admit a moduli description given in terms of the Kuga-Satake abelian scheme over
X_{K}
.

3.3.
CM cycles and geodesic sets

Let
V_{0}\subset V
be any rational quadratic subspace of signature
(0,2)
with corresponding lattice
L_{0}=V_{0}\cap L
.
The Clifford algebra
C(L_{0})
then determines an order in a quaternion algebra over
{\bf{Q}}
, and its even part
C^{0}(L_{0})

an order in some imaginary quadratic field
k(V_{0})
determined by
V_{0}
. The corresponding spin group

\operatorname{GSpin}(V_{0})\cong\operatorname{Res}_{k(V_{0})/{\bf{Q}}}{\bf{G}}_{m}
forms a rank-two torus
T(V_{0})

in
\operatorname{GSpin}(V)
. Fixing an embedding
k(V_{0})\subset{\bf{C}}
, the left multiplication in
V_{0}({\bf{R}})

gives
V_{0}({\bf{R}})
the structure of a complex vector space, and determines an orientation. In this way, we see that
each of the two oriented negative definite subspaces

z_{0}^{\pm}=V_{0}({\bf{R}})
determines a point in
D(V)=D^{\pm}(V)
,
and
(T(V_{0}),z_{0}^{\pm})
a Shimura datum associated to the zero-dimensional complex orbifold

(6)

\displaystyle Z(V_{0})({\bf{C}})

\displaystyle=T(V_{0})({\bf{Q}})\backslash\left\{z_{0}^{\pm}\right\}\times T(V_{0})({\bf{A}}_{f})/K_{0},\quad K_{0}=K_{L_{0}}=T(V_{0})({\bf{A}}_{f})\cap K_{L}.

We call the corresponding zero cycle
Z(V_{0})\subset X_{K}
the
CM cycle associated to
V_{0}
.

As explained in
[
1
]
, if we assume that
C^{0}(V_{0})\cong\mathcal{O}_{k(V_{0})}
is the maximal order,
then the
{\bf{Z}}/2{\bf{Z}}
-grading on
C(L_{0})
takes the form
C(L_{0})\cong\mathcal{O}_{k(V_{0})}\oplus L_{0}
,
where
L_{0}
is both a left and right
\mathcal{O}_{k(V_{0})}
-module. In this case, there exists a proper fractional

\mathcal{O}_{k(V_{0})}
-ideal
\mathfrak{b}
and left
\mathcal{O}_{k(V_{0})}
-module isomorphism

L_{0}\cong\mathfrak{b}
which identifies the corresponding quadratic form
Q_{0}=Q|_{V_{0}}
with the norm form

Q_{0}(\cdot)={\bf{N}}_{k(V_{0})/{\bf{Q}}}(\cdot)/{\bf{N}}\mathfrak{b}
. The dual lattice
L_{0}^{\vee}
is then
identified with
\mathfrak{d}_{k(V_{0})}^{-1}L_{0}
for
\mathfrak{d}_{k(V_{0})}^{-1}
the inverse
different of
k(V_{0})
. In this setting, the zero cycle
Z(V_{0})
can be reinterpreted as the moduli
stack of elliptic curves with complex multiplication by
\mathcal{O}_{k(V_{0})}
.
We refer to
[
1
,
\lx@sectionsign
4]
for details, and note that there appears to be some subtlety
in extending this discussion to the setting where
C^{0}(V_{0})\cong\mathcal{O}
is a nonmaximal order
(which at the time of writing remains an open problem).

Motivated by the study of real quadratic fields, we consider sets attached to rational quadratic subspaces
W\subset V
of signature
(1,1)
.
Writing
D(W)=D^{\pm}(W)=\left\{z\subset W({\bf{R}}):\dim(z)=1,\text{orientation $\pm$},Q_{W}|_{z}<0\right\}
to denote the corresponding domain
of oriented lines in
W({\bf{R}})
, we consider the sets defined

(7)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{G}(W)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{GSpin}(W)({\bf{Q}})\backslash D(W)\times\operatorname{GSpin}(W)({\bf{A}}_{f})/(K_{L}\cap\operatorname{GSpin}(W)({\bf{A}}_{f})).

We call
\mathfrak{G}(W)
the
geodesic set associated to
W
.

3.4.
Classical description as Hilbert modular surfaces

Fix any class
A
in
C(\mathcal{O}_{k})
, together with any integral ideal representative
\mathfrak{a}
,
and consider the corresponding rational quadratic space
(V_{A},Q_{A})
of signature (2,2) introduced in
(\ref{V_A})
.
By Proposition
2.3
, we have an accidental isomorphism

(8)

\displaystyle\zeta:\operatorname{GSpin}(V_{A})\cong\operatorname{GL}_{2}^{2}

of algebraic groups over
{\bf{Q}}
. Write
L_{A}\subset V_{A}

for the integral lattice whose corresponding compact open subgroup

K_{A}=K_{L_{A}}\subset\operatorname{GSpin}(V_{A})({\bf{A}}_{f})

given by
K_{A}=\prod_{p<\infty}K_{A,p}=\prod_{p<\infty}K_{\Lambda_{A},p}

has the property that each
K_{A,p}\subset\operatorname{GSpin}(V_{A})({\bf{Q}}_{p})

corresponds under
(\ref{acc})
to the Cartesian product of congruence subgroups

\displaystyle\zeta(K_{A,p})

\displaystyle=K_{0,p}(N)^{2},\quad K_{0,p}(N):=\left\{\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}a&b\\
c&d\end{array}\right)\in\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\bf{Z}}_{p}):c\in N{\bf{Z}}_{p}\right\}\subseteq\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\bf{Z}}_{p})

for some fixed integer
N\geq 1
. That is, we assume that
K_{A}
is identified under
(\ref{acc})

with the Cartesian self-product
K_{0}(N)^{2}
of the congruence subgroup
K_{0}(N)=\prod_{p<\infty}K_{0,p}(N)
of

\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\widehat{\bf{Z}})\subset\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\bf{A}}_{f})
.

3.4.1.
Hermitian symmetric domains

Recall that we consider the Grassmannian

\displaystyle D(V_{A})=D^{\pm}(V_{A})

\displaystyle=\left\{z\subset V_{A}({\bf{R}}):\dim(z)=2,~~~\text{orientation $\pm$}~~~,Q_{A}|_{z}<0\right\}

of oriented negative definite hyperplanes in

V_{A}({\bf{R}})\cong\mathfrak{a}_{{\bf{R}}}+\mathfrak{a}_{{\bf{R}}}
. Extending the bilinear pairing
(\cdot,\cdot)_{A}
to
{\bf{C}}
, we saw that the real manifold
D(V_{A})
is isomorphic to the complex surface defined by the quadric

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}(V_{A})

\displaystyle=\left\{w\in V_{A}({\bf{C}}):(w,w)_{A}=0,(w,\overline{w})_{A}<0\right\}/{\bf{C}}^{\times}\subset{\bf{P}}(V_{A}({\bf{C}}))

via the isomorphism sending a properly oriented hyperplane
z
with standard basis
z=[x,y]\in D(V_{A})
such that
(x,x)_{A}=(y,y)_{A}=-1

and
(x,y)_{A}=0
to the complex point
w=w(z):=x+iy\in\mathcal{Q}(V_{A})
. Here, we remark that the quadric
\mathcal{Q}(V_{A})
determines
a complex surface with two connected components
\mathcal{Q}^{\pm}(V_{A})
. Our choice of orientation
D^{\pm}(V_{A})
determines one of these,
so that we have the corresponding identification
D^{\pm}(V_{A})\cong\mathcal{Q}^{\pm}(V_{A})
.
This identification is sometimes referred to as the
projective model
for
D(V_{A})=D^{\pm}(V_{A})
.
It is useful for identifying the complex structure on
D(V_{A})
, which makes it a hermitian symmetric domain.

We also have the following equivalent description. Fix a Witt decomposition
V_{A}=W_{A}\oplus{\bf{Q}}e_{1}\oplus{\bf{Q}}e_{2}
,
with nonzero isotropic basis vectors
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
chosen so that
(e_{1},e_{1})_{A}=(e_{2},e_{2})_{A}=0
and
(e_{1},e_{2})_{A}=1
.
Hence,
W_{A}\subset V_{A}
denotes the Lorentzian rational quadratic subspace of signature
(1,1)
determined by the intersection

W_{A}=V_{A}\cap e_{1}^{\perp}\cap e_{2}^{\perp}
. We can then identify
D(V_{A})\cong\mathcal{Q}(V_{A})
with the corresponding tube domain

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(V_{A})

\displaystyle=\left\{\mathfrak{z}\in W_{A}({\bf{C}}):Q_{A}(\Im(\mathfrak{z}))<0\right\}.

To be more precise, given an element
w\in V_{A}({\bf{C}})=W_{A}({\bf{C}})\oplus{\bf{C}}e_{1}\oplus{\bf{C}}e_{2}
, let us write its
corresponding Witt decomposition
w=\mathfrak{z}+ae_{1}+be_{2}
for
\mathfrak{z}\in W_{A}({\bf{C}})
and
a,b\in{\bf{C}}
as
w=(\mathfrak{z},a,b)
.
Given an element
w\in V_{A}({\bf{C}})
, we also write
[w]
to denotes its image in
\mathcal{Q}(V_{A})\subset{\bf{P}}(V_{A}({\bf{C}}))
.
We have a biholomorphic map

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(V_{A})\cong\mathcal{Q}(V_{A}),\quad\mathfrak{z}\longmapsto\left[\left(\mathfrak{z},1,-Q_{A}(\mathfrak{z})-Q_{A}(e_{2})\right)\right]=\left[\mathfrak{z}+e_{1}-Q_{A}(\mathfrak{z})e_{2}+Q_{A}(e_{2})e_{2}\right].

We refer to
[
7
, Lemma 2.18]
(for instance) for more details.
The domain
\mathcal{H}(V_{A})\subset W_{A}({\bf{C}})\cong{\bf{C}}^{2}
has two connected components

\mathcal{H}^{\pm}(V_{A})
corresponding to the two cones of negative norm vectors in the Lorenzian subspace
W_{A}({\bf{R}})
,
and we have natural identifications
\mathcal{H}^{\pm}(V_{A})\cong\mathfrak{H}^{2}
with products of two copies of the Poincaré upper-half plane

\mathfrak{H}=\{\tau\in{\bf{C}},\Im(\tau)>0\}
. The corresponding identification

D^{\pm}(V_{A})\cong\mathcal{H}^{\pm}(V_{A})\cong\mathfrak{H}^{2}

is sometimes referred to as the
tube domain model
for
D(V_{A})=D^{\pm}(V_{A})
.

3.4.2.
Hilbert modular surfaces

Taking for granted the various identifications of domains

\displaystyle D^{\pm}(V_{A})\cong\mathcal{Q}^{\pm}(V_{A})\cong\mathcal{H}^{\pm}(V_{A})\cong\mathfrak{H}^{2}

with the accidental isomorphism
(\ref{acc})
and the choice of level structure
(\ref{ll})
, we obtain the identifications

(11)

\displaystyle X_{K_{A}}({\bf{C}})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{GSpin}(V_{A})({\bf{Q}})\backslash D^{\pm}(V_{A})\times\operatorname{GSpin}(V_{A})({\bf{A}}_{f})/K_{A}

\displaystyle\cong\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\bf{Q}})^{2}\backslash\mathfrak{H}^{2}\times\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\bf{A}}_{f})^{2}/\zeta(K_{A})=Y_{0}(N)\times Y_{0}(N),

where
