\displaystyle Y_{0}(N)=\Gamma_{0}(N)\backslash\mathfrak{H}\cong\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\bf{Q}})\backslash\mathfrak{H}\times\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\bf{A}}_{f})/K_{0}(N),\quad K_{0}(N):=\prod_{p<\infty}K_{0,p}(N)

denotes the noncompactified modular curve of level
\Gamma_{0}(N)\subset\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\bf{Z}})
.
Hence, we can identify each spin Shimura surface
X_{K_{A}}({\bf{C}})=X_{K_{L_{A}}}({\bf{C}})
with the
Hilbert modular surface
Y_{0}(N)\times Y_{0}(N)
.

3.4.3.
Hirzebruch-Zagier divisors

We see from
(\ref{acc})
,
(\ref{ll})
, and
(\ref{SSVid})
that each divisor
Z(\mu,m)=Z_{A}(\mu,m)
defined in

(\ref{special})
above for
\mu\in L_{A}^{\vee}/L_{A}
and
m\in{\bf{Q}}_{>0}
is given more explicitly by the analytic divisor

\displaystyle Z_{A}(\mu,m)({\bf{C}})=\coprod\limits_{h\in\operatorname{GSpin}(V_{A})({\bf{Q}})\backslash\operatorname{GSpin}(V_{A})({\bf{A}}_{f})/K_{A}}\Gamma_{h}\Big\backslash\left(\coprod\limits_{x\in\mu_{h}+{L_{A}}_{h}\atop Q_{A}(x)=m}D(V_{A})_{x}\right)

\displaystyle\cong\Gamma_{0}(N)^{2}\Big\backslash\coprod\limits_{x\in\mu+L_{A}\atop Q_{A}(x)=m}D(V_{A})_{x}=\Gamma_{0}(N)^{2}\Big\backslash\coprod\limits_{x\in\mu+L_{A}\atop Q_{A}(x)=m}\left\{z\in D^{\pm}(V_{A}):(z,x)_{A}=0\right\}

\displaystyle\cong\Gamma_{0}(N)^{2}\Big\backslash\coprod\limits_{x\in\mu+L_{A}\atop Q_{A}(x)=m}\left\{z=(z_{1},z_{2})\in\mathfrak{H}^{2}:Q_{A}(z+x)-Q_{A}(z)=m\right\}\subset Y_{0}(N)({\bf{C}})\times Y_{0}(N)({\bf{C}}).

Note that these divisors can be viewed as embeddings of modular curves into
Y_{0}(N)\times Y_{0}(N)
.
This is apparent from the description above, as well as their more intrinsic characterization as analytic divisors in
[
40
,
\lx@sectionsign
2]
.
That is, we choose a positive norm vector
x\in V_{A}
, or more precisely, an element of the quadratic

\displaystyle\Omega_{A,\mu,m}({\bf{Q}})

\displaystyle=\left\{x\in\mu+L_{A}:Q_{A}(x)=m\right\}.

We consider the corresponding one-dimensional subspace

V_{A,+}:={\bf{Q}}x\subset V_{A}
, with its orthogonal complement
U_{A}:=V_{A,+}^{\perp}\subset V_{A}
.
Hence,
U_{A}\subset V_{A}
determines a subspace of signature
(1,2)
.
Its spin group
\operatorname{GSpin}(U_{A})
is isomorphic
to the stabilizer of
V_{A,+}
in
\operatorname{GSpin}(V_{A})\cong\operatorname{GL}_{2}^{2}
(
[
40
, Lemma 2.1]
).
The natural subspace embedding
U_{A}\subset V_{A}
gives rise to an embedding of reductive algebraic groups

\operatorname{GSpin}(U_{A})\rightarrow\operatorname{GSpin}(V_{A})
.
Writing
D(U_{A})=D^{\pm}(U_{A})=\left\{z\subset U_{A}({\bf{R}}):\dim(z)=2,Q_{A}|_{z}<0\right\}
for the
corresponding Grassmannian, and
K_{A,U}=K_{A}\cap\operatorname{GSpin}(U_{A})({\bf{A}}_{f})
the corresponding
compact open subgroup, we can identity
Z_{A}(\mu,m)
with the Shimura subcurve

\displaystyle Z_{A}(\mu,m)({\bf{C}})=\operatorname{GSpin}(U_{A})({\bf{Q}})\backslash D(U_{A})\times\operatorname{GSpin}(U_{A})({\bf{A}}_{f})/K_{A,U}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow X_{A}({\bf{C}})\cong Y_{0}(N)\times Y_{0}(N)

\displaystyle\operatorname{GSpin}(U_{A})({\bf{Q}})(z,h)K_{A,U}

\displaystyle\longmapsto\operatorname{GSpin}(V_{A})({\bf{Q}})(z,h)K_{A}.

To be more precise, we know from the discussion above that
Z_{A}(\mu,m)

can be identified with the modular curve
\Gamma(U_{A})\backslash D(U_{A})
,
where
D(U_{A})=D^{\pm}(U_{A})\cong\mathfrak{H}
and

\Gamma(U_{A})=\operatorname{GSpin}(U_{A})({\bf{Q}})\cap K_{A,U}\subseteq\Gamma_{0}(N)^{2}
is a congruence subgroup.
As can be seen through this description, the sums over cosets
\mu\in L_{A}^{\vee}/L_{A}
of these divisors
Z_{A}(\mu,m)

give the classical Hirzebruch-Zagier divisors of the forms described in
[
30
]
and
[
7
,
\lx@sectionsign
2]
. We shall return
to this relation to the classical Hirzebruch-Zagier divisors on
Y_{0}(N)\times Y_{0}(N)
given in terms of the moduli discussion
(see e.g.
[
30
]
) in our discussion of arithmetic heights below.

3.4.4.
CM cycles

Let
V_{0,A}\subset V_{A}
be any rational quadratic subspace of signature
(0,2)
,
with corresponding lattice
L_{0,A}=V_{0,A}\cap V_{A}
and quadratic form
Q_{A,0}=Q_{A}|_{V_{A,0}}
.
The even Clifford algebra
C^{0}(L_{A,0})\subset C^{0}(V_{A,0})
determines
an order in the imaginary quadratic field
k(V_{A,0})
determined by
V_{A,0}
.
Recall that any such subspace
V_{A,0}\subset V_{A}
determines a rank-two torus

\operatorname{GSpin}(V_{0,A})\cong\operatorname{Res}_{k(V_{A,0})/{\bf{Q}}}{\bf{G}}_{m}
and
the even Clifford algebra
C^{0}(L_{A,0})\cong\mathcal{O}
an order in
\mathcal{O}_{k(V_{A,0})}
.
Again, if
C^{0}(L_{A,0})\cong\mathcal{O}_{k(V_{A,0})}
is maximal,
then the
{\bf{Z}}/2{\bf{Z}}
-grading on
C(L_{A,0})
takes the form
C(L_{A,0})\cong\mathcal{O}_{k(V_{A,0})}\oplus L_{A,0}
,
with
L_{A,0}
being both a left and right
\mathcal{O}_{k(V_{A,0})}
-module, and
there exists a fractional
\mathcal{O}_{k(V_{A,0})}
-ideal
\mathfrak{b}
and an isomorphism

L_{A,0}\cong\mathfrak{b}
of left
\mathcal{O}_{k(V_{A,0})}
-modules which identifies the quadratic form

Q_{A,0}
on
L_{A,0}
with the norm form
-{\bf{N}}_{k(V_{A,0})/{\bf{Q}}}(\cdot)/{\bf{N}}\mathfrak{b}
and

L_{A,0}^{\vee}\cong\mathfrak{d}_{k(V_{A,0})}^{-1}L_{A,0}
.

Remark 3.1
.

If we start with
k={\bf{Q}}(\sqrt{d})
an imaginary quadratic field in our setup, each of the anisotropic subspaces

(V_{A,0},Q_{A,0})=(V_{A,1},-Q_{A,1})=(V_{\mathfrak{a}},-Q_{\mathfrak{a}})

is a rational quadratic subspace of signature
(0,2)
associated to the imaginary quadratic field
k(V_{A,1})=k
.
In the same way, each of the anisotropic subspaces

(V_{A,0},Q_{A,0})=(V_{A,2},Q_{A,2})=(V_{\mathfrak{a}},Q_{\mathfrak{a}})

determines a rational quadratic subspace of signature
(0,2)
associated to the same imaginary quadratic field
k(V_{A,2})=k
.

If on the other hand we start with
k={\bf{Q}}(\sqrt{d})
a real quadratic field, any subspace
(V_{A,0},Q_{A,0})\subset(V_{A},Q_{A})
of signature
(0,2)

will also determine an imaginary quadratic field
k(V_{A,0})
. For instance, writing
\tau
to denote the nontrivial automorphism of

\operatorname{Gal}(k/{\bf{Q}})
, we see that
U_{A,0}:=V_{A,1}^{\tau}\oplus(V_{A,2}/V_{A,2}^{\tau})
with
Q_{A}|_{U_{A,0}}
is a rational
quadratic subspace
(V_{A},Q_{A})
of signature
(2,0)
, and that
V_{A,0}=U_{A,0}
with
Q_{A,0}=-Q_{A}|_{U_{A,0}}
is a rational quadratic
subspace
V_{A}
of signature
(0,2)
which determines an imaginary quadratic field
k(U_{A,0})
.

Each sublattice
L_{A,0}\subset L_{A}
of signature
(0,2)
gives rise to a group scheme
T_{A}
over
{\bf{Z}}
with functor
of points
T_{A}(R)=(C^{0}(L_{A,0})\otimes_{\bf{Z}}R)^{\times}
for any
{\bf{Z}}
-algebra
R
. This gives a rank-two torus

T_{A}\otimes_{\bf{Z}}{\bf{Q}}=\operatorname{GSpin}(V_{A,0})
which appears as a maximal subgroup of

\operatorname{GSpin}(V_{A})\cong\operatorname{GL}_{2}^{2}
. Writing
\Lambda_{A,0}=L_{A,0}^{\perp}\subset L_{A}

for the complement of the lattice
L_{A,0}
, this maximal subgroup acts trivially on the corresponding
subspace
V_{A,0}^{\perp}=\Lambda_{A,0}\otimes_{\bf{Z}}{\bf{Q}}\subset V_{A}
.
Let
K_{A,0}=T_{A}({\bf{A}}_{f})\cap K_{L_{A}}

denote the corresponding compact open subgroup of
T_{A}({\bf{A}}_{f})=\operatorname{GSpin}(V_{A,0})({\bf{A}}_{f})
.

Fixing an embedding
\mathcal{O}_{k(V_{A,0})}\subset{\bf{C}}
, we can view
V_{A,0}({\bf{R}})=V_{A,0}\otimes_{\bf{Q}}{\bf{R}}

as an oriented hyperplane of
{\bf{C}}
, and hence as a point
z_{A,0}=V_{A,0}({\bf{R}})\subset V_{A}({\bf{R}})

in
D(V_{A})\cong\mathcal{Q}(V_{A})\cong\mathcal{H}(V_{A})\cong\mathfrak{H}^{2}
.
This makes
(T_{A}\otimes_{\bf{Z}}{\bf{Q}},z_{A,0})=(\operatorname{GSpin}(V_{A,0}),z_{A,0})

a Shimura datum with reflex field
k(V_{A,0})
. The corresponding orbifold

\displaystyle Z(V_{A,0})({\bf{C}})

\displaystyle=T_{A}({\bf{Q}})\backslash\left\{z_{A,0}\right\}\times T_{A}({\bf{A}}_{f})/K_{A,0}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{GSpin}(V_{A,0})({\bf{Q}})\backslash\left\{V_{0,A}({\bf{R}})\right\}\times\operatorname{GSpin}(V_{A,0})({\bf{A}}_{f})/\left(\operatorname{GSpin}(V_{A,0})({\bf{A}}_{f})\cap K_{L_{A}}\right)

can be viewed as the complex points of a zero-dimensional Shimura variety
Z(V_{A,0})\longrightarrow\operatorname{Spec}(k(V_{A,0}))
,
or a complex fibre on the moduli stack of elliptic curves with complex multiplication by

\mathcal{O}\cong C^{0}(L_{A,0})\subset\mathcal{O}_{k(V_{A,0})}
and
\Gamma_{0}(N)
-level structure.

3.4.5.
Geodesic sets

Let
W_{A}\subset V_{A}
be any Lorentzian quadratic subspace of signature
(1,1)
, with lattice
M_{A}=W_{A}\cap L_{A}
.
Note that the complement
N_{A}=M_{A}^{\perp}\subset L_{A}
, also determines a Lorentzian subspace

U_{A}=N_{A}\otimes_{\bf{Z}}{\bf{Q}}\subset V_{A}

of signature
(1,1)
. We consider the corresponding domain

\displaystyle D(W_{A})=D^{\pm}(W_{A})

\displaystyle=\left\{\mathfrak{y}=[\alpha,\beta]\subset W_{A}({\bf{R}}):\dim(\mathfrak{y})=1,\text{orientation $\pm$},Q_{A}|_{W_{A}}(\mathfrak{y})<0\right\}

of oriented hyperbolic lines
\mathfrak{y}=[\alpha,\beta]\equiv[\alpha:\beta]\in{\bf{P}}^{1}({\bf{R}})
, given equivalently as a space of projective lines

\displaystyle D(W_{A})=D^{\pm}(W_{A})

\displaystyle=\left\{\mathfrak{y}=[\alpha:\beta]\subset{\bf{P}}^{1}({\bf{R}}):\text{orientation $\pm$},Q_{A}|_{W_{A}}(\alpha,\beta)<0\right\}.

Recall that after fixing an oriented basis
z=[x,y]
of each negative definite hyperplane
z\subset V_{A}({\bf{R}})
, and fixing a Witt
decomposition
V_{A}=W_{A}\oplus{\bf{Q}}e_{1}\oplus{\bf{Q}}e_{2}
corresponding to
W_{A}
, we have identifications

\displaystyle D^{\pm}(V_{A})\cong\mathcal{Q}^{\pm}(V_{A})\cong\mathcal{H}^{\pm}(V_{A}),\quad z=[x,y]\mapsto[w(z)=x+iy]\mapsto\mathfrak{z}(w)=\mathfrak{z}(w(z)).

Here, we write the corresponding Witt decomposition of a point
w\in V_{A}({\bf{C}})
as
w=\mathfrak{z}(w)+a(w)e_{1}+b(w)e_{1}.

Note that while the point
w(\mathfrak{y})=\alpha+i\beta\in{\bf{C}}
determined by a hyperbolic line
\mathfrak{y}=[\alpha:\beta]\in{\bf{P}}^{1}({\bf{R}})

does not lie in the upper-half plane
\mathfrak{H}
, the roots of the quadratic polynomial
Q_{A}|_{W_{A}}(X,1)=0

(or
Q_{A}|_{W_{A}}(1,Y)=0
) determine endpoints of a geodesic arc in
\mathfrak{H}
.
For this reason, we shall sometimes call the corresponding sets

\displaystyle\mathfrak{G}(W_{A})

\displaystyle:=\operatorname{GSpin}(W_{A})({\bf{Q}})\backslash D^{\pm}(W_{A})\times\operatorname{GSpin}(W_{A})({\bf{A}}_{f})/\overline{K}_{A},\quad\overline{K}_{A}:=K_{A}\cap\operatorname{GSpin}(W_{A})({\bf{A}}_{f})

geodesic sets
associated to the Hilbert modular surface
X_{A}\cong Y_{0}(N)\times Y_{0}(N)
.
We shall later see that the summation of automorphic Green’s functions associated to certain linear combinations of the special divisors

Z_{A}(\mu,m)
gives information about central derivative values of certain Rankin-Selberg
L
-functions. Hence, we have reason to
believe that these geodesic sets are related to (Hirzebruch-Zagier) special arithmetic divisors on
X_{A}\cong Y_{0}(N)\times Y_{0}(N)
.
In fact, clarifying such a relation forms the main motivation for this note.

4.
Green’s functions for special divisors

We describe the automorphic Green’s functions that can be constructed from regularized theta lifts for the special divisors
Z(\mu,m)
.
We start with the general setting, following
[
1
]
,
[
41
]
,
[
7
]
,
[
13
]
, and
[
55
]
, then specialize to the case of
Hilbert modular surfaces parametrized by the rational quadratic spaces
V_{A}
of signature
(2,2)
.

4.1.
Siegel theta functions

Fix
(V,Q)
a rational quadratic space of signature
(n,2)
, with integral lattice
L\subset V
.
We write
L^{\vee}/L
for the discriminant group, and
\mathfrak{S}_{L}
the finite-dimensional
space of
C
-valued functions on
L^{\vee}/L
. Writing
\operatorname{Mp}_{2}

for the two-fold metaplectic cover of
\operatorname{SL}_{2}\cong\operatorname{Sp}_{2}
, we consider the Weil representation

\displaystyle\omega_{L}:\operatorname{Mp}_{2}({\bf{Z}})\longrightarrow\mathfrak{S}_{L},

which for
n\geq 1
even factors through
\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\bf{Z}})
as

\omega_{L}:\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\bf{Z}})\rightarrow\mathfrak{S}_{L}
.
We define the conjugate action
\overline{\omega}_{L}
by

\overline{\omega}_{L}(\gamma)\Phi=\overline{\omega_{L}(\gamma)\overline{\Phi}}
, and write
\omega_{L}^{\vee}
for the
contragredient action of
\operatorname{Mp}_{2}({\bf{Z}})
on the complex linear dual
\mathfrak{S}_{L}^{\vee}
.

We now describe how for each
h\in\operatorname{GSpin}(V)({\bf{A}}_{f})/K_{L}
, we can use
\omega_{L}
to construct a Siegel theta function

\displaystyle\theta_{L}(\tau,z):\mathfrak{H}\times D(V)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathfrak{S}_{L}^{\vee},

which in the variable
z\in D(V)=D^{\pm}(V)
is
\Gamma_{h}
-invariant, and in the variable
\tau=u+iv\in\mathfrak{H}

transforms as a nonholomorphic modular form of weight
\frac{n}{2}-1
and representation
\omega_{L}^{\vee}
. We give
the precise definition in
(\ref{Stheta3})
.

4.1.1.
Theta kernels

To give a more precise account of the Weil representation for later constructions of theta series,
let
\psi=\otimes_{v}\psi_{v}
denote the standard additive character of
{\bf{A}}/{\bf{Q}}
,
which has archimedean component
\psi_{\infty}(x)=e(x)=\exp(2\pi ix)
for
x\in{\bf{R}}\cong{\bf{Q}}_{\infty}
.

Recall that the two-fold metaplectic cover
\operatorname{Mp}_{2}
fits into a short exact sequence

\displaystyle 1\longrightarrow\left\{\pm 1\right\}\longrightarrow\operatorname{Mp}_{2}\longrightarrow\operatorname{SL}_{2}\longrightarrow 1,

and that the general spin group
\operatorname{GSpin}(V)
fits into a short exact sequence

\displaystyle 1\longrightarrow{\bf{G}}_{m}\longrightarrow\operatorname{GSpin}(V)\longrightarrow\operatorname{SO}(V)\longrightarrow 1.

Both groups act on the space of Schwartz-Bruhat functions
\Phi=\otimes_{v}\Phi_{v}\in\mathcal{S}(V({\bf{A}}_{f}))
by the Weil representation

\displaystyle\omega_{L}=\omega_{L,\psi}:\operatorname{Mp}_{2}({\bf{A}})\times\operatorname{GSpin}(V)({\bf{A}})\longrightarrow\mathcal{S}(V({\bf{A}})).

This gives a natural theta kernel, defined on
g\in\operatorname{Mp}_{2}({\bf{A}})
,

h\in\operatorname{GSpin}(V)({\bf{A}})
, and
\Phi=\otimes_{v}\Phi_{v}\in\mathcal{S}(V({\bf{A}}))
by

(12)

\displaystyle\vartheta_{L}(g,h;\Phi)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{x\in V({\bf{Q}})}\left(\omega_{L}(g,h)\Phi\right)(x).

This function
\vartheta_{L}(g,h;\Phi)
is seen by inspection to be left
\operatorname{GSpin}(V)({\bf{Q}})
-invariant,
and by Poisson summation to be left
\operatorname{Mp}_{2}({\bf{Q}})
-invariant.
It is referred to as the
theta kernel associated to the Weil representation
\omega_{L}
.

4.1.2.
Choice of local Schwartz functions

Following
[
3
]
,
[
6
]
, and
[
13
,
\lx@sectionsign
2]
, we choose the following decomposable
Schwartz functions
\Phi=\otimes_{v}\Phi_{v}\in\mathcal{S}(V({\bf{A}}))
to construct Siegel
theta functions from the theta kernel
(\ref{thetakernel})
.

We first define the following Gaussian function
\Phi_{\infty}\in\mathcal{S}(V({\bf{R}}))
.
Given a hyperplane
z\in D(V)=D^{\pm}(V)
, we define the corresponding majorant

(x,x)_{z}=(x_{z^{\perp}},x_{z^{\perp}})-(x_{z},x_{z})
, which can be viewed as a positive
definite quadratic form on
V({\bf{R}})
. We then define the Gaussian function

(13)

\displaystyle\Phi_{\infty}(x,z)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(-(x,x)_{z}\right),\quad z\in D(V)=D^{\pm}(V),~~~x\in V({\bf{R}}).

As a function
x\in V({\bf{R}})
, this determines an archimedean local Schwartz function
\Phi_{\infty}\in\mathcal{S}(V({\bf{R}}))
.
It satisfies the transformation property
\Phi_{\infty}(hx,hz)=\Phi_{\infty}(x,z)
for all
h\in\operatorname{GSpin}(V)({\bf{R}})
. It
has weight
\frac{n}{2}-1
under the action of the maximal compact subgroup of
\operatorname{Mp}_{2}({\bf{R}})
.

For the remaining finite part
\Phi_{f}=\otimes_{v<\infty}\Phi_{v}\in\mathcal{S}(V({\bf{A}})_{f})
, we shall later take the characteristic functions

\displaystyle\Phi_{f}

\displaystyle={\bf{1}}_{\mu}:=\operatorname{char}\left(\mu+L\otimes\widehat{{\bf{Z}}}\right)\quad\text{for a coset $\mu\in L^{\vee}/L$}.

4.1.3.
Construction of Siegel theta functions

Fix a basepoint
z_{0}\in D(V)=D^{\pm}(V)
. For any finite archimedean Schwartz function

\Phi_{f}=\otimes_{v<\infty}\Phi_{v}\in\mathcal{S}(V({\bf{A}})_{f})
, we can define from
(\ref{thetakernel})
the theta function

(14)

\displaystyle\theta_{L}(g,h;\Phi_{f})

\displaystyle:=\vartheta_{L}(g,h;\Phi_{\infty}(\cdot,z_{0})\otimes\Phi_{f}(\cdot)).

We obtain a classical Siegel theta series on
\mathfrak{H}\times D(V)
from this as follows. Given any oriented hyperplane

z=D(V)=D^{\pm}(V)
, we choose an element
h_{z}\in\operatorname{GSpin}(V)({\bf{R}})
for which
h_{z}z_{0}=z
. Note that

\displaystyle\omega_{L}(h_{z})\Phi_{\infty}(\cdot,z_{0})

\displaystyle=\Phi_{\infty}(\cdot,z).

Choosing
i\in\mathfrak{H}
as the basepoint, let us for any
\tau=u+iv\in\mathfrak{H}
write
g_{\tau}
to denote the mirabolic matrix

\displaystyle g_{\tau}

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&u\\
&1\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}v^{\frac{1}{2}}&\\
&v^{-\frac{1}{2}}\end{array}\right)\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\bf{R}}),

and
g_{\tau}^{\prime}=(g_{\tau},1)
its image in
\operatorname{Mp}_{2}({\bf{R}})
.
Note that
g_{\tau}^{\prime}\cdot i=\tau
. Via
(\ref{Stheta})
, we can then define the Siegel theta series

\displaystyle\theta_{L}(\tau,z,h_{f};\Phi_{f})

\displaystyle=v^{-\frac{n}{4}+\frac{1}{2}}\vartheta_{L}(g_{\tau}^{\prime},h_{z}h_{f};\Phi_{\infty}(\cdot,z_{0})\otimes\Phi_{f}(\cdot))=v^{-\frac{n}{4}+\frac{1}{2}}\sum\limits_{x\in V({\bf{Q}})}\omega_{L}(g_{\tau}^{\prime})\left(\Phi_{\infty}(\cdot,z)\otimes\omega(h_{f})\Phi_{f}\right)(x)

for
\tau=u+iv\in\mathfrak{H}
,
z\in D(V)=D^{\pm}(V)
,
h_{f}\in\operatorname{GSpin}(V)({\bf{A}}_{f})
,
and
\Phi_{f}=\otimes_{v<\infty}\Phi_{v}\in\mathcal{S}(V({\bf{A}}_{f}))
. Since

\displaystyle v^{-\frac{n}{4}+\frac{1}{2}}\omega_{L}(g_{\tau}^{\prime})\left(\Phi_{\infty}(\cdot,z)\right)(x)

\displaystyle=ve\left(Q(x_{z^{\perp}})\tau+Q(x_{z})\overline{\tau}\right),

we have the more explicit expansion

(15)

\displaystyle\theta_{L}(\tau,z,h_{f};\Phi_{f})

\displaystyle=v\sum\limits_{x\in V({\bf{Q}})}e\left(Q(x_{z^{\perp}})\tau+Q(x_{z})\overline{\tau}\right)\otimes\Phi_{f}(h_{f}^{-1}x).

As explained for
[
13
, (2.5)]
, this theta series satisfies a transformation property for
\operatorname{Mp}_{2}({\bf{Q}})
.
Viewing
\theta_{L}(\tau,z,h_{f};\cdot)
as a function on
\tau\in\mathfrak{H}
taking values in the dual space
\mathcal{S}(V({\bf{A}}_{f}))^{\vee}

of
\mathcal{S}(V({\bf{A}}_{f}))
, we see that
\theta_{L}(\tau,z,h_{f};\cdot)
determines a nonholomorphic modular form of weight
\frac{n}{2}-1

and representation
\omega_{L}^{\vee}
. In fact, it determines a harmonic weak Maass form

\theta_{L}(\tau,\cdot)\in H_{\frac{n}{2}-1}(\omega_{L}^{\vee})
in the sense of the definition given below.

Let
\mathfrak{S}_{L}
denote the subspace of
\mathcal{S}(V({\bf{A}}_{f}))
which are supported on
L^{\vee}\otimes\widehat{\bf{Z}}
,
and constant on cosets of
L\otimes\widehat{{\bf{Z}}}
. For instance,
\mathcal{S}_{L}
contains the characteristic function

{\bf{1}}_{\mu}=\operatorname{char}(\mu+L\otimes\widehat{{\bf{Z}}})
for a given coset
\mu\in L^{\mu}/L
. In fact, these
functions form a basis for the space, and we have the decompositon

\displaystyle\mathfrak{S}_{L}

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{\mu\in L^{\vee}/L}{\bf{C}}{{\bf{1}}}_{\mu}\subset\mathcal{S}(V({\bf{A}}_{f})).

In particular, it follows that
\dim_{\bf{C}}\mathfrak{S}_{L}=|L^{\vee}/L|
is finite. Writing
\mathfrak{e}_{\mu}
for the standard
basis element in
{\bf{C}}[L^{\vee}/L]
, we also have a natural identification
\mathfrak{S}_{L}\cong{\bf{C}}[L^{\vee}/L],{\bf{1}}_{\mu}\mapsto\mathfrak{e}_{\mu}
.
This space
\mathfrak{S}_{L}
is stable under the image of
\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\widehat{\bf{Z}})
in
\operatorname{Mp}_{2}({\bf{A}}_{f})
.
We define from
(\ref{Stheta2})
the corresponding
\mathfrak{S}_{L}
-valued Siegel theta series

(16)

\displaystyle\theta_{L}(\tau,z,h_{f})

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\mu\in L^{\vee}/L}\theta_{L}(\tau,z,h_{f};{\bf{1}}_{\mu}){\bf{1}}_{\mu}

We refer to
[
13
,
\lx@sectionsign
2]
for more on these theta series, which coincide with those considered by Borcherds in
[
3
]
.

4.2.
Harmonic weak Maass forms

Fix a half-integer
l\in\frac{1}{2}{\bf{Z}}
. Recall that a twice-differentiable function
f:\mathfrak{H}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{S}_{L}

is said to be a
harmonic weak Maass form of weight
l
and representation
\omega_{L}
if

(i)

f|_{l,\omega_{L}}\gamma=f
for all
\gamma\in\Gamma=\operatorname{SL}_{2}{\bf{Z}}
, where
|_{l,\omega_{L}}

denotes the Petersson weight-
l
operator.

(ii)

There exists an
\mathfrak{S}_{L}
-value Fourier polynomial

\displaystyle P_{f}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\mu\in L^{\vee}/L}\sum\limits_{m\geq 0}c_{f}^{+}(\mu,m)e(m\tau){\bf{1}}_{\mu},\quad{\bf{1}}_{\mu}:=\operatorname{char}(\mu+L\otimes\widehat{{\bf{Z}}})

known as the
principal part of
f
for which
f(\tau)=P_{f}(\tau)+O(e^{-\varepsilon v})
for some
\varepsilon>0
as
v=\Im(\tau)\rightarrow\infty
.

(iii)

The function is harmonic:
\Delta_{l}f=0
for
\Delta_{l}
the hyperbolic Laplacian of weight
l
defined by

\displaystyle\Delta_{l}:=-v^{2}\left(\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial u^{2}}+\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial v^{2}}\right)+il\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial u}+i\frac{\partial}{\partial v}\right),\quad\tau=u+iv\in\mathfrak{H}.

We write
H_{l}(\omega_{L})
to denote the
{\bf{C}}
-vector space of harmonic weak Maass forms of weight
l
and representation.
Each harmonic weak Maass form
f\in H_{l}(\omega_{L})
has a unique decomposition
f=f^{+}+f^{-}
where

\displaystyle f^{+}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\mu\in L^{\vee}/L}\sum\limits_{m\in{\bf{Q}}\atop m\gg-\infty}c_{f}^{+}(\mu,m)e(m\tau){\bf{1}}_{\mu}

and

\displaystyle f^{-}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\mu\in L^{\vee}/L}\sum\limits_{m\in{\bf{Q}}\atop m<0}c_{f}^{-}(\mu,m)W_{l}(2\pi mv)e(m\tau){\bf{1}}_{\mu},

where
W_{l}(a):=\int_{-2a}^{\infty}e^{-t}t^{-l}dt=\Gamma(1-l,2|a|)
denotes the Whittaker function given by the partial Gamma function,
and
e(\tau)=\exp(2\pi i\tau)
for
\tau=u+iv\in\mathfrak{H}
. We call
f^{+}
the
holomorphic part of
f
and
f^{-}
the
non-holomorphic part of
f
.
We consider the subspace
M_{l}^{!}(\omega_{L})\subset H_{l}(\omega_{L})
of
weakly holomorphic forms
whose poles are supported at the cusps,
as well as the suspace of holomorphic forms
M_{l}(\omega_{L})\subset M_{l}^{!}(\omega_{L})
, and the subspace of holomorphic cusp forms

S_{l}(\omega_{L})\subset M_{l}(\omega_{L})\subset M_{L}^{!}(\omega_{L})\subset H_{l}(\omega_{L})
.

Recall that we have the Maass weight lowering operator
L_{l}
and the Maass weight raising operator
R_{l}
,

(17)

\displaystyle L_{l}:=-2iv^{2}\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial\overline{\tau}},\quad R_{l}:=2i\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial\tau}+l\cdot v^{-1}.

Bruinier and Funke
[
10
]
define an antilinear differential operator

(18)

\displaystyle\xi_{l}:H_{l}(\omega_{L})\longrightarrow S_{2-l}(\overline{\omega}_{L}),\quad f(\tau)\longmapsto v^{l-2}\overline{L_{l}f(\tau)},

and show that it sits in a short exact sequence

\displaystyle\begin{CD}0@>{}>{}>M_{l}^{!}(\omega_{L})@>{}>{}>H_{l}(\omega)@>{\xi_{l}}>{}>S_{2-l}(\overline{\omega}_{L})@>{}>{}>0\end{CD}

so that
\ker(\xi_{l})=M_{l}^{!}(\omega_{L})
. We refer to
[
10
]
and
[
13
,
\lx@sectionsign
3]
for more details and basic properties.

4.2.1.
Definition of the divisor
Z(f)

Given
f\in H_{1-\frac{n}{2}}(\omega_{L})
, we define the corresponding divisor

(19)

\displaystyle Z(f)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\mu\in L^{\vee}/L}\sum\limits_{m\in{\bf{Q}}\atop m>0}c_{f}^{+}(\mu,-m)Z(\mu,m)

on
X_{K}=X_{K_{L}}
.

In the special case of the quadratic spaces
(V_{A},Q_{A})
of signature
(2,2)
with integral lattices
L_{A}\subset V_{A}
,
we consider for any
f_{0}=f_{0,A}\in H_{0}(\omega_{L_{A}})
the corresponding divisors on
X_{A}=X_{K_{A}}\cong Y_{0}(N)^{2}
defined by

(20)

\displaystyle Z_{A}(f_{0})

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\mu\in L_{A}^{\vee}/L_{A}}\sum\limits_{m\in{\bf{Q}}\atop m>0}c_{f_{0}}^{+}(\mu,-m)Z_{A}(\mu,m).

4.3.
Regularized theta lifts

We describe the regularized theta lifts
\Phi(f,z,h)
associated to
f\in H_{1-\frac{n}{2}}(\omega_{L})
.

4.3.1.
The tautological pairing

Let
\langle\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle\rangle:\mathfrak{S}_{L}\times\mathfrak{S}_{L}^{\vee}\longrightarrow{\bf{C}}

denote the tautological pairing. Hence, given

\displaystyle f(\tau)=\sum\limits_{\mu\in L^{\vee}/L}f_{\mu}(\tau){\bf{1}}_{\mu}\in H_{l}(\omega_{L})\quad\text{and}\quad g(\tau)=\sum\limits_{\mu\in L^{\vee}/L}g_{\mu}(\tau){\bf{1}}_{\mu}\in H_{-l}(\omega_{L}^{\vee}),

we have

\displaystyle\langle\langle f(\tau),g(\tau)\rangle\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\mu\in L^{\vee}/L}f_{\mu}(\tau)g_{\mu}(\tau).

4.3.2.
Regularized theta integrals

Given
f\in H_{1-\frac{n}{2}}(\omega_{L})
a harmonic weak Maass form of weight
1-\frac{n}{2}
and representation
\omega_{L}
, we define the
corresponding regularized theta lift
\Phi(f,z,h)
for
z\in D(V)=D^{\pm}(V)

and
h\in\operatorname{GSpin}(V)({\bf{A}}_{f})
by the regularized theta integral

\displaystyle\Phi(f,z,h)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{F}}^{\star}\langle\langle f(\tau),\theta_{L}(\tau,z,h)\rangle\rangle d\mu(\tau)=\operatorname{CT}_{s=0}\left\{\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\int_{\mathcal{F}_{T}}\langle\langle f(\tau),\theta_{L}(\tau,z,h)\rangle\rangle v^{-s}d\mu(\tau)\right\}.

Here, we write
\mathcal{F}=\left\{\tau\in\mathfrak{H}:-1/2\leq\Re(\tau)\leq 1/2,\tau\overline{\tau}\geq 1\right\}
to denote
the standard fundamental domain for the action of
\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\bf{Z}})
on
\mathfrak{H}
, and
\mu(\tau)=\frac{dudv}{v^{2}}

the Poincaré measure on
\mathfrak{H}
. The regularized theta integral
\Phi(f,z,h)

is given by the constant term in the Laurent series around
s=0
of the function

\displaystyle\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\int_{\mathcal{F}_{T}}\langle\langle f(\tau),\theta_{L}(\tau,z,h)\rangle\rangle v^{-s}d\mu(\tau),

where the limit is taken over truncated domains

\mathcal{F}_{T}=\left\{\tau\in\mathfrak{H}:-1/2\leq\Re(\tau)\leq 1/2,\tau\overline{\tau}\geq 1,\Im(\tau)\leq T\right\}
.

4.3.3.
Arithmetic automorphic forms and Petersson norms

Let
\mathcal{L}_{D(V)}
be the restriction to
D(V)\cong\mathcal{Q}(V)
of the tautological bundle on
{\bf{P}}(V({\bf{C}}))
.
The natural action of
\operatorname{O}(V)({\bf{R}})
on
V({\bf{C}})
induces one of the connected component of the identity

\operatorname{GSpin}(V)({\bf{R}})^{0}\subset\operatorname{GSpin}(V)({\bf{R}})
on
\mathcal{L}_{D(V)}
. This gives a holomorphic line bundle

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{GSpin}(V)({\bf{Q}})\backslash\mathcal{L}_{D(V)}\times\operatorname{GSpin}(V)({\bf{A}}_{f})/K\longrightarrow X_{K},

which is known to have a canonical model defined over
{\bf{Q}}
by work of Harris
[
28
]
, cf.
[
41
,
\lx@sectionsign 1
]
,
[
29
]
.
Note that on the component
\Gamma_{h}\backslash D(V)
, it takes the form
\Gamma_{h}\backslash\mathcal{L}_{D(V)}
.
We define a hermitian metric
h_{\mathcal{L}_{D(V)}}
on
\mathcal{L}_{D(V)}
by

\displaystyle h_{\mathcal{L}_{D(V)}}(w_{1},w_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\cdot(w_{1},\overline{w}_{2}).

Observe that this metric is fixed by the action of
\operatorname{O}(V)({\bf{R}})
, and hence descends to
\mathcal{L}
.

We now describe the Petersson inner product on sections of
\mathcal{L}^{\otimes l}
for
l\in{\bf{Z}}
any integer.
Fix a Witt decomposition
V=W\oplus{\bf{Q}}e_{1}\oplus{\bf{Q}}e_{2}
for basis vectors
e_{1},e_{2}
satisfying
(e_{1},e_{2})=1

and
(e_{1},e_{1})=(e_{2},e_{2})=0
, so that
W=V\cap e_{1}^{\perp}\cap e_{2}^{\perp}
determines a rational quadratic subspace of signature
(n-1,1)
.
Given any vector
w\in V({\bf{C}})
, we then write the corresponding decomposition for the Witt decomposition as
w=\mathfrak{z}+ae_{1}+be_{2}
.
Note that
D(V)\cong\mathcal{Q}(V)
is isomorphic to the tube domain

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(V)

\displaystyle=\left\{\mathfrak{z}\in W({\bf{C}}):\Im(\mathfrak{z})\in C^{-}(V)\right\},\quad C^{-}(V):=\left\{y\in W:(y,y)<0\right\}

via

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(V)\longrightarrow V({\bf{C}})=W({\bf{C}})+{\bf{C}}e_{1}+{\bf{C}}e_{2}\longrightarrow\mathcal{Q}(V),\quad\mathfrak{z}\longmapsto w(\mathfrak{z}):=\mathfrak{z}+e_{1}-Q(\mathfrak{z})e_{2}\longmapsto[w(\mathfrak{z})].

The map
\mathfrak{z}\longmapsto w(\mathfrak{z}):=\mathfrak{z}+e_{1}-Q(\mathfrak{z})_{2}
can be viewed as a nowhere vanishing
holomorphic section of
\mathcal{L}_{D(V)}
. Observe that this section has norm for the hermitian metric
h_{\mathcal{L}_{D(V)}}
given by

\displaystyle||w(\mathfrak{z})||^{2}=-\frac{1}{2}\cdot\left(w(\mathfrak{z}),w(\overline{\mathfrak{z}})\right)=-\left(\Im(\mathfrak{z}),\Im(\mathfrak{z})\right)=-(y,y)=:|y|^{2}.

Let us now write
z=[x,y]\in D(V)=D^{\pm}(V)
for the basis
[x,y]
of an oriented hyperplane
z\subset V({\bf{R}})

w(z):=x+iy
, and
[w(z)]
its image in
\mathcal{Q}(V)=\mathcal{Q}^{\pm}(V)\cong D^{\pm}(V)
.
Given
h\in\operatorname{GSpin}(V)({\bf{R}})
, we have

\displaystyle h\cdot w(z)

\displaystyle=w(hz)j(h,z)

for a holomorphy factor

\displaystyle j:\operatorname{GSpin}(V)({\bf{R}})\times D(V)\longrightarrow{\bf{C}}.

In this way, we can identify the holomorphic sections of
\mathcal{L}^{\otimes l}
with functions

\displaystyle\Psi:D(V)\times\operatorname{GSpin}(V)({\bf{A}}_{f})\longrightarrow{\bf{C}}

satisfying the transformation properties

•

\Psi(z,hk)=\Psi(z,h)
for all
k\in K

•

\Psi(\gamma z,\gamma h)=j(\gamma,z)^{l}\Psi(z,h)
for all
\gamma\in\operatorname{GSpin}(V)({\bf{Q}})
.

The norm of the section

\displaystyle(z,h)

\displaystyle\longmapsto\Psi(z,h)\cdot w(z)^{\otimes l}

corresponding to any such function
\Psi
is given by

\displaystyle||\Psi(z,h)||^{2}

\displaystyle=|\Psi(z,h)|^{2}|y|^{2l},

and referred to as the
Petersson norm of
\Psi
.

4.3.4.
Borcherd’s products and automorphic Green’s functions

We now summarize several important results.

Theorem 4.1

(Borcherds)
.

Let
f\in M_{1-\frac{n}{2}}^{!}(\omega_{L})
be a weakly holomorphic form with Fourier series expansion

\displaystyle f(\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\mu\in L^{\vee}/L}\sum\limits_{m\in{\bf{Q}}\atop m\gg-\infty}c_{f}(\mu,m)e(m\tau){\bf{1}}_{\mu},\quad c_{f}(\mu,m)\in{\bf{Z}}.

Then,

(21)

\displaystyle\Phi(f,z,h)

\displaystyle=-2\log|\Psi(f,z,h)|^{2}-c_{f}^{+}(0,0)\cdot\left(2\log|y|+\Gamma^{\prime}(1)\right)

for
\Psi(f,z,h)
a meromorphic modular form on
D(V)\times\operatorname{GSpin}(V)({\bf{A}}_{f})
of weight
\frac{1}{2}\cdot c_{f}^{+}(0,0)
with divisor

\displaystyle\operatorname{Div}\left(\Psi(f,\cdot)^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=Z(f):=\sum\limits_{\mu\in L^{\vee}/L}\sum\limits_{m\in{\bf{Q}}\atop m<0}c_{f}^{+}(\mu,m)Z(\mu,m).

Proof.

See
[
3
, Theorem 13.3]
with
[
41
, Theorems 1.2 and 1.3]
and relevant discussions in Bruiner
[
6
]
,
[
7
]
. ∎

Theorem 4.2

(Borcherds/Bruinier)
.

Let
f\in H_{1-\frac{n}{2}}(\omega_{L})
be any harmonic weak Maass form of weight
1-\frac{n}{2}
and representation
\omega_{L}
.
The regularized theta lift
\Phi(f,\cdot)
is an automorphic Green’s function in the sense of Arakelov theory for the divisor
Z(f)
on
X_{K}
.
That is,
\Phi(f,z,h)
satifies the following characterizing properties:

(i)

\Phi(f,z,h)
is a smooth function on
X_{K}\backslash Z(f)
with a logarithmic singularity along
-2\log Z(f)
.

(ii)

The
(1,1)
-form
dd^{c}\Phi(f,z,h)
has a smooth extension to all of
X_{K}
, and satisfies the Green’s current equation

dd^{c}[\Phi(f,z,h)]=\delta_{Z(f)}+[dd^{c}\Phi(f,z,h)]
for
\delta_{Z(f)}
the Dirac current for
Z(f)
.

(iii)

\Phi(f,z,h)
is an eigenvector for the generalized Laplacian
\Delta_{z}
on
z\in D(V)
, and more precisely

\displaystyle\Delta_{z}\Phi(f,z,h)

\displaystyle=\frac{n}{4}\cdot c_{f}^{+}(0,0)\cdot\Phi(f,z,h).

Proof.

See
[
13
, Theorem 4.3]
and more generally
[
6
]
. ∎

As explained in
[
29
,
\lx@sectionsign 1.1
]
, the Shimura variety
X_{K}=X_{K_{L}}
comes equipped with a metrized line bundle

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\in\widehat{\operatorname{Pic}}(X_{K})

of weight one modular forms, which has an extension to the integral model
\widehat{\operatorname{Pic}}(\mathcal{X}_{K})
.

Theorem 4.3

(Borcherds/Howard-Madapusi Pera)
.

Let
f\in M_{1-\frac{n}{2}}^{!}(\omega_{L})
be a weakly holomorphic form with integral Fourier coefficients,

\displaystyle f(\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\mu\in L^{\vee}/L}\sum\limits_{m\in{\bf{Q}}\atop m\gg-\infty}c_{f}(\mu,m)e(m\tau){\bf{1}}_{\mu},\quad c_{f}(\mu,m)\in{\bf{Z}}.

Replacing
f
by a suitable integer multiple if needed, there exists a rational section
\Psi(f)
of the line bundle

\mathcal{L}^{c_{f}(0,0)}
on
\mathcal{X}_{K}
whose norm under the metric defined by

\displaystyle||z||

\displaystyle=\frac{(z,\overline{z})}{4\pi e^{\gamma}},\quad(z,\overline{z})=Q(z,\overline{z})-Q(z)-Q(\overline{z})

satisfies the relation

\displaystyle-2\log||\Psi(f)||

\displaystyle=\Phi(f)

and hence

\displaystyle\operatorname{Div}(\Psi(f))

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\mu\in L^{\vee}/L}\sum\limits_{m\in{\bf{Q}}\atop m>0}c_{f}(\mu,-m)Z(\mu,m).

In particular, the Borcherds product
\Phi(f)
is defined over
{\bf{Q}}
, and takes algebraic values.
To be more precise,
\Phi(f,z,h)
takes values in the algebraic number field to which the point
(z,h)\in X_{K}
belongs.

Proof.

See
[
29
, Theorem 9.1.1]
, which refines the original theorem of Borcherds
[
3
]
(cf.
[
29
, Theorem 5.2.2]
). ∎

4.4.
Hejhal Poincaré series and Green’s functions of special divisors

For future reference, we now describe the automorphic Green’s functions
\Phi_{\mu,m}(z,h)=\Phi(F_{\mu,m},z,h)

for each of the special divisors
Z(\mu,m)
on
X=X_{K}
following
[
6
]
(cf.
[
9
,
\lx@sectionsign
4]
).
This appears in the discussion leading to Corollary
4.7
below. We then describe the setup more
explicitly for the case of Hilbert modular surfaces corresponding to
n=2
, leading to classical higher
Green’s functions on
X_{0}(1)\times X_{0}(1)
and more generally
X_{0}(N)\times X_{0}(N)
.

4.4.1.
Hejhal Maass-Poincaré series

We follow the discussion in
[
6
,
\lx@sectionsign 1.3
]
. Hence, for complex numbers
\alpha,\beta,z\in{\bf{C}}
,
we consider the standard Whittaker functions
W_{\alpha,\beta}(z)

and
M_{\alpha,\beta}(z)
giving linearly independent solutions of the Whittaker differential equation

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}w}{dz^{2}}+\left(-\frac{1}{4}+\frac{\alpha}{z}-\frac{\beta^{2}-1/4}{z^{2}}\right)w

\displaystyle=0.

Note that these functions are related by

\displaystyle W_{\alpha,\beta}(z)

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma(-2\beta)}{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}-\beta-\alpha)}\cdot M_{\alpha,\beta}(z)+\frac{\Gamma(2\beta)}{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}+\beta-\alpha)}\cdot M_{\alpha,-\beta}(z),

from which it follows by inspection
W_{\alpha,\beta}(z)=W_{\alpha,-\beta}(z)
.
As
z\rightarrow 0
, these functions behave as

\displaystyle M_{\alpha,\beta}(z)

\displaystyle\sim z^{\beta+\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\quad\beta\notin-\frac{1}{2}{\bf{Z}}_{>0}

\displaystyle W_{\alpha,\beta}(z)

\displaystyle\sim\frac{\Gamma(2\beta)}{\Gamma(\beta-\alpha+\frac{1}{2})}\cdot z^{-\beta+\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\quad\beta\geq\frac{1}{2}.

As
y=\Im(z)\rightarrow\infty
, they behave as

\displaystyle M_{\alpha,\beta}(y)

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma(1+2\beta)}{\Gamma(\beta-\alpha+\frac{1}{2})}\cdot e^{\frac{y}{2}}\cdot y^{-\alpha}\left(1+O(y^{-1})\right)

\displaystyle W_{\alpha,\beta}(y)

\displaystyle=e^{-\frac{y}{2}}\cdot y^{\alpha}\left(1+O(y^{-1})\right).

Let
l=1-n/2
. Given
s\in{\bf{C}}
and
y\in{\bf{R}}_{>0}
, we define the normalized functions

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{s}(y)

\displaystyle=y^{-\frac{l}{2}}M_{-\frac{l}{2},s-\frac{1}{2}}(y),\quad\mathcal{W}_{s}(|y|)=|y|^{-\frac{l}{2}}W_{\frac{l}{2}\operatorname{sgn}(y),s-\frac{1}{2}}(|y|).

Note that these functions are both holomorphic in
s
, and also that we have the identities

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\frac{l}{2}}(y)

\displaystyle=y^{-\frac{l}{2}}M_{-\frac{l}{2},\frac{l}{2}-\frac{1}{2}}(y)=e^{\frac{y}{2}},\quad\mathcal{W}_{1-\frac{l}{2}}(y)=y^{-\frac{l}{2}}W_{\frac{l}{2},\frac{1}{2}-\frac{l}{2}}(y)=e^{-\frac{y}{2}}.

Recall that we consider the weight
l
hyperbolic Laplacian operator
\Delta_{l}
, defined on
\tau=u+iv\in\mathfrak{H}
by

\displaystyle\Delta_{l}

\displaystyle=-v^{2}\left(\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial u^{2}}+\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial v^{2}}\right)+ilv\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial u}+i\frac{\partial}{\partial v}\right).

This operator acts on smooth functions
f:\mathfrak{H}\longrightarrow{\bf{C}}[L^{\vee}/L]
component-wise. Recall as well that we have the Petersson
slash operator
|_{l}
acting on such functions. To be more precise, for any generic element
(M,\zeta)
in the two-fold metaplectic cover

\operatorname{Mp}_{2}({\bf{Z}})
of
\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\bf{Z}})\cong\operatorname{Sp}_{2}({\bf{Z}})
, this operator acts on such a function
f
via

(22)

\displaystyle(f|_{l}(M,\zeta))(\tau)

\displaystyle=\zeta(\tau)^{-2l}\omega_{L}(M,\zeta)^{-1}f(M\tau).

Note that the actions of
\Delta_{l}
and
\operatorname{Mp}_{2}({\bf{Z}})
via
(\ref{PSO})
on any smooth function

f:\mathfrak{H}\rightarrow{\bf{C}}[L^{\vee}/L]
commute in that

(23)

\displaystyle\Delta_{l}(f|_{l}(M,\zeta))

\displaystyle=(\Delta_{l}f)|_{l}(M,\zeta).

Given a coset
\mu\in L^{\vee}/L
with characteristic function
{\bf{1}}_{\mu}
as above,
and a negative integer
m\in{\bf{Z}}+Q(\mu)
, is it not hard to check that the function defined on
\tau=u+iv\in\mathfrak{H}
by

(24)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{s}(4\pi|m|v){\bf{1}}_{\mu}

is invariant under the action of the unipotent generator

\displaystyle T=\left(\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&1\\
&1\end{array}\right),1\right)\in\operatorname{Mp}_{2}({\bf{Z}})

via the slash operator
(\ref{PSO})
.
Remarkably, this function
(\ref{MWhit})
is an eigenfunction of the Laplacian
\Delta_{l}
, with eigenvalue
s(1-s)+(l^{2}-2l)/4
.
We can take sums of these functions to obtain the following Poincaré series.

Definition 4.4
.

Fix an even quadratic lattice
L=(L,Q)
of signature
(n,2)
, and let
l=1-n/2
.
Given any complex number
s\in{\bf{C}}
, coset
\mu\in L^{\vee}/L
, and integer
m\in{\bf{Z}}+Q(\mu)
,
let
F_{\mu,m}(\tau,s)
denote the Poincaré series defined on
\tau=u+iv\in\mathfrak{H}
by the summation

\displaystyle F_{\mu,m}(\tau,s)=F_{\mu,m}^{L}(\tau,s)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\Gamma(2s)}\sum\limits_{(M,\zeta)\in\Gamma^{\prime}_{\infty}\backslash\operatorname{Mp}_{2}({\bf{Z}})}\left[\mathcal{M}_{s}(4\pi|m|v){\bf{1}}_{\mu}e(mu)\right]|_{l}(M,\zeta),

where

\Gamma^{\prime}_{\infty}=(\Gamma_{\infty},1)\subset\operatorname{Mp}_{2}({\bf{Z}})

denotes the image of the unipotent subgroup

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\infty}=\left\{\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&n\\
&1\end{array}\right):n\in{\bf{Z}}\right\}\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\bf{Z}})

in the metaplectic group
\operatorname{Mp}_{2}({\bf{Z}})
. This series converges normally for
\tau=u+iv\in\mathfrak{H}

and
s=\sigma+it\in{\bf{C}}
with
\sigma>1
. Via the commutativity of the actions of
\Delta_{l}
and
\operatorname{Mp}_{2}({\bf{Z}})

described in
(\ref{commute})
, we deduce that the Poincaré series
F_{\mu,m}(\tau,s)
is an eigenfunction for the Laplacian
\Delta_{l}
, with

\displaystyle\Delta_{l}F_{\mu,m}(\tau,s)

\displaystyle=\left(s(1-s)+(l^{2}-2l)/4\right)F_{\mu,m}(\tau,s).

In particular,
F_{\mu,m}(\tau,1-l/2)=F_{\mu,m}(\tau,s)|_{s=1-l/2}
determines an eigenfunction of eigenvalue zero for
\Delta_{l}
.

Note that the Fourier series expansion of each
F_{\mu,m}(\tau,s)
is computed in
[
6
, Theorem 1.9]
,
with simplifications for the special case of
F_{\mu,m}(\tau,1-l/2)
detailed in
[
6
, Proposition 1.10]
.
Here, we note that the Fourier series expansion of each of the latter functions
F_{\mu,m}(\tau,1-l/2)
can be described here as

(25)

\displaystyle F_{\mu,m}(\tau,1-l/2)

\displaystyle={\bf{1}}_{\mu}e(m\tau)+{\bf{1}}_{-\mu}e(m\tau)+O(1).

4.4.2.
Decompositions of cuspidal harmonic weak Maass forms

We now explain how the harmonic weak Maass forms we consider above can be decomposed into linear combinations of Hejhal Maass Poincaré series.

Proposition 4.5
.

Let
L=(L,Q)
be a even lattice of signature
(n,2)
, and put
l=1-n/2
. Let
f\in H^{\operatorname{cusp}}_{l}(\overline{\omega}_{L})

be any cuspidal harmonic weak Maass form of weight
l
and representation
\overline{\omega}_{L}
, with holomorphic part

\displaystyle f^{+}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\mu\in L^{\vee}/L}\sum\limits_{m\in{\bf{Z}}+Q(\mu)\atop m<0}c_{f}^{+}(\mu,m)e(mu){\bf{1}}_{\mu}.

Then, we have the decomposition

\displaystyle f(\tau)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum\limits_{\mu\in L^{\vee}/L}\sum\limits_{m\in{\bf{Z}}+Q(\mu)\atop m<0}c_{f}^{+}(\mu,m)F_{\mu,m}(\tau,1-l/2).

Proof.

We give a minor variation of
[
6
, Proposition 1.12]
. Consider the form
g(\tau)
defined by the difference

\displaystyle g(\tau)

\displaystyle=f(\tau)-\frac{1}{2}\sum\limits_{\mu\in L^{\vee}/L}\sum\limits_{m\in{\bf{Z}}+Q(\mu)\atop m<0}c_{f}^{+}(\mu,m)F_{\mu,m}(\tau,1-l/2).

Note that this form
g(\tau)
is invariant under the action of

\displaystyle Z=\left(\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}-1&\\
&-1\end{array}\right),i\right)\in\operatorname{Mp}_{2}({\bf{Z}}),

and hence
c_{f}^{+}(\mu,m)=c_{f}^{+}(-\mu,m)
for all
\mu\in L^{\vee}/L
and
m\in{\bf{Z}}+Q(\mu)
. Using these properties together
with the expansion
(\ref{crudeFSE})
, we deduce that
g(\tau)
must be bounded as
\Im(\tau)\rightarrow\infty
.
On the other hand, it is apparent that
g|_{l}(M,\zeta)=g
for all
(M,\zeta)\in\operatorname{Mp}_{2}({\bf{Z}})
with
\Delta_{l}g=0
.
It follows that
g(\tau)
must be identically zero. ∎

4.4.3.
Unfolding against Maass-Poincaré series

We now explain how to compute the regularized theta lifts
\Phi_{\mu,m}(z,h)
associated to the Poincaré
series
F_{\mu,m}(\tau,s)
against the Siegel theta series
\theta_{L}(\tau,z)
defined
above
3
3

3

Note that Bruinier
[
6
,
\lx@sectionsign
2.2]
considers the isomorphic lattice
-L=(L,-Q)
of signature
(2,n)

for this discussion, and hence that we have to alter signs of weights accordingly to work with the quadratic lattice
(L,Q)
directly.
In particular, his signature
(2,n)=(b^{+},b^{-})
will correspond to our signature
(n,2)
in the unfolding calculations below.
.
Recall we start with
L=(L,Q)
an even full-rank lattice in
V
of signature
(n,2)
. We consider the Siegel theta series

\theta_{L}(\tau,z)\in H_{\frac{n}{2}-1}(\omega_{L})
, defined for
z\in D(V)
,

\tau=u+iv\in\mathfrak{H}
, and
h\in\operatorname{GSpin}(V)({\bf{A}}_{f})
by

\displaystyle\theta_{L}(\tau,z,h)

\displaystyle=v\sum\limits_{\lambda\in L^{\vee}}e\left(\tau Q(\lambda_{z^{\perp}})+\overline{\tau}Q(\lambda_{z})\right){\bf{1}}_{h^{-1}(\lambda+L^{\vee})},

which we decompose into coset components as

\displaystyle\theta_{L}(\tau,z,h)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\mu\in L^{\vee}/L}\theta_{\mu}(\tau,z,h){\bf{1}}_{\mu},\quad\theta_{\mu}(\tau,z,h):=\sum\limits_{\lambda\in h(\mu+L)}e\left(\tau Q(\lambda_{z^{\perp}})+\overline{\tau}Q(\lambda_{z})\right).

Here, we write
\lambda_{z}
to denote the orthogonal projection of
\lambda
to
z
, with
\lambda_{z^{\perp}}
the orthogonal projection
to the complement
z^{\perp}\subset D(V)
.
Note that the Fourier series expansion of each constituent theta series is given by

\displaystyle\theta_{\mu}(\tau,z,h)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\lambda\in h(\mu+L)}e\left(-2\pi Q(\lambda_{z})+2\pi vQ(\lambda_{z^{\perp}})\right)e(Q(\lambda)u),

which via the elementary identity

\displaystyle Q(\gamma_{z})+Q(\gamma_{z^{\perp}})=Q(\gamma)\implies 2\pi vQ(\gamma_{z^{\perp}})=2\pi vQ(\gamma)-2\pi Q(\gamma_{z})

is the same as

\displaystyle\theta_{\mu}(\tau,z,h)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\lambda\in h(\mu+L)}\exp\left(2\pi vQ(\lambda)\right)\exp\left(-4\pi vQ(\lambda_{z})+2\pi vQ(\lambda)\right)e(Q(\lambda)u)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\lambda\in h(\mu+L)}\exp\left(2\pi vQ(\lambda)-4\pi vQ(\lambda_{z})\right)e(Q(\lambda)u)

and hence

(26)

\displaystyle\overline{\theta_{\mu}(\tau,z)}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\lambda\in h(\mu+L)}\exp\left(2\pi vQ(\lambda)-4\pi vQ(\lambda_{z})\right)e(-Q(\lambda)u).

Theorem 4.6
.

Let
L=(L,Q)
be an even lattice of signature
(n,2)
.
Fix any coset
\mu\in L^{\vee}/L
and negative integer
m\in{\bf{Z}}+Q(\mu)
.
Then, for any point
z\in D(V)
which is not contained in the set

\displaystyle Z(\mu,m)

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{\lambda\in\mu+L\atop Q(\lambda)=m}\lambda^{\perp}

and element
h\in\operatorname{Spin}(V)({\bf{A}}_{f})
together with any

s,w\in{\bf{C}}
satisfying
\Re(s)>-l/2+\Re(w)=n/4+1/2+\Re(w)
, the regularized theta lift
defined
4
4

4

A priori,
\Phi_{\mu,m}(z,h)
is defined as in
[
6
, Definition 2.10]
to be the constant term in the Laurent series
expansion around
s=1-l/2=n/4+1/2
of the analytic continuation of
\Phi_{\mu,m}(z,h,s)=\Phi_{\mu,m}(z,h,s;0)
. However, by
to
[
6
, Proposition 2.11]
, we can define it equivalently as the stated limit of truncated theta integrals when
s\neq 1-l/2=n/4+1/2
.

according to
[
6
, Proposition 2.11]
by the limit of truncated integrals

\displaystyle\Phi_{\mu,m}(z,s,h;w)

\displaystyle:=\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\int\limits_{\mathcal{F}_{T}}\langle\langle F_{\mu,m}(\tau,s),\overline{\theta_{L}(\tau,z,h)}\rangle\rangle v^{w}\frac{dudv}{v^{2}}

is given by the explicit (absolutely convergent) formula

\displaystyle\Phi_{\mu,m}(z,s,h;w)

\displaystyle=\frac{2(4\pi m)^{s+\frac{n}{4}-\frac{1}{2}}\Gamma\left(\frac{n}{4}-\frac{1}{2}+s+w\right)}{\Gamma(2s)}\sum\limits_{\lambda\in h(\mu+L)\atop Q(\lambda)=m}\frac{{}_{2}F_{1}\left(s+\frac{n}{4}-\frac{1}{2}+w,s-\frac{n}{4}+\frac{1}{2},2s;\frac{m}{Q(\lambda_{z_{0}^{\perp}})}\right)}{(4\pi Q(\lambda_{z^{\perp}}))^{s+\frac{n}{4}-\frac{1}{2}+w}}.

In particular, at
w=0
, we obtain for
\Re(s)>n/4+1/2
the formula

\displaystyle\Phi_{\mu,m}(z,s,h;0)

\displaystyle=\frac{2\Gamma\left(s+\frac{n}{4}-\frac{1}{2}\right)}{\Gamma(2s)}\sum\limits_{\lambda\in h(\mu+L)\atop Q(\lambda)=m}\left(\frac{m}{Q(\lambda_{z^{\perp}})}\right)^{s+\frac{n}{4}-\frac{1}{2}}{}_{2}F_{1}\left(s+\frac{n}{4}-\frac{1}{2},s-\frac{n}{4}+\frac{1}{2},2s;\frac{m}{Q(\lambda_{z^{\perp}})}\right).

Proof.

See Bruinier
[
6
, Theorem 2.14]
. We present a slight generalization to help make our later deductions more explicit.
Fix
s\in{\bf{C}}
with
\Re(s)>n/4+1/2+\Re(w)
. Fix a point
z\in D(V)\backslash Z(\mu,m)
. We have that

\displaystyle\Phi_{\mu,m}(z,s,h;w)

\displaystyle=\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}I_{T}(\mu,m,s,h;w),

where

\displaystyle I_{T}(\mu,m,s,h;w)=\int\limits_{\mathcal{F}_{T}}\langle\langle F_{\mu,m}(\tau,s),\theta_{L}(\tau,z,h)\rangle\rangle v^{w}\frac{dudv}{v^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\Gamma(2s)}\int\limits_{\mathcal{F}_{T}}\sum\limits_{M\in\Gamma_{\infty}\backslash\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\bf{Z}})}\langle\langle\left[\mathcal{M}_{s}(4\pi|m|v){\bf{1}}_{\mu}e(mu)\right]|_{l}(M,1),\overline{\theta_{L}(\tau,z,h)}\rangle\rangle v^{w}\frac{dvdu}{v^{2}},

which via the transformation of the theta series is the same as

\displaystyle I_{T}(\mu,m,s,h;w)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\Gamma(2s)}\int\limits_{\mathcal{F}_{T}}\sum\limits_{M\in\Gamma_{\infty}\backslash\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\bf{Z}})}\mathcal{M}_{s}(4\pi|m|\Im(M\tau))\Im(M\tau)^{w}\langle\langle{\bf{1}}_{\mu}e(m\Re(M\tau)),\overline{\theta_{L}(\tau,z,h)}\rangle\rangle\frac{dudv}{v^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\Gamma(2s)}\int\limits_{\mathcal{F}_{T}}\mathcal{M}_{s}(4\pi|m|v)v^{w}e(mu)\overline{\theta_{\mu}(\tau,z,h)}\frac{dudv}{v^{2}}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\Gamma(2s)}\int\limits_{\mathcal{F}_{T}}\sum\limits_{M=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}a&b\\
c&d\end{array}\right)\in\Gamma_{\infty}\backslash\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\bf{Z}}),c\neq 0}\mathcal{M}_{s}(4\pi|m|\Im(M\tau))\Im(M\tau)^{w}e(m\Re(M\tau))\overline{\theta_{\mu}(\tau,z,h)}\frac{dudv}{v^{2}}.

Using a standard unfolding argument, we can evaluate the second integral in the latter expression as

\displaystyle\frac{2}{\Gamma(2s)}\int\limits_{\mathcal{G}_{T}}\mathcal{M}_{s}(4\pi|m|v)v^{w}\overline{\theta_{\mu}(\tau,z)}\frac{dudv}{v^{2}},

where
\mathcal{G_{T}}=\{\tau=u+iv\in\mathfrak{H}:|u|\leq 1/2,u^{2}+v^{2}\leq 1,v\leq T\}

denotes the truncated fundamental domain for the action of
\Gamma_{\infty}
on
\bigcup_{M-\Gamma_{\infty}}M\mathcal{F}
.
In this way, we compute

\displaystyle I_{T}(\mu,m,s,h;w)=\frac{2}{\Gamma(2s)}\int\limits_{\mathcal{F}_{T}}\mathcal{M}_{s}(4\pi|m|v)v^{w}e(mu)\overline{\theta_{\mu}(\tau,z,h)}\frac{dudv}{v^{2}}+\frac{2}{\Gamma(2s)}\int\limits_{\mathcal{G}_{T}}\mathcal{M}_{s}(4\pi|m|v)v^{w}\overline{\theta_{\mu}(\tau,z,h)}\frac{dudv}{v^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{2\Gamma(2s)}\int\limits_{v=0}^{T}\int\limits_{u=0}^{1}\mathcal{M}_{s}(4\pi|m|v)v^{w}\overline{\theta_{\mu}(u+iv,z,h)}\frac{dudv}{v^{2}},

which after opening the Fourier series expansion
(\ref{thetaFSE})
of
\overline{\theta_{\mu}(u+iv,z,h)}
, switching the order of summation,
and evaluating the unipotent integral via the orthogonality of characters on
{\bf{R}}/{\bf{Z}}\cong[0,1]
is the same as

\displaystyle I_{T}(\mu,m,s,h;w)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\Gamma(2s)}\int\limits_{v=0}^{T}\sum\limits_{\lambda\in h(\mu+L)}\mathcal{M}_{s}(4\pi|m|v)v^{w}\exp\left(2\pi vQ(\lambda)-4\pi vQ(\lambda_{z})\right)\int\limits_{u=0}^{1}e(mu-Q(\lambda)u)dudv

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\Gamma(2s)}\int\limits_{v=0}^{T}\sum\limits_{\lambda\in h(\mu+L)\atop Q(\lambda)=m}\mathcal{M}_{s}(4\pi|m|v)v^{w}\exp\left(-4\pi vQ(\lambda_{z})+2\pi vm\right)dv

\displaystyle=\frac{2(4\pi|m|)^{-\frac{l}{2}}}{\Gamma(2s)}\int\limits_{v=0}^{T}\sum\limits_{\lambda\in h(\mu+L)\atop Q(\lambda)=m}M_{-\frac{l}{2},s-\frac{1}{2}}(4\pi|m|v)v^{w-\frac{l}{2}-2}\exp\left(-4\pi vQ(\lambda_{z})+2\pi vm\right)dv

\displaystyle=\frac{2(4\pi|m|)^{\frac{n}{4}-\frac{1}{2}}}{\Gamma(2s)}\sum\limits_{\lambda\in h(\mu+L)\atop Q(\lambda)=m}\int\limits_{v=0}^{T}M_{\frac{n}{4}-\frac{1}{2},s-\frac{1}{2}}(4\pi|m|v)v^{w+\frac{n}{4}-\frac{1}{2}-2}\exp\left(-4\pi vQ(\lambda_{z})+2\pi vm\right)dv.

Here, we view the latter sum as one over lattice points
\lambda\in h(\mu+L)
for which

\displaystyle Q(\lambda)=Q(\lambda_{z})+Q(\lambda_{z^{\perp}})=m,

where
z^{\perp}
denotes the orthogonal complement of
z
in D(V). Taking the limit with
T\rightarrow\infty
, we obtain

(27)

\displaystyle\Phi_{\mu,m}(z,s,h;w)

\displaystyle=\frac{2(4\pi|m|)^{\frac{n}{4}-\frac{1}{2}}}{\Gamma(2s)}\sum\limits_{\lambda\in h(\mu+L)\atop Q(\lambda)=m}\int\limits_{v=0}^{\infty}M_{\frac{n}{4}-\frac{1}{2},s-\frac{1}{2}}(4\pi|m|v)v^{\left(w+\frac{n}{4}-\frac{1}{2}-1\right)-1}\exp\left(-4\pi vQ(\lambda_{z})+2\pi mv\right)dv.

Now, for each
\lambda\in h(\mu+L)
with
\lambda_{z}\neq 0
, we view the corresponding integral in
(\ref{PTLint})

as a Laplace transform of the type computed in
[
21
, p. 215 (11)]
, with

\nu=w-l/2-1=w+(n/4-1/2)-1
,

\kappa=-l/2=-1/2+n/4
,

\varpi=s-1/2
,

\alpha=4\pi|m|
, and

p=(4\pi Q_{0}(\lambda_{z})-2\pi m)=(4\pi Q(\lambda_{z})+2\pi|m|)
to
obtain
5
5

5

Noting that
Q(\lambda_{z})+|m|=Q(\lambda_{z^{\perp}})
to simplify the expression for
p

(28)

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\int\limits_{v=0}^{\infty}M_{\frac{n}{4}-\frac{1}{2},s-\frac{1}{2}}(4\pi|m|v)v^{\left(w+\frac{n}{4}-\frac{1}{2}-1\right)-1}e^{-v\left(4\pi Q(\lambda_{z})-2\pi m\right)}dv

\displaystyle=\alpha^{\mu+1/2}\Gamma\left(\mu+\nu+1/2\right)\cdot\frac{{}_{2}F_{1}\left(\varpi+\nu+1/2,\varpi-\kappa+1/2,2\varpi+1;\alpha/(p+\alpha/2)\right)}{(p+\alpha/2)^{\varpi+\nu+1/2}}

\displaystyle=(4\pi|m|)^{s}\Gamma\left(\frac{n}{4}-\frac{1}{2}+s+w\right)\cdot\frac{{}_{2}F_{1}\left(s+\frac{n}{4}-\frac{1}{2}+w,s-\frac{n}{4}+\frac{1}{2},2s;\frac{|m|}{Q(\lambda_{z_{0}})}\right)}{(4\pi Q(\lambda_{z}))^{s+\frac{n}{4}-\frac{1}{2}+w}}.

We treat the remaining contributions from
\lambda_{z}=0
the same way,
using that
Q(\lambda_{z})=m-Q(\lambda_{z^{\perp}})
and hence

4\pi Q(\lambda_{z})-2\pi m+2\pi|m|=-4\pi Q(\lambda_{z^{\perp}})+2\pi m+2\pi|m|=-4\pi Q(\lambda_{z^{\perp}})
.
Here, note that
m<0
implies
2\pi m+2\pi|m|=0
.
We can and do express all contributions
\lambda
via this latter substitution as in
[
6
, Theorem 2.14]
.
In this way, we deduce the stated formula from
(\ref{PTLint})
via
(\ref{laplace})
. ∎

4.4.4.
Regularized theta lifts
\Phi_{\mu,m}
of the Hejhal-Maass Poincaré series
F_{\mu,m}

Note that the function of Theorem
4.6
above, defined for
\Re(s)>u=u(n):=1-l/2=n/4+1/2
by the absolutely convergent series

(29)

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\Phi_{\mu,m}(z,h,s)=\Phi_{\mu,m}(z,h,s,0)=\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\int\limits_{\mathcal{F}_{T}}\langle\langle F_{\mu,m}(\tau,s),\overline{\theta_{L}(\tau,h,z)}\rangle\rangle\frac{dudv}{v^{2}}

\displaystyle=2\cdot\frac{\Gamma\left(s+\frac{n}{4}-\frac{1}{2}\right)}{\Gamma\left(2s\right)}\sum\limits_{\lambda\in g(\mu+L)\atop Q(\lambda)=m}\left(\frac{m}{Q(\lambda_{z}^{\perp})}\right)^{s+\frac{n}{4}-\frac{1}{2}}{}_{2}F_{1}\left(s+\frac{n}{4}-\frac{1}{2},s-\frac{n}{4}+\frac{1}{2},2s;\frac{m}{Q(\lambda_{z^{\perp}})}\right),

determines a real analytic function on
z\in D(V)
, with logarithmic singularities along the special divisors

\displaystyle Z(\mu,m)

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{\lambda\in\mu+L\atop Q(\lambda)=m}\lambda^{\perp}.

Defined a priori for
\Re(s)>u
, this function
\Phi_{\mu,m}(z,h,s)

has an analytic continuation to all
s\in{\bf{C}}
, with a simple pole at
u
.
Hence, the sum defining
(\ref{HG})
converges normally, and
\Phi_{\mu,m}
determines a smooth
function on
X\backslash Z(\mu,m)
with logarithmic singularities along
Z(\mu,m)
, and an analytic
continuation to a meromorphic function in
s\in{\bf{C}}
.
It is also an eigenfunction for the Laplacian
\Delta_{z}
described in Theorem
4.2
, satisfying

(30)

\displaystyle\Delta_{z}\Phi_{\mu,m}(z,h,s)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\cdot(s-u)\cdot(s+u-1)\cdot\Phi_{\mu,m}(z,h,s).

It can also be viewed as a square integrable function. We refer to the discussion of
[
9
,
\lx@sectionsign
4]
for more details.
At the point
u=n/4+1/2
, it determines the automorphic Green’s function of the special divisor
Z(\mu,m)
:

Corollary 4.7
.

The regularized theta lift
\Phi_{\mu,m}(z,h,n/4+1/2)=\Phi(F_{\mu,m},z,h)

of the harmonic weak Maass form
F_{\mu,m}(\tau)=F_{\mu,m}(\tau,(2-l)/2)=F_{\mu,m}(\tau,n/4+1/2)\in H_{1-n/2}(\omega_{L})

is the automorphic Green’s function for the special divisor
Z(\mu,m)
on the spin Shimura variety
X=X_{K}=X_{K_{L}}
.

Proof.

Cf.
[
10
, Remark 3.10]
with
[
6
, Definiton 1.8 and Proposition 1.10]
,
[
9
,
\lx@sectionsign
4]
, and
[
1
,
\lx@sectionsign
5]
. ∎

4.4.5.
Extension to compactifications

Fix
f\in H_{1-n/2}(\omega_{L})
a harmonic weak Maass form whose holomorphic part
f^{+}
has integral Fourier coefficients.
Fix a compactification
X^{\star}
of the Shimura variety
X=X_{K}
.
For the divisor
Z(f)\subset X
defined in
(\ref{Z(f)})
above, there exists a divisor
C(f)
supported
on the boundary
\partial X^{\star}=X^{\star}\backslash X
such that
\Phi(f,\cdot)
is the automorphic
Green’s function in the sense of Theorem
4.2
for the corresponding divisor

(31)

\displaystyle Z^{c}(f)

\displaystyle=Z(f)+C(f)

of degree zero on
X
.
For a more precise description of this in the setting of the modular curve, with the
quadratic space given in Example
7.2
below, we refer to the discussion in
[
13
,
\lx@sectionsign
7.3]
.

4.4.6.
The special case of Hilbert modular surfaces

Fix
k
any quadratic field with class group
C(\mathcal{O}_{K})
, and
A=[\mathfrak{a}]\in C(\mathcal{O}_{K})
any ideal class.
We now explain how to specialize the definition
(\ref{HG})
to the setup we consider with the spaces
(V_{A},Q_{A})

of signature
(2,2)
with lattices
L_{A}\subset V_{A}
giving rise to the Hilbert modular surface
X_{K_{A}}\cong Y_{0}(N)^{2}
.
Here, we adapt the discussion of
[
9
,
\lx@sectionsign 6.1
]
as follows.
As explained above, we have a natural identification
D(V_{A})=D^{\pm}(V_{A})\cong\mathfrak{H}^{2}
,
so can view the variable
z\in D(V_{A})
as a pair
z=(z_{1},z_{2})\in\mathfrak{H}^{2}
.
Let
\mathcal{Q}_{s-1}
denote the classical Legendre function of the second kind

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{s-1}(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(t+\sqrt{t^{2}-1}\cosh(u)\right)^{-s}du=\frac{\Gamma(s)^{2}}{2\Gamma(2s)}\left(\frac{2}{1+t}\right)^{s}{}_{2}F_{1}\left(s,s,2s;\frac{2}{1+t}\right).

Hence, we can rewrite the corresponding theta lift
(\ref{HG})
in this setting as

\displaystyle\Phi_{\mu,m}(z,s)=\Phi_{\mu,m}(z,1,s)

\displaystyle=2\cdot\frac{\Gamma\left(s+\frac{2}{4}-\frac{1}{2}\right)}{\Gamma\left(2s\right)}\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\mu+L_{A}\atop Q_{A}(\lambda)=m}\left(\frac{m}{Q_{A}(\lambda_{z}^{\perp})}\right)^{s}{}_{2}F_{1}\left(s,s,2s;\frac{m}{Q(\lambda_{z^{\perp}})}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{\Gamma(s)}\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\mu+L_{A}\atop Q_{A}(\lambda)=m}\mathcal{Q}_{s-1}\left(1-\frac{2Q_{A}(\lambda_{z})}{m}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{\Gamma(s)}\sum\limits_{\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\in\mu+L_{A}=\mathfrak{a}+N^{-1}\mathfrak{a}\atop Q_{A}(\lambda)=m}\mathcal{Q}_{s-1}\left(1-\frac{2}{m{\bf{N}}\mathfrak{a}}\left(\lambda_{1,z}\lambda_{1,z}^{\tau}-\lambda_{2,z}\lambda_{2,z}^{\tau}\right)\right).

Here again, we write
\tau\in\operatorname{Gal}(k/{\bf{Q}})
to denote the nontrivial automorphism,
with
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\in L_{A}
a vector, and

\lambda_{j,z}=\lambda_{j,(z_{1},z_{2})}
the corresponding projections to
z=(z_{1},z_{2})\in\mathfrak{H}^{2}
.
In this way, we see that

\displaystyle\Phi_{m}(z_{1},z_{2},s)

\displaystyle=\Phi_{m}^{L_{A}}(z_{1},z_{2},s):=\sum\limits_{\mu\in L_{A}^{\vee}/L_{A}}\Phi_{\mu,m}(z_{1},z_{2},s)=-\frac{2}{\Gamma(s)}G_{s}^{\Gamma_{0}(N),m}(z_{1},z_{2}),

where
G_{s}^{\Gamma_{0}(N),m}(z_{1},z_{2},s)
denotes a translate the higher Green’s function characterized in

[
26
,
\lx@sectionsign
II.2, (2.3)]
and
[
65
,
\lx@sectionsign
3.4]
, cf.
[
9
,
\lx@sectionsign
6.1]
.
To be more precise, we see from
(\ref{LE})
that
\Phi_{m}(z,s)=\Phi_{m}^{L_{A}}(z_{1},z_{2},s)
is an eigenvector
for the hyperbolic Laplacian operator
\Delta_{z}=\Delta_{(z_{1},z_{2})}=\Delta_{z_{1}}\times\Delta_{z_{2}}
on
X_{0}(N)\times X_{0}(N)
satisfying

\displaystyle\Delta_{z}\Phi_{m}(z_{1},z_{2},s)=\frac{s}{2}(s-1)\Phi_{m}(z_{1},z_{2},s).

On the other hand, there is a unique resolvent kernel function
G_{s}^{\Gamma_{0}(N)}(z_{1},z_{2})
on
\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{H}
characterized by:

(i)

G_{s}^{\Gamma_{0}(N)}(z_{1},z_{2})
is smooth
\mathfrak{H}\times\mathfrak{H}\backslash\{(\tau,\gamma\tau):\gamma\in\Gamma_{0}(N)\}

(ii)

G_{s}^{\Gamma_{0}(N)}(\gamma_{1}z_{1},\gamma_{2}z_{2})=G_{s}^{\Gamma_{0}(N)}(z_{1},z_{2})
for all
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\in\Gamma_{0}(N)

(iii)

\Delta_{z_{j}}G_{s}^{\Gamma_{0}(N)}(z_{1},z_{2})=s(s-1)G_{s}^{\Gamma_{0}(N)}(z_{1},z_{2})
for each of
j=1,2
,
where
\Delta_{z_{j}}-y_{j}^{2}\left(\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x_{j}^{2}}+\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial y_{j}^{2}}\right)

denotes the hyperbolic Laplacian of weight zero in either variable
z_{j}=x_{j}+iy_{j}\in\mathfrak{H}

(iv)

G_{s}^{\Gamma_{0}(N)}(z_{1},z_{2})=e_{z_{2}}\log|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{2}+O(1)
as
z_{1}\rightarrow z_{2}
,
where
e_{z_{2}}=\#\operatorname{Stab}_{\Gamma_{0}(N)}(z_{2})
is the order of the stabilizer of
z_{2}
in
\Gamma_{0}(N)

(v)

In a neighbourhood of a cusp
\alpha^{-1}\infty
, the function
\Im(\alpha z_{1})^{s-1}G_{s}^{\Gamma_{0}(N)}(z_{1},z_{2})
extends to a continuous function.

The existence of such a function follows from the construction given in
[
26
,
\lx@sectionsign
II.2]
.
That properties (iii)-(v) characterize it uniquely for
\Re(s)>1
is shown in
[
65
,
\lx@sectionsign
3.4]
. We deduce
that the function
-\frac{\Gamma(s)}{2}\Phi_{m}(z_{1},z_{1},s)
at
m=1
satisfies these properties, and that the corresponding “higher” Green’s functions

-\frac{\Gamma(s)}{2}\Phi_{m}(z_{1},z_{2},s)
for
m>1
can be viewed as translates of this resolvent kernel function
G_{s}^{\Gamma_{0}(N)}(z_{1},z_{2})
.

5.
Summation along isotropic quadratic subspaces

We now compute the regularized theta lifts
\Phi(f,z,h)
along CM cycles
Z(V_{0})
and geodesic sets
\mathfrak{G}(W)
.

5.1.
Eisenstein series and Siegel-Weil formulae

Fix
V_{0}\subset V
any quadratic subspace of signature
(0,2)
, writing
L_{0}=V_{0}\cap L
for the corresponding lattice
and
Q_{0}=Q|_{V_{0}}
the corresponding quadratic form. We also fix
W\subset V
any rational quadratic space of signature

(1,1)
, writing
L_{W}=W\cap L
for the corresponding lattice and
Q_{W}=Q|_{W}
the corresponding quadratic form.
We now describe the Eisenstein series associated with these quadratic spaces.

5.1.1.
Langlands Eisenstein series and the Siegel-Weil formula

We first describe the construction in more general terms.
Let
(U,Q)
be any anisotropic rational quadratic space of even dimension
\dim(U)
and signature
(p(U),q(U))
.
Fix a lattice
L\subset U
, and consider the corresponding Weil representation

\omega_{L}:\operatorname{Mp}_{2}({\bf{Z}})\longrightarrow\mathfrak{S}_{L}
.

Let us write
P=MN\subset\operatorname{SL}_{2}
to denote the parabolic group of upper-triangular matrices,
with Levi subgroup
M
and unipotent radical
N
parametrized with the standard shorthand notations

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} M&=\left\{m(a):a\in{\bf{G}}_{m}\right\},&\quad m(a):&=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}a^{\frac{1}{2}}&\\
&a^{-\frac{1}{2}}\end{array}\right)\\
N&=\left\{n(b):b\in{\bf{G}}_{a}\right\},&\quad n(b):&=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&b\\
&1\end{array}\right).\end{aligned}

Hence, writing
K_{\infty}=\operatorname{SO}_{2}({\bf{R}})
for the maximal compact subgroup of
\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\bf{R}})

and
K=\operatorname{SL}_{2}(\widehat{\bf{Z}})
for the maximal compact subgroup of
\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\bf{A}}_{f})
,
we have the Iwasawa decomposition
\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\bf{A}})=N({\bf{A}})M({\bf{A}})K_{\infty}K
.
Writing
M^{\prime}
,
N^{\prime}
,
K^{\prime}
, and
K_{\infty}^{\prime}
to denote the respective images in
\operatorname{Mp}_{2}
,
we have the corresponding Iwasawa decomposition
\operatorname{Mp}_{2}({\bf{A}})=N({\bf{A}})^{\prime}M({\bf{A}})^{\prime}K_{\infty}^{\prime}K^{\prime}
.
To describe elements of the metaplectic group, we identify
\operatorname{Mp}_{2}({\bf{A}})=\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\bf{A}})\times\{\pm 1\}
,
with multiplication on the right given by
[g_{1},\epsilon_{1}][g_{2},\epsilon_{2}]=[g_{1}g_{2},\epsilon_{1}\epsilon_{2}c(g_{1},g_{2})]

for
c
the cocycle defined in
[
24
]
and
[
56
]
. Note that any idele class character
\chi
of
{\bf{Q}}
determines

\displaystyle\chi^{\psi}([m(a),\epsilon])

\displaystyle=\epsilon\chi(a)\gamma(a,\psi)^{-1}

of
\chi^{\psi}
of
M^{\prime}({\bf{A}})=\{[m(a),\epsilon],a\in{\bf{A}}^{\times}\}
, where
\gamma(\cdot,\psi)
denotes the global Weil index.

Let
\chi_{U}
denote the quadratic idele class character of
{\bf{Q}}
given on
x\in{\bf{A}}^{\times}
by

\displaystyle\chi_{U}(x)

\displaystyle=\left(x,(-1)^{\frac{\dim(U)}{2}}\det(U)\right)_{\bf{A}},

where
\left(\cdot,\cdot\right)_{\bf{A}}
denotes the Hilbert symbol on
{\bf{A}}^{\times}
, and
\det(U)
the Gram determinant of
U
.
Given
s\in{\bf{C}}
, let
I(s,\chi_{U})
denote the principal series representation of
\operatorname{Mp}_{2}({\bf{A}})

induced by the quasicharacter
\chi_{U}(\cdot)|\cdot|^{s}
. Hence,
I(s,\chi_{U})
consists of all smooth functions

\phi(g,s)
on
g^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Mp}_{2}({\bf{A}})
satisfying the transformation property

\displaystyle\phi([n(b),1][m(a),\epsilon]g^{\prime},s)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\chi^{\psi}_{U}([m(a),\epsilon])|a|^{s+1}\phi(g^{\prime},s)&\text{if $p(U)\equiv 1\bmod 2$}\\
\chi_{U}(a)|a|^{s+1}\phi(g,s)&\text{if $p(U)\equiv 0\bmod 2$}\end{cases}

for all
a\in{\bf{A}}^{\times}
and
b\in{\bf{A}}
, with
\operatorname{Mp}_{2}({\bf{A}})
acting by right translation.
There is an
\operatorname{Mp}_{2}({\bf{A}})
-intertwining map

\displaystyle\lambda:\mathcal{S}(U({\bf{A}}))\longrightarrow I(s_{0}(U),\chi_{U}),\quad\lambda(\Phi^{\prime})(g):=\left(\omega_{L}(g^{\prime})\Phi\right)(0)\quad\text{ for ~~$s_{0}(U):=\frac{\dim(U)}{2}-1$}.

Recall that a section
\phi(s)\in I(s,\chi_{U})
is
standard
if its restriction to the maximal compact subgroup
K_{\infty}^{\prime}K\subset^{\prime}\operatorname{Mp}_{2}({\bf{A}})

does not depend on
s
. Via the Iwasawa decomposition, we see that any
\lambda(\Phi)\in I(s_{0}(U),\chi_{U})
has a unique extension to a standard
section
\lambda(\Phi)(s)\in I(s,\chi_{U})
such that
\lambda(\Phi)(s_{0}(U))=\lambda(\phi)
. We consider the following standard sections.
Let us for any
l\in{\bf{Z}}
write
\chi_{l}
to denote the character of
K_{\infty}
defined by

\displaystyle\chi_{l}(k_{\theta})=e^{il\theta}=\exp(il\theta),\quad k_{\theta}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\cos\theta&\sin\theta\\
-\sin\theta&\cos\theta\end{array}\right)\in K_{\infty}.

Let
\phi_{\infty}^{l}(s)\in I(s,\chi_{U})
be the unique standard section for which
\phi_{\infty}^{l}(k_{\theta},s)=\chi_{l}(k_{\theta})=e^{il\theta}
.
In terms of the Iwasawa decomposition, this section can be characterized by the transformation property

\displaystyle\phi_{\infty}^{l}(n(b)m(a)k_{\infty},s)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\chi^{\psi}_{U}([m(a),\epsilon])|a|^{s+1}e^{il\theta}&\text{if $p(U)\equiv 1\bmod 2$}\\
\chi_{U}(a)|a|^{s+1}e^{il\theta}&\text{if $p(U)\equiv 0\bmod 2$}.\end{cases}

Now, recall we defined the Gaussian
\Phi_{\infty}\in\mathcal{S}(U({\bf{R}}))
in
(\ref{Gaussian})
for
(V,Q)
of signature
(n,2)
.
More generally, writing
D(U)=\left\{z\subset U({\bf{R}}):\dim(u)=p(U),Q|_{z}<0\right\}

for the corresponding domain, and defining for a given
z\in D(U)
the
corresponding majorant
(x,x)_{z}=(x_{z^{\perp}},x_{z^{\perp}})-(x_{z},x_{z})
on
x\in U({\bf{R}})
, we let

\displaystyle\Phi_{\infty}(x,z)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(-(x,x)_{z}\right).

Again, we see that
\Phi_{\infty}(hx,hz)=\Phi_{\infty}(x,z)
for all
h\in\operatorname{GSpin}(U)({\bf{R}})
.
Viewed as a function of
x\in U({\bf{R}})
, we obtain an archimedean local Schwartz function
\Phi_{\infty}\in\mathcal{S}(U({\bf{R}}))
.
Through the Weil representation
\omega_{L}
, we also know that
K^{\prime}_{\infty}
acts on
\Phi_{\infty}(x,z)
with weight
\frac{p(U)-q(U)}{2}
.
Hence, we see in general that

\displaystyle\lambda_{\infty}(\Phi_{\infty}(\cdot,z))

\displaystyle=\phi_{\infty}^{\frac{p(U)-q(U)}{2}}(s_{0}(U)),\quad s_{0}(U):=\frac{\dim(U)}{2}-1.

Given any standard section
\phi(s)\in I(s,\chi_{U})
, we consider the corresponding Langlands Eisenstein
series on
g^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Mp}_{2}({\bf{A}})
, defined first for
\Re(s)>1
by the summation

\displaystyle E_{L}(g^{\prime},s;\phi)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\gamma^{\prime}\in P^{\prime}({\bf{Q}})\backslash\operatorname{Mp}_{2}({\bf{Q}})}\phi(\gamma^{\prime}g^{\prime},s).

This sum
E_{L}(g^{\prime},s;\phi)
has a meromorphic continuation to all
s\in{\bf{C}}

via the Langlands functional equation, which relates
E_{L}(g^{\prime},s;\phi)
to
E_{L}(g^{\prime},-s;M(s)\phi)
for the corresponding intertwining operator
M(s)
.
It determines an automorphic form on
g\in\operatorname{Mp}_{2}({\bf{A}})
.
Its value at
s_{0}(U)
is known classically to be holomorphic, as it is given as an average
of the theta series
\vartheta_{L}(g^{\prime},h;\Phi)
we considered above by the following classical theorem.

Theorem 5.1

(Siegel-Weil)
.

Let
(U,Q)
be any anisotropic quadratic space of signature
(p(U),q(U))
and even dimension
p(U)+q(U)
.
Let
L\subset U
be any integral lattice, and
\Phi\in\mathcal{S}(U({\bf{A}}))
any Schwartz function.
The Eisenstein series
E_{L}(g,s;\Phi)
is holomorphic at
s_{0}(U)=(p(U)+q(U))/2-1
, and given by the formula

\displaystyle\frac{\alpha}{2}\int\limits_{\operatorname{SO}(U)({\bf{Q}})\backslash\operatorname{SO}(U)({\bf{A}})}\vartheta_{L}(g,h;\Phi)dh

\displaystyle=E_{L}(g,s_{0}(U);\lambda(\Phi)),\quad\alpha:=\begin{cases}2&\text{ if $p(U)=0$}\\
1&\text{ if $p(U)>1$}\end{cases}.

Here, we write
dh
to denote the Tamagawa measure on
\operatorname{SO}(U)({\bf{A}})
.

Proof.

See e.g.
[
41
, Theorem 4.1]
or
[
13
, Theorem 2.1]
. ∎

5.1.2.
The CM case

We now consider the special case of the negative definite subspace
(L_{0},Q_{0})
.
Note that the corresponding Weil representation
\omega_{L_{0}}
and Langlands Eisenstein series factor through
\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\bf{A}})
in this case.
Hence, we consider for each integer
l\in{\bf{Z}}
the
\mathfrak{S}_{L_{0}}
-valued Langlands Eisenstein
series defined by

\displaystyle E_{L_{0}}(\tau,s;l)

\displaystyle:=\sum\limits_{\mu\in L_{0}^{\vee}/L_{0}}E_{L_{0}}(g_{\tau},s;\phi_{\infty}^{l}\otimes\lambda_{f}({\bf{1}}_{\mu})){\bf{1}}_{\mu},\quad g_{\tau}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}v^{\frac{1}{2}}&uv^{-\frac{1}{2}}\\
&v^{-\frac{1}{2}}\end{array}\right)\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\bf{R}}),\tau=u+iv\in\mathcal{F}.

We can then describe Theorem
5.1
in terms of the Siegel theta series
(\ref{Stheta3})
as

(34)

\displaystyle\int\limits_{\operatorname{SO}(V_{0})({\bf{Q}})\backslash\operatorname{SO}(V_{0})({\bf{A}}_{f})}\theta_{L_{0}}(\tau,z_{0},h_{f})dh

\displaystyle=E_{L_{0}}(\tau,0;-1).

Here (cf.
[
13
, Proposition 2.2]
), we write
z_{0}\in D(V_{0})=D^{\pm}(V_{0})

to denote the oriented hypersurface determined by
V_{0}({\bf{R}})
.
We also normalize the measure on
\operatorname{SO}(V_{0})({\bf{R}})\cong\operatorname{SO}_{2}({\bf{R}})

so that
\operatorname{vol}(\operatorname{SO}(V_{0})({\bf{R}}))=1
.
This determines a normalization of the measure on
\operatorname{SO}(V_{0})({\bf{A}}_{f})

so that
\operatorname{vol}\left(\operatorname{SO}(V_{0})({\bf{Q}})\backslash\operatorname{SO}(V_{0})({\bf{A}}_{f})\right)=2
.

The Eisenstein series
(\ref{E-CM})
has the following classical description (cf.
[
13
,
\lx@sectionsign
2.2]
).
Writing
\Gamma=\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\bf{Z}})
and
\Gamma_{\infty}=P({\bf{Q}})\cap\Gamma=\{n(b):b\in{\bf{Z}}\}
,
we see that
P({\bf{Q}})\backslash\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\bf{Q}})=\Gamma_{\infty}\backslash\Gamma
.
Using the Iwasawa decomposition, we can write the action of each matrix in the sum as

\displaystyle\gamma=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}a&b\\
c&d\end{array}\right)\in\Gamma\quad\implies\quad\gamma g_{\tau}=n(\beta)m(\alpha)k_{\infty}\quad

for some
\alpha\in{\bf{R}}_{>0}
and
\beta,\theta\in{\bf{R}}
.

A direct computation reveals that

\displaystyle\alpha=v^{\frac{1}{2}}|c\tau+d|^{-1}\quad\text{and}\quad e^{i\theta}=\frac{c\overline{\tau}+d}{|c\tau+d|}

and hence

\displaystyle\phi_{\infty}^{l}(\gamma g_{\tau})

\displaystyle=v^{\frac{s}{2}+\frac{1}{2}}(c\tau+d)^{-l}|c\tau+d|^{l-s-1},

so that

\displaystyle E_{L_{0}}(g_{\tau},s;\phi_{\infty}^{l}\otimes\lambda_{f}({\bf{1}}_{\mu}))

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\gamma=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}a&b\\
c&d\end{array}\right)\in\Gamma_{\infty}\backslash\Gamma}(c\tau+d)^{-l}\cdot\frac{v^{\frac{s}{2}+\frac{1}{2}}}{|c\tau+d|^{s+1-l}}\cdot\lambda_{f}({\bf{1}}_{\mu})(\gamma)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\gamma=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}a&b\\
c&d\end{array}\right)\in\Gamma_{\infty}\backslash\Gamma}(c\tau+d)^{-l}\cdot\frac{v^{\frac{s}{2}+\frac{1}{2}}}{|c\tau+d|^{s+1-l}}\cdot\langle{\bf{1}}_{\mu},\left(\omega_{L_{0}}^{-1}(\gamma)\right){\bf{1}}_{0}\rangle.

It follows that

(35)

\displaystyle E_{L_{0}}(\tau,s;l):=\sum\limits_{\mu\in L_{0}^{\vee}/L_{0}}E_{L_{0}}(g_{\tau},s;\phi_{\infty}^{l}\otimes\lambda_{f}({\bf{1}}_{\mu}))

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\gamma\in\Gamma_{\infty}\backslash\Gamma}\left[\Im(\tau)^{\frac{s+1-l}{2}}{\bf{1}}_{0}\right]\Big|_{l,\omega_{L_{0}}}\gamma.

Recall that we consider the Maass weight raising and lowering operators
R_{l}
and
L_{l}
, as defined in
(\ref{Maassops})
.
A simple computation with the series expansion on the right-hand side of
(\ref{E-CM-classical})
reveals that

\displaystyle L_{l}E_{L_{0}}(\tau,s;l)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(s+1-l)\cdot E_{L_{0}}(\tau,s;l-2)

\displaystyle R_{l}E_{L_{0}}(\tau,s;l)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(s+1+l)\cdot E_{L_{0}}(\tau,s;l+2),

and in particular

(36)

\displaystyle L_{1}E_{L_{0}}(\tau,s;1)

\displaystyle=\frac{s}{2}\cdot E_{L_{0}}(\tau,s;-1).

Since the Eisenstein series
E_{L_{0}}(\tau,s;-1)
on the right-hand side of
(\ref{FI-CM})
is holomorphic
at
s=s_{0}(V_{0})=0
by the Siegel-Weil formula
(\ref{SW-CM})
, we deduce that the Eisenstein series

E_{L_{0}}(\tau,s;1)
appearing on the left-hand side must vanish at its central point
s=0
for its functional equation.
We also obtain from
(\ref{FI-CM})
the relation

(37)

\displaystyle L_{1}E_{L_{0}}^{\prime}(\tau,0;1)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}E_{L_{0}}(\tau,0;-1)

for the derivative
E_{L_{0}}^{\prime}(\tau,0;1)=\frac{d}{ds}E_{L_{0}}(\tau,s;0)\big|_{s=0}
at
s=0
.
Writing
\partial
and
\overline{\partial}
to denote the Dolbeault operators, so that the exterior
derivative on differential forms on
\mathfrak{H}
is given by
d=\partial+\overline{\partial}
,
and again
d\mu(\tau)=\frac{dudv}{v^{2}}
for
\tau=u+iv\in\mathfrak{H}
, we can express the relation
(\ref{FI2-CM})
in terms of differential forms as

(38)

\displaystyle-2\overline{\partial}\left(E_{L_{0}}^{\prime}(\tau,0;1)d\tau\right)

\displaystyle=E_{L_{0}}(\tau,0;-1)d\mu(\tau).

More generally, we have the following useful description of the operator
L_{l}
.

Lemma 5.2
.

The Maass weight-lowering operator
L_{l}
can be described in terms of differential forms as

\displaystyle\overline{\partial}(fd\tau)

\displaystyle=-v^{2-l}\xi_{l}(f)d\mu(\tau)=-L_{l}fd\mu(\tau).

Proof.

See
[
22
, Lemma 2.5]
and
[
13
, Lemma 2.3]
. ∎

Let us now consider the Fourier series expansion of the Eisenstein series
E_{L_{0}}(\tau,s;1)
, which we write as

\displaystyle E_{L_{0}}(\tau,s;1)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\mu\in L_{0}^{\vee}/L_{0}}\sum\limits_{m\in{\bf{Q}}}A_{L_{0}}(s,\mu,m,v)e(m\tau){\bf{1}}_{\mu}.

Following the discussion of Kudla
[
41
, Theorem 2.12]
(cf.
[
13
,
\lx@sectionsign
2.2]
), and using the fact that
E_{L_{0}}(\tau,0;1)=0

by
(\ref{FI-CM})
, we compute the Laurent series expansions of each of the coefficients
A_{L_{0}}(s,\mu,m,v)
around
s=0
as

\displaystyle A_{L_{0}}(s,\mu,m,v)

\displaystyle=b_{L_{0}}(\mu,m,v)s+O(s^{2}).

We deduce from this that
E_{L_{0}}^{\prime}(\tau,0;1)
has the Fourier series expansion

\displaystyle E_{L_{0}}^{\prime}(\tau,0;1)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\mu\in L_{0}^{\vee}/L_{0}}\sum\limits_{m\in{\bf{Q}}}b_{L_{0}}(\mu,m,v)e(m\tau){\bf{1}}_{\mu}.

Viewing
E_{L_{0}}^{\prime}(\tau,0;1)=E_{L_{0}}^{\prime+}(\tau,0;1)+E_{L_{0}}^{\prime-}(\tau,0;1)\in H_{1}(\omega_{L_{0}}^{\vee})

as a harmonic weak Maass form of weight
1
and representation
\omega_{L_{0}}^{\vee}
,
we also use the general calculation of Kudla
[
41
, Theorem 2.12]
to compute the Fourier series expansion
of the principal/holomorphic part
\mathcal{E}_{L_{0}}(\tau):=E_{L_{0}}^{\prime+}(\tau,0;1)
as

(39)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{L_{0}}(\tau)=E_{L_{0}}^{\prime+}(\tau,0;1)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\mu\in L_{0}^{\vee}/L_{0}}\sum_{m\in{\bf{Q}}}\kappa_{L_{0}}(\mu,m)e(m\tau){\bf{1}}_{\mu},

where the coefficients are given explicitly by the convergent limits

\displaystyle\kappa_{L_{0}}(\mu,m)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\lim_{v\rightarrow\infty}b_{L_{0}}(\mu,m,v)&\text{ if $\mu\neq 0$ or $m\neq 0$}\\
\lim_{v\rightarrow\infty}b_{L_{0}}(0,0,v)-\log(v)&\text{ if $\mu=0$ and $m=0$}.\end{cases}

Let us now specialize the setting we consider below, where the negative definite space
(L_{0},Q_{0})

is
incoherent
in the sense that it is constructed from an ideal
L_{0}=\mathfrak{a}\subset\mathcal{O}_{k}

in an imaginary quadratic field
k=k(V_{0})
, with its positive definite norm form

Q_{\mathfrak{a}}(\cdot):={\bf{N}}_{k/{\bf{Q}}}(\cdot)/{\bf{N}}\mathfrak{a}
, but we take
(V_{0},Q_{0})=(\mathfrak{a}_{\bf{Q}},-Q_{\mathfrak{a}})

to get a negative definite space of signature
(0,2)
. This construction amounts to taking the positive definite quadratic space

(\mathfrak{a}_{\bf{Q}},Q_{\mathfrak{a}})
at all of the finite places, but then switching invariants at the real place in replacing by

(\mathfrak{a}_{{\bf{Q}}},-Q_{\mathfrak{a}})
. Such a choice of
(V_{0},Q_{0})=(\mathfrak{a}_{\bf{Q}},-Q_{\mathfrak{a}})

makes sense locally at each place of
{\bf{Q}}
, but does not correspond globally to any quadratic number field – hence the name “incoherent”.

Proposition 5.3
.

Suppose
(L_{0},Q_{0})=(\mathfrak{a},-Q_{\mathfrak{a}})
for
\mathfrak{a}\subset\mathcal{O}_{k}
a nonzero integral
ideal of an imaginary quadratic field
k=k(V_{0})
of discriminant
d_{k}
and odd quadratic Dirichlet character
\eta_{k}(\cdot)=\left(\frac{d_{k}}{\cdot}\right)
,
with
Q_{\mathfrak{a}}(\cdot)={\bf{N}}_{k/{\bf{Q}}}(\cdot)/{\bf{N}}\mathfrak{a}
the corresponding positive definite norm form.
Let
E_{L_{0}}(\tau,s)=E_{L_{0}}(\tau;s;1)
denote the corresponding Eisenstein series of weight
l=1
defined in
(\ref{E-CM})

and
(\ref{E-CM-classical})
above. Writing

\displaystyle\Lambda(s,\eta_{k})

\displaystyle=|d_{k}|^{\frac{s}{2}}\Gamma_{\bf{R}}(s+1)L(s,\eta_{k}),\quad\Gamma_{\bf{R}}(s):=\pi^{-\frac{s}{2}}\Gamma(s)

to denote the completed Dirichlet
L
-function
L(s,\eta_{k})
of the character
\eta_{k}
, the completed Eisenstein series

\displaystyle E_{L_{0}}^{\star}(\tau,s)

\displaystyle:=\Lambda(s+1,\eta_{k})E_{L_{0}}(\tau,s)=\Lambda(s+1,\eta_{k})E_{L_{0}}(\tau,s;1)

satisfies the odd, symmetric functional equation

\displaystyle E_{L_{0}}^{\star}(\tau,s)

\displaystyle=-E_{L_{0}}^{\star}(\tau,-s).

Proof.

See
[
13
, Proposition 2.5]
. The functional equation is deduced from the Langlands functional equation of
each of the constituent incoherent Eisenstein series
E_{L_{0}}(g_{\tau},s;\phi_{\infty}^{1}\otimes\lambda_{f}({\bf{1}}_{\mu}))
,
where switching invariants at infinity as we describe above leads to switching the corresponding archimedean local sign to
-1
. ∎

5.1.3.
The geodesic case

We summarize the discussion of
[
55
,
\lx@sectionsign
4.6]
for the subspace
(L_{W},Q_{W})
of signature
(1,1)
.
Consider for each
l\in{\bf{Z}}
the
\mathfrak{S}_{L_{W}}
-valued Eisenstein
series on
\tau=u+iv\in\mathfrak{H}
and
s\in{\bf{C}}
defined by

(40)

\displaystyle E_{L_{W}}(\tau,s;l)

\displaystyle:=\sum\limits_{\mu\in L_{W}^{\vee}/L_{W}}E_{L_{W}}(g^{\prime}_{\tau},s;\phi_{\infty}^{l}\otimes\lambda_{f}({\bf{1}}_{\mu})){\bf{1}}_{\mu}.

We can then describe Theorem
5.1
in terms of the Siegel theta series
(\ref{Stheta3})
as

(41)

\displaystyle\int\limits_{\operatorname{SO}(W)({\bf{Q}})\backslash\operatorname{SO}(W)({\bf{A}}_{f})}\theta_{L_{W}}(\tau,z_{W},h_{f})dh

\displaystyle=E_{L_{W}}(\tau,0;0).

Here, we fix an oriented hyperbolic line
z_{W}\in D(W)=D^{\pm}(W)
,
and again normalize the measure on
\operatorname{SO}(V)({\bf{R}})
so that

\operatorname{vol}(\operatorname{SO}(W)({\bf{R}}))=1
and

\operatorname{vol}(\operatorname{SO}(W)({\bf{Q}})\backslash\operatorname{SO}(W)({\bf{A}}_{f}))=2
.

In a similar way as for
(\ref{E-CM})
, we can describe the Eisenstein series
(\ref{E-geo})
in classical terms as

(42)

\displaystyle E_{L_{W}}(\tau,s;l):=\sum\limits_{\mu\in L_{W}^{\vee}/L_{W}}E_{L_{W}}(g^{\prime}_{\tau},s;\phi_{\infty}^{l}\otimes\lambda_{f}({\bf{1}}_{\mu}))

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\gamma\in\Gamma_{\infty}\backslash\Gamma}\left[\Im(\tau)^{\frac{s+1-l}{2}}{\bf{1}}_{0}\right]\Big|_{l,\omega_{L_{W}}}\gamma.

Let us now specialize immediately to the setting we consider later on, where the quadratic space
(L_{W},Q_{W})
is given by a nonzero
integer ideal
L_{W}=\mathfrak{a}\subset\mathcal{O}_{k}
in a real quadratic field
k=k(W)
, with indefinite quadratic form

Q_{W}
given by the norm form
Q_{W}(\cdot)=Q_{\mathfrak{a}}(\cdot)={\bf{N}}_{k/{\bf{Q}}}(\cdot)/{\bf{N}}\mathfrak{a}
.
Unlike in the CM setup above with the negative definite subspaces constructed from the positive definite subspaces attached
to imaginary quadratic fields, these quadratic spaces
(L_{W},Q_{W})=(\mathfrak{a},Q_{\mathfrak{a}})
are
coherent
in
that they correspond globally to integral ideals in some real quadratic number field
k=k(W)
. This has the following
consequences for the corresponding Eisenstein series
E_{L_{W}}(\tau,s)=E_{L_{W}}(\tau,s;0)
.

Proposition 5.4
.

Suppose
(L_{W},Q_{W})=(\mathfrak{a},Q_{\mathfrak{a}})
for
\mathfrak{a}\subset\mathcal{O}_{k}
a nonzero
integral ideal of a real quadratic field
k=k(W)
of discriminant
d_{k}
and even quadratic Dirichlet character
\eta_{k}(\cdot)=\left(\frac{d_{k}}{\cdot}\right)
,
with
Q_{\mathfrak{a}}(\cdot)={\bf{N}}_{k/{\bf{Q}}}(\cdot)/{\bf{N}}\mathfrak{a}
the corresponding indefinite norm form.
Let
E_{L_{W}}(\tau,s)=E_{L_{W}}(\tau;s;0)
denote the corresponding Eisenstein series of weight
l=0
defined in
(\ref{E-geo})

and
(\ref{E-geo-classical})
above. Writing

\displaystyle\Lambda(s,\eta_{k})
