\displaystyle=|d_{k}|^{\frac{s}{2}}\Gamma_{\bf{R}}(s+1)L(s,\eta_{k}),\quad\Gamma_{\bf{R}}(s):=\pi^{-\frac{s}{2}}\Gamma(s)

to denote the completed Dirichlet
L
-function
L(s,\eta_{k})
of the character
\eta_{k}
, the completed Eisenstein series

\displaystyle E_{L_{W}}^{\star}(\tau,s)

\displaystyle:=\Lambda(s+1,\eta_{k})E_{L_{W}}(\tau,s)=\Lambda(s+1,\eta_{k})E_{L_{W}}(\tau,s;0)

satisfies the even, symmetric functional equation

\displaystyle E_{L_{W}}^{\star}(\tau,s)

\displaystyle=E_{L_{W}}^{\star}(\tau,-s).

Proof.

See
[
55
, Proposition 4.10]
, this can be deduced as a direct consequence of the Langlands functional
equation for the Eisenstein series on the right-hand side of
(\ref{E-geo})
corresponding to the coherent quadratic space

(L_{W},Q_{W})=(\mathfrak{a},Q_{\mathfrak{a}})
of signature
(1,1)
. ∎

Fixing such a coherent choice of Lorentzian quadratic space
(L_{W},Q_{W})
henceforth, we consider the images
of the Eisenstein series
E_{L_{W}}(\tau,s;l)
under the Maass raising and lowering operators
(\ref{Maassops})
.
Here, we see by inspection of the series expansion on the right-hand side of
(\ref{E-geo-classical})
that

\displaystyle L_{l}E_{L_{W}}(\tau,s;l)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\cdot(s+1-l)\cdot E_{L_{W}}(\tau,s;l-2)

\displaystyle R_{l}E_{L_{W}}(\tau,s;l)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\cdot(s+1+l)\cdot E_{L_{W}}(\tau,s;l+2),

and in particular

(43)

\displaystyle L_{2}E_{L_{W}}(\tau,s;2)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\cdot(s-1)\cdot E_{L_{W}}(\tau,s;0).

Now, the Eisenstein series
E_{L_{W}}(\tau,s;0)
on the right-hand side of
(\ref{FI-geo})
is holomorphic at

s=s_{0}(W)=0
, and so we can evaluate this relation at
s=0
to obtain the identity

\displaystyle L_{2}E_{L_{W}}(\tau,0;2)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\cdot E_{L_{W}}(\tau,0;0).

On the other hand, we can differentiate each side of
(\ref{FI-geo})
with respect to
s
to obtain the relation

\displaystyle L_{2}E_{L_{W}}^{\prime}(\tau,s;2)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\cdot(s-1)\cdot E^{\prime}_{L_{W}}(\tau,s;0)+\frac{1}{2}\cdot E_{L_{W}}(\tau,s;0),

then evaluate this latter relation at
s=0
to obtain the identity

\displaystyle L_{2}E_{L_{W}}^{\prime}(\tau,0;2)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\cdot E_{L_{W}}(\tau,0;0)-\frac{1}{2}\cdot E^{\prime}_{L_{W}}(\tau,0;0),

equivalently

(44)

\displaystyle 2L_{2}E_{L_{W}}^{\prime}(\tau,0;2)

\displaystyle=E_{L_{W}}(\tau,0;0)-E^{\prime}_{L_{W}}(\tau,0;0).

We now use the even functional equation of
E^{\star}_{L_{W}}(\tau,s)
(Proposition
5.4
) to deduce that
E_{L_{W}}^{\prime}(\tau,0;0)=0
.

Corollary 5.5
.

We have
E_{L_{W}}^{\prime}(\tau,0;0)=0
, and hence via
(\ref{FI-geo})
the relation
-2L_{2}E_{L_{W}}^{\prime}(\tau,0;2)=-E_{L_{W}}(\tau,0;0)
.
Expressed in terms of differential forms according to Lemma
5.2
, we obtain the relation

\displaystyle-2L_{2}E_{L_{W}}^{\prime}(\tau,0;2)d\mu(\tau)=2\overline{\partial}\left(E_{L_{W}}^{\prime}(\tau,0;2)d\tau\right)

\displaystyle=-E_{L_{W}}(\tau,0;0),

equivalently

(45)

\displaystyle E_{L_{W}}(\tau,0;0)d\mu(\tau)

\displaystyle=-2\overline{\partial}\left(E_{L_{W}}^{\prime}(\tau,0;2)d\tau\right).

Proof.

See
[
55
, Proposition 4.12]
. We know by the Siegel-Weil formula that
E_{L_{W}}(\tau,s;0)
is analytic at
s=0
.
Hence,
E_{L_{W}}(\tau,s;0)
and all of its derivatives with respect to
s
are analytic at
s=0
. In particular, both values
E_{L_{W}}(\tau,0;0)

and
E_{L_{W}}^{\prime}(\tau,0;0)
are defined (finite), and we can expand
E_{L_{2}}(\tau,s;0)
into its Taylor series expansion around
s=0
.
Now, we know by Proposition
5.4
that the completed Eisenstein series
E_{L_{W}}^{\star}(\tau,s;0)
satisfies the even, symmetric
functional equation
E_{L_{W}}^{\star}(\tau,s;0)=E_{L_{W}}^{\star}(\tau,-s;0)
. Comparing the Taylor series expansions around
s=0
as we
may, we then derive for any
s\in{\bf{C}}
with
0\leq\Re(s)<1
the relation

\displaystyle E_{L_{W}}^{\star}(\tau,0;0)+E_{L_{W}}^{\star\prime}(\tau,0;0)s+O(s^{2})

\displaystyle=E_{L_{W}}^{\star}(\tau,0;0)-E_{L_{W}}^{\star\prime}(\tau,0;0)s+O(s^{2}),

equivalently

(46)

\displaystyle E_{L_{W}}^{\star\prime}(\tau,0;0)s+O(s^{2})

\displaystyle=-E_{L_{W}}^{\star\prime}(\tau,0;0)s+O(s^{2}).

Taking the limit as
\Re(s)\rightarrow 0
of
(\ref{vanish})
, we see that
E_{L_{W}}^{\star\prime}(\tau,0;0)
must vanish,
and hence
E_{L_{W}}^{\prime}(\tau,0;0)=0
. ∎

Remark 5.6
.

Observe that we could also have considered the lattice
(L_{W},-Q_{W})
of signature
(1,1)
, denoted by
-L_{W}
,
together with the corresponding incoherent Eisenstein series
E_{-L_{W}}(\tau,s;0)
. Writing
k=k(W)

again to denote the real quadratic field attached to the genuine space
(L_{W},Q_{W})=(\mathfrak{a},Q_{\mathfrak{a}})
,
with
\eta_{k}(\cdot)=\left(\frac{d_{k}}{\cdot}\right)
its character, a minor variation of the argument for

[
13
, Proposition 2.5]
shows that the completed Eisenstein series

E^{\star}_{-L_{W}}(\tau,s):=\Lambda(s+1,\eta_{k})E_{-L_{W}}(\tau,s;0)
satisfies an odd, symmetric functional equation

\displaystyle E_{-L_{W}}^{\star}(\tau,s)=-E_{-L_{W}}^{\star}(\tau,-s),

and hence that
E_{-L_{W}}^{\star}(\tau,s)
vanishes at the central point
s=0
.
In particular,
E_{-L_{W}}(\tau,0;0)=0
. Deriving the corresponding identities
(\ref{FI-geo})
and
(\ref{FI-geo})

in the same way for the incoherent Eisenstein series
E_{-L_{W}}(\tau,s;0)
, the vanishing
E_{-L_{W}}(\tau,0;0)=0

at the central point implies the corresponding functional identity

(47)

\displaystyle 2L_{2}E_{-L_{W}}^{\prime}(\tau,0;2)

\displaystyle=-E_{-L_{W}}^{\prime}(\tau,0;0).

While this latter identity
(\ref{FI-igeo})
describes the true analogue of the CM setup for the incoherent Eisenstein
series
E_{-L_{W}}(\tau,s;0)
, it is unfortunately not useful for the derivation of integral presentations as we describe
below (cf.
[
13
, Theorem 4.7]
), in particular as it is the vanishing central value
E_{-L_{W}}(\tau,0;0)=0

which appears in the corresponding average over theta series
\theta_{-L_{W}}(\tau,z,h)
according to Siegel-Weil (Theorem
5.1
).
It could be of interest to investigate vanishing averages of regularized theta integrals of this type.

Let us now consider the Fourier series expansion of the Eisenstein series
E_{L_{W}}(\tau,s;2)
,

\displaystyle E_{L_{W}}(\tau,s;2)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\mu\in L_{W}^{\vee}/L_{W}}\sum\limits_{m\in{\bf{Q}}}A_{L_{W}}(s,\mu,m,v)e(m\tau){\bf{1}}_{\mu}.

Following
[
41
, Theorem 2.12]
, we write the Laurent series expansions around
s=0
of the coefficients as

\displaystyle A_{L_{W}}(s,\mu,m,v)

\displaystyle=a_{L_{W}}(\mu,m,v)+b_{L_{W}}(\mu,m,v)s+O(s^{2}),

and deduce that the derivative Eisenstein series
E_{L_{W}}^{\prime}(\tau,0;2)
at
s=0
has the Fourier series expansion

\displaystyle E_{L_{W}}^{\prime}(\tau,0;2)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\mu\in L_{W}^{\vee}/L_{W}}\sum\limits_{m\in{\bf{Q}}}b_{L_{W}}(\mu,m,v)e(m\tau){\bf{1}}_{\mu}.

Viewing this derivative Eisenstein series as a harmonic weak Maass form

\displaystyle E_{L_{W}}^{\prime}(\tau,0;2)=E_{L_{W}}^{\prime~+}(\tau,0;2)+E_{L_{W}}^{\prime-}(\tau,0;2)\in H_{2}(\omega_{L_{W}}^{\vee})

of weight
2
and representation
\omega_{L_{W}}^{\vee}
,
we consider the principal/holomorphic part
E_{L_{W}}(\tau):=E_{L_{W}}^{\prime~+}(\tau,0;2)
.
Using the argument of
[
41
, Theorem 2.12]
again, we can compute the coefficients in its Fourier series expansion

(48)

\displaystyle E_{L_{W}}(\tau)=E_{L_{W}}^{\prime~+}(\tau,0;2)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\mu\in L_{W}^{\vee}/L_{W}}\sum\limits_{m\in{\bf{Q}}}\kappa_{L_{W}}(\mu,m)e(m\tau){\bf{1}}_{\mu}

as the convergent limits

\displaystyle\kappa_{L_{W}}(\mu,m)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\lim_{v\rightarrow\infty}b_{L_{W}}(\mu,m,v)&\text{ if $\mu\neq 0$ or $m\neq 0$}\\
\lim_{v\rightarrow\infty}b_{L_{W}}(0,0,v)-\log(v)&\text{ if $\mu=0$ and $m=0$}.\end{cases}

5.2.
Summation formulae

Fix
f\in H_{1-\frac{n}{2}}(\omega_{L})
. Write
l=1-\frac{n}{2}
for simplicity.

5.2.1.
Decompositions of theta series

We first justify how to decompose the Siegel theta series
\theta_{L}(\tau,z,h)
for later calculation.
Let
L_{j}
for
j=1,2
be any pair of even lattices, with corresponding Weil representations

\displaystyle\omega_{L_{j}}:\operatorname{Mp}_{2}({\bf{Z}})\longrightarrow{\bf{C}}[L_{j}^{\vee}/L_{j}].

The Weil representation of the direct sum
L_{1}\oplus L_{2}
is given by the tensor product

\omega_{L_{1}}\otimes\omega_{L_{2}}
. Given

\displaystyle f(\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\mu\in L_{1}^{\vee}/L_{1}}f_{\mu}(\tau){\bf{1}}_{\mu}\in H_{l_{1}}(\omega_{L_{1}})

and

\displaystyle g(\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\nu\in L_{2}^{\vee}/L_{2}}g_{\nu}(\tau){\bf{1}}_{\nu}\in H_{l_{2}}(\omega_{L_{2}})

harmonic weak Maass forms of weights
l_{j}
and representations
\omega_{L_{j}}
, the corresponding tensor product

\displaystyle f(\tau)\otimes g(\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\mu\in L_{1}^{\vee}/L_{1}\atop\nu\in L_{2}^{\vee}/L_{2}}f_{\mu}(\tau)g_{\nu}(\tau){\bf{1}}_{\mu+\nu}\in H_{l_{1}+l_{2}}(\omega_{L_{1}\oplus L_{2}})=H_{l_{1}+l_{2}}(\omega_{L_{1}}\otimes\omega_{L_{2}})

determines a harmonic weak Maass form of weight
l_{1}+l_{2}
and representation

\omega_{L_{1}\oplus L_{2}}=\omega_{L_{1}}\otimes\omega_{L_{2}}
.

Suppose now that
M\subset L
is any sublattice of finite index. Observe that we have inclusions

\displaystyle M\subset L\subset L^{\vee}\subset M^{\vee}\quad\implies\quad L/M\subset L^{\vee}/M\subset M^{\vee}/M,

and hence an inclusion of spaces
H_{l}(\omega_{L})\subset H_{l}(\omega_{M})
for any weight
l\in\frac{1}{2}{\bf{Z}}
.
Consider the natural map

\displaystyle L^{\vee}/M\longrightarrow L^{\vee}/L,\quad\mu\longmapsto\overline{\mu}.

Lemma 5.7
.

Let
M\subset L
be any sublattice of finite index. We have natural restriction and trace maps

\displaystyle\operatorname{res}_{L/M}:H_{l}(\omega_{L})\longrightarrow H_{l}(\omega_{M}),\quad f(\tau)=\sum\limits_{\overline{\mu}\in L^{\vee}/L}f_{\overline{\mu}}(\tau){\bf{1}}_{\overline{\mu}}\longmapsto f_{M}(\tau)=\sum\limits_{\mu\in M^{\vee}/M}f_{M,\mu}(\tau){\bf{1}}_{\mu}

and

\displaystyle\operatorname{tr}_{L/M}:H_{l}(\omega_{M})\longrightarrow H_{l}(\omega_{L}),\quad g(\tau)=\sum\limits_{\mu\in M^{\vee}/M}g_{\mu}(\tau){\bf{1}}_{\mu}\longmapsto g^{L}(\tau)=\sum\limits_{\overline{\mu}\in L^{\vee}/L}g^{L}_{\overline{\mu}}(\tau){\bf{1}}_{\overline{\mu}}

such that for any pair of vector-valued forms
f\in H_{l}(\omega_{L})
and
g\in H_{l}(\omega_{M})
, we have

\displaystyle\langle\langle f(\tau),\overline{g}^{L}(\tau)\rangle\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\langle f_{M}(\tau),\overline{g}(\tau)\rangle\rangle.

Explicitly, the restriction map is given for any
\mu\in M^{\vee}/M
and
f\in H_{l}(\omega_{L})
by

\displaystyle f_{M,\mu}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}f_{\overline{\mu}}(\tau)&\text{ if $\mu\in L^{\vee}/M$}\\
0&\text{ if $\mu\notin L^{\vee}/M$}\end{cases}

The trace map is given for any
\overline{\mu}\in L^{\vee}/L
with fixed preimage
\mu\in L^{\vee}/M
and
g\in H_{l}(\omega_{M})
by

\displaystyle g^{L}_{\overline{\mu}}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\nu\in L/M}g_{\nu+\mu}(\tau).

Proof.

See
[
13
, Lemma 3.1]
. ∎

As explained in
[
13
, Remark 3.2]
, we have for the Siegel theta series we consider the relation

(49)

\displaystyle\theta_{L}

\displaystyle=(\theta_{M})^{L}.

We shall use this relation
(\ref{relation})
for the finite-index subgroups
M=L_{0}\oplus L_{0}^{\perp}\subset L
and
M=L_{W}\oplus L_{W}^{\perp}\subset L
.
In particular, we obtain from
(\ref{relation})
the relations

\displaystyle\theta_{L_{0}\oplus L_{0}^{\perp}}

\displaystyle=\theta_{L_{0}}\otimes\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}}\quad\implies\quad\theta_{L}=(\theta_{L_{0}\oplus L_{0}^{\perp}})^{L}

\displaystyle\theta_{L_{W}\oplus L_{W}^{\perp}}

\displaystyle=\theta_{L_{W}}\otimes\theta_{L_{W}^{\perp}}\quad\implies\quad\theta_{L}=(\theta_{L_{W}\oplus L_{W}^{\perp}})^{L}

which via Lemma
(\ref{lattice})
imply the corresponding relations

(50)

\displaystyle\langle\langle f,\theta_{L}\rangle\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\langle f,(\theta_{L_{0}\oplus L_{0}^{\perp}})^{L}\rangle\rangle=\langle\langle f_{L_{0}\oplus L_{0}^{\perp}},\theta_{L_{0}\oplus L_{0}^{\perp}}\rangle\rangle=\langle\langle f_{L_{0}\oplus L_{0}^{\perp}},\theta_{L_{0}}\otimes\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}}\rangle\rangle

\displaystyle\langle\langle f,\theta_{L}\rangle\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\langle f,(\theta_{L_{W}\oplus L_{W}^{\perp}})^{L}\rangle\rangle=\langle\langle f_{L_{W}\oplus L_{W}^{\perp}},\theta_{L_{W}\oplus L_{W}^{\perp}}\rangle\rangle=\langle\langle f_{L_{W}\oplus L_{W}^{\perp}},\theta_{L_{W}}\otimes\theta_{L_{W}^{\perp}}\rangle\rangle.

We shall take these relations
(\ref{IPrelations})
for granted in what follows,
and drop various subscripts and bars from the notations for simpler reading. That is, we shall simply write

\langle\langle f,\theta_{L_{0}}\otimes\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}}\rangle\rangle=\langle\langle f,\theta_{L_{W}}\otimes\theta_{L_{W}^{\perp}}\rangle\rangle

to denote the right-hand side(s)
\langle\langle f_{L_{0}\oplus L_{0}^{\perp}},\theta_{L_{0}}\otimes\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}}\rangle\rangle=\langle\langle f_{L_{W}\oplus L_{W}^{\perp}},\theta_{L_{W}}\otimes\theta_{L_{W}^{\perp}}\rangle\rangle
of
(\ref{IPrelations})
from now on.

5.2.2.
Preliminary calculations

We first relate our sums to the integrals appearing in the Siegel-Weil formula (Theorem
5.1
),
(\ref{SW-CM})
and
(\ref{SW-geo})
.
Recall we consider the CM cycle
Z(V_{0})
on
X_{K}
with complex points as described in
(\ref{CMcycle})
,
as well as the geodesic set
\mathfrak{G}(W)
as described in
(\ref{geodesic})
. Let us write

\displaystyle T_{0}

\displaystyle=T(V_{0}):=\operatorname{GSpin}(V_{0})\cong\operatorname{Res}_{k(V_{0})/{\bf{Q}}}{\bf{G}}_{m}\quad\quad\text{ $[k(V_{0}):{\bf{Q}}]=2$ imaginary quadratic}

\displaystyle T_{W}

\displaystyle=T(W):=\operatorname{GSpin}(W)\cong\operatorname{Res}_{k(W)/{\bf{Q}}}{\bf{G}}_{m}\quad\quad\text{ $[k(W):{\bf{Q}}]=2$ real quadratic}

for the corresponding maximal tori in
\operatorname{GSpin}(V)
and quadratic number fields attached to these spaces.

Let us again write
U
to denote either of these subspaces
V_{0},W\subset V
,
with
T_{U}=\operatorname{GSpin}(U)=\operatorname{Res}_{k(U)/{\bf{Q}}}{\bf{G}}_{m}
for
k(U)
the corresponding quadratic field.
We have in each case a short exact sequence of algebraic groups

(51)

\displaystyle 1\longrightarrow{\bf{G}}_{m}\longrightarrow\operatorname{GSpin}(U)\longrightarrow\operatorname{SO}(U)\longrightarrow 1,

which after taking adelic points modulo rational points recovers the Hilbert exact sequence for
k=k(U)
,

\displaystyle 1\longrightarrow{\bf{Q}}^{\times}\backslash{\bf{A}}^{\times}\longrightarrow k^{\times}\backslash{\bf{A}}_{k}^{\times}\longrightarrow k^{1}\backslash{\bf{A}}_{k}^{1}\longrightarrow 1.

Recall that we fix the Haar measure on
\operatorname{SO}(U)({\bf{A}})
so that
\operatorname{vol}(\operatorname{SO}(U)({\bf{R}}))=1
and

\displaystyle\operatorname{vol}(\operatorname{SO}(U)({\bf{Q}})\backslash\operatorname{SO}(U)({\bf{A}}_{f}))=\operatorname{vol}(\operatorname{SO}(U)({\bf{Q}})\backslash\operatorname{SO}(U)({\bf{A}}))=2.

We fix the standard Haar measure on
{\bf{A}}^{\times}
with
\operatorname{vol}({\bf{Z}}_{p}^{\times})=1
for each prime
p
so that

\operatorname{vol}(\widehat{\bf{Z}})=1
, as well as
\operatorname{vol}({\bf{Q}}^{\times}\backslash{\bf{A}}^{\times})=1
,
and
\operatorname{vol}({\bf{A}}_{f}^{\times}/{\bf{Q}}^{\times})=\frac{1}{2}
.
This determines a measure on
T_{U}({\bf{A}})\cong{\bf{A}}_{k(U)}^{\times}
via
(\ref{SES})
, with

\displaystyle\operatorname{vol}(k^{\times}\backslash{\bf{A}}_{k,f}^{\times})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{vol}({\bf{Q}}^{\times}\backslash{\bf{A}}_{f}^{\times})\cdot\operatorname{vol}(k^{1}\backslash{\bf{A}}_{k,f}^{1})=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\operatorname{vol}\left(\operatorname{SO}(U)({\bf{Q}})\backslash\operatorname{SO}(U)({\bf{A}}_{f})\right)=1

and

\displaystyle\operatorname{vol}(k^{\times}\backslash{\bf{A}}_{k}^{\times})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{vol}({\bf{Q}}^{\times}\backslash{\bf{A}}^{\times})\cdot\operatorname{vol}(k^{1}\backslash{\bf{A}}_{k}^{1})=1\cdot\operatorname{vol}\left(\operatorname{SO}(U)({\bf{Q}})\backslash\operatorname{SO}(U)({\bf{A}})\right)=2.

Hence, we derive the following result for future use.

Lemma 5.8
.

We have
\operatorname{vol}(T_{U}({\bf{Q}})\backslash T_{U}({\bf{A}}_{f}))=1

for either choice of quadratic space
U=V_{0},W
.
Writing
k=k(U)
for either choice of quadratic field, let
w_{k}=\#\mu(k)
denote the number of roots of unity in
k
.
Hence, by Dirichlet’s unit theorem, we know that
\#\mathcal{O}_{k}=w_{k}
when
k=k(U)=k(V_{0})
is an imaginary quadratic field.
When
k=k(U)=k(W)
is a real quadratic field, we know that

\mathcal{O}_{k}^{\times}=\langle\varepsilon_{k}\rangle\times\mu(k)
for
\varepsilon_{k}
the fundamental unit,
so the solution
\varepsilon_{k}=\frac{1}{2}(t+u\sqrt{d_{k}})
with
u
minimal to Pell’s equation
t^{2}-d_{k}u^{2}=4
. We have that

\displaystyle\operatorname{vol}(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{k}^{\times})

\displaystyle=\frac{w_{k}}{h_{k}},

and that

\displaystyle\operatorname{vol}(k_{\infty}^{\times}\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{k}^{\times})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\frac{2w_{k}}{h_{k}}&\text{if $k$ is imaginary quadratic}\\
\frac{2w_{k}\ln(\varepsilon_{k})}{h_{k}}&\text{if $k$ is real quadratic}.\end{cases}

Proof.

Cf.
[
13
, Lemma 6.3]
. The first claim follows from the discussion above. Now, observe that

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{k^{\times}\backslash{\bf{A}}_{k,f}^{\times}}d^{\times}x=\int\limits_{k^{\times}\backslash{\bf{A}}_{k,f}^{\times}/\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{k}^{\times}}\int\limits_{\mathcal{O}_{k}^{\times}\backslash\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{k}^{\times}}d^{\times}x=\frac{h_{k}}{w_{k}}\cdot\operatorname{vol}(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{k}^{\times}).

More generally, we have that

\displaystyle 2

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{k^{\times}\backslash{\bf{A}}_{k}^{\times}}d^{\times}x=\int\limits_{k^{\times}\backslash{\bf{A}}_{k}^{\times}/k_{\infty}^{\times}\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{k}^{\times}}\int\limits_{\mathcal{O}_{k}^{\times}\backslash k_{\infty}^{\times}\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{k}^{\times}}d^{\times}x=\begin{cases}\frac{h_{k}}{w_{k}}\cdot\operatorname{vol}(k_{\infty}^{\times}\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{k}^{\times})&\text{if $k$ is imaginary quadratic}\\
\frac{h_{k}}{w_{k}\ln(\varepsilon_{k})}\cdot\operatorname{vol}(k_{\infty}^{\times}\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{k}^{\times})&\text{if $k$ is real quadratic}.\end{cases}

∎

Let us now turn to the sums we wish to compute, which we now denote by

(52)

\displaystyle\Phi(f,Z(V_{0}))

\displaystyle:=\sum\limits_{(z_{0},h)\in Z(V_{0})({\bf{C}})}\frac{\Phi(f,z_{0},h)}{\#\operatorname{Aut}(z_{0},h)}

\displaystyle\Phi(f,\mathfrak{G}(W))

\displaystyle:=\sum\limits_{(z_{W},h)\in\mathfrak{G}(W)}\frac{\Phi(f,z_{W},h)}{\#\operatorname{Aut}(z_{W},h)}.

Note that we have two orientations
z_{0}^{\pm}\in D(V_{0})
and
z_{W}^{\pm}\in D(W)
in each case.
We fix an orientation
\pm
consistently in each case, then drop this choice from the notation henceforth.

Lemma 5.9
.

We have the following expressions for the sums
(\ref{Asums})
in terms of integrals over the corresponding
adelic quotients of orthogonal groups
\operatorname{SO}(V_{0})
and
\operatorname{SO}(W)
.

(i)

If
V_{0}
is a rational quadratic space of signature
(0,2)
, then we have

\displaystyle\Phi(f,Z(V_{0}))

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\operatorname{vol}(K_{0})}\int\limits_{h\in\operatorname{SO}(V_{0})({\bf{Q}})\backslash\operatorname{SO}(V_{0})({\bf{A}}_{f})}\Phi(f,z_{0},h)dh

\displaystyle=\frac{\operatorname{deg}(Z(V_{0}))}{2}\int\limits_{h\in\operatorname{SO}(V_{0})({\bf{Q}})\backslash\operatorname{SO}(V_{0})({\bf{A}}_{f})}\Phi(f,z_{0},h)dh,

where

\displaystyle\operatorname{deg}(Z(V_{0}))

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{\operatorname{vol}(K_{0})}.

(ii)

If
W
is a rational quadratic space of signature
(1,1)
, then we have

\displaystyle\Phi(f,\mathfrak{G}(W))

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\operatorname{vol}(K_{W})}\int\limits_{(z_{W},h)\in\operatorname{SO}(W)({\bf{Q}})\backslash\operatorname{SO}(W)({\bf{A}})}\Phi(f,z_{W},h)dh.

Proof.

See
[
48
, Lemma 2.13]
with
[
13
, Lemma 4.4]
for (i).
In general, write
U=V_{0},W\subset V
for either of the quadratic subspaces we consider,
with corresponding torus
T_{U}=\operatorname{GSpin}(U)\cong\operatorname{Res}_{k(U)/{\bf{Q}}}{\bf{G}}_{m}
,
quadratic field
k(U)
, and compact open subgroup
K_{U}=T_{U}({\bf{A}}_{f})\cap K_{L}
.
Note that while
T_{V_{0}}({\bf{R}})
is compact for
V_{0}
negative definite (with
k(V_{0})
imaginary quadratic)
and
T_{W}({\bf{R}})
is split for
W
Lorentzian (with
k(W)
real quadratic), we have chosen measures so
that the corresponding adelic quotients
T_{U}({\bf{Q}})\backslash T_{U}({\bf{A}}_{f})
and
T_{U}({\bf{Q}})\backslash T_{U}({\bf{A}})

have finite volume in either case on
U
, with
\operatorname{vol}(k(U)_{\infty})=2
.
In each case, we can also use that
\operatorname{SO}(U)({\bf{R}})
acts simply transitively on
D(U)

to identify
D(U)\cong\operatorname{SO}(U)({\bf{R}})/\operatorname{Aut}(z)
, where
\operatorname{Aut}(z)
is the stabilizer of
z
.

Let
B(h)
denote any function of
h\in T_{U}({\bf{A}}_{f})
which depends only on the image of
h
in
\operatorname{SO}(U)({\bf{A}}_{f})
,
and which is both left
T_{U}({\bf{Q}})
-invariant and right
K_{U}
-invariant. We have the basic identity

\displaystyle\int\limits_{\operatorname{SO}(U)({\bf{Q}})\backslash\operatorname{SO}(U)({\bf{A}}_{f})}B(h)dh

\displaystyle=\operatorname{vol}(K_{U})\sum\limits_{h\in T_{U}({\bf{Q}})\backslash T_{U}({\bf{A}}_{f})/K_{U}}\frac{B(h)}{\#\Gamma_{h}},\quad\Gamma_{h}=\operatorname{SO}(U)({\bf{Q}})\cap hK_{U}h^{-1}.

Indeed, fix a set of representatives
h
for the finite set
T_{U}({\bf{Q}})\backslash T_{U}({\bf{A}}_{f})/K_{U}
.
We partition
\operatorname{SO}(U)({\bf{Q}})\backslash\operatorname{SO}(U)({\bf{A}}_{f})

into disjoint pieces
\operatorname{SO}(U)({\bf{Q}})\backslash\operatorname{SO}(U)({\bf{Q}})hK_{U}
,
then pull back to
T_{U}({\bf{A}}_{f})
. Since
B(h)
is left
\operatorname{SO}(U)({\bf{Q}})
-invariant
and right
K_{U}
-invariant, with each piece having measure
\operatorname{vol}(K_{U})/\#\Gamma_{h}
, we obtain the identity.

For (i), we apply this to the function
B(h)=\Phi(f,z_{0},h)
to obtain the relation

\displaystyle\Phi(f,Z(V_{0}))

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\operatorname{vol}(K_{0})}\int\limits_{h\in\operatorname{SO}(V_{0})({\bf{Q}})\backslash\operatorname{SO}(V_{0})({\bf{A}}_{f})}\Phi(f,z_{0},h)dh,

and to the constant function
B(h)=1
to obtain the relation

\displaystyle\operatorname{deg}(Z(V_{0}))

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{(z,h)\in\operatorname{supp}(Z(V_{0}))}\frac{1}{\#\operatorname{Aut}(z,h)}=\frac{1}{\operatorname{vol}(K_{0})}\int\limits_{h\in\operatorname{SO}(V_{0})({\bf{Q}})\backslash\operatorname{SO}(V_{0})({\bf{A}}_{f})}dh=\frac{2}{\operatorname{vol}(K_{0})}.

For (ii), we apply this to the function
B(h)=\Phi(f,z_{W},h)
, we also obtain

\displaystyle\int\limits_{(z_{W},h)\in\operatorname{SO}(W)({\bf{Q}})\backslash\operatorname{SO}(W)({\bf{A}})}\Phi(f,z_{W},h)dh

\displaystyle=\operatorname{vol}(K_{W})\cdot\Phi(f,\mathfrak{G}(W)).

In each case, we identify
\operatorname{Aut}(z,h)
with
\operatorname{Aut}(z)\times\Gamma_{h}
.
∎

We now give the following more convenient expression for
\Phi(f,z,h)
.

Proposition 5.10
.

We have the following expressions for the regularized theta integral
\Phi(z,h)
as limits of truncated sums of integrals.
Here, we take for granted the relation of scalar products
(\ref{IPrelations})
.

(i)

In the CM case with negative definite lattice
L_{0}\subset L
, we have for any
(z_{0},h)\in D(V_{0})\times T_{0}({\bf{A}}_{f})
that

\displaystyle\Phi(f,z_{0},h)

\displaystyle=\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\left[\int_{\mathcal{F}_{T}}\langle\langle f(\tau),\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}}(\tau)\otimes\theta_{L_{0}}(\tau,z_{0},h)\rangle\rangle d\mu(\tau)-A_{0}\log(T)\right],

where

\displaystyle A_{0}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{CT}\langle\langle f^{+}(\tau),\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}}(\tau)\otimes{\bf{1}}_{0+L_{0}}\rangle\rangle.

Here, we write
\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}}(\tau)=\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}}(\tau,1,1)
, and note that the underlying theta
series
\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}}(\tau,z_{0},h)
for the positive definite lattice
L_{0}^{\perp}
of signature
(n,0)
is holomorphic in the variable
\tau\in\mathfrak{H}
.

(ii)

In the case with the signature
(1,1)
lattice
L_{W}\subset L
, we have for any
(z_{W},h)\in D(W)\times T_{0}({\bf{A}}_{f})
that

\displaystyle\Phi(f,z_{W},h)

\displaystyle=\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\left[\int_{\mathcal{F}_{T}}\langle\langle f(\tau),\theta_{L_{W}^{\perp}}(\tau)\otimes\theta_{L_{W}}(\tau,z_{W},h)\rangle\rangle d\mu(\tau)-A_{0}\log(T)\right],

where

\displaystyle A_{0}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{CT}\langle\langle f^{+}(\tau),\theta_{L_{W}^{\perp}}^{+}(\tau)\otimes{\bf{1}}_{0+L_{W}}\rangle\rangle.

Here, we write
\theta_{L_{W}^{\perp}}(\tau)=\theta_{L_{W}^{\perp}}(\tau,1,1)
, and note that the underlying theta series

\theta_{L_{W}^{\perp}}(\tau,z_{W},h)
for the Lorenztian lattice
L_{W}^{\perp}
of signature
(n-1,1)
is nonholomorphic in the variable
\tau\in\mathfrak{H}
.
We write
\theta_{L_{W}^{\perp}}^{+}(\tau,z_{W},h)
to denote its holomorphic/principal part.

Proof.

See
[
41
, Proposition 2.5]
, a minor generalization of the same main argument works in each case.
See
[
13
, Lemma 4.5]
for (i) and
[
55
, Lemma 4.12]
for (ii). To sketch it,
let
L_{U}
denote either of the lattices
L_{0},L_{W}\subset L
, with
M=L_{U}\oplus L_{U}^{\perp}\subset L

the corresponding finite index lattice for
(\ref{IPrelations})
above, which again we express simply as

\langle\langle f,\theta_{L}\rangle\rangle=\langle\langle f,\theta_{L_{U}^{\perp}}\otimes\theta_{L_{U}}\rangle\rangle
.
Hence, for any
z_{U}\in D(U)
and
h\in T_{U}({\bf{A}}_{f})
, we have

\displaystyle\Phi(f,z_{U},h)=\int_{\mathcal{F}}^{\star}\langle\langle f(\tau),\theta_{L}(\tau,z_{U},h)\rangle\rangle d\mu(\tau)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{F}}^{\star}\langle\langle f(\tau),\theta_{L_{U}^{\perp}}(\tau)\otimes\theta_{L_{U}}(\tau,z_{U},h)\rangle\rangle d\mu(\tau).

Here, we write
\theta_{L_{U}^{\perp}}(\tau)=\theta_{L_{U}^{\perp}}(\tau,1,1)
. In either case, we compute the regularized theta lift

\displaystyle\Phi(f,z_{U},h)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{CT}_{s=0}\left(\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\int\limits_{\mathcal{F}_{T}}\langle\langle f(\tau),\theta_{L_{U}^{\perp}}(\tau)\otimes\theta_{L_{U}}(\tau,z_{U},h)\rangle\rangle v^{-s}d\mu(\tau)\right).

Since the first integral in the limit

\displaystyle\int\limits_{\mathcal{F}_{1}}\langle\langle f(\tau),\theta_{L_{U}^{\perp}}(\tau)\otimes\theta_{L_{U}}(\tau,z_{U},h)\rangle\rangle v^{-s}d\mu(\tau)

determines a holomorphic function of
s
, we can express the regularized integral equivalently as

\displaystyle\Phi(f,z_{U},h)

\displaystyle=\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\int_{1}^{T}C(v,h)v^{-s}\frac{dv}{v}+\int\limits_{\mathcal{F}_{1}}\langle\langle f(\tau),\theta_{L_{U}^{\perp}}(\tau)\otimes\theta_{L_{U}}(\tau,z_{U},h)\rangle\rangle v^{-s}d\mu(\tau),

with the first term corresponding to a Mellin transform of the constant terms

\displaystyle C(v,h)

\displaystyle=C^{+}(v,h)+C^{-}(v,h),\quad C^{\pm}(h,v):=v^{-1}\int_{-1/2}^{1/2}\langle\langle f^{\pm}(u+iv),\theta_{L_{U}^{\perp}}(u+iv)\otimes\theta_{L_{U}}(u+iv,z_{U},h)\rangle\rangle du.

Writing the Fourier series expansion of the Siegel theta series
\theta_{M}(\tau,z_{U},h)
abstractly as

\displaystyle\theta_{M}(\tau,z_{U},h)

\displaystyle=v\sum\limits_{\mu\in M^{\vee}/M}\theta_{M,\mu}(\tau,z_{U},h){\bf{1}}_{\mu}=v\sum\limits_{\mu\in M^{\vee}/M}\sum\limits_{x\in V({\bf{Q}})\atop x\in h\mu}e\left(\tau Q(x_{z_{U}^{\perp}})+\overline{\tau}Q(x_{z_{U}})\right){\bf{1}}_{\mu},

we compute

\displaystyle C^{+}(v,h)

\displaystyle=v^{-1}\int_{-1/2}^{1/2}\sum\limits_{\mu\in M^{\vee}/M}f_{\mu}^{+}(u+iv)\theta_{M,\mu}(u+iv,z_{U},h)du

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\mu\in M^{\vee}/M}\sum\limits_{m\in{\bf{Q}}\atop m\gg-\infty}c_{f}^{+}(\mu,m)e(miv)\sum\limits_{x\in V({\bf{Q}})\atop x\in h\mu}e\left(ivQ(x_{z_{U}^{\perp}})-ivQ(x_{z_{U}})\right)\int_{-1/2}^{1/2}e\left(mu+Q(x_{z_{U}^{\perp}})u+Q(x_{z_{U}})u\right)du

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\mu\in M^{\vee}/M}\sum\limits_{m\in{\bf{Q}}\atop m\gg-\infty}\sum\limits_{{x\in V({\bf{Q}})\atop x\in h\mu}\atop m=-Q(x_{z_{U}^{\perp}})-Q(x_{z_{U}})}c_{f}^{+}(\mu,m)e(miv)e\left(ivQ(x_{z_{U}^{\perp}})-ivQ(x_{z_{U}})\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\mu\in M^{\vee}/M}\sum\limits_{x\in V({\bf{Q}})\atop x\in h\mu}c_{f}^{+}(\mu,-Q(x_{z_{U}^{\perp}})-Q(x_{z_{U}}))e^{4\pi vQ(x_{z_{U}^{\perp}})}

and

\displaystyle C^{-}(v,h)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\mu\in M^{\vee}/M}\sum\limits_{x\in V({\bf{Q}})\atop x\in h\mu}c_{f}^{-}(\mu,-Q(x_{z_{U}^{\perp}})-Q(x_{z_{U}}))W_{\frac{p(U)-q(U)}{2}}\left(2\pi v\left(-Q(x_{z_{U}^{\perp}})-Q(x_{z_{U}})\right)\right)e^{4\pi vQ(x_{z_{U}^{\perp}})}.

Observe that since we assume
Q|_{Z_{U}}<0
, we can deduce from well-known bounds for the Fourier coefficients that the Mellin transform

\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\int_{1}^{T}C(v,t)v^{-s}\frac{dv}{v}
is absolutely convergent.
Consider the orthogonal terms
x
in these expansions with
(x,z_{U})=0
, equivalently
x_{Z_{U}}=0
with
x\in L_{U}^{\perp}
, which are given by the constants

\displaystyle C_{U^{\perp}}^{+}(v,h)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\lambda\in(L_{U}^{\perp})^{\vee}/L_{U^{\perp}}}\sum\limits_{x\in U^{\perp}({\bf{Q}})\atop x\in\lambda}c_{f}^{+}(\lambda,-Q(x))=C_{U^{\perp}}^{+}

and

\displaystyle C_{U^{\perp}}^{-}(v,h)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\lambda\in(L_{U}^{\perp})^{\vee}/L_{U^{\perp}}}\sum\limits_{x\in U^{\perp}({\bf{Q}})\atop x\in\lambda}W_{\frac{p(U)-q(U)}{2}}\left(-2\pi vQ(x_{z_{U}^{\perp}})\right)c_{f}^{-}(\lambda,-Q(x_{z_{U}^{\perp}}))=C_{U^{\perp}}^{-}(v).

To be clear, neither of the coefficients
C^{\pm}_{U^{\perp}}
depends on
h
, and
C^{+}_{U^{\perp}}
does not depend on
v
.
That is, these terms arise from vectors
x
with
x_{z_{U}}=0
, equivalently
x\in U^{\perp}
, hence do not depend on the
h\in T_{U}({\bf{A}}_{f})
acting on the
U
-component.
Now for
\Re(s)>0

\displaystyle\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\int_{1}^{T}C^{+}_{U^{\perp}}(v,h)v^{-s}\frac{dv}{v}

\displaystyle=C^{+}_{U^{\perp}}\cdot\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\int_{1}^{T}v^{-s}\frac{dv}{v}=C^{+}_{U^{\perp}}\cdot\frac{\left(1-T^{-s}\right)}{s},

with

\displaystyle\lim_{s\rightarrow 0}\left(\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\int_{1}^{T}C^{+}_{U^{\perp}}(v,h)v^{-s}\frac{dv}{v}\right)

\displaystyle=C^{+}_{U^{\perp}}\cdot\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\log(T).

We deduce that this term does not contribute Laurent series expansion around
s=0
.
It is easy to see that the remaining non-orthogonal
x
with
(x,z_{U})\neq 0
contribute

\displaystyle C_{U}^{+}(v,h)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\lambda\in L_{U}^{\vee}/L_{U}}\sum\limits_{x\in U({\bf{Q}})\atop x\in h\lambda}c_{f}^{+}(\lambda,-Q(x_{z_{U}})-Q(x_{z_{U}^{\perp}}))e^{4\pi vQ(x_{z_{U}})}

and

\displaystyle C_{U}^{-}(v,h)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\lambda\in L_{U}^{\vee}/L_{U}}\sum\limits_{x\in U({\bf{Q}})\atop x\in h\lambda}c_{f}^{-}(\lambda,-Q(x_{z_{U}})-Q(x_{z_{U}^{\perp}}))W_{\frac{p(U)-q(U)}{2}}\left(-2\pi v\left(Q(x_{z_{U}})+Q(x_{z_{U}^{\perp}})\right)\right)e^{4\pi vQ(x_{z_{U}})}.

Note that the integrals defined for
t>0
by
\beta_{s+1}(t)=\int_{1}^{\infty}e^{-tv}v^{-s}\frac{dv}{v}

are known to be convergent for all
s\in{\bf{C}}
, and to determine holomorphic functions of
s
. From this, we deduce that

\displaystyle\operatorname{CT}_{s=0}\left(\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\int_{1}^{T}C(v,h)v^{-s}\frac{dv}{v}\right)

\displaystyle=\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\left(\int_{1}^{T}C(v,h)v^{-s}\frac{dv}{v}-C^{+}_{U^{\perp}}\cdot\log(T)\right),

and hence that

\displaystyle\Phi(f,z_{U},h)

\displaystyle=\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\int_{1}^{T}C(v,h)v^{-s}\frac{dv}{v}+\int\limits_{\mathcal{F}_{1}}\langle\langle f(\tau),\theta_{L_{U}^{\perp}}(\tau)\otimes\theta_{L_{U}}(\tau,z_{U},h)\rangle\rangle v^{-s}d\mu(\tau)

\displaystyle=\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\left(\int\limits_{\mathcal{F}_{T}}\langle\langle f(\tau),\theta_{L_{U^{\perp}}}(\tau)\otimes\theta_{L_{U}}(\tau,z_{U},h)\rangle\rangle d\mu(\tau)-C^{+}_{U^{\perp}}\cdot\log(T)\right).

To conclude, observe that

\displaystyle A_{0}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\sum\limits_{m\in M^{\vee}/M}\sum\limits_{m\gg-\infty}f_{\mu}^{+}(u+iv),\theta_{L_{U^{\perp}},\mu}^{+}(u+iv)\otimes{\bf{1}}_{0+L_{U}}du

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{m\in M^{\vee}/M}\sum\limits_{m\gg-\infty}c_{f}^{+}(\mu,m)e(miv)\sum\limits_{x\in U^{\perp}({\bf{Q}})}e\left(Q(x)iv\right)\int_{0}^{1}e(mu+Q(x)u)du

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{m\in M^{\vee}/M}\sum\limits_{m\gg-\infty}\sum\limits_{x\in U^{\perp}({\bf{Q}})\atop m=-Q(x)}c_{f}^{+}(\mu,m)e\left(Q(x)iv+miv\right)=\sum\limits_{m\in M^{\vee}/M}\sum\limits_{x\in U^{\perp}({\bf{Q}})}c_{f}^{+}(\mu,-Q(x))=C^{+}_{U^{\perp}}.

Here, we write
\theta_{L_{U^{\perp}},\mu}^{+}(\tau)=\theta_{L_{U^{\perp}},\mu}(u+iv)
for the case where
U=V_{0}
has signature
(0,2)
,
whence the theta series
\theta_{L_{U^{\perp}}}(\tau)=\sum_{\mu}\theta_{L_{U^{\perp}},\mu}(\tau){\bf{1}}_{\mu}
is holomorphic. ∎

Corollary 5.11
.

We have the following preliminary expressions for the sums
(\ref{Asums})
.

(i)

In the CM case with the negative definite lattice
L_{0}\subset L
of signature
(0,2)
, we have

\displaystyle\Phi(f,Z(V_{0}))

\displaystyle=\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\left[\frac{2}{\operatorname{vol}(K_{0})}\int_{\mathcal{F}_{T}}\langle\langle f(\tau),\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}}(\tau)\otimes E_{L_{0}}(\tau,0,-1)\rangle\rangle d\mu(\tau)-A_{0}\log(T)\right].

(ii)

In the geodesic case with the Lorenzian lattice
L_{W}\subset L
of signature
(1,1)
, we have

\displaystyle\Phi(f,\mathfrak{G}(W))

\displaystyle=\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\left[\frac{2}{\operatorname{vol}(K_{W})}\int_{\mathcal{F}_{T}}\langle\langle f(\tau),\theta_{L_{W}^{\perp}}(\tau)\otimes E_{L_{W}}(\tau,0;0)\rangle\rangle d\mu(\tau)-A_{0}\log(T)\right].

Proof.

We start with the expressions of Lemma
5.9
, evaluating the regularized theta integrals according to Proposition
5.10
.
Switching the order of summation and applying the Siegel-Weil formula (Theorem
5.1
), so
(\ref{SW-CM})
for (i) and
(\ref{SW-geo})
for (ii),
we obtain the stated formulae. ∎

5.2.3.
Summation along CM cycles

We now evaluate
\Phi(f,Z(V_{0}))
. Let
g(\tau)=g_{f}(\tau):=\xi_{l}(f)=\xi_{1-\frac{n}{2}}(f)(\tau)

be the holomorphic modular form of weight
2-l=1+\frac{n}{2}
obtained by applying the antilinear
differential operator
\xi_{l}
to the initial harmonic weak Maass form
f\in H_{l}(\omega_{L})
.
We consider the Rankin-Selberg
L
-function

\displaystyle L(s,g\times\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}})

\displaystyle:=\langle g(\tau),\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}}(\tau)\otimes E_{L_{0}}(\tau,s;1)\rangle,

as well as its completion

\displaystyle L^{\star}(s,g\times\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}})

\displaystyle:=\Lambda(s+1,\eta_{k})\langle g(\tau),\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}}(\tau)\otimes E_{L_{0}}(\tau,s;1)\rangle=\langle g(\tau),E^{\star}_{L_{0}}(\tau,s)\rangle.

Here, we write
k=k(V_{0})
for the imaginary quadratic field attached to the
(incoherent) quadratic space
(V_{0},Q_{0})=(\mathfrak{a},-Q_{\mathfrak{a}})
,
with discriminant
d_{k}
and character
\eta_{k}(\cdot)=\left(\frac{d_{k}}{\cdot}\right)
,
and
E_{L_{0}}(s,\tau;1)
for the corresponding (incoherent) Eisenstein series with completion

E_{L_{0}}^{\star}(s,\tau):=\Lambda(s+1,\eta_{k})E_{L_{0}}(s,\tau;1)=-E_{L_{0}}^{\star}(-s,\tau)
.
Writing

\displaystyle g(\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\mu\in L^{\vee}/L}\sum\limits_{m\in{\bf{Q}}\atop m>0}c_{g}(\mu,m)e(m\tau){\bf{1}}_{\mu}

and

\displaystyle\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\mu\in(L_{0}^{\perp})^{\vee}/L_{0}^{\perp}}\sum\limits_{m\in{\bf{Q}}\atop m>0}c_{\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}}}(\mu,m)e(m\tau){\bf{1}}_{\mu}=\sum\limits_{\mu\in(L_{0}^{\perp})^{\vee}/L_{0}^{\perp}}\sum\limits_{m\in{\bf{Q}}\atop m>0}r_{L_{0}^{\perp}}(\mu,m)e(m\tau){\bf{1}}_{\mu}

for the Fourier series expansions of the holomorphic forms
g(\tau)\in S_{2-l}(\omega_{L})

and
\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}}(\tau)\in H_{\frac{n}{2}}(\omega_{L_{0}}^{\vee})
, the
L
-function

L(s,g\times\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}})=\langle g,\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}}\otimes E_{L_{0}}(\cdot,s;1)\rangle

has for
\Re(s)\gg 1
the Dirichlet series expansion

(53)

\displaystyle L(s,g\times\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}})

\displaystyle=(4\pi)^{-\left(\frac{s+n}{2}\right)}\Gamma\left(\frac{s+n}{2}\right)\sum\limits_{\mu\in(L_{0}^{\perp})^{\vee}/L_{0}^{\perp}}\sum\limits_{m\geq 1}c_{g}(\mu,m)r_{L_{0}^{\perp}}(\mu,m)m^{-\left(\frac{s+n}{2}\right)}.

Theorem 5.12

(Bruinier-Yang)
.

We have that

\displaystyle\Phi(f,Z(V_{0}))

\displaystyle=-\frac{4}{\operatorname{vol}(K_{0})}\left(\operatorname{CT}\langle\langle f^{+}(\tau),\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}}(\tau)\otimes\mathcal{E}_{L_{0}}(\tau)\rangle\rangle+L^{\prime}(0,g\times\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}})\right)

\displaystyle=-\operatorname{deg}(Z(V_{0}))\left(\operatorname{CT}\langle\langle f^{+}(\tau),\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}}(\tau)\otimes\mathcal{E}_{L_{0}}(\tau)\rangle\rangle+L^{\prime}(0,g\times\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}})\right).

Proof.

See
[
13
, Theorem 4.7]
. As there seems to be at least one sign error in their
formula
6
6

6

See also
[
1
, Theorem 5.7.1]
, where the same sign error
for the contribution of
L^{\prime}(0,\xi_{1-n/2}(f)\times\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}})
in
[
13
, Theorem 4.7]

is acknowledged. That is, the integral in the last line of
[
13
, p. 654]
should be evaluated
using the differential forms identity
\overline{\partial}(f\tau)=-v^{2-l}\overline{\xi_{l}(f)}d\mu(\tau)=-L_{l}fd\mu(\tau)
,
and the substitution made implicity for the first identity in
[
13
, p. 655]
misses the sign change.
Moreover, the application of Stokes’ theorem for the remaining integral does not involve a change of
sign after identifying the boundary
\partial\mathcal{F}_{T}
with the interval
[iT,1+iT]
.
, we supply a detailed proof.
We know from Corollary
5.11
that we have

\displaystyle\Phi(f,Z(V_{0}))

\displaystyle=\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\left[\frac{2}{\operatorname{vol}(K_{0})}\cdot I_{T}(f)-A_{0}\log(T)\right],

where

\displaystyle\quad I_{T}(f)

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{\mathcal{F}_{T}}\langle\langle f(\tau),\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}}(\tau)\otimes E_{L_{0}}(\tau,0;-1)\rangle\rangle d\mu(\tau)

and

\displaystyle A_{0}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{CT}\langle\langle f^{+}(\tau),\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}}^{+}(\tau)\otimes{\bf{1}}_{0+L_{0}}\rangle\rangle.

To evaluate the integral
I_{T}(f)
, we first use the identity
(\ref{FI-CM3})
and the relation
d=\partial+\overline{\partial}
to compute

\displaystyle I_{T}(f)

\displaystyle=-2\int\limits_{\mathcal{F}_{T}}\langle\langle f(\tau),\theta_{L_{0}^{\vee}}\otimes\overline{\partial}E_{L_{0}}^{\prime}(\tau,0;1)\rangle\rangle d\tau=-2\int\limits_{\mathcal{F}_{T}}\langle\langle\partial f(\tau),\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}}\otimes E_{L_{0}}^{\perp}(\tau,0;1)\rangle\rangle d\tau

\displaystyle=-2\int\limits_{\mathcal{F}_{T}}d\langle\langle f(\tau),\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}}\otimes E_{L_{0}}^{\perp}(\tau,0;1)\rangle\rangle d\tau+2\int\limits_{\mathcal{F}_{T}}\langle\langle\overline{\partial}f(\tau),\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}}\otimes E_{L_{0}}^{\prime}(\tau,0;1)\rangle\rangle d\tau,

which after using Lemma
5.2
to compute the second integral in the latter expression becomes

\displaystyle I_{T}(f)

\displaystyle=-2\int\limits_{\mathcal{F}_{T}}d\langle\langle f(\tau),\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}}\otimes E_{L_{0}}^{\prime}(\tau,0;1)\rangle\rangle d\tau-2\int\limits_{\mathcal{F}_{T}}\langle\langle\xi_{l}(f)(\tau),\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}}\otimes E_{L_{0}}^{\prime}(\tau,0;1)\rangle\rangle v^{2-l}d\mu(\tau),

and which after using Stokes’ theorem to evaluate the first integral becomes

\displaystyle I_{T}(f)

\displaystyle=-2\int\limits_{\partial\mathcal{F}_{T}}\langle\langle f(\tau),\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}}\otimes E_{L_{0}}^{\prime}(\tau,0;1)\rangle\rangle d\tau-2\int\limits_{\mathcal{F}_{T}}\langle\langle\xi_{l}(f)(\tau),\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}}\otimes E_{L_{0}}^{\prime}(\tau,0;1)\rangle\rangle v^{2-l}d\mu(\tau)

\displaystyle=-2\int\limits_{\tau=iT}^{1+iT}\langle\langle f(\tau),\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}}\otimes E_{L_{0}}^{\prime}(\tau,0;1)\rangle\rangle d\tau-2\int\limits_{\mathcal{F}_{T}}\langle\langle\xi_{1-\frac{n}{2}}(f)(\tau),\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}}\otimes E_{L_{0}}^{\prime}(\tau,0;1)\rangle\rangle v^{1+\frac{n}{2}}d\mu(\tau).

Inserting this back into the initial formula, we obtain

\displaystyle\Phi(f,Z(V)_{0})

\displaystyle=-\frac{4}{\operatorname{vol}(K_{0})}\cdot\langle g(\tau),\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}}(\tau)\otimes E_{L_{0}}^{\prime}(\tau,0;1)\rangle

\displaystyle-\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\left[\frac{4}{\operatorname{vol}(K_{0})}\int\limits_{\tau=iT}^{1+iT}\langle\langle f(\tau),\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}}\otimes E_{L_{0}}^{\prime}(\tau,0;1)\rangle\rangle d\tau-A_{0}\log(T)\right].

To evaluate the limiting term in this latter expression, we first split the integral into parts according to the decomposition

f(\tau)=f^{+}(\tau)+f^{-}(\tau)
of
f(\tau)
into principal/holomorphic and nonholomorphic parts as

\displaystyle\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\int\limits_{\tau=iT}^{1+iT}\langle\langle f(\tau),\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}}(\tau)\otimes E_{L_{0}}^{\prime}(\tau,0;1)\rangle\rangle d\tau

\displaystyle=\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\int\limits_{\tau=iT}^{1+iT}\langle\langle f^{+}(\tau),\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}}(\tau)\otimes E_{L_{0}}^{\prime}(\tau,0;1)\rangle\rangle d\tau+\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\int\limits_{\tau=iT}^{1+iT}\langle\langle f^{-}(\tau),\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}}(\tau)\otimes E_{L_{0}}^{\prime}(\tau,0;1)\rangle\rangle d\tau.

We argue that the second integral on the right of this latter expression vanishes (cf.
[
22
, Theorem 3.5]
).
To be more precise, let us write the Fourier series expansion as

\displaystyle\langle\langle f^{-}(\tau),\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}}(\tau)\otimes E_{L_{0}}^{\prime}(\tau,0;1)\rangle\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{m\in{\bf{Z}}}a(m,iv)e(m\tau),\quad\tau=u+iv\in\mathfrak{H}.

Using the orthogonality of additive characters, we find that

\displaystyle\int_{\tau=iT}^{1+iT}\langle\langle f^{-}(\tau),\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}}(\tau)\otimes E_{L_{0}}^{\prime}(\tau,0;1)\rangle\rangle d\tau

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\langle\langle f^{-}(u+iT),\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}}(u+iT)\otimes E_{L_{0}}^{\prime}(u+iT,0;1)\rangle\rangle du

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{m\in{\bf{Z}}}a(m,iT)\int_{0}^{1}e(mu)du=a(0,iT).

Here,

\displaystyle a(0,iT)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\mu\in L^{\vee}/L}\sum\limits_{m\in{\bf{Q}}\atop m>0}c_{f}^{-}(\mu,-m)W_{l}(-2\pi mv)c_{F}(\mu,m,v)

denotes the constant coefficient of the scalar-valued form

\langle\langle f^{-}(\tau),\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}}(\tau)\otimes E_{L_{0}}^{\prime}(\tau,0;1)\rangle\rangle
, and we write

\displaystyle F(\tau)

\displaystyle:=\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}}(\tau)\otimes E^{\prime}_{L_{0}}(\tau,0;1)=\sum\limits_{\mu\in(L_{0}^{\perp}\oplus L_{0})^{\vee}/(L_{0}^{\perp}\oplus L_{0})}\sum\limits_{m\in{\bf{Q}}}c_{F}(\mu,m,v)e(m\tau){\bf{1}}_{\mu}.

Recall that the Whittaker coefficients
W_{l}(y):=\int_{-2y}^{\infty}e^{-t}t^{-l}dt=\Gamma(1-l,2|y|)
decay rapidly for
y\rightarrow-\infty
.
Using this together with standard bounds for the Fourier coefficients of
f^{-}(\tau)
and
F(\tau)
,
we deduce that for some integer
M>0
and constant
C>0
we have for each integer
m\geq M
the bounds

\displaystyle c_{f}^{-}(\mu,-m)W_{l}(-2\pi mv)c_{F}(\mu,m,v)

\displaystyle=O(e^{-mCv}).

Hence, via geometric series, we derive the bound

\displaystyle a(0,iT)

\displaystyle=O\left(\frac{e^{-CT}}{\left(1-e^{-CT}\right)}\right).

It is then apparent that

\displaystyle\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}a(0,iT)

\displaystyle=\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\int_{\tau=iT}^{1+iT}\langle\langle f^{-}(\tau),\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}}(\tau)\otimes E_{L_{0}}^{\prime}(\tau,0;1)\rangle\rangle d\tau=0.

Thus, it remains to evaluate the streamlined expression

\displaystyle\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\left[\frac{4}{\operatorname{vol}(K_{0})}\int\limits_{\tau=iT}^{1+iT}\langle\langle f^{+}(\tau),\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}}\otimes E_{L_{0}}^{\prime}(\tau,0;1)\rangle\rangle d\tau-A_{0}\log(T)\right].

Here, we first use the calculation of coefficients
(\ref{E+CM})
to see that

\displaystyle\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\left[\int\limits_{\tau=iT}^{1+iT}\langle\langle f^{+}(\tau),\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}}\otimes E_{L_{0}}^{\prime}(\tau,0;1)\rangle\rangle d\tau-A_{0}\log(T)\right]

\displaystyle=\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\int_{\tau=iT}^{1+iT}\langle\langle f^{+}(\tau),\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}}(\tau)\otimes\sum\limits_{\mu\in L_{0}^{\vee}/L_{0}}\sum\limits_{m\in{\bf{Q}}}\left(b_{L_{0}}(\mu,m,v)-\delta_{\mu,0}\delta_{m,0}\log(v)\right)e(m\tau){\bf{1}}_{\mu}\rangle\rangle d\tau

\displaystyle=\operatorname{CT}\langle\langle f^{+}(\tau),\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}}(\tau)\otimes\mathcal{E}_{L_{0}}(\tau)\rangle\rangle.

To compute the remaining integral

\displaystyle\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\left(\frac{4}{\operatorname{vol}(K_{0})}-1\right)\int\limits_{\tau=iT}^{1+iT}\langle\langle f^{+}(\tau),\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}}\otimes E_{L_{0}}^{\prime}(\tau,0;1)\rangle\rangle d\tau,

we decompose the Eisenstein series
E_{L_{0}}^{\prime}(\tau)\in H_{1}(\omega_{L_{0}})
into principal/holomorphic nonholomorphic parts

E_{L_{0}}^{\prime}(\tau)=E_{L_{0}}^{\prime+}(\tau)+E_{L_{0}}^{\prime-}(\tau)
to get the corresponding decomposition of integrals.
Again, we argue that the contributions from the nonholomorphic parts vanish. To be more precise, we claim here that

\displaystyle\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\int\limits_{\tau=iT}^{1+iT}\langle\langle f^{+}(\tau),\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}}\otimes E_{L_{0}}^{\prime-}(\tau,0;1)\rangle\rangle d\tau=0.

To see this, we again open up Fourier series expansions and use the orthogonality of characters to find that

\displaystyle\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\int\limits_{\tau=iT}^{1+iT}\langle\langle f^{+}(\tau),\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}}(\tau)\otimes E_{L_{0}}^{\prime-}(\tau,0;1)\rangle\rangle d\tau

\displaystyle=\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\int\limits_{0}^{1}\langle\langle f^{+}(u+iT),\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}}(u+iT)\otimes E_{L_{0}}^{\prime-}(u+iT,0;1)\rangle\rangle d\tau

\displaystyle=\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\sum\limits_{\mu\in(L_{0}^{\perp}+L_{0})^{\vee}/(L_{0}^{\perp}+L_{0})}\sum\limits_{m\in{\bf{Q}}\atop m>0}c_{f}^{+}(\mu,m)c_{\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}}\otimes E_{L_{0}}^{\prime-}}(-\mu,-m)W_{1}(-2\pi mT)

\displaystyle=\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\sum\limits_{\mu\in(L_{0}^{\perp}+L_{0})^{\vee}/(L_{0}^{\perp}+L_{0})}\sum\limits_{m\in{\bf{Q}}\atop m>0}c_{f}^{+}(\mu,m)\sum\limits_{{\mu_{1}\in(L_{0}^{\perp})^{\vee}/L_{0}^{\perp}\atop\mu_{2}\in L_{0}^{\vee}/L_{0}}\atop\mu_{1}+\mu_{2}\equiv-\mu\bmod(L_{0}^{\perp}+L_{0})}\sum\limits_{{m_{1}\in{\bf{Q}}_{\geq 0}\atop m_{2}\in{\bf{Q}}_{<0}}\atop m_{1}+m_{2}=-m}c_{\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}}}(\mu_{1},m_{1})c_{E_{L_{0}}^{\prime}}^{-}(\mu_{2},m_{2})W_{1}(-2\pi m_{2}T).

Again, we use the rapid decay of the Whittaker function
W_{1}(y):=\int_{-2y}^{\infty}e^{-t}t^{-1}dt=\Gamma(-1,2|y|)
with
y\rightarrow-\infty

to see that each inner sum tends to zero with
T\rightarrow\infty
. In this way, we derive the stated formula

\displaystyle\Phi(f,Z(V_{0}))

\displaystyle=-\frac{4}{\operatorname{vol}(K_{0})}\left(\operatorname{CT}\langle\langle f^{+}(\tau),\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}}(\tau)\otimes\mathcal{E}_{L_{0}}(\tau)\rangle\rangle+L^{\prime}(0,g\times\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}})\right).

∎

5.2.4.
Summation along geodesic sets

We now evaluate
\Phi(f,\mathfrak{G}(W))
. Again, we consider
g(\tau)=\xi_{1-\frac{n}{2}}(f)(\tau)
the holomorphic modular
form of weight
2-l=1+\frac{n}{2}
obtained by applying
\xi_{l}
to
f\in H_{l}(\omega_{L})
. We consider the Rankin-Selberg
L
-function

\displaystyle L(s,g\times\theta_{L_{W}^{\perp}})

\displaystyle:=\langle g(\tau),\theta_{L_{W}^{\perp}}(\tau)\otimes E_{L_{W}}(\tau,s;2)\rangle,

as well as its completion

\displaystyle L^{\star}(s,g\times\theta_{L_{W}^{\perp}})

\displaystyle:=\Lambda(s+1,\eta_{k})\langle g(\tau),\theta_{L_{W}^{\perp}}(\tau)\otimes E_{L_{W}}(\tau,s;2)\rangle=\langle g(\tau),E^{\star}_{L_{W}}(\tau,s;2)\rangle.

Here, we write
k=k(W)
for the real quadratic field attached to the
quadratic space
(W,Q_{W})=(\mathfrak{a},Q_{\mathfrak{a}})
,
with discriminant
d_{k}
and character
\eta_{k}(\cdot)=\left(\frac{d_{k}}{\cdot}\right)
,
and
E_{L_{W}}(s,\tau;2)
for the corresponding (coherent) Eisenstein series of weight
l=2
with completion

E_{L_{W}}^{\star}(s,\tau;2):=\Lambda(s+1,\eta_{k})E_{L_{W}}(s,\tau;2)
.
Notice that by Corollary
5.5
, the image under the Maass weight
lowering operator
L_{2}
of the first derivative
E_{L_{W}}^{\star\prime}(\tau,s;2)=\frac{d}{ds}E_{L_{W}}^{\star}(\tau,s;2)

of this Eisenstein series
E_{L_{W}}^{\star}(\tau,s;2)
at the central point
s=0
satisfies the functional identity

\displaystyle L_{2}E_{L_{W}}^{\star\prime}(\tau,0;2)

\displaystyle=E_{L_{W}}^{\star}(\tau,0;0).

Here,
E_{L_{W}}^{\star}(\tau,s;0):=\Lambda(s+1,\eta_{k})E_{L_{W}}(\tau,s;0)
is the coherent
Eisenstein series associated to the quadratic space
(\mathfrak{a},Q_{\mathfrak{a}})
satisfying
the even, symmetric Langlands functional equation
E^{\star}_{L_{W}}(\tau,s;0)=E_{L_{W}}^{\star}(\tau,-s;0)
.

Remark 5.13
.

Notice that while the latter Eisenstein series
E_{L_{W}}(\tau,s;0)
of weight
0
(at
s=0
) appears in the Siegel-Weil
formula
(\ref{SW-geo})
for the average over theta series
\theta_{L_{W}}(\tau,s,h)
, it is rather the Eisenstein series
E_{L_{W}}(\tau,s;2)

of weight
2
that appears in integral presentation for the Rankin-Selberg
L
-function
L(s,g\times\theta_{L_{W}^{\perp}})
.

To describe the Dirichlet series expansion of
L(s,g\times\theta_{L_{W}^{\perp}})
, let us write the Fourier series
expansion of the holomorphic/principal part
\theta_{L_{W}^{\perp}}^{+}(\tau)
of the theta series

\theta_{L_{W}^{\perp}}(\tau)=\theta_{L_{W}^{\perp}}^{+}(\tau)+\theta_{L_{W}^{\perp}}^{-}(\tau)\in H_{\frac{n-1}{2}}(\omega_{L_{W}^{\perp}}^{\vee})
as

\displaystyle\theta_{L_{W}^{\perp}}^{+}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\mu\in(L_{W}^{\perp})^{\vee}/L_{W}^{\perp}}\sum\limits_{m\in{\bf{Q}}\atop m\gg-\infty}c_{\theta_{L_{W}^{\perp}}}^{+}(\mu,m)e(m\tau){\bf{1}}_{\mu}

with positive coefficients denoted by
r_{L_{W}^{\perp}}(\mu,m):=c_{\theta_{L_{W}^{\perp}}}^{+}(\mu,m)
for
m>0
.
We then have the expansion

(54)

\displaystyle L(s,g\times\theta_{L_{W}^{\perp}})

\displaystyle=(4\pi)^{-\left(\frac{s+n}{2}\right)}\Gamma\left(\frac{s+n}{2}\right)\sum\limits_{\mu\in(L_{W}^{\perp})^{\vee}/L_{W}^{\perp}}\sum\limits_{m\geq 1}c_{g}(\mu,m)r_{L_{W}^{\perp}}(\mu,m)m^{-\left(\frac{s+n}{2}\right)}.

Theorem 5.14
.

We have that

\displaystyle\Phi(f,\mathfrak{G}(W))

\displaystyle=-\frac{4}{\operatorname{vol}(K_{W})}\left(\operatorname{CT}\langle\langle f^{+}(\tau),\theta^{+}_{L_{W}^{\perp}}(\tau)\otimes\mathcal{E}_{L_{W}}(\tau)\rangle\rangle+L^{\prime}(0,g\times\theta_{L_{W}^{\perp}})\right).

Proof.

See
[
55
, Theorem 4.16]
. A minor generalization of the same argument works here,
or that of Theorem
5.12
using the identity of differential forms
(\ref{FI-geo3})
in lieu of
(\ref{FI-CM3})
.
To be sure, we know from Corollary
5.11
that

\displaystyle\Phi(f,\mathfrak{G}(W))

\displaystyle=\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\left[\frac{2}{\operatorname{vol}(K_{W})}\cdot I_{T}(f)-A_{0}\log(T)\right],

where

\displaystyle\quad I_{T}(f)

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{\mathcal{F}_{T}}\langle\langle f(\tau),\theta_{L_{W}^{\perp}}(\tau)\otimes E_{L_{W}}(\tau,0;0)\rangle\rangle d\mu(\tau)

and

\displaystyle A_{0}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{CT}\langle\langle f^{+}(\tau),\theta_{L_{W}^{\perp}}^{+}(\tau)\otimes{\bf{1}}_{0+L_{W}}\rangle\rangle.

To evaluate the integral
I_{T}(f)
, we first use the identity
(\ref{FI-geo3})
and the relation
d=\partial+\overline{\partial}
to compute

\displaystyle I_{T}(f)

\displaystyle=-2\int\limits_{\mathcal{F}_{T}}\langle\langle f(\tau),\theta_{L_{W}^{\perp}}\otimes\overline{\partial}E_{L_{W}}^{\prime}(\tau,0;2)\rangle\rangle d\tau

\displaystyle=-2\int\limits_{\mathcal{F}_{T}}d\langle\langle f(\tau),\theta_{L_{W}^{\perp}}\otimes E_{L_{W}}^{\prime}(\tau,0;2)\rangle\rangle d\tau+2\int\limits_{\mathcal{F}_{T}}\langle\langle\overline{\partial}f(\tau),\theta_{L_{W}^{\perp}}\otimes E_{L_{W}}^{\prime}(\tau,0;2)\rangle\rangle d\tau,

which after using Lemma
5.2
to compute the second integral in the latter expression becomes

\displaystyle I_{T}(f)

\displaystyle=-2\int\limits_{\mathcal{F}_{T}}d\langle\langle f(\tau),\theta_{L_{W}^{\perp}}\otimes E_{L_{W}}^{\prime}(\tau,0;2)\rangle\rangle d\tau-2\int\limits_{\mathcal{F}_{T}}\langle\langle\xi_{l}(f)(\tau),\theta_{L_{W}^{\perp}}\otimes E_{L_{W}}^{\prime}(\tau,0;2)\rangle\rangle v^{2-l}d\mu(\tau),

and which after using Stokes’ theorem to evaluate the first integral becomes

\displaystyle I_{T}(f)

\displaystyle=-2\int\limits_{\tau=iT}^{1+iT}\langle\langle f(\tau),\theta_{L_{W}^{\perp}}\otimes E_{L_{W}}^{\prime}(\tau,0;2)\rangle\rangle d\tau-2\int\limits_{\mathcal{F}_{T}}\langle\langle\xi_{1-\frac{n}{2}}(f)(\tau),\theta_{L_{W}^{\perp}}\otimes E_{L_{W}}^{\prime}(\tau,0;2)\rangle\rangle v^{1+\frac{n}{2}}d\mu(\tau).

Inserting this back into the initial formula, we obtain the preliminary formula

\displaystyle\Phi(f,\mathfrak{G}(W))

\displaystyle=-\frac{4}{\operatorname{vol}(K_{W})}\cdot\langle g(\tau),\theta_{L_{W}^{\perp}}(\tau)\otimes E_{L_{W}}^{\prime}(\tau,0;2)\rangle

\displaystyle-\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\left[\frac{4}{\operatorname{vol}(K_{W})}\int\limits_{\tau=iT}^{1+iT}\langle\langle f(\tau),\theta_{L_{W}^{\perp}}\otimes E_{L_{W}}^{\prime}(\tau,0;2)\rangle\rangle d\tau-A_{0}\log(T)\right].

As in the proof of Theorem
5.12
, we argue that the limiting constant coefficient integral depends only on the holomorphic parts.
The only difference is that the theta series
\theta_{L_{W}}(\tau)
is not holomorphic. Hence, we decompose it into
holomorphic/principal and nonholomorphic parts
\theta_{L_{W}}(\tau)=\theta_{L_{W}}^{+}(\tau)+\theta_{L_{W}}^{-}(\tau)
.
That is, we split the constant coefficient term in this preliminary expression into three parts

\displaystyle\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\int_{\tau=iT}^{iT+1}\langle\langle f(\tau),\theta_{L_{W}^{\perp}}(\tau)\otimes E^{\prime}_{L_{W}}(\tau,0;2)\rangle\rangle d\tau

\displaystyle=\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\int_{\tau=iT}^{iT+1}\langle\langle f^{+}(\tau),\theta^{+}_{L_{W}^{\perp}}(\tau)\otimes E^{\prime}_{L_{W}}(\tau,0;2)\rangle\rangle d\tau

\displaystyle+\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\int_{\tau=iT}^{iT+1}\langle\langle f^{+}(\tau),\theta^{-}_{L_{W}^{\perp}}(\tau)\otimes E^{\prime}_{L_{W}}(\tau,0;2)\rangle\rangle d\tau

\displaystyle+\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\int_{\tau=iT}^{iT+1}\langle\langle f^{-}(\tau),\theta_{L_{W}^{\perp}}(\tau)\otimes E^{\prime}_{L_{W}}(\tau,0;2)\rangle\rangle d\tau.

We argue in the same way as for the proof of Theorem
5.12
that the third integral on the right-hand side vanishes.
The same argument also shows that the second integral in this expression vanishes. Hence, only the first integral contributes.
To evaluate its contribution to the initial expression

\displaystyle\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\left[\frac{4}{\operatorname{vol}(K_{W})}\int\limits_{\tau=iT}^{1+iT}\langle\langle f^{+}(\tau),\theta^{+}_{L_{W}^{\perp}}\otimes E_{L_{W}}^{\prime}(\tau,0;2)\rangle\rangle d\tau-A_{0}\log(T)\right],

we again use the calculations of
(\ref{E+geo})
to find that

\displaystyle\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\left[\int\limits_{\tau=iT}^{1+iT}\langle\langle f^{+}(\tau),\theta_{L_{W}^{\perp}}\otimes E_{L_{W}}^{\prime}(\tau,0;2)\rangle\rangle d\tau-A_{0}\log(T)\right]

\displaystyle=\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\int_{\tau=iT}^{1+iT}\langle\langle f^{+}(\tau),\theta_{L_{W}^{\perp}}(\tau)\otimes\sum\limits_{\mu\in L_{W}^{\vee}/L_{W}}\sum\limits_{m\in{\bf{Q}}}\left(b_{L_{W}}(\mu,m,v)-\delta_{\mu,0}\delta_{m,0}\log(v)\right)e(m\tau){\bf{1}}_{\mu}\rangle\rangle d\tau

\displaystyle=\operatorname{CT}\langle\langle f^{+}(\tau),\theta_{L_{W}^{\perp}}(\tau)\otimes\mathcal{E}_{L_{W}}(\tau)\rangle\rangle,

and argue the same way via the rapid decay of the Whittaker functions
W_{2}(y)
that

\displaystyle\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\int\limits_{\tau=iT}^{1+iT}\langle\langle f^{+}(\tau),\theta_{L_{W}^{\perp}}\otimes E_{L_{W}}^{\prime-}(\tau,0;2)\rangle\rangle d\tau=0.

In this way, we see that

\displaystyle-\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\left[\frac{4}{\operatorname{vol}(K_{W})}\int\limits_{\tau=iT}^{1+iT}\langle\langle f(\tau),\theta_{L_{W}^{\perp}}\otimes E_{L_{W}}^{\prime}(\tau,0;2)\rangle\rangle d\tau-A_{0}\log(T)\right]

\displaystyle=-\frac{4}{\operatorname{vol}(K_{W})}\cdot\operatorname{CT}\langle\langle f^{+}(\tau),\theta^{+}_{L_{W}^{\perp}}(\tau)\otimes\mathcal{E}_{L_{W}}(\tau)\rangle\rangle.

The stated formula now follows from the preliminary formula. ∎

6.
Integral presentations of Rankin-Selberg
L
-functions

We now explain how to identify the Rankin-Selberg
L
-functions
L^{\star}(s,\xi_{1-n/2}(f)\times\theta_{L_{U}^{\perp}})

appearing in Theorems
5.12
and
5.14
with standard Rankin-Selberg
L
-functions
for
\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\bf{A}})\times\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\bf{A}})
.

Let
k
be any (real or imaginary) quadratic field of discriminant
d_{k}
and corresponding Dirichlet character

\eta_{k}(\cdot)=\left(\frac{d_{k}}{\cdot}\right)
. We consider the ideal class group
7
7

7

More generally,
we could consider the ring class group
C(\mathcal{O})
of any order
\mathcal{O}\subset\mathcal{O}_{k}

for all of the analytic/archimedean discussion here. However, since the discussion of integral models and arithmetic
heights in
[
1
]
is so far only understood for the maximal order
\mathcal{O}_{k}
, we stick to this case for simplicity.

C(\mathcal{O}_{k})
of
k
.
Recall that we fix an integer ideal representative
\mathfrak{a}\subset\mathcal{O}_{k}
for each class

A=[\mathfrak{a}]\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})
, and write
Q_{\mathfrak{a}}(z)={\bf{N}}_{k/{\bf{Q}}}(z)/{\bf{N}}\mathfrak{a}

for the corresponding norm form. Again, each space
(\mathfrak{a},Q_{\mathfrak{a}})
has signature
(2,0)

when
k
is an imaginary quadratic field, and signature
(1,1)
when
k
is a real quadratic field.
We consider for each class
A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})
the rational quadratic space
(V_{A},Q_{A})
of signature
(2,2)

given by
V_{A}=\mathfrak{a}_{\bf{Q}}\oplus\mathfrak{a}_{\bf{Q}}
and quadratic form

Q_{A}(z)=Q_{A}(z_{1},z_{2})=Q_{\mathfrak{a}}(z_{1})-Q_{\mathfrak{a}}(z_{2})
. We fix a level
N
prime to
d_{k}
,
and consider the lattice
L_{A}\subset V_{A}
whose adelization corresponds
to the compact open subgroup
K_{A}
of
\operatorname{GSpin}(V_{A})({\bf{A}}_{f})\cong\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\bf{A}}_{f})^{2}

given by
K_{0}(N)^{2}
, as in Proposition
2.4
.
Hence, the corresponding spin Shimura variety
X_{K_{A}}
can be identified with
Y_{0}(N)\times Y_{0}(N)
.
In this setting, we explain two ways to associate with a cuspidal newform

\phi\in S_{l}(\Gamma_{0}(N))
a vector-valued cusp form
g_{\phi}=g_{\phi,A}\in S_{l}(\omega_{L_{A}})
.
As we explain below, we can use the Doi-Naganuma lift (see e.g.
[
7
,
\lx@sectionsign 3.1
]
,
[
62
]
)
to show the existence of such a form. We can also use the theorem of Strömberg

[
52
, Theorem 5.4]
– see also Scheithauer
[
48
, Theorem 3.1]
,
Zhang
[
61
, Theorem 4.15]
, and Bruinier-Bundschuh
[
8
]
– to construct such a form more explicity.
We then show that we have identifications of completed Rankin-Selberg
L
-functions

(55)

\displaystyle L^{\star}(2s-s,g_{\phi,A}\times\theta_{A})

\displaystyle=\Lambda(s-1/2,\phi\times\theta_{A}),

where
\theta_{A}
denotes the Hecke theta series associated to the class
A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})
, and hence that

(56)

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})}\chi(A)L^{\star}(2s-s,g_{\phi,A}\times\theta_{A})

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})}\chi(A)\Lambda(s-1/2,\phi\times\theta_{A})=\Lambda(s-1/2,\pi\times\theta(\chi)).

We then use this to reinterpret the calculations of Theorems
5.12
and
5.14
in terms of
\Lambda(s,\phi\times\theta(\chi))
.

6.1.
Equivalences of
L
-functions

We now show the identifications
(\ref{E1})
and
(\ref{E2})
.

6.1.1.
Hecke theta series associated to class group characters of quadratic fields

Given a quadratic field
k
as above and a class group character

\chi:C(\mathcal{O}_{k})\longrightarrow{\bf{C}}^{\times}
, we consider the corresponding Hecke theta series

\displaystyle\theta(\chi)(\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})}\chi(A)\theta_{A}(\tau).

Here, each
\theta_{A}(\tau)
denotes the theta series associated to the class

A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})
and quadratic space
(\mathfrak{a},Q_{\mathfrak{a}})
.
Hence, when
k
is an imaginary quadratic field, this theta series has the explicit expansion

\displaystyle\theta_{A}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{w_{k}}\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}}e\left(Q_{\mathfrak{a}}(\lambda)\tau\right)=\sum\limits_{m\in{\bf{Z}}_{\geq 0}}r_{A}(m)e(m\tau),

where
w_{k}=\#\mu(k)/2
denotes half the number of roots of unity in
k
, and
r_{A}(m)
the counting function

\displaystyle r_{A}(m)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{w_{k}}\cdot\#\left\{\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}:Q_{\mathfrak{a}}(\lambda)=m\right\}.

A classical theorem of Hecke shows that this theta series
\theta_{A}\in M_{1}(\Gamma_{0}(|d_{k}|),\eta_{k})

is a modular form of weight
1=(2-0)/2
, level
\Gamma_{0}(|d_{k}|)
,
and character
\eta_{k}
. Hence,
\theta(\chi)\in M_{1}(\Gamma_{0}(|d_{k}|),\eta_{k})
when
k
is imaginary quadratic.
When
k
is a real quadratic field, the unit group
\mathcal{O}_{k}^{\times}\cong{\bf{Z}}\times\mu(k)=\langle\varepsilon_{k}\rangle\times\mu(k)

is no longer torsion, and we must fix a fundamental domain
\mathfrak{a}^{\star}
for the action of

\mathcal{O}_{k}^{\times}/\mu(k)=\langle\varepsilon_{k}\rangle
on the lattice

\mathfrak{a}\subset k_{\infty}\cong{\bf{R}}^{2}
. We can then describe the
corresponding theta series via the explicit expansion

\displaystyle\theta_{A}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{w_{k}}\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}^{\star}}e\left(Q_{\mathfrak{a}}(\lambda)\tau\right)=\sum\limits_{m\in{\bf{Z}}_{\geq 0}}r_{A}(m)e(m\tau),

where
w_{k}=\#\mu(k)/2
again denotes half the number of roots of unity in
k
, and
r_{A}(m)
the counting function

\displaystyle r_{A}(m)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{w_{k}}\cdot\#\left\{\lambda\in\mathfrak{a}^{\star}:Q_{\mathfrak{a}}(\lambda)=m\right\}.

The theorem of Hecke shows that this
\theta_{A}\in M_{0}(\Gamma_{0}(d_{k}),\eta_{k})

is a modular form of weight
0=(1-1)/2
, level
\Gamma_{0}(|d_{k}|)
, and character
\eta_{k}
.
Hence,
\theta(\chi)\in M_{0}(\Gamma_{0}(d_{k}),\eta_{k})
when
k
is real quadratic.

Let us henceforth fix such a Hecke theta series

\displaystyle\theta(\chi)\in M_{l(k)}(\Gamma(|d_{k}|),\eta_{k}),\quad l(k):=\begin{cases}1&\text{ if $k$ is imaginary quadratic}\\
0&\text{ if $k$ is real quadratic}\end{cases}.

6.1.2.
Rankin-Selberg
L
-functions

Let
\phi\in S_{l(\phi)}(\Gamma_{0}(N))
be a holomorphic cusp form of weight
l(\phi)
on
\Gamma_{0}(N)
. We write the Fourier series expansion as

\displaystyle\phi(\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{m\geq 1}c_{\phi}(m)e(m\tau)=\sum\limits_{m\geq 1}a_{\phi}(m)m^{\frac{l(\phi)-1}{2}}e(m\tau).

so that the finite part
L(s,\phi)
of the standard
L
-function
\Lambda(s,\phi)=L_{\infty}(s,\phi)L(s,\phi)

has Dirichlet series expansion for
\Re(s)>1
given by
L(s,f)=\sum_{m\geq 1}a_{\phi}(m)m^{-s}=\sum_{m\geq 1}c_{\phi}(m)m^{-(s+1/2)}
.
Let us also write the Fourier series expansion of the theta series
\theta(\chi)\in M_{l(k)}(\Gamma_{0}(|d_{k}|),\eta_{k})
as

\displaystyle\theta(\chi)(\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{m\geq 1}c_{\theta(\chi)}(m)e(m\tau)=\sum\limits_{m\geq 1}a_{\theta(\chi)}(m)m^{\frac{l(k)-1}{2}}e(m\tau).

We look at the corresponding Rankin-Selberg
L
-functions

\displaystyle L(s,\phi\times\theta(\chi))

\displaystyle=L(2s,\eta_{k})\sum\limits_{m\geq 1}c_{\phi}(m)c_{\theta(\chi)}(m)m^{-\left(s+\left\{\frac{l(\phi)+l(k)}{2}\right\}-1\right)}

\displaystyle=L(2s,\eta_{k})\sum\limits_{m\geq 1}a_{\phi}(m)a_{\theta(\chi)}(m)m^{-s}.

Implicitly, we consider the corresponding partial Rankin-Selberg
L
-functions, defined first for
\Re(s)>1
by

\displaystyle L(s,\phi\times\theta_{A})

\displaystyle=L(2s,\eta_{k})\sum\limits_{m\geq 1}c_{\phi}(m)r_{A}(m)m^{-\left(s+\left\{\frac{l(\phi)+l(k)}{2}\right\}-1\right)}

whose
\chi
-twisted linear combinations give the
\chi
-twisted Rankin-Selberg
L
-function

\displaystyle L(s,\phi\times\theta(\chi))

\displaystyle=L(2s,\eta_{k})\sum\limits_{A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})}\chi(A)\sum\limits_{m\geq 1}c_{\phi}(m)r_{A}(m)m^{-\left(s+\left\{\frac{l(\phi)+l(k)}{2}\right\}-1\right)}.

By the method of unfolding or convolution (cf. e.g.
[
26
,
\lx@sectionsign
V.1]
), we have the integral presentation

(57)

\displaystyle\frac{\Gamma\left(s+\left\{\frac{l(\phi)+l(k)}{2}\right\}-1\right)}{(4\pi)^{s+\left\{\frac{l(\phi)+l(k)}{2}\right\}-1}}\sum\limits_{m\geq 1}c_{\phi}(m)c_{\theta(\chi)}(m)m^{-\left(s+\left\{\frac{l(\phi)+l(k)}{2}\right\}-1\right)}

\displaystyle=\langle\phi,\theta_{A}E_{A,\eta_{k}}(\cdot,s;l(\phi)+l(k))\rangle

for
E_{A,\eta_{k}}(\tau,s;l(\phi)-l(k))\in M_{l(\phi)-l(k)}(\Gamma_{0}(\operatorname{lcm}(d_{k},N)),\eta_{k})
some uniquely-determined
Eisenstein series of weight
l(\phi)-l(k)
, level
\Gamma_{0}(\operatorname{lcm}(d_{k},N))
, and character
\eta_{k}
.
That is, the method of Rankin-Selberg convolution shows that these Rankin-Selberg
L
-functions
have analytic continuations given by a functional equation inherited from the Eisenstein series
appearing in
(\ref{IP})
. Here, we have the following more precise result.

Proposition 6.1
.

Let
\phi\in S_{l(\phi)}(\Gamma_{0}(N))
be a normalized newform.
Assume that
(N,d_{k})=1
and that
l(\phi)>l(k)
, where
l(k)=\{0,1\}
denotes the weight of the
Hecke theta series
\theta(\chi)\in M_{l(k)}(\Gamma_{0}(|d_{k}|),\eta_{k})
. Put

\displaystyle L_{\infty}(s,\phi\times\theta(\chi))

\displaystyle=(2\pi)^{-2s}\Gamma\left(s-\left\{\frac{l(\phi)-l(k)}{2}\right\}\right)\Gamma\left(s+\left\{\frac{l(\phi)+l(k)}{2}\right\}-1\right).

Then, the completed
L
-function

\displaystyle\Lambda(s,\phi\times\theta(\chi)):=L_{\infty}(s,\phi\times\theta(\chi))L(s,\phi\times\theta(\chi))

satisfies the symmetric functional equation

\displaystyle\Lambda(s,\phi\times\theta(\chi))

\displaystyle=\eta_{k}(-N)|d_{k}N|^{1-2s}\Lambda(1-s,\phi\times\theta(\chi)).

Proof.

The proof is well-known for the more general setup of
\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\bf{A}})\times\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\bf{A}})

Rankin-Selberg
L
-functions given by Jacquet
[
31
]
and Jacquet-Langlands
[
32
]
. Here, we give the more explicit classical
calculation of Li
[
44
, Theorem 2.2, Example 2]
. ∎

Remark 6.2
.

Note that the class group characters
\chi
are wide ray class characters,
and hence the archimedean factor
L_{\infty}(s,\phi\times\theta(\chi))
does not depend
on the choice of
\chi
, although it does depend on the choice of quadratic field
k
.
This is also apparent by inspection of the formula for
L_{\infty}(s,\phi\times\theta(\chi))
given in Proposition
6.1
.

6.1.3.
Quadratic basechange equivalences

Fix a cuspidal newform
\phi\in S_{l(\phi)}(\Gamma_{0}(N))
of weight
l(\phi)>l(k)
on
\Gamma_{0}(N)
of trivial central character
as in Proposition
6.1
. Let
\pi(\phi)=\otimes_{v}\pi(\phi)_{v}
denote the cuspidal automorphic representation of

\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\bf{A}})
determined by
\phi
, with
\Lambda(s,\pi(\phi))=\prod_{v\leq\infty}L(s,\pi(\phi)_{v})

its standard
L
-function. Note that this coincides with the corresponding completed
L
-function

\Lambda(s,\phi)=L_{\infty}(s,\phi)L(s,\phi)
of
\phi
. Let us also write
\pi(\chi)
to denote the automorphic
representation of
\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\bf{A}})
determined by the class group character
\chi\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})^{\vee}
,
determined by the corresponding theta series
\theta(\chi)\in M_{l(k)}(\Gamma_{0}(d_{k}),\eta_{k})
.
Hence, we consider the corresponding Rankin-Selberg
L
-function

\displaystyle\Lambda(s,\pi(\phi)\times\pi(\chi))=\prod_{v\leq\infty}L(s,\pi(\phi)_{v}\times\pi(\chi)_{v})=\Lambda(s,\phi\times\theta(\chi))=L_{\infty}(s,\phi\times\theta(\chi))L(s,\phi\times\theta(\chi)).

Let us now consider the quadratic basechange lifting

\displaystyle\Pi(\phi)=\otimes_{w}\Pi(\phi)_{w}=\operatorname{BC}_{k/{\bf{Q}}}(\pi(\phi))

of
\pi(\phi)
to a cuspidal automorphic representation of
\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\bf{A}}_{k})
. Such a lifting exists by the theta
lifting construction of Shintani (c.f.
[
7
,
\lx@sectionsign
2.7]
), and more generally for any
\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\bf{A}})
-automorphic
representation by Langlands
[
43
]
, and for any
\operatorname{GL}_{n}({\bf{A}})
-automorphic representation by Arthur-Clozel
[
2
]
.
We refer to the article
[
25
]
for more background on these quadratic basechange liftings and their
L
-functions.
In brief, writing
\Lambda(s,\Pi(\phi))=\prod_{w\leq\infty}L(s,\Pi(\phi)_{w})
to denote the corresponding completed
standard
L
-function of
\Pi(\chi)
, we have an equivalence of standard
L
-functions

(58)

\displaystyle\Lambda(s,\Pi(\phi))

\displaystyle=\Lambda(s,\pi(\phi))\Lambda(s,\pi(\phi)\otimes\eta_{k})=\Lambda(s,\phi)\Lambda(s,\phi\otimes\eta_{k}).

To be clear, the identity
(\ref{RSBC})
relates the
\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\bf{A}}_{k})\times\operatorname{GL}_{1}({\bf{A}}_{k})

automorphic
L
-function
\Lambda(s,\Pi(\phi)\otimes\chi)
to the
\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\bf{A}})\times\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\bf{A}})

Rankin-Selberg
L
-function
\Lambda(s,\pi(\phi)\times\pi(\chi))=\Lambda(s,\phi\times\theta(\chi))
.

6.1.4.
Vector-valued lifts of cuspidal eigenforms via the Doi-Naganuma lift

Let us return to the quadratic spaces
(L_{A},Q_{A})
of signature
(2,2)
described in Proposition
2.1
and Corollary
2.3
.
Hence, we fix an integer
N\geq 1
prime to the discriminant
d_{k}
of the quadratic field
k
.
We then consider the lattice

\displaystyle L_{A}=L_{A}(N)=N^{-1}\mathfrak{a}\oplus N^{-1}\mathfrak{a}\subset V_{A}

of level
N
and square
8
8

8

A minor variation of the calculation given in Proposition
2.3
, replacing
\alpha_{\mathfrak{a}}

with
\alpha_{\mathfrak{a}}/N
and
z_{\mathfrak{a}}
with
z_{\mathfrak{a}}/N
to get the basis

\displaystyle v_{1}=(\alpha_{\mathfrak{a}}/N,0),\quad v_{2}=(z_{\mathfrak{a}}/N,0),\quad v_{3}=(0,\alpha_{\mathfrak{a}}/N),\quad v_{4}=(0,z_{\mathfrak{a}}/N),

shows that the discriminant
d(L_{A})=\det((v_{i},v_{j})_{A})
is a perfect square.

discriminant
d(L_{A})
whose adelization
L_{A}\otimes\widehat{\bf{Z}}
is fixed under the conjugation action of

\operatorname{GSpin}(V_{A})({\bf{A}}_{f})\cong\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\bf{A}})^{2}
by the compact open subgroup
K_{0}(N)\oplus K_{0}(N)
.

Here, we introduce the Doi-Naganuma lift (see e.g.
[
7
,
\lx@sectionsign
3.1]
) to describe how to construct from the scalar-valued
cusp form
\phi\in S_{l(\phi)}(\Gamma_{0}(N))
and the classical scalar-valued Siegel theta series

\Theta_{L_{A},l(\phi)}(\tau,z)
associated to
(L_{A},Q_{A})
a Hilbert modular form
F_{\phi,L_{A}}(z)
of parallel
weight
l(\phi)
on
X_{A}({\bf{C}})\cong Y_{0}(N)\times Y_{0}(N)
. As we explain, this construction allows us to find a unique
vector-valued cusp form
g_{\phi}=g_{\phi,A}\in H_{l(\phi)}(\omega_{L_{A}})
which lifts
\phi
, and
consequently for which the corresponding Rankin-Selberg
L
-function
L^{\star}(s,g_{\phi}\times\theta_{L_{A,U}^{\vee}})

describes the partial completed Rankin-Selberg
L
-function
\Lambda(s,\phi\times\theta_{A})
.

Let us first describe the Siegel theta series
\Theta_{L_{A},l}(\tau,z)
of weight
l\in{\bf{Z}}
associated to the signature
(2,2)

lattice
L_{A}\subset V_{A}
, as described in Corollary
2.3
above. We refer to
[
7
,
\lx@sectionsign
2.6]
for more background.
Hence, this lattice
L_{A}=L_{A}(N)
has level
N
and discriminant
d(V_{A})=1
, so that the corresponding Dirichlet character

\eta_{d(V_{A})}(\cdot)=(\frac{d(V_{A})}{\cdot})
can be identified with the trivial/principal character modulo
N
. Given
z\in D(V_{A})
and
\lambda\in V_{A}({\bf{R}})
, we have a unique decomposition
\lambda=\lambda_{z}+\lambda_{z^{\perp}}
,
where
\lambda_{z}
and
\lambda_{z^{\perp}}
denote the corresponding projections to
z
and
z^{\perp}
.
Let us write
Q_{A}(\lambda)_{z}:=Q_{A}(\lambda_{z})-Q_{A}(\lambda_{z^{\perp}})
for the corresponding majorant.
We consider the Siegel theta function
\Theta_{L_{A},l}:\mathfrak{H}\times D(V_{A})\rightarrow{\bf{C}}

of weight
l
associated to the lattice
L_{A}
, defined on
\tau=u+iv\in\mathfrak{H}
and
z\in D(V_{A})
by the series

\displaystyle\Theta_{L_{A},l}(\tau,z)

\displaystyle=v\sum\limits_{\lambda\in L_{A}^{\vee}}\frac{(\lambda,z)_{A}^{l}}{(z,z)_{A}^{l}}\cdot e\left(Q_{A}(\lambda_{z^{\perp}})N\tau+Q_{A}(\lambda_{z})N\overline{\tau}\right).

This series converges normally, is nonholomorphic in both variables, and satisfies the transformation property

\displaystyle\Theta_{L_{A},l}(\gamma z,z)

\displaystyle=\eta_{d(V_{A})}(d)(c\tau+d)^{l}\Theta_{L_{A},l}(\tau,z)=(c\tau+d)^{l}\Theta_{L_{A},l}(\tau,z)\quad\quad\forall\quad\gamma=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}a&b\\
c&d\end{array}\right)\in\Gamma_{0}(N).

Theorem 6.3

(Doi-Naganuma)
.

Let
\Theta_{L_{A},l}(\tau,z)
denote the Siegel theta series of weight
l
associated to the signature
(2,2)
lattice

L_{A}\subset V_{A}
chosen according to Corollary
2.4
, whose adelization
L_{A}\otimes\widehat{{\bf{Z}}}

is fixed by the compact open subgroup subgroup
K_{0}(N)\oplus K_{0}(N)
of

\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\widehat{\bf{Z}})^{2}\subset\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\bf{A}}_{f})^{2}\cong\operatorname{GSpin}(V_{A})({\bf{A}}_{f})
.
Hence, we identify this function
\Theta_{L_{A},l}(\tau,\cdot)
in the variable
\tau\in\mathfrak{H}

as a nonholomorphic modular form of weight
l
, level
\Gamma_{0}(N)
, and trivial character.
Let
\varphi\in S^{\operatorname{new}}_{l}(\Gamma_{0}(N))
be a cuspidal holomorphic newform of the same weight,
level, and character. Assume that
\varphi
lies in the corresponding Kohnen plus space

\displaystyle S_{l}^{+}(\Gamma_{0}(N))=\left\{f\in S_{l}(\Gamma_{0}(N)):c_{f}(n)=-1\implies c_{f}(n)=0\right\}\subset S^{\operatorname{new}}_{l}(\Gamma_{0}(N)).

Then, the theta lift defined on
z\in D(V_{A})=D^{\pm}(V_{A})\cong\mathfrak{H}^{2}
by the convergent integral

\displaystyle F_{\varphi,L_{A}}(z)

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{\mathcal{F}}\varphi(\tau)\Theta_{L_{A},l}(\tau,z)v^{l}\frac{dudv}{v^{2}}

determines a cuspidal eigenform of parallel weight
l
on the Hilbert modular surface
X_{0}(N)\times X_{0}(N)
.
Here again, we write
\mathcal{F}
to denote the standard fundamental domain for the action of
\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\bf{Z}})
on
\mathfrak{H}
.

Proof.

This is a special case of the Doi-Naganuma lifting for the setup we consider above for Proposition
2.3
,
leading to the identifications
(\ref{acc})
and
(\ref{ll})
with Remark
2.4
. See
[
7
,
\lx@sectionsign
3.1]
, and more generally
the relevant discussions in Doi-Naganuma
[
20
]
, Naganuma
[
45
]
, van der Geer
[
54
,
\lx@sectionsign
4]
, and Zagier
[
62
]
for more background. ∎

Let us now return to the setup of Theorems
5.12
and
5.14
above with vector-valued forms for
L_{A}\subset V_{A}
.
We now write
\theta_{L_{A},l}(\tau,z)=\theta_{L_{A},l}(\tau,z)
to denote the Siegel theta function

\theta_{L_{A},l}:\mathfrak{H}\times D(V_{A})\longrightarrow\mathfrak{S}_{L_{A}}^{\vee}
of weight
l
constructed
from the Weil representation
\omega_{L_{A}}:\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\bf{Z}})\rightarrow\mathfrak{S}_{L_{A}}^{\vee}
in
the same way as
(\ref{Stheta})
above. To be more precise, we make the following modification to the choice of
Gaussian archimedean Schwartz function
\Phi_{\infty}(x,z):=\exp(-(x,x)_{A,z})
for
z\in D(V_{A})\cong\mathfrak{H}^{2}

and
x\in V_{A}({\bf{R}})
in
(\ref{Gaussian})
. Let
\mathcal{P}_{l}(x,z)
be a weight
l
harmonic polynomial, so that

\omega_{L_{A}}(k_{\theta})\mathcal{P}_{l}(x,z)=e^{il\theta}\mathcal{P}_{l}(x,z)
for all
k_{\theta}\in\operatorname{SO}_{2}({\bf{R}})
.
We then define the corresponding function
\Phi_{\infty}^{(l)}(x,z)=\mathcal{P}_{l}(x,z)\Phi_{\infty}(x,z)
.
Hence, in the variable
x\in V({\bf{R}})
, we obtain an archimedean local Schwartz function

\Phi_{\infty}(x,\cdot)\in\mathcal{S}(V({\bf{R}}))
which transforms with weight
l
under the action of the
maximal compact subgroup
\operatorname{SO}_{2}({\bf{R}})\subset\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\bf{R}})
.
Using the same conventions and notations as above with the Iwasawa decomposition, we then define the corresponding theta series

\displaystyle\theta_{L_{A},l}(\tau,z)=\theta_{L_{A},l}(\tau,z,1)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\mu\in L_{A}^{\vee}/L_{A}}\vartheta_{L_{A}}(g_{\tau},1;\Phi^{(l)}_{\infty}(\cdot,z_{0})\otimes{\bf{1}}_{\mu}){\bf{1}}_{\mu}

from the theta kernel

\displaystyle\vartheta_{L_{A}}(g,h;\Phi)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{x\in V_{A}({\bf{Q}})}\left(\omega_{L_{A}}(g,h)\Phi\right)(x).

Here again, we fix a basepoint
z_{0}\in D(V_{A})=D^{\pm}(V_{A})\cong\mathfrak{H}^{2}
, and we write

g\in\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\bf{A}})
,
h\in\operatorname{GSpin}(V_{A})({\bf{A}})
,
and
\Phi=\otimes_{v}\Phi_{v}\in\mathcal{S}(V_{A}({\bf{A}}))
to denote generic elements.
We also write
\overline{\theta}_{L_{A},l}(\tau,z)
to denote the theta series obtained from
the conjugate Weil representation
\overline{\omega}_{L_{A}}
.

Corollary 6.4
.

Fix a holomorphic cuspidal newform
\phi\in S_{l}(\Gamma_{0}(N))
. Assume
\phi

lies in the Kohnen plus space
S_{l}^{+}(\Gamma_{0}(N))\subset S_{l}(\Gamma_{0}(N))
.
There exists a unique
g_{\phi}=g_{\phi,A}\in H_{l}(\overline{\omega}_{L_{A}})
for which

(59)

\displaystyle\langle\langle g_{\phi}(\tau),\overline{\theta}_{L_{A},l}(\tau,z)\rangle\rangle

\displaystyle=\Theta_{L_{A},l}(\tau,z)\phi(\tau),

so that the Doi-Naganuma lifting
F_{\phi,L_{A}}(z)
can be characterized equivalently as the theta integral

\displaystyle F_{\phi,L_{A}}(z)

\displaystyle=\int\limits_{\mathcal{F}}\langle\langle g_{\phi}(\tau),\overline{\theta}_{L_{A},l}(\tau,z)\rangle\rangle v^{l}\frac{dudv}{v^{2}}.

Remark 6.5
.

The lifting
g_{\phi}\in H_{l}(\overline{\omega}_{L_{A}})
of
\phi\in S_{l}(\Gamma_{0}((N))
can be described explicitly
in special cases by Zhang
[
61
, Theorem 4.15]
and Scheithauer
[
47
, Theorem 3.1]
.
More generally, the theorem of Strömberg
9
9

9

taking the isotropic subgroup
S_{0}=\{0\}\subset L^{\vee}/L

[
52
, Theorems 5.2 and 5.4]

allow us to construct such a lift of any modular form
\phi\in M_{l}(\Gamma_{0}(M(L)),\eta_{|d(L)|})
of level
M(L)
equal to that of the lattice

L
and quadratic character
\eta_{|d(L)|}(\cdot)=(\frac{|d(L)|}{\cdot})
with
d=d(L)
the discrimnant of the lattice
to a vector-valued form
g_{\phi}\in M_{l}(\omega_{L})
via the expansion

\displaystyle g_{\phi}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{M\in\Gamma_{0}(M(L))\backslash\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\bf{Z}})}\omega_{L}(M)^{-1}{\bf{1}}_{0}\phi|_{l}M(\tau).

6.1.5.
Equivalences of Rankin-Selberg
L
-functions

We now return to Theorems
5.12
and
5.14
for the special case of the quadratic space
(V_{A},Q_{A})
of signature
(2,2)
with lattice

L_{A}
corresponding to the congruence subgroup
K_{0}(N)\subset\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\bf{A}}_{f})
, as described in Corollary
2.4
.

Proposition 6.6
.

Fix a holomorphic cuspidal newform
\phi\in S^{\operatorname{new}}_{2}(\Gamma_{0}(N))
of weight
2
, level
\Gamma_{0}(N)
, and trivial character.
Let
g_{\phi,A}\in S_{2}(\overline{\omega}_{L_{A}})
denote the lifting of
\phi
to a vector-valued cusp form of weight
2

and conjugate Weil representation
\overline{\omega}_{L_{A}}
. We have the following identifications of completed Rankin-Selberg
L
-functions.

(i)

If
k
is the imaginary quadratic field associated to the negative definite subspace
V_{A,0}\subset V_{A}

with
L_{A,0}=L_{A}\cap V_{A,0}
, then we have the identifications of completed Rankin-Selberg
L
-functions

\displaystyle L^{\star}(2s-2,g_{\phi,A}\times\theta_{L_{A,0}^{\perp}})=\Lambda(s-1/2,\phi\times\theta_{A})

for each class
A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})
, and for each class group character
\chi\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})^{\vee}
the identification

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})}\chi(A)L^{\star}(2s-2,g_{\phi,A}\times\theta_{L_{A,0}^{\perp}})=\Lambda(s-1/2,\phi\times\theta(\chi)).

(ii)

If
k
is the real quadratic field associated to the Lorentzian subspace
W_{A}\subset V_{A}
with
L_{A,W}\cap W_{A}\cap L_{A}
,
then we have the identifications of completed Rankin-Selberg
L
-functions
