\displaystyle L^{\star}(2s-2,g_{\phi,A}\times\theta_{L_{A,W}^{\perp}})

\displaystyle=\Lambda(s-1/2,\phi\times\theta_{A})

for each class
A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})
, and for each class group character
\chi\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})^{\vee}
that

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})}\chi(A)L^{\star}(2s-2,g_{\phi,A}\times\theta_{L_{A,W}^{\perp}})

\displaystyle=\Lambda(s-1/2,\phi\times\theta(\chi)).

Proof.

Cf.
[
55
, Corollary 4.18]
. Fix any class
A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})
.
If
k
is imaginary quadratic as for (i), we write the Fourier series expansions of
the corresponding holomorphic vector-valued forms as

\displaystyle g_{\phi,A}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\mu\in L_{A}^{\vee}/L_{A}}\sum\limits_{m>0}c_{\phi,A}(\mu,m)e(m\tau){\bf{1}}_{\mu}\in S_{2}(\overline{\omega}_{L_{A}})

and

\displaystyle\theta_{L_{A,0}^{\perp}}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\mu\in(L_{A,0}^{\perp})^{\vee}/L_{A,0}^{\perp}}\sum\limits_{m\geq 0}r_{L_{A,0}^{\perp}}(\mu,m)e(m\tau){\bf{1}}_{\mu}\in M_{0}(\omega_{L_{A,0}^{\perp}}),

and consider the Dirichlet series of the corresponding Rankin-Selberg
L
-function for
\Re(s)\gg 1
,

\displaystyle L(s,g_{\phi,A}\times\theta_{L_{A,0}^{\perp}})

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{s+2}{2}\right)}{(4\pi)^{\frac{s+2}{2}}}\sum\limits_{\mu\in(L_{A,0}^{\perp})^{\vee}/L_{A,0}^{\perp}}\sum\limits_{m\geq 1}\frac{c_{g_{\phi,A}}(\mu,m)r_{L_{A,0}^{\perp}}(\mu,m)}{m^{\frac{s+2}{2}}}.

Here, we can identify the discriminant group as

\displaystyle(L_{A,0}^{\perp})^{\vee}/L_{A,0}^{\perp}\cong\mathfrak{d}_{k}^{-1}N^{-1}\mathfrak{a}/N^{-1}\mathfrak{a}\cong\mathfrak{d}_{k}^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{k}/\mathcal{O}_{k},

and the counting functions appearing in the Fourier series expansion of the theta series as

\displaystyle r_{L_{A,0}^{\perp}}(\mu,m)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{w_{k}}\cdot\#\left\{\lambda\in\mu+L_{A,0}^{\perp}:Q_{A}|_{L_{A,0}^{\perp}}(\lambda)=m\right\}=\frac{1}{w_{k}}\cdot\#\left\{\lambda\in\mu+N^{-1}\mathfrak{a}:Q_{\mathfrak{a}}(\lambda)=m\right\}.

It is easy to see from this that we have the identification
10
10

10

More formally, we use that

[N^{-1}\mathfrak{a}]=[(N^{-1})\mathfrak{a}]=[\mathfrak{a}]\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})=I(k)/P(k)
.
of counting functions

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\mu\in(L_{A,0}^{\perp})^{\vee}/L_{A,0}^{\perp}}r_{L_{A,0}^{\perp}}(\mu,m)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{w_{k}}\cdot\#\left\{\lambda\in N^{-1}\mathfrak{a}:Q_{\mathfrak{a}}(\lambda)=m\right\}=r_{A}(m).

Similarly, as a consequence of the relation

\displaystyle\langle\langle g_{\phi,A}(\tau),\overline{\theta}_{L_{A},2}(\tau,z)\rangle\rangle

\displaystyle=\Theta_{L_{A},2}(\tau,z)\phi(\tau)

implied by
(\ref{DNrel})
, we deduce that we have the relation of Fourier coefficients

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\mu\in(L_{A,0}^{\perp})^{\vee}/L_{A,0}^{\perp}}c_{\phi,A}(\mu,m)

\displaystyle=c_{\phi}(m),

and more generally, that we have an identification of scalar-valued forms

\displaystyle\langle\langle g_{\phi,A}(\tau),\theta_{L_{A,0}^{\perp}}(\tau)\otimes E_{L_{A,0}}(\tau,s;1)\rangle\rangle

\displaystyle=\phi(\tau)\theta_{A}(\tau)E_{A}(\tau,s;1),

where
E_{A}(\tau,s;1)
denotes the Eisenstein series in the Rankin-Selberg integral presentation
(\ref{IP})

corresponding to
E_{L_{A,0}}(\tau,s;1)\in H_{1}(\omega_{L_{A,0}})
.
This implies the corresponding identification of Rankin-Selberg products

\displaystyle L(s,g_{\phi,A}\times\theta_{L_{A,0}^{\perp}})

\displaystyle=\langle\langle g_{\phi,A}(\tau),\theta_{L_{A,0}^{\perp}}(\tau)\otimes E_{L_{A,0}}(\tau,s;1)\rangle=\int\limits_{\mathcal{F}}\langle\langle g_{\phi,A}(\tau),\theta_{L_{A,0}^{\perp}}(\tau)\otimes E_{L_{A,0}}(\tau,s;1)\rangle\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{s+2}{2}\right)}{(4\pi)^{\frac{s+2}{2}}}\sum\limits_{\mu\in(L_{A,0}^{\perp})^{\vee}/L_{A,0}^{\perp}}\sum\limits_{m\geq 1}\frac{c_{g_{\phi,A}}(\mu,m)r_{L_{A,0}^{\perp}}(\mu,m)}{m^{\frac{s+2}{2}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{s+2}{2}\right)}{(4\pi)^{\frac{s+2}{2}}}\sum\limits_{m\geq 1}\frac{c_{\phi}(m)r_{A}(m)}{m^{\frac{s+2}{2}}}=\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{s+2}{2}\right)}{(4\pi)^{\frac{s+2}{2}}}\sum\limits_{m\geq 1}\frac{c_{\phi}(m)r_{A}(m)}{m^{\frac{s+2}{2}}}.

We then deduce from
(\ref{IP})
that Proposition
6.1
that we have the identification of completed
L
-functions

\displaystyle L^{\star}(2s-2,g_{\phi,A}\times\theta_{L_{A,0}^{\perp}})

\displaystyle=\Lambda(s-1/2,\phi\times\theta_{A})

and hence that

\displaystyle\sum_{A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})}\chi(A)L^{\star}(2s-2,g_{\phi,A}\times\theta_{L_{A,0}^{\perp}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})}\chi(A)\Lambda(s-1/2,\phi\times\theta_{A})=\Lambda(s-1/2,\phi\times\theta(\chi)).

If
k
is real quadratic as for (ii), we again first open up the Dirichlet series expansion (for
\Re(s)\gg 1
)

\displaystyle L(s,g_{\phi,A}\times\theta_{L_{A,W}^{\perp}})

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{s+2}{2}\right)}{(4\pi)^{\frac{s+2}{2}}}\sum\limits_{\mu\in(L_{A,W}^{\perp})^{\vee}/L_{A,W}^{\perp}}\sum\limits_{m\geq 1}\frac{c_{g_{\phi,A}}(\mu,m)r_{L_{A,W}^{\perp}}(\mu,m)}{m^{\frac{s+2}{2}}}.

Again, we can identify the discriminant group as

\displaystyle(L_{A,W}^{\perp})^{\vee}/L_{A,W}^{\perp}\cong\mathfrak{d}_{k}^{-1}N^{-1}\mathfrak{a}/N^{-1}\mathfrak{a}\cong\mathfrak{d}_{k}^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{k}/\mathcal{O}_{k},

and the counting functions appearing in the Fourier series
expansion of the theta series
\theta_{L_{A,W}^{\perp}}^{+}(\tau)
as

\displaystyle r_{L_{A,W}^{\perp}}(\mu,m)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{w_{k}}\cdot\#\left\{\lambda\in\mu+L_{A,W}^{\perp}/\langle\varepsilon_{k}\rangle:Q_{A}|_{L_{A,W}^{\perp}}(\lambda)=m\right\}=\frac{1}{w_{k}}\cdot\#\left\{\lambda\in\mu+N^{-1}\mathfrak{a}^{\star}:Q_{\mathfrak{a}}(\lambda)=m\right\}

so that

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\mu\in(L_{A,W}^{\perp})^{\vee}/L_{A,W}^{\perp}}r_{L_{A,W}^{\perp}}(\mu,m)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{w_{k}}\cdot\#\left\{\lambda\in N^{-1}\mathfrak{a}^{\star}:Q_{\mathfrak{a}}(\lambda)=m\right\}=r_{A}(m).

We obtain from the corresponding relation
(\ref{DNrel})
the identification of Fourier coefficients

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\mu\in(L_{A,W}^{\perp})^{\vee}/L_{A,W}^{\perp}}c_{\phi,A}(\mu,m)

\displaystyle=c_{\phi}(m),

and more generally the identification of scalar-valued forms

\displaystyle\langle\langle g_{\phi,A}(\tau),\theta_{L_{A,W}^{\perp}}(\tau)\otimes E_{L_{A,W}}(\tau,s;2)\rangle\rangle

\displaystyle=\phi(\tau)\theta_{A}(\tau)E_{A}(\tau,s;2),

where
E_{A}(\tau,s;2)
denotes the Eisenstein series in the Rankin-Selberg integral presentation
(\ref{IP})

corresponding to
E_{L_{A,W}}(\tau,s;2)\in H_{2}(\omega_{L_{A,W}})
.
Taking Petersson inner products, we then obtain the same identifications

\displaystyle L(s,g_{\phi,A}\times\theta_{L_{A,W}^{\perp}})

\displaystyle=\langle\langle g_{\phi,A}(\tau),\theta_{L_{A,W}^{\perp}}(\tau)\otimes E_{L_{A,W}}(\tau,s;2)\rangle=\int\limits_{\mathcal{F}}\langle\langle g_{\phi,A}(\tau),\theta_{L_{A,W}^{\perp}}(\tau)\otimes E_{L_{A,W}}(\tau,s;2)\rangle\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{s+2}{2}\right)}{(4\pi)^{\frac{s+2}{2}}}\sum\limits_{\mu\in(L_{A,W}^{\perp})^{\vee}/L_{A,W}^{\perp}}\sum\limits_{m\geq 1}\frac{c_{g_{\phi,A}}(\mu,m)r_{L_{A,W}^{\perp}}(\mu,m)}{m^{\frac{s+2}{2}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{s+2}{2}\right)}{(4\pi)^{\frac{s+2}{2}}}\sum\limits_{m\geq 1}\frac{c_{\phi}(m)r_{A}(m)}{m^{\frac{s+2}{2}}}=\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{s+2}{2}\right)}{(4\pi)^{\frac{s+2}{2}}}\sum\limits_{m\geq 1}\frac{c_{\phi}(m)r_{A}(m)}{m^{\frac{s+2}{2}}}

of the corresponding Rankin-Selberg inner products.
We then deduce from
(\ref{IP})
that Proposition
6.1
that we have the identification of completed
L
-functions

\displaystyle L^{\star}(2s-2,g_{\phi,A}\times\theta_{L_{A,W}^{\perp}})

\displaystyle=\Lambda(s-1/2,\phi\times\theta_{A})

and hence that

\displaystyle\sum_{A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})}\chi(A)L^{\star}(2s-2,g_{\phi,A}\times\theta_{L_{A,W}^{\perp}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})}\chi(A)\Lambda(s-1/2,\phi\times\theta_{A})=\Lambda(s-1/2,\phi\times\theta(\chi)).

∎

6.2.
Relations to sums of Green’s functions along anisotropic subspaces

Putting all of these observations together, we derive the following consequences of Theorems
5.12
and
5.14
.

Theorem 6.7
.

We retain the setup of Proposition
6.6
. For each class
A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})
,
let
f_{0,A}\in H_{0}(\omega_{L_{A}})
be any harmonic weak Maass form
whose image under the antilinear differential operator
\xi_{0}
equals
g_{\phi,A}
, so

\displaystyle\xi_{0}(f_{0,A})(\tau)

\displaystyle=g_{\phi,A}(\tau).

We have the following integral presentations of completed Rankin-Selberg
L
-functions,
given in terms of sums over CM cycles or geodesic sets as in Theorems
5.12
and
5.14
above respectively.

(i)

If
k
is imaginary quadratic and
\eta_{k}(-N)=-\eta_{k}(N)=-1
, then

\displaystyle\Lambda^{\prime}(1/2,\phi\times\theta(\chi))

\displaystyle=-\Lambda(1,\eta_{k})\sum\limits_{A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})}\chi(A)\left[\left(\frac{\operatorname{vol}(K_{A,0})}{4}\right)\Phi(f_{0,A},Z(V_{A,0}))+\operatorname{CT}\langle\langle f_{0,A}^{+}(\tau),\theta_{L_{A,0}^{\perp}}(\tau)\otimes\mathcal{E}_{L_{A,0}}(\tau)\rangle\rangle\right]

\displaystyle=-\Lambda(1,\eta_{k})\sum\limits_{A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})}\chi(A)\left[\operatorname{deg}(Z(V_{A,0}))\Phi(f_{0,A},Z(V_{A,0}))+\operatorname{CT}\langle\langle f_{0,A}^{+}(\tau),\theta_{L_{A,0}^{\perp}}(\tau)\otimes\mathcal{E}_{L_{A,0}}(\tau)\rangle\rangle\right].

(ii)

If
k
is real quadratic and
\eta_{k}(-N)=\eta_{k}(N)=-1
, then

\displaystyle\Lambda^{\prime}(1/2,\phi\times\theta(\chi))

\displaystyle=-\Lambda(1,\eta_{k})\sum\limits_{A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})}\chi(A)\left[\left(\frac{\operatorname{vol}(K_{A,W})}{4}\right)\Phi(f_{0,A},\mathfrak{G}(W_{A}))+\operatorname{CT}\langle\langle f_{0,A}^{+}(\tau),\theta_{L_{A,W}^{\perp}}(\tau)\otimes\mathcal{E}_{L_{A,W}}(\tau)\rangle\rangle\right].

Proof.

For (i), we have for each class
A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})
the relation

\displaystyle\Phi(f_{0,A},Z(V_{A,0}))

\displaystyle=-\frac{4}{\operatorname{vol}(K_{A,0})}\left(\operatorname{CT}\langle\langle f_{0,A}^{+}(\tau),\theta_{L_{A,0}^{\perp}}(\tau)\otimes\mathcal{E}_{L_{A,0}}(\tau)\rangle\rangle+L^{\prime}(0,g_{\phi,A}\times\theta_{L_{A,0}^{\perp}})\right)

and hence

(60)

\displaystyle-\left(\frac{\operatorname{vol}(K_{A,0})}{4}\right)\Phi(f_{0,A},Z(V_{A,0}))-\operatorname{CT}\langle\langle f_{0,A}^{+}(\tau),\theta_{L_{A,0}^{\perp}}(\tau)\otimes\mathcal{E}_{L_{A,0}}(\tau)\rangle\rangle

\displaystyle=L^{\prime}(0,g_{\phi,A}\times\theta_{L_{A,0}^{\perp}})

by Theorem
5.12
. Observe that since

L^{\star}(s,g_{\phi,A}\times\theta_{L_{A,0}^{\perp}})=-L^{\star}(s,g_{\phi,A}\times\theta_{L_{A,0}^{\perp}})

by the odd, symmetric functional equation
E_{L_{A,0}}^{\star}(\tau,s;1)=-E_{L_{A,0}}^{\star}(\tau,-s;1)
described in Proposition
5.3
,
we have the vanishing of the central value

L^{\star}(0,g_{\phi,A}\times\theta_{L_{A,0}^{\perp}})=L(0,g_{\phi,A}\times\theta_{L_{A,0}^{\perp}})=0
, and hence that

(61)

\displaystyle\Lambda^{\star\prime}(0,g_{\phi,A}\times\theta_{L_{A,0}^{\perp}})=\Lambda(1,\eta_{k})L^{\prime}(0,g_{\phi,A}\times\theta_{L_{A,0}^{\perp}}).

Moreover, observe that by the equivalence of
L
-functions shown in Proposition
6.6
(i)
with the functional equation for
\Lambda(s,\phi\times\theta(\chi))
described in Proposition
6.1
,
we are only in the non-degenerate situation when
\eta_{k}(-N)=-\eta_{k}(N)=-1
.
Hence, we can multiply each side of
(\ref{CMIP})
to obtain the corresponding relation

(62)

\displaystyle

\displaystyle-\Lambda(1,\eta_{k})\left[\left(\frac{\operatorname{vol}(K_{A,0})}{4}\right)\Phi(f_{0,A},Z(V_{A,0}))+\operatorname{CT}\langle\langle f_{0,A}^{+}(\tau),\theta_{L_{A,0}^{\perp}}(\tau)\otimes\mathcal{E}_{L_{A,0}}(\tau)\rangle\rangle\right]

\displaystyle=L^{\star\prime}(0,g_{\phi,A}\times\theta_{L_{A,0}^{\perp}}).

Taking a twisted linear combination of the
L
-values on each side of
(\ref{CMIP2})
and using Proposition
6.6
, we obtain

(63)

\displaystyle

\displaystyle-\Lambda(1,\eta_{k})\sum\limits_{A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})}\chi(A)\left[\left(\frac{\operatorname{vol}(K_{A,0})}{4}\right)\Phi(f_{0,A},Z(V_{A,0}))+\operatorname{CT}\langle\langle f_{0,A}^{+}(\tau),\theta_{L_{A,0}^{\perp}}(\tau)\otimes\mathcal{E}_{L_{A,0}}(\tau)\rangle\rangle\right]

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})}\chi(A)L^{\star\prime}(0,g_{\phi,A}\times\theta_{L_{A,0}^{\perp}})=\Lambda^{\prime}(1/2,\phi\times\theta(\chi))=\Lambda^{\prime}(1/2,\phi\times\theta(\chi)).

For (ii), we have for each class
A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})
the relation

\displaystyle\Phi(f_{0,A},\mathfrak{G}(W_{A}))

\displaystyle=-\frac{4}{\operatorname{vol}(K_{A,W})}\left(\operatorname{CT}\langle\langle f_{0,A}^{+}(\tau),\theta_{L_{A,W}^{\perp}}^{+}(\tau)\otimes\mathcal{E}_{L_{A,W}}(\tau)\rangle\rangle+L^{\prime}(0,g_{\phi,A}\times\theta_{L_{A,W}^{\perp}})\right)

and hence

(64)

\displaystyle-\left(\frac{\operatorname{vol}(K_{A,W})}{4}\right)\Phi(f_{0,A},\mathfrak{G}(W_{A}))+\operatorname{CT}\langle\langle f_{0,A}^{+}(\tau),\theta^{+}_{L_{A,W}^{\perp}}(\tau)\otimes\mathcal{E}_{L_{A,W}}(\tau)\rangle\rangle

\displaystyle=L^{\prime}(0,g_{\phi,A}\times\theta_{L_{A,W}^{\perp}})

by Theorem
5.14
. Using the identification of completed
L
-functions of Proposition
6.6
(ii)
with the functional equation of Proposition
6.1
, we see that
L^{\star}(s,0,g_{\phi,A}\times\theta_{L_{A,W}^{\perp}})

satisfies an odd symmetric functional equation when
\eta_{k}(-N)=\eta_{k}(N)=-1
, whence

L^{\star}(0,g_{\phi,A}\times\theta_{L_{A,W}^{\perp}})=L(0,g_{\phi,A}\times\theta_{L_{A,W}^{\perp}})=0
, and

\displaystyle\Lambda^{\star\prime}(0,g_{\phi,A}\times\theta_{L_{A,W}^{\perp}})=\Lambda(1,\eta_{k})L^{\prime}(0,g_{\phi,A}\times\theta_{L_{A,W}^{\perp}}).

Hence, we can multiply each side of
(\ref{geoIP})
to obtain the corresponding relation

(65)

\displaystyle

\displaystyle-\Lambda(1,\eta_{k})\left[\left(\frac{\operatorname{vol}(K_{A,W})}{4}\right)\Phi(f_{0,A},\mathfrak{G}(W_{A}))+\operatorname{CT}\langle\langle f_{0,A}^{+}(\tau),\theta_{L_{A,W}^{\perp}}(\tau)\otimes\mathcal{E}_{L_{A,W}}(\tau)\rangle\rangle\right]

\displaystyle=L^{\star\prime}(0,g_{\phi,A}\times\theta_{L_{A,W}^{\perp}}).

Taking a twisted linear combination of the
L
-values on each side of
(\ref{geoIP2})
and using Proposition
6.6
, we obtain

(66)

\displaystyle

\displaystyle-\Lambda(1,\eta_{k})\sum\limits_{A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})}\chi(A)\left[\left(\frac{\operatorname{vol}(K_{A,W})}{4}\right)\Phi(f_{0,A},\mathfrak{G}(W_{A}))+\operatorname{CT}\langle\langle f_{0,A}^{+}(\tau),\theta_{L_{A,W}^{\perp}}(\tau)\otimes\mathcal{E}_{L_{A,W}}(\tau)\rangle\rangle\right]

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})}\chi(A)L^{\star\prime}(0,g_{\phi,A}\times\theta_{L_{A,W}^{\perp}})=\Lambda^{\prime}(1/2,\phi\times\theta(\chi))=\Lambda^{\prime}(1/2,\phi\times\theta(\chi)).

∎

Now, using the Dirichlet analytic class number formula

(67)

\displaystyle L(1,\eta_{k})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\frac{2\pi h_{k}}{w_{k}\sqrt{|d_{k}|}}&\text{ if $k$ is imaginary quadratic}\\
\frac{2\ln(\varepsilon_{k})h_{k}}{\sqrt{d_{k}}}&\text{ if $k$ is real quadratic}\end{cases}

to evaluate

(68)

\displaystyle\Lambda(1,\eta_{k})=|d_{k}|^{\frac{1}{2}}\Gamma_{\bf{R}}(2)L(1,\eta_{k})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\frac{2\pi h_{k}}{w_{k}}&\text{ if $k$ is imaginary quadratic}\\
2\ln(\varepsilon_{k})h_{k}&\text{ if $k$ is real quadratic}\end{cases},

we can simplify the formulae of Theorem
6.7
. Here, we observe that each of the compact open subgroups

K_{A,0}\subset T_{A,0}({\bf{A}}_{f})
and
K_{A,W}\in T_{A,W}({\bf{A}}_{f})
must be the maximal compact group
\mathcal{O}_{k}^{\times}
.
We then calculate
\operatorname{vol}(K_{A,0})=w_{k}/h_{k}
and
\operatorname{vol}(K_{A,W})=(w_{k}\ln(\varepsilon_{k}))/h_{k}

for each class
A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})
using Lemma
5.8
to obtain

Corollary 6.8
.

We have the following identities for the central derivative value
\Lambda^{\prime}(1/2,\phi\times\theta(\chi))
.

(i)

If
k
is imaginary quadratic and
\eta_{k}(-N)=-\eta_{k}(N)=-1
, then

\displaystyle\Lambda^{\prime}(1/2,\phi\times\theta(\chi))

\displaystyle=-\frac{2\pi h_{k}}{w_{k}}\sum\limits_{A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})}\chi(A)\left[\left(\frac{\operatorname{vol}(K_{A,0})}{4}\right)\Phi(f_{0,A},Z(V_{A,0}))+\operatorname{CT}\langle\langle f_{0,A}^{+}(\tau),\theta_{L_{A,0}^{\perp}}(\tau)\otimes\mathcal{E}_{L_{A,0}}(\tau)\rangle\rangle\right].

(ii)

If
k
is real quadratic and
\eta_{k}(-N)=\eta_{k}(N)=-1
, then

\displaystyle\Lambda^{\prime}(1/2,\phi\times\theta(\chi))

\displaystyle=-2\ln(\varepsilon_{k})h_{k}\sum\limits_{A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})}\chi(A)\left[\left(\frac{\operatorname{vol}(K_{A,W})}{4}\right)\Phi(f_{0,A},\mathfrak{G}(W_{A}))+\operatorname{CT}\langle\langle f_{0,A}^{+}(\tau),\theta_{L_{A,W}^{\perp}}(\tau)\otimes\mathcal{E}_{L_{A,W}}(\tau)\rangle\rangle\right].

7.
Arithmetic implications

We now explain how to compute the Faltings heights of arithmetic divisors
Z(f)
along zero cycles to prove higher Gross-Zagier formulae.
We also explain how to derive a new proof/variant of the theorem of Gross-Zagier
[
26
,
\lx@sectionsign
I. (6.3)]
in terms of arithmetic Hirzebruch-Zagier
divisors on the Hilbert modular surface
Y_{0}(N)\times Y_{0}(N)
. We also explain some applications to the refined conjecture of Birch-Swinnerton-Dyer.

7.1.
Arithmetic heights and higher Gross-Zagier formulae

Let us first explain how to derive from the theorems of Bruinier-Yang
[
13
, Theorem 4.7]
– as presented in Theorem
5.12

above – and Andreatta-Goren-Howard-Madapusi Pera
[
1
, Theorem A]
the following “higher Gross-Zagier formula”,
relating the central derivative values
L^{\prime}(0,\xi_{1-\frac{n}{2}}(f)\times\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}})
of the Rankin-Selberg
L
-functions

\displaystyle L(s,\xi_{1-\frac{n}{2}}(f)\times\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}})=\langle\xi_{1-\frac{n}{2}}(f)(\tau),\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}}(\tau)\otimes E_{L_{0}}(\tau,s;1)\rangle,\quad\quad f\in H_{1-\frac{1}{2}}(\omega_{L})

to Faltings heights of arithmetic divisors
\widehat{Z}(f)=(Z(f),\Phi(f,\cdot))
along the CM cycles

Z(V_{0})
on the spin Shimura variety
X=X_{K}=X_{K_{L}}
introduced in
(\ref{complexpoints})
and
(\ref{CMcycle})
above.

7.1.1.
Extension to integral models

Let us henceforth fix the level structure
K=K_{L}\subset\operatorname{GSpin}(V)({\bf{A}}_{f})
associated to a choice of lattice

L\subset V
in the quadratic space
(V,Q)
, and simply write
X=X_{K}
for the corresponding spin Shimura variety.
Hence, the orbifold
X({\bf{C}})=X_{K}({\bf{C}})
describes the set of complex points of a quasi-projective Shimura variety
X
over
{\bf{Q}}

of dimension
n
. As explained in
[
1
]
,
X({\bf{C}})
can be viewed as the space of complex points of an algebraic
Mumford-Deligne stack
X\longrightarrow\operatorname{Spec}({\bf{Q}})
. In general, apart from some cases of small dimension (
n\leq 3
),
the Shimura variety
X
is not of PEL type, and hence does not generally represent a moduli space of abelian variety with PEL structure.
it is however of Hodge type, and so the theorems of Kisin
[
37
]
, Madapusi Pera
[
42
]
, and Kim-Madapusi Pera
[
36
]
apply to
show the existence of a regular, flat integral model
\mathcal{X}\longrightarrow\operatorname{Spec}({\bf{Z}})
.

Recall from
(\ref{special})
that we have for each coset
\mu\in L^{\vee}/L
and rational number
m\in{\bf{Q}}
for which the quadric

\displaystyle\Omega_{m}({\bf{Q}})

\displaystyle=\left\{x\in V:Q(x)=m\right\}

is nonempty the special divisor
Z(\mu,m)\rightarrow X
defined by the sum

\displaystyle Z(\mu,m)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{x\in\left(\operatorname{GSpin}(V)({\bf{Q}})\cap K\right)\backslash\Omega_{m}({\bf{Q}})}{\bf{1}}_{\mu}(x)\operatorname{pr}(D(V)_{x}).

As explained in
[
1
]
, each of these special divisors admits an extension
\mathcal{Z}(\mu,m)\longrightarrow\mathcal{X}
to the integral model.
Roughly speaking, this is obtained as follows through the Kuga-Satake abelian scheme
\mathcal{A}\longrightarrow\mathcal{X}
.
That is, the Shimura variety
X=X_{K}
comes equipped with a family of Kuga-Satake abelian varieties
A_{z}\longrightarrow X
indexed by points
z\in D(V)
.
To describe the construction of such an abelian variety
A_{z}\longrightarrow X
, consider that we have a natural functor

\displaystyle\left\{\text{algebraic representations of $\operatorname{GSpin}(V)$}\right\}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\left\{\text{local systems of ${\bf{Q}}$-vectorspaces on $X({\bf{C}})$}\right\}

\displaystyle(\operatorname{GSpin}(V)\rightarrow\operatorname{GL}(W))

\displaystyle\longmapsto(W_{\operatorname{Betti},{\bf{Q}}}\rightarrow X({\bf{C}}))

where

\displaystyle W_{\operatorname{Betti},{\bf{Q}}}

\displaystyle:=\operatorname{GSpin}(V)({\bf{Q}})\backslash\left(W\times D(V)\right)\times\operatorname{GSpin}({\bf{A}}_{f})/K.

This allows us to associate to each algebraic representation

\operatorname{GSpin}(V)\rightarrow\operatorname{GL}(W)
a pair

(W_{\operatorname{dR}},\nabla)
consisting of a locally free
\mathcal{O}_{X({\bf{C}})}
-module

W_{\operatorname{dR}}=W_{\operatorname{Betti},{\bf{Q}}}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X({\bf{C}})}
and
a connection
\nabla=1\otimes d
. Each such pair can be viewed as a vector bundle
W_{\operatorname{dR}}

with integrable connection
\nabla
such that
W_{\operatorname{dR}}^{\nabla=0}=W_{\operatorname{Betti},{\bf{Q}}}\otimes_{\bf{Q}}{\bf{C}}
and:

(i)

For all
z\in D(V)
, the map
h_{z}:{\bf{S}}\longrightarrow\operatorname{GSpin}(V)({\bf{R}})\longrightarrow\operatorname{GL}(W_{\bf{R}})

induces a map
{\bf{S}}({\bf{C}})\rightarrow\operatorname{GL}(W_{\bf{C}})
.

(ii)

The fibre
W_{\operatorname{dR},z}
at
z\in D(V)
has a bigradation
W_{\operatorname{dR},z}=\bigoplus_{p,q}W_{\operatorname{dR},z}^{p,q}

induced by the action of
{\bf{S}}({\bf{C}})
.

(iii)

The
\mathcal{O}_{X({\bf{C}})}
-module
W_{\operatorname{dR}}
is endowed with a
decreasing filtration
\operatorname{Fil}_{J}(W_{\operatorname{dR}})\subseteq W_{\operatorname{dR}}

of submodules, defined pointwise by

\operatorname{Fil}^{J}(W_{\operatorname{dR},z})=\bigoplus_{p\geq J}W_{\operatorname{dR},z}^{p,q}
.

Here, have have natural identifications
{\bf{S}}({\bf{C}})={\bf{C}}^{\times}\times{\bf{C}}^{\times}

and
\operatorname{GL}(W_{\bf{C}})=\operatorname{GL}(W_{\bf{Q}}\otimes{\bf{C}})

Now, consider the representation of the Clifford algebra
C(V)
on
\operatorname{GSpin}(V)
induced by the inclusion

\displaystyle\operatorname{GSpin}(V)\subset C^{0}(V)^{\times}:=C^{0}(V)\backslash\{0\},

with action given by left multiplication of
C^{0}(V)^{\times}
on
\operatorname{GSpin}(V)
. The corresponding vector bundle

C(V)_{\operatorname{dR}}
gives rise to the following variation of Hodge structures: For each
z\in D(V)
, we have

(69)

\displaystyle C(V)_{\operatorname{dR},z}

\displaystyle=C(V)_{z}^{-1,0}\oplus C(V)_{z}^{0,-1}.

Note that having such a variation of Hodge structures
(\ref{VHS})
for each
z\in D(V)
is equivalent to
having a complex structure on the Clifford algebra
C(V_{\bf{R}})=C(V_{\bf{Q}}\otimes{\bf{R}})
for each
z\in D(V)
.
In particular, we obtain from this complex structure for each
z\in D(V)
a corresponding abelian variety

\displaystyle A_{z}

\displaystyle=C(V_{\bf{R}})/C(L)

of dimension
2^{n+1}
known as the
Kuga-Satake abelian variety associated to
X=X_{K_{L}}
at a point
z\in D(V)
.
As explained in
[
1
]
, this construction extends
11
11

11

They also show that the associated vector bundle with
connection
(C(V)_{\operatorname{dR}},\nabla)
extends to the integral model
\mathcal{X}
, with vector bundle
C(V)_{\operatorname{dR}}

given by the relative de Rham cohomology
H^{1}_{\operatorname{dR}}(\mathcal{A})
, connection
\nabla
given by the Gauss-Manin
connection, and filtration
\operatorname{Fil}^{J}(C(V)_{\operatorname{dR},z})
given by the Hodge filtration

0\longrightarrow R_{!}\pi_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{A}})\longrightarrow H^{1}_{\operatorname{dR}}(\mathcal{A})\longrightarrow\pi_{*}(\Omega_{\mathcal{A}}^{1})\longrightarrow 0
.
to give an abelian scheme
\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{X}
.

Example 7.1
.

Suppose we consider a rational quadratic space
(V_{0},Q_{0})
of signature
(0,2)
with integral lattice
L_{0}\subset V_{0}
.
In this case, the submodule
C^{0}(L_{0})\subset C^{0}(V_{0})
corresponds to an order
\mathcal{O}\subset\mathcal{O}_{k(V_{0})}
of the imaginary quadratic
field
k(V_{0})
associated to
V_{0}
, as described above. Each point
z\in D(V_{0})
determines a Kuga-Satake abelian surface

A_{z}=A_{z}^{+}\times A_{z}^{-}=C(V_{0}({\bf{R}}))/C(L_{0})
, where
A_{z}^{+}
is an elliptic curve with complex multiplication by the order

\mathcal{O}\cong C^{0}(V_{0})
, and
A_{z}
is the elliptic curve with CM by
\mathcal{O}
given by

A_{z}^{-}=A_{z}^{+}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}L_{0}=A_{z}^{+}\otimes_{C^{0}(L_{0})}L_{0}
.

Example 7.2
.

Consider the rational quadratic space
(V,Q)
of signature
(1,2)

given by
V=M_{2}^{\operatorname{tr}=0}({\bf{Q}})
and
Q(\cdot)=N\det(\cdot)
.
As explained in
[
13
,
\lx@sectionsign
7.3]
and Appendix A below, we have an accidental isomorphism

\operatorname{GSpin}(V)\cong\operatorname{GL}_{2}
of algebraic groups over
{\bf{Q}}
.
Let
L\subset V
denote the lattice

\displaystyle L

\displaystyle=\left\{\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}b&-a/N\\
c&-b\end{array}\right):a,b,c\in{\bf{Z}}\right\}

with dual lattice

\displaystyle L^{\vee}

\displaystyle=\left\{\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}b/2n&-a/N\\
c&-b/2N\end{array}\right):a,b,c\in{\bf{Z}}\right\},

so that the discriminant group can be identified as

\displaystyle{\bf{Z}}/2N{\bf{Z}}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow L^{\vee}/L,\quad r\longmapsto\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}r/2N&\\
&-r/2N\end{array}\right).

Hence,
L
has level
4N
, and the corresponding quadric

\displaystyle\Omega_{\mu,m}({\bf{Q}})

\displaystyle:=\left\{x\in\mu+L:Q(x)=m\right\}

is nonempty unless
Q(\mu)\equiv m\bmod 1
. The corresponding compact open subgroup
K=K_{L}
is given by

\displaystyle K=\prod_{p<\infty}K_{p}\subset\operatorname{GSpin}(V)({\bf{A}}_{f})\cong\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\bf{Q}}_{p}),\quad K_{p}:=\left\{\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}a&b\\
c&d\end{array}\right)\in\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\bf{Z}}_{p}):c\in N{\bf{Z}}_{p}\right\}.

In this setting, we have an isomorphism of Shimura varieties

\displaystyle Y_{0}(N):=\Gamma_{0}(N)\backslash\mathfrak{H}

\displaystyle\cong X_{K}({\bf{C}}),\quad\Gamma_{0}(N)z\longmapsto\operatorname{GSpin}(V)({\bf{Q}})(z,1)K.

In the other direction, using the moduli description of the noncompactified modular curve
Y_{0}(N)
, we have

\displaystyle X_{K}({\bf{C}}):=\operatorname{GSpin}(V)({\bf{Q}})\backslash D(V)\times\operatorname{GSpin}(V)({\bf{A}}_{f})/K\cong Y_{0}(N),\quad z\longmapsto(E_{z}\rightarrow E_{z}^{\prime})

for
(E_{z},E_{z}^{\prime})
a pair of elliptic curves with CM by some order
\mathcal{O}\subset\mathcal{O}_{k(V_{0})}

in the imaginary quadrtic field
k(V_{0})
determined by a negative definite subspace
V_{0}\subset V
(given by a Heegner embedding).
In this case, each point
z\in D(V)=D^{\pm}(V)\cong\mathfrak{H}
has the corresponding Kuga-Satake abelian fourfold
A_{z}=C(V({\bf{R}}))/C(L)
given by

\displaystyle A_{z}=A_{z}^{+}\times A_{z}^{-},\quad\quad A_{z}^{+}=A_{z}^{-}=E_{z}\times E_{z}^{\prime}.

We shall return to this example in Appendix A below to explain how to recover the formula of Gross-Zagier
[
26
, Theorem I (6.3)]

from
[
13
, Theorem 4.7, Theorem 7.7]
and
[
1
, Theorem A]
.

Now, the Kuga-Satake abelian scheme
\mathcal{A}\longrightarrow\mathcal{X}
comes equipped with an action of the Clifford algebra

C(L)=C^{0}(L)\oplus C^{1}(L)
, and acquires from this a
{\bf{Z}}/2{\bf{Z}}
-grading
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}^{+}\times\mathcal{A}^{-}
.
For each scheme
S\longrightarrow\mathcal{X}
, we can associate to the pullback

\mathcal{A}_{S}
a distinguished
{\bf{Z}}
-module of
special endomorphisms

\displaystyle V(\mathcal{A}_{S})\subset\operatorname{End}(\mathcal{A}_{S}),

together with an associated quadratic form
q:V(\mathcal{A}_{S})\longrightarrow{\bf{Z}}
defined by
x\circ x=q(x)\cdot\operatorname{id}
.
More generally, for each coset
\mu\in L^{\vee}/L
, we can associate to the pullback
\mathcal{A}_{S}
a distinguished
subset
V_{\mu}(\mathcal{A}_{S})\subset V(\mathcal{A}_{S})\otimes{\bf{Q}}
with the property that
V_{0}(\mathcal{A}_{S})=V(\mathcal{A}_{S})
.

As explained in
[
1
]
, we can define from each of these subsets
V_{\mu}(\mathcal{A}_{S})
a divisor on
\mathcal{X}
as follows:
For each coset
\mu\in L^{\vee}/L
and rational number
m\in{\bf{Q}}
, let

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(\mu,m)\longrightarrow\mathcal{X}

denote the moduli stack that assigns to each
\mathcal{X}
-scheme
S\longrightarrow\mathcal{X}
the set

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(\mu,m)(S)

\displaystyle:=\left\{x\in V_{\mu}(\mathcal{A}_{S}):q(x)=m\right\}.

This morphism
\mathcal{Z}(\mu,m)\longrightarrow\mathcal{X}
turns out to be finite and relatively representable,
and to determine a Cartier divisor on
\mathcal{X}
. It agrees on the generic fibre with the special cycles
(\ref{special})

defined above,
\mathcal{Z}(\mu,m)({\bf{C}})=Z(\mu,m)
.

Recall that we write
T_{0}=\operatorname{GSpin}(V_{0})=\operatorname{Res}_{k(V_{0})/{\bf{Q}}}{\bf{G}}_{m}
to denote the
torus corresponding to a rational quadratic subspace
(V_{0},Q_{0})
of signature
(0,2)
and associated imaginary quadratic field
k(V_{0})
.
We consider the corresponding zero-dimensional Shimura variety
Z(V_{0})\longrightarrow\operatorname{Spec}(k(V_{0}))
with complex points
given by
(\ref{CMcycle})
. Note that we can identify the corresponding compact open subgroup
K_{0}:=K\cap T_{0}({\bf{A}}_{f})
with

K_{0}=\widehat{O}_{k(V_{0})}^{\times}
, and that this acts trivially on the discriminant group
L_{0}^{\vee}/L_{0}
. Hence, we can identify the complex points

\displaystyle Z(V_{0})({\bf{C}})

\displaystyle=k(V_{0})^{\times}\backslash\{z_{V_{0}}^{\pm}\}\times{\bf{A}}_{k(V_{0}),f}^{\times}/\widehat{O}_{k(V_{0})}^{\times}

with two copies of the ideal class group
C(\mathcal{O}_{k(V_{0})})
. Observe that by Lemma
5.8
(cf.
[
13
, Lemma 6.3]
)
with the Dirichlet analytic class number formula
(\ref{Dirichlet})
, we have that the degree
\operatorname{deg}Z(V_{0})=4/\operatorname{vol}(K_{0})

of
Z(V_{0})
as defined in Lemma
5.9
(i) is given by the relation

\displaystyle\frac{\operatorname{deg}Z(V_{0})}{4}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\operatorname{vol}(K_{0})}=\frac{h_{k(V_{0})}}{w_{k(V_{0})}}=\frac{|d_{k(V_{0})}|^{\frac{1}{2}}}{2\pi}\cdot L(1,\eta_{k(V_{0})}).

Viewing
Z(V_{0})\longrightarrow\operatorname{Spec}(k(V_{0}))
as the moduli space of elliptic curves with complex
multiplication by
\mathcal{O}_{k(V_{0})}
, we obtain a smooth integral model
\mathcal{Z}(V_{0})\longrightarrow\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{O}_{k(V_{0})})
.
As explained in
[
1
]
, if the imaginary quadratic field
k=k(V_{0})
has odd discriminant
d_{k(V_{0})}
,
then the embedding of reductive groups
T_{0}\subset\operatorname{GSpin}(V)
induced by the embedding of quadratic spaces
V_{0}\subset V

gives a finite, relatively representable, umramified morphism

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(V_{0})\longrightarrow\mathcal{X}.

This algebraic stack has its own Kuga-Satake abelian scheme
\mathcal{A}_{0}\longrightarrow\mathcal{Z}(V_{0})

equipped with an action of the Clifford algebra
C(L_{0})=C^{0}(L_{0})\oplus C^{1}(L_{0})
and hence a
{\bf{Z}}/2{\bf{Z}}
-grading

\mathcal{A}_{0}\cong\mathcal{A}_{0}^{+}\times\mathcal{A}_{0}^{-}
. Here,
\mathcal{A}_{0}^{+}
can be identified with the universal
elliptic curve with CM by
\mathcal{O}_{k(V_{0})}
, with
\mathcal{A}_{0}\cong\mathcal{A}_{0}^{+}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{k(V_{0})}}C(L_{0})

and
\mathcal{A}_{0}^{-}\cong\mathcal{A}_{0}^{+}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}(k(V_{0}))}L_{0}
. Moreover, this Kuga-Satake abelian scheme

\mathcal{A}_{0}\longrightarrow\mathcal{Z}(V_{0})
is related to the Kuga-Satake abelian scheme
\mathcal{A}\longrightarrow\mathcal{X}

by a
C(L)
-linear isomorphism

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}|_{\mathcal{Z}(V_{0})}\cong\mathcal{A}_{0}|_{\mathcal{Z}(L_{0})}\otimes_{C(L_{0})}C(L).

7.1.2.
Arithmetic degrees along CM cycles and central derivative Rankin-Selberg
L
-values

Recall we saw in Theorem
5.12
above that we have for any harmonic weak Maass form
f\in H_{1-\frac{n}{2}}(\omega_{L})
the formula

(70)

\displaystyle\Phi(f,Z(V_{0}))

\displaystyle=-\operatorname{deg}(Z(V_{0}))\cdot\left(\operatorname{CT}\langle\langle f^{+}(\tau),\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}}(\tau)\otimes\mathcal{E}_{L_{0}}(\tau)\rangle\rangle+L^{\prime}(0,\xi_{1-n/2}(f)\times\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}}\right).

We can now describe this formula in terms of arithmetic heights, according to the calculations of
[
13
,
\lx@sectionsign
5-6]

and more generally
[
1
, Theorem A]
, which we now summarize. Recall that an arithmetic divisor

\widehat{x}=(x,G_{x})
on the integral model
\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\operatorname{Spec}({\bf{Z}})
consists
of a divisor
x
on
\mathcal{X}
and a corresponding Green’s function
G_{x}
for the the divisor
x({\bf{C}})

induced by
x
on the complex variety
\mathcal{X}({\bf{C}})=X({\bf{C}})
. That is, the
G_{x}
is a smooth
function on
\mathcal{X}({\bf{C}})\backslash x({\bf{C}})
with a logarithmic singularity along
x({\bf{C}})

which satisfies the Green’s current equation

\displaystyle dd^{c}[G_{x}]+\delta_{x({\bf{C}})}=[\Omega_{x}]

for some smooth
(1,1)
-form
\Omega_{x}
on
\mathcal{X}({\bf{C}})
.
Let
\widehat{\operatorname{Ch}}^{1}(\mathcal{X})
denote the first arithmetic Chow group of
\mathcal{X}
,
so the free abelian group generated by arithmetic divisors on
\mathcal{X}
modulo rational equivalence. Let

\displaystyle\left[\cdot,\cdot\right]:\widehat{\operatorname{Ch}}^{1}(\mathcal{X})\times Z^{n}(\mathcal{X})\longrightarrow{\bf{R}}

denote the height pairing defined in Bost-Gillet-Soulé
[
4
,
\lx@sectionsign
2.3]
.
Given an arithmetic divisor
\widehat{x}\in\widehat{\operatorname{Ch}}^{1}(\mathcal{X})

and an
n
-cycle
y\in Z^{n}(\mathcal{X})
intersecting properly, we know that this pairing is given by the Faltings height

\displaystyle\left[\widehat{x},y\right]=\left[\widehat{x},y\right]_{\operatorname{Fal}}

\displaystyle=\left[x,y\right]_{\operatorname{fin}}+\left[\widehat{x},y\right]_{\infty}.

Here, the archimedean component is given by half the value of the Green function
G_{x}
along
y({\bf{C}})
,

\displaystyle\left[\widehat{x},y\right]_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\cdot G_{x}\left(y({\bf{C}})\right).

The setup we consider above with regularized theta lifts
\Phi(f)=\Phi(f,\cdot)
is relevant here as it provides us with a
supply of such arithmetic divisors
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}(f)=(\mathcal{Z}(f),\Phi(f))
on the integral model

\mathcal{X}\longrightarrow\operatorname{Spec}({\bf{Z}})
.
To be more precise, each CM-cycle
\mathcal{Z}(V_{0})\longrightarrow\mathcal{X}_{\mathcal{O}_{k(V_{0})}}

associated to a rational quadratic subspace
V_{0}\subset V
of signature
(0,2)

provides us with a zero-cycle
y\in Z^{0}(\mathcal{X})
which intersects
\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}(f)=(\mathcal{Z}(f),\Phi(f))
properly.
In particular, this allows us to reinterpret
(\ref{BYf})
in terms of the archimedean local height as

(71)

\displaystyle\left[\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}(f),\mathcal{Z}(V_{0})\right]_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\Phi(f,Y)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\deg(Z(V_{0}))}{2}\cdot\left(\operatorname{CT}\langle\langle f^{+}(\tau),\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}}(\tau)\otimes\mathcal{E}_{L_{0}}(\tau)\rangle\rangle+L^{\prime}(0,\xi_{1-n/2}(f),\theta_{\Lambda})\right).

Remark 7.3
.

When the Shimura variety
X=X_{K}
is not compact, we can add suitable boundary components

\mathcal{C}(f)
to the divisor
\mathcal{Z}(f)
as in
(\ref{Z^c(f)})
to get an arithmetic divisor

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{c}(f)=(\mathcal{Z}^{c}(f),\Phi)\in\widehat{\operatorname{Ch}}^{1}(\mathcal{X}^{\star})

on the integral model
\mathcal{X}^{\star}
of the compactification
X^{\star}
. See
[
13
,
\lx@sectionsign
5-7]
for more details.
When
f\in H_{1-n/2}(\omega_{L})
is not cuspidal, we also have to work with generalized arithmetic Chow groups in the sense of
[
17
]
.

Suppose now that
f=f^{+}+f^{-}\in H_{1-n/2}(\omega_{L})
has integral holomorphic part
f^{+}
, so that the Fourier coefficients

c_{f}^{+}(\mu,m)
are integers for all
m\in{\bf{Q}}
and
\mu\in L^{\vee}/L
. Then,

\displaystyle Z(f)=\sum\limits_{\mu\in L^{\vee}/L}\sum\limits_{m\in{\bf{Q}}\atop m>0}c_{f}^{+}(\mu,-m)Z(\mu,m)

determines a divisor on
X
, with extension

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(f)=\sum\limits_{\mu\in L^{\vee}/L}\sum\limits_{m\in{\bf{Q}}\atop m>0}c_{f}^{+}(\mu,-m)\mathcal{Z}(\mu,m)

to a Cartier divisor on the integral model
\mathcal{X}
, and with corresponding arithmetic divisor

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}(f)=(\mathcal{Z}(f),G_{\mathcal{Z}(f)})\in\widehat{\operatorname{Ch}}^{1}(\mathcal{X}).

If
f\in\operatorname{ker}(\xi_{1-n/2})\cong M_{1-n/2}^{!}(\omega_{L})
is weakly holomorphic,
then we expect
\widehat{Z}(f)=(\mathcal{Z}(f),\Phi(f,\cdot))
to be rationally equivalent to a torsion element,
by the relation given by the Borcherds lift
\Psi(f,\cdot)
described in
(\ref{BF})
above (Theorem
4.1
).
If this rational equivalence to zero were known to be true, we would derive the
corresponding vanishing of the Faltings height the relation

(72)

\displaystyle\left[\widehat{Z}(f),\mathcal{Z}(V_{0})\right]=\left[\mathcal{Z}(f),\mathcal{Z}(V_{0})\right]_{\operatorname{fin}}+\frac{1}{2}\cdot\Phi(f,Z(V_{0}))=0,

from which it would follow that the nonarchimedean height pairing is given by the constant coefficient term

(73)

\displaystyle\left[\mathcal{Z}(f),\mathcal{Z}(V_{0})\right]_{\operatorname{fin}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\deg(V_{0})}{2}\cdot\operatorname{CT}\langle\langle f(\tau),\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}}(\tau)\otimes\mathcal{E}_{L_{0}}(\tau)\rangle\rangle.

Expanding out both sides of this relation
(\ref{relation})
leads to the following expectation for the general case.

Conjecture 7.4

(Bruinier-Yang)
.

Let
f=f^{+}+f^{-}\in H_{1-n/2}(\omega_{L})
be any weakly harmonic Maass form whose holomorphic part has integral Fourier coefficients,

\displaystyle f^{+}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\mu\in L^{\vee}/L}\sum\limits_{m\in{\bf{Q}}\atop m\gg-\infty}c^{+}_{f}(\mu,m)e(m\tau){\bf{1}}_{\mu},\quad c^{+}_{f}(\mu,m)\in{\bf{Z}}.

We have for each coset
\mu\in L^{\vee}/L
and each positive rational
m\in Q(\mu)+{\bf{Z}}
that the nonarchimedean
local height
\left[\mathcal{Z}(\mu,m),\mathcal{Y}\right]_{\operatorname{fin}}
is given by the
(\mu,m)
-th Fourier coefficient
of the modular form
\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}}(\tau)\otimes\mathcal{E}_{L_{0}}(\tau)
,

\displaystyle\left[\mathcal{Z}(\mu,m),\mathcal{Z}(V_{0})\right]_{\operatorname{fin}}

\displaystyle=-\sum\limits_{\mu_{1}\in(L_{0}^{\perp})^{\vee}/L_{0}^{\perp},\mu_{2}\in L_{0}^{\vee}/L_{0}\atop\mu_{1}+\mu_{2}\equiv\mu\bmod L}\sum\limits_{m_{1},m_{2}\in{\bf{Q}}_{\geq 0}\atop m_{1}+m_{2}=m}r_{L_{0}^{\perp}}(\mu_{1},m_{1})\kappa_{L_{0}}(\mu_{2},m_{2}).

Putting this into
(\ref{BYf})
(Theorem
5.12
), taking the sum over
m>0
, we obtain the arithmetic height formula

(74)

\displaystyle\left[\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}(f),\mathcal{Z}(V_{0})\right]=\left[\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}(f),\mathcal{Z}(V_{0})\right]_{\operatorname{Fal}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\deg(Z(V_{0}))}{2}\cdot\left(c_{f}^{+}(0,0)\cdot\kappa_{L_{0}}(0,0)+L^{\prime}(0,\xi_{1-n/2}(f),\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}})\right).

Theorem 7.5

(Bruinier-Yang, Andreatta-Goren-Howard-Madapusi Pera)
.

Let
(V_{0},Q_{0})=(\mathfrak{a}_{\bf{Q}},-Q_{\mathfrak{a}}(\cdot))
be a rational quadratic subspace of signature
(0,2)

given by a fractional ideal
\mathfrak{a}_{\bf{Q}}
in an imaginary quadratic field
k(V_{0})
of odd discrimant
d_{k(V_{0})}

determined by a nonzero integral ideal
\mathfrak{a}\subset\mathcal{O}_{k(V_{0})}
. Let
L_{0}=\mathfrak{a}
denote the corresponding lattice.
Assume that the even part
C^{0}(L_{0})
of the Clifford algebra
C(L_{0})
is identified with the maximal order
\mathcal{O}_{k(V_{0})}\cong C^{0}(V_{0})
.
Then, Conjecture
7.4
is true. In particular, the arithmetic height formula
(\ref{AHF})
is true.
Equivalently, writing
\widehat{\bf{T}}\in\widehat{\operatorname{Ch}}^{1}(\mathcal{X})

to denote the metrized cotautological defined in
[
1
,
\lx@sectionsign
5.3]
,

\displaystyle\left[\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}(f):\mathcal{Z}(V_{0})\right]+c_{f}^{+}(0,0)\cdot\left[\widehat{\bf{T}}:\mathcal{Z}(f)\right]

\displaystyle=-\frac{h_{k}}{w_{k}}\cdot L^{\prime}(0,\xi_{1-n/2}(f)\times\theta_{L_{0}^{\perp}}).

Proof.

This follows from the combined results of
[
13
, Theorem 1.2]
and
[
1
, Theorem A, Theorem 5.7.3]
. ∎

Remark 7.6
.

Note that Conjecture
7.4
is not yet established in general; see
[
13
, Conjectures 5.1 and 5.2]
.
That is, the conjecture is posed more generally for
(V_{0},Q_{0})
any negative definite quadratic subspace of signature
(0,2)
.
In particular, it should be possible to take
(V_{0},Q_{0})=(\mathfrak{a},-Q_{\mathfrak{a}}(\cdot))
with
C^{+}(L_{0})\cong\mathcal{O}

any (non-maximal) order
\mathcal{O}\subset\mathcal{O}_{k(V_{0})}
, and without any condition on the partity of the discriminant
d_{k(V_{0})}
.

7.2.
Gross-Zagier via special (Hirzebruch-Zagier) divisors on
X_{0}(N)\times X_{0}(N)

We now return to the quadratic spaces
(V_{A},Q_{A})
of signature
(2,2)
parametrizing
X_{K_{A}}=Y_{0}(N)\times Y_{0}(N)
.
Here, we give a geometric interpretation of the formulae of Theorem
6.7
and Corollary
6.8
above.
In case (i) where
k
is imaginary quadratic, this will give a new proof of the formula of Gross and Zagier
[
26
, I Theorem (6.3)]
,
including a comparison of the arithmetic heights of Heegner divisorss on
X_{0}(N)
and the corresponding arithmetic heights
of Hirzebruch-Zagier divisors on
X_{0}(N)\times X_{0}(N)
. For the convenience of the reader, we explain in Appendix A how
the Gross-Zagier formula
[
26
, Theorem I (6.3)]
can be derived by a variation of the proof of Bruinier-Yang
[
13
, Theorem 7.7]
,
developing Theorem
7.5
for the special case of signature
(1,2)
described in Example
7.2
above.
Here, we give a distinct deduction of the formula via the Hilbert modular surfaces
Y_{0}(N)\times Y_{0}(N)
and
X_{0}(N)\times X_{0}(N)
.

For each class
A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})
, recall that we fix a representative
\mathfrak{a}\subset\mathcal{O}_{k}
,
and consider the quadratic space
(V_{A},Q_{A})
of signature (2,2) defined by

V_{A}=\mathfrak{a}_{\bf{Q}}\oplus\mathfrak{a}_{\bf{Q}}
and quadratic form
Q_{A}(z_{1},z_{2})=Q_{\mathfrak{a}}(z_{1})-Q_{\mathfrak{a}}(z_{2})

(for
Q_{\mathfrak{a}}(z):={\bf{N}}_{k/{\bf{Q}}}(z)/{\bf{N}}\mathfrak{a}
the norm form),
so that
(V_{A,0},Q_{A,0})=(\mathfrak{a},-Q_{\mathfrak{a}})
determines a rational quadratic space of signature
(0,2)
.
Recall that we have an accidental isomorphism
\operatorname{GSpin}(V_{A})\cong\operatorname{GL}_{2}^{2}
of algebraic
groups over
{\bf{Q}}
by Proposition
2.3
, and that we take
L_{A}\subset V_{A}
to be the integral lattice
corresponding to the compact open subgroup
K_{A}=K_{L_{A}}\cong K_{0}(N)^{2}\subset\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\widehat{\bf{Z}})^{2}

so that
X_{A}\cong Y_{0}(N)\times Y_{0}(N)
as in Corollary
2.4
. Hence, we consider the corresponding integral model

\mathcal{X}_{A}\cong\mathcal{Y}_{0}(N)\times\mathcal{Y}_{0}(N)
.
Let us fix a compactification
X_{A}^{\star}\cong X_{0}(N)\times X_{0}(N)
(see e.g.
[
7
,
\lx@sectionsign\lx@sectionsign
1.2 and 2.4]
),
so that we can identify the corresponding integral model
\mathcal{X}_{A}^{\star}
with
\mathcal{X}_{0}(N)\times\mathcal{X}_{0}(N)
.

Remark 7.7
.

Note that we have the following moduli descriptions of these Hilbert modular surfaces and their special divisors.
Recall that the noncompactified modular curve
Y_{0}(N)
has the following moduli description (see e.g.
[
26
]
).
For any scheme
S
over
Q
,
Y_{0}(N)(S)
represents the isomorphism class of triples
(E,E^{\prime},\varphi)

consisting of a pair of elliptic curves
E/S
,
E^{\prime}/S
and an isogeny
\varphi:E\rightarrow E^{\prime}
of degree
N
.
Hence,
\varphi
is finite flat of degree
N
, and its kernel
\ker(\varphi)\cong{\bf{Z}}/N{\bf{Z}}
is a finite locally free group scheme over
S
.
The compactified modular curve
X_{0}(N)(S)
represents triples
(E,E^{\prime},\varphi)
of generalized elliptic curves
E/S
,
E^{\prime}/S
and a cyclic
isogeny
\varphi:E\rightarrow E^{\prime}
of degree
N
. We use this to deduce that
X_{A}(S)=Y_{0}(N)(S)\times Y_{0}(N)(S)
represents
pairs of triples
(E_{1},E_{1}^{\prime},\varphi_{1})
,
(E_{2},E_{2}^{\prime},\varphi_{2})
. More precisely,
Y_{0}(N)(S)\times Y_{0}(N)(S)
represents the
moduli space of triples
{\bf{A}}=(A,\kappa_{A}(\varphi),\lambda_{A})
made up of the abelian surface
A=E_{1}\times E_{2}
, the endomorphism

\kappa_{A}(\varphi)
of
A
determined by the isogeny
\varphi=\varphi_{1}\times\varphi_{2}:A\rightarrow A^{\prime}
for
A^{\prime}=E_{1}^{\prime}\times E_{2}^{\prime}

and its dual
\varphi^{\vee}:A^{\prime}\rightarrow A
, and the product principal polarization
\lambda_{A}=\lambda_{E_{1}}\times\lambda_{E_{2}}
.
Similarly,
X_{0}(N)(S)\times X_{0}(N)(S)
represents the
moduli space of triples
{\bf{A}}=(A,\kappa_{A}(\varphi),\lambda_{A})
made up of the abelian surface
A=E_{1}\times E_{2}

with
E_{1}/S
,
E_{2}/S
generalized elliptic curves, special endomorphism
\kappa_{A}(\varphi)\in\operatorname{End}(A)

determined by the isogeny
\varphi:A\rightarrow A^{\prime}
and its dual
\varphi^{\vee}:A^{\prime}\rightarrow A
, and the product principal
polarization
\lambda_{A}
. We can then describe the special arithmetic (Hirzebruch-Zagier) divisor
Z_{A}(\mu,m)
in either
of these spaces as the moduli of triples
(A,\kappa_{A}(x),\lambda_{A})=(E_{1}\times E_{2},\kappa_{E_{1}\times E_{2}}(x),\lambda_{E_{1}}\times\lambda_{E_{2}})

with endomorphism
\kappa_{A}(x)
of degree
\deg(\kappa_{A}(\varphi))=m
supported on
\mu+L_{A}
.
We can also describe the CM cycles
\mathcal{Z}(V_{A,0})
– cf.
[
13
, Proposition 7.2]
,
and the descriptions of Heegner divisors in
[
26
]
and
[
27
]
. We can identify the CM cycles
\mathcal{Z}(V_{A,0})

with Heegner divisors corresponding in the moduli descriptions of
Y_{0}(N)
and
X_{0}(N)
to a triple
(E,E^{\prime},\varphi)
consisting
of elliptic curves
E
and
E^{\prime}
with complex multiplication by
\mathcal{O}_{k}
and a cyclic isogeny
\varphi:E\rightarrow E^{\prime}

of degree
N
annihilated by a primitive ideal of the form
\mathfrak{n}=[N,(r+\sqrt{d_{k}})/2]
. We then deduce that the CM
cycles
\mathcal{Z}(V_{A,0})
we consider will correspond to triples
{\bf{A}}=(A,\kappa_{A}(\varphi),\lambda_{A})
with

A=E\times E
the self-product of the elliptic curve
E
with CM by
\mathcal{O}_{k}
, and
\kappa_{A}(\varphi)
the endomorphism
corresponding to the cyclic isogeny
\varphi:E\rightarrow E^{\prime}
and its dual
\varphi^{\vee}:E^{\prime}\rightarrow E
.
We refer to the discussions in
[
54
]
and
[
30
]
for a more general description of these moduli spaces
of abelian surfaces with special endomorphisms.

As in Remark
7.3
, we extend each arithmetic divisor

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}_{A}(f)=(\mathcal{Z}_{A}(f),\Phi(f,\cdot)),\quad\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}_{A}(\mu,m)=(\mathcal{Z}_{A}(\mu,m),\Phi_{\mu,m}(\cdot))\in\widehat{\operatorname{Ch}}^{1}\left(\mathcal{Y}_{0}(N)\times\mathcal{Y}_{0}(N)\right)

to the compatification

(75)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}_{A}^{c}(f):=(\mathcal{Z}_{A}^{c}(f),\Phi(f,\cdot)),\quad\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}_{A}^{c}(\mu,m):=(\mathcal{Z}_{A}^{c}(\mu,m),\Phi_{\mu,m}(\cdot))\in\widehat{\operatorname{Ch}}^{1}\left(\mathcal{X}_{0}(N)\times\mathcal{X}_{0}(N)\right).

We derive the following consequence of Theorem
7.5
in this setting,
using Proposition
6.6
and Theorem
6.7
.

Theorem 7.8
.

Let
\phi\in S_{2}^{\operatorname{new}}(\Gamma_{0}(N))
be a cuspidal newform of level
N
and trivial character.
Let
k
be an imaginary quadratic field of odd discriminant
d_{k}
and (odd) quadratic Dirichlet character
\eta_{k}(\cdot)=(\frac{d_{k}}{\cdot})
.
Assume that
(N,d_{k})=1
, and that
\eta_{k}(-N)=-\eta_{k}(N)=-1
.
Let
g_{\phi,A}\in S_{2}(\overline{\omega}_{L_{A}})
denote the vector-valued lift of
\phi
described lift in Corollary
6.4
.
Let
f_{0,A}\in H_{0}(\omega_{L_{A}})
be a harmonic weak Maass form of weight zero and representation for which

\displaystyle\xi_{0}(f_{0,A})(\tau)

\displaystyle=g_{\phi,A}(\tau)\in S_{2}(\overline{\omega}_{L_{A}}),

where
\xi_{0}:H_{0}(\omega_{L_{A}})\rightarrow S_{2}(\overline{\omega}_{L_{A}})
denotes the antilinear differential operator defined in
(\ref{xi})
.
Then, for any class group character
\chi\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})^{\vee}
, we have the central derivative value formula

\displaystyle\Lambda^{\prime}(1/2,\phi\times\theta(\chi))

\displaystyle=-2\pi\sum\limits_{A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})}\chi(A)\left[\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}_{A}(f_{0,A}):\mathcal{Z}(V_{A,0})\right]

for the completed Rankin-Selberg
L
-function
\Lambda(s,\phi\times\theta(\chi))
of
\phi
times
the Hecke theta series
\theta(\chi)
, where each term on the right-hand side denotes the arithmetic height of the arithmetic special divisor

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}_{A}(f_{0,A})

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\mu\in L_{A}^{\vee}/L_{A}}\sum\limits_{m\in{\bf{Q}}\atop m>0}c_{f_{0,A}}^{+}(\mu,-m)\mathcal{Z}_{A}(\mu,m)

on the
integral model
\mathcal{X}=\mathcal{Y}_{0}(N)\times\mathcal{Y}_{0}(N)
of the Hilbert modular surface

X=Y_{0}(N)\times Y_{0}(N)
evaluated along the corresponding CM cycle

\mathcal{Z}(V_{A,0})\subset\mathcal{X}=\mathcal{Y}_{0}(N)\times\mathcal{Y}_{0}(N)
. Here, each
\mathcal{Z}_{A}(\mu,m)

is the Hirzebruch-Zagier divisor

\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}_{A}(\mu,m)=(\mathcal{Z}_{A}(\mu,m),\Phi^{L_{A}}_{\mu,m})

on
\mathcal{X}=\mathcal{Y}_{0}(N)\times\mathcal{Y}_{0}(N)
described above.
We can also extend arithmetic divisors to the compactification

\mathcal{X}^{\star}\cong\mathcal{X}_{0}(N)\times\mathcal{X}_{0}(N)

as described in
(\ref{Hilbertboundary})
to get the corresponding formula

\displaystyle\Lambda^{\prime}(1/2,\phi\times\theta(\chi))

\displaystyle=-2\pi\sum\limits_{A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})}\chi(A)\left[\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}_{A}^{c}(f_{0,A}):\mathcal{Z}(V_{A,0})\right]

Proof.

For each class
A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})
, we have by Theorem
7.5
the arithmetic height formula

\displaystyle\left[\widehat{Z}_{A}(f_{0,A}):\mathcal{Z}(V_{A,0})\right]

\displaystyle=-\frac{h_{k}}{w_{k}}\cdot L^{\prime}(0,\xi_{1-n/2}(f_{0,A})\times\theta_{L_{A,0}^{\perp}})=-\frac{h_{k}}{w_{k}}\cdot L^{\prime}(0,g_{\phi,A}\times\theta_{L_{A,0}^{\perp}}).

Using the relation
(\ref{CCDV})
from the proof of Theorem
6.7
, we can extend this to the completed Rankin-Selberg
L
-function

L^{\star}(s,g_{\phi,A}\times\theta_{L_{A,0}^{\perp}})=\Lambda(1+s,\eta_{k})L(s,g_{\phi,A}\times\theta_{L_{A,0}^{\perp}})

to get the corresponding formula

\displaystyle\Lambda(1,\eta_{k})\left[\widehat{Z}_{A}(f_{0,A}):\mathcal{Z}(V_{A,0})\right]

\displaystyle=-\frac{h_{k}}{w_{k}}\cdot\Lambda(1,\eta_{k})L^{\prime}(0,g_{\phi,A}\times\theta_{L_{A,0}^{\perp}})=-\frac{h_{k}}{w_{k}}\cdot L^{\star\prime}(0,g_{\phi,A}\times\theta_{L_{A,0}^{\perp}}),

which by Proposition
6.6
(i) is the same as

\displaystyle\Lambda(1,\eta_{k})\left[\widehat{Z}_{A}(f_{0,A}):\mathcal{Z}(V_{A,0})\right]

\displaystyle=-\frac{h_{k}}{w_{k}}\cdot\Lambda^{\prime}(1/2,\phi\times\theta_{A}),

and which after evaluating
\Lambda(1,\eta_{k})
via
(\ref{CDirichlet})
then dividing each side by
-h_{k}/w_{k}
is the same as

\displaystyle-2\pi\left[\widehat{Z}_{A}(f_{0,A}):\mathcal{Z}(V_{A,0})\right]

\displaystyle=\Lambda^{\prime}(1/2,\phi\times\theta_{A}).

Taking the twisted sum
\Lambda^{\prime}(1/2,\phi\times\theta(\chi))=\sum\limits_{A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})}\chi(A)\Lambda^{\prime}(1/2,\phi\times\theta_{A})

then gives the stated formula. ∎

Corollary 7.9
.

Let
E
be an elliptic curve of level
N
defined over
{\bf{Q}}
, parametized via modularity by a cuspidal newform

\phi=\phi_{E}\in S_{2}(\Gamma_{0}(N))
, so that the Hasse-Weil
L
-function
L(E,s)
has an analytic continuation

\Lambda(E,s)=\Lambda(s-1/2,\phi)
given by a shift of the standard
L
-function
\Lambda(s,\phi)=L_{\infty}(s,\phi)L(s,\phi)
of
\phi
.
Let
k
be an imaginary quadratic field of odd discriminant
d_{k}
and (odd) quadratic Dirichlet character
\eta_{k}(\cdot)=(\frac{d_{k}}{\cdot})
.
Assume that
(N,d_{k})=1
, and that the “Heegner hypothesis”
\eta_{k}(-N)=-\eta_{k}(N)=-1
holds.
Then, for any class group character
\chi\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})^{\times}
, we have the following central derivative value formula

\displaystyle\Lambda^{\prime}(E/K,\chi,1)

\displaystyle=-2\pi\sum\limits_{A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})}\chi(A)\left[\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}_{A}(f_{0,A}):\mathcal{Z}(V_{A,0})\right]

for the Hasse-Weil
L
-function
\Lambda(E/K,\chi,s)=\Lambda(s-1/2,\phi\times\theta(\chi))
of
E
over
K
twisted by
\chi

in terms of arithmetic divisors on the Hilbert modular surface
\mathcal{Y}_{0}(N)\times\mathcal{Y}_{0}(N)\longrightarrow\operatorname{Spec}({\bf{Z}})
.
Extending to the compactification
\mathcal{X}_{0}(N)\times\mathcal{X}_{0}(N)\longrightarrow\operatorname{Spec}({\bf{Z}})
,
we also have the central derivative value formula

\displaystyle\Lambda^{\prime}(E/K,\chi,1)

\displaystyle=-2\pi\sum\limits_{A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})}\chi(A)\left[\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}_{A}^{c}(f_{0,A}):\mathcal{Z}(V_{A,0})\right].

Note that by comparing with the formula of Gross-Zagier
[
26
, Theorem I (6.3)]
, we also obtain a relation of
arithmetic heights on
\mathcal{X}_{0}(N)
and
\mathcal{X}_{0}(N)\times\mathcal{X}_{0}(N)
. We explain this in
more detail in Appendix A below, where we develop the discussion of
[
13
,
\lx@sectionsign 7.3
, Theorem 7.7]

to prove the full version of
[
26
, Theorem I (6.3)]
this way, i.e. via Theorem
7.5

applied to the setup described in Example
7.2
.

7.3.
Relations to Birch-Swinnerton-Dyer constants and periods

We now return to Theorem
1.5
.

Proof of Theorem
1.5
.

Cf.
[
55
, Theorem 5.1]
. In either case on
k
, we use the product rule with the Artin decomposition

\Lambda(E/k,s)=\Lambda(E,s)\Lambda(E^{(d_{k})},s)=\Lambda(E/{\bf{Q}},s)\Lambda(E^{(d_{k})}/{\bf{Q}},s)
to compute

\displaystyle\Lambda^{\prime}(E/k,1)

\displaystyle=\Lambda^{\prime}(E,1)\Lambda(E^{(d_{k})},1)+\Lambda^{\prime}(E^{(d_{k})},1)\Lambda(E,1),

equivalently

\displaystyle\Lambda^{\prime}(1/2,\Pi(\pi))

\displaystyle=\Lambda^{\prime}(1/2,\phi)\Lambda(1/2,\phi\otimes\eta_{k})+\Lambda^{\prime}(1/2,\phi\otimes\eta_{k})\Lambda(1/2,\phi),

where exactly one of the summands on the right vanishes. For the nonvanishing summand, we can take for granted the refined conjecture
of Birch and Swinnerton-Dyer up to powers of
2
and
3
using the combinations of various theorems on the Iwasawa main conjectures and subsequent Euler
characteristic calculations; see
[
15
]
and
[
16
]
for details. In brief, we use the combined works of
Kato
[
34
]
, Kolyvagin
[
38
]
, Rohrlich
[
46
]
, and Skinner-Urban
[
49
]
to establish the cyclotomic main
conjectures
12
12

12

We also consider the anticyclotomic main conjecture for the rank one factor, after passing to an imaginary quadratic field,
then descend back down to
{\bf{Q}}
using converse theorems and Euler characteristic calculations – see
[
15
]
and
[
16
]
for a desciption of
the state of the art.
, followed by the Euler characteristic calculations of Burungale-Skinner-Tian
[
15
]
,
[
16
]
,
and Castella
[
18
]
for the rank zero factor, and those of Jetchev-Skinner-Wan
[
33
]
, Skinner-Zhang
[
51
]
, and Zhang
[
67
]

for the rank one factor. This allows us to deduce that
\Lambda^{\prime}(E/k,1)\approx\kappa_{E}({\bf{Q}})\cdot\kappa_{E^{(d_{k})}}({\bf{Q}})
.
We then identify the central derivative values according to Theorem
6.7
and Corollary
6.8
, as well as Theorem
7.8

and Corollary
7.9
when
k
is imaginary quadratic. ∎

Recall that a complex number
\alpha=\sigma+it
is said to be a
period
if its real and imaginary parts
\sigma
and
it

can be expressed as integrals of rational functions, over domains in
{\bf{R}}^{n}
given by polynomial inequalities with rational
coefficients. We write
\mathcal{P}\subset{\bf{C}}
to denote the set of all such numbers.
We refer to the paper of Kontsevich-Zagier
[
39
]
for a definitive expository account of this countable subring

\mathcal{P}
of
C
, which contains the algebraic numbers
\overline{{\bf{Q}}}
and their logarithms

\log\overline{\bf{Q}}
(for instance). This paper also describes the conjecture of Birch-Swinnerton-Dyer from
this perpective, including the conjecture
[
39
, Question 4]
that the central derivative value

\Lambda^{(r_{E}(k))}(E/k,1)
should lie in the ring of periods
\mathcal{P}
.
Assuming the finiteness of the Tate-Shafarevich group, the argument of
[
39
,
\lx@sectionsign 3.5
]

shows that the Birch-Swinnerton-Dyer constant
\kappa_{E}({\bf{Q}})\in\mathcal{P}
is a period.
The same argument works for the more general setting of number fields, to show that
\kappa_{E}(k)\in\mathcal{P}
.

Corollary 7.10
.

We retain the setup of Theorem
1.5
.

(i)

If
k
is imaginary quadratic with
\eta_{k}(-N)=-\eta_{k}(N)=-1
, then the central derivative value

\displaystyle\Lambda^{\prime}(E/k,1)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\pi}{2}\sum\limits_{A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})}\Phi(f_{0,A},Z(V_{A,0}))=-2\pi\sum\limits_{A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})}\left[\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}_{A}^{c}(f_{0,A}):\mathcal{Z}(V_{A,0})\right]

lies in the ring of periods
\mathcal{P}
.

(ii)

If
k
is real quadratic with
\eta_{k}(-N)=\eta_{k}(N)=-1
, then the central derivative value

\displaystyle\Lambda^{\prime}(E/k,1)

\displaystyle=-2\ln(\varepsilon_{k})h_{k}\sum\limits_{A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})}\left[\left(\frac{w_{k}\ln(\varepsilon_{k})}{4h_{k}}\right)\Phi(f_{0,A},\mathfrak{G}(W_{A}))+\operatorname{CT}\langle\langle f_{0,A}^{+}(\tau),\theta_{L_{A,W}^{\perp}}(\tau)\otimes\mathcal{E}_{L_{A,W}}(\tau)\rangle\rangle\right]

lies in the ring of periods
\mathcal{P}
.

Proof.

We use the argument of
[
39
,
\lx@sectionsign 3.5
]
to deduce that
\kappa_{E}(k)\in\mathcal{P}
, up to powers of
2
and
3
.
That is, we take for granted the conditions of Theorem
1.5
so that various theorems on Iwasawa main conjectures
described above allow us to bound the
p
-primary Tate-Shafarevich groups
{\mbox{{Sh}}}(E/{\bf{Q}})[p^{\infty}]
and

{\mbox{{Sh}}}(E^{(d_{k})}/{\bf{Q}})[p^{\infty}]
for primes
p\geq 5
through the corresponding bounds for the
p
-primary Selmer groups.
The argument
[
39
,
\lx@sectionsign
3.5]
then shows that each of the corresponding Birch-Swinnerton-Dyer constants
\kappa_{E}({\bf{Q}})

and
\kappa_{E^{(d_{k})}}({\bf{Q}})
, up to powers of
2
and
3
, lies in the ring of periods
\mathcal{P}
. ∎

Appendix A
Gross-Zagier via the signature (1,2) setting

We now explain how a variation of the argument of Bruinier-Yang
[
13
, Theorem 7.7 and Corollary 7.8]

can be used to deduce the full Gross-Zagier formula
[
26
, Theorem I (6.3)]
, for twists by class group characters.
This generalizes
[
26
, Theorem 7.7]
, which recovers the formula of
[
26
, Theorem I(6.3)]
for the case of trivial/principal ring class character

\chi_{0}={\bf{1}}\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})^{\vee}
. Although perhaps well-known to experts, we include details for lack of reference.
We also do this to compare with the arithmetic Hirzebruch-Zagier divisors in Theorem
7.8
and Corollary
7.9
.

A.1.
X_{0}(N)
as spin Shimura variety

See
[
12
,
\lx@sectionsign 2.4
]
and
[
13
,
\lx@sectionsign
7.3]
. Fix an integer
N\geq 1
. Let
(V,Q)
be the rational quadratic space with underlying vector space

\displaystyle V=\operatorname{Mat}_{2\times 2}^{\operatorname{tr}=0}({\bf{Q}})

given by
2\times 2
matrices with rational coordinates and trace zero, and quadratic form given by

Q(x)=N\det(x)
. The corresponding bilinear form is then given by
(x,y)=-N\operatorname{tr}(xy)
for
x,y\in V
.
This rational quadratic space
(V,Q)
has signature
(1,2)
.
The group
\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\bf{Q}})
acts on the trace zero matrices
V
by conjugation

\gamma\cdot x=\gamma x\gamma^{-1}
for
x\in V
and
\gamma\in\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\bf{Q}})
. This action
leaves the form
Q
invariant, and induces isomorphisms

\displaystyle\operatorname{GSpin}(V)\cong\operatorname{GL}_{2},\quad\quad\operatorname{Spin}(V)\cong\operatorname{SL}_{2}

of algebraic groups over
{\bf{Q}}
. The Grassmannian
D(V)=D^{\pm}(V)
can be identified with
\mathfrak{H}\cup\overline{\mathfrak{H}}
via the map

\displaystyle z=x+iy\in\mathfrak{H}\longmapsto{\bf{R}}\Re\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}z&-z^{2}\\
1&-z\end{array}\right)+{\bf{R}}\Im\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}z&-z^{2}\\
1&-z\end{array}\right)\in D(V).

Note that
\operatorname{GSpin}(V)({\bf{R}})
acts on
D(V)\cong\mathfrak{H}\cup\overline{\mathfrak{H}}
by fractional linear transformation.
The congruence subgroup
\Gamma_{0}(N)\subset\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\bf{Z}})
determines both a lattice
L\subset V
and a
compact open subgroup
K=K_{L}=\prod_{p}K_{p}
of
\operatorname{GSpin}(V)({\bf{A}}_{f})
. To be more concrete, we take the lattice

\displaystyle L

\displaystyle=\left\{\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}b&-a/N\\
c&-b\end{array}\right):a,b,c\in{\bf{Z}}\right\},

with dual lattice

\displaystyle L^{\vee}

\displaystyle=\left\{\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}b/2N&-a/N\\
c&-b/2N\end{array}\right):a,b,c\in{\bf{Z}}\right\}.

We have a natural identification of the corresponding discriminant group

\displaystyle({\bf{Z}}/N{\bf{Z}})\cong L^{\vee}/L,\quad r\longmapsto\mu_{r}:=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}r/2N&\\
&-r/2N\end{array}\right).

The lattice
L\subset V
has level
4N
, and the quadratic form on
L^{\vee}/L
can be identified with
x\mapsto-x^{2}
on
{\bf{Z}}/4N{\bf{Z}}
.
The compact open subgroup
K=K_{L}\subset\operatorname{GSpin}(V)({\bf{A}}_{f})\cong\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\bf{A}}_{f})

is given by
K=\prod_{p}K_{p}
, with each
K_{p}\subset\operatorname{GSpin}(V)({\bf{Z}}_{p})\cong\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\bf{Z}}_{p})
defined by

\displaystyle K_{p}

\displaystyle=\left\{\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}a&b\\
c&d\end{array}\right)\in\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\bf{Z}}_{p}):c\in N{\bf{Z}}_{p}\right\}.

In this way, we obtain the identification of Shimura curves

(80)

\displaystyle Y_{0}(N)=\Gamma_{0}(n)\backslash\mathfrak{H}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow X_{K}({\bf{C}})=\operatorname{GSpin}(V)({\bf{Q}})\backslash D(V)\times\operatorname{GSpin}(V)({\bf{A}}_{f})/K,\quad\Gamma_{0}(N)z\longmapsto\operatorname{GSpin}(V)({\bf{Q}})[z,1]K.

A.2.
Heegner divisors as special divisors

A.2.1.
Special divisors and CM cycles associated to the lattice
L

We have the following correspondence between the special divisors
Z(\mu,m)

and Heegner divisors
P_{D,r}
described
13
13

13

To be more precise, let
\tau\in\mathfrak{H}
be a root of the quadratic equation
a\tau^{2}+b\tau+c=0

for
a,b,c\in{\bf{Z}}
,
a>0
,
a\equiv 0\bmod N
,
b\equiv r\bmod 2N
and
D=b^{2}-4ac
. The image
\tau_{a,b,c}
of such a root
in
X_{0}(N)
is rational over the Hilbert class field
k[1]
of the imaginary quadratic field
k
of discriminant
D
, and the
Galois group
\operatorname{Gal}(k[1]/k)\cong C(\mathcal{O}_{k})
permutes these images simply transitively. We then define

P_{D,r}=\frac{w_{k}}{2}\sum_{[a,b,c]\in\mathcal{Q}_{D}\cong C(\mathcal{O}_{k})}\tau_{a,b,c}
as
\frac{w_{k}}{2}
times the sum of these

h_{k}
points. In the moduli description of
X_{0}(N)
, this point
P_{D,r}
corresponds to a triple
(E,E^{\prime},\varphi)
of elliptic curves
E
and
E^{\prime}

with complex multiplication by
\mathcal{O}_{k}
and
\varphi:E\rightarrow E^{\prime}
is an isogeny of kernel annihilated by the primitive ideal

\mathfrak{n}=[N,(r+\sqrt{D})/2]
of norm
N
.

in Gross-Kohnen-Zagier
[
27
]
.
Given
m\in{\bf{Q}}_{>0}
and a coset
\mu\in L^{\vee}/L
such that
Q(\mu)\equiv m\bmod L
, we again consider the quadric

\displaystyle\Omega_{\mu,m}({\bf{Q}})

\displaystyle=\left\{x\in\mu+L:Q(x)=m\right\}.

Note that
\Omega_{\mu,m}({\bf{Q}})=\emptyset
unless
Q(\mu)\equiv m\bmod L
.

Let us for each
m\in{\bf{Q}}_{>0}
and
\mu\in L^{\vee}/L
with
Q(\mu)\equiv m\bmod L
consider the fundamental discriminant

\displaystyle D=-4Nm\in{\bf{Z}}.

Given an integer
r\in{\bf{Z}}
with coset representative
\mu=\mu_{r}
under the natural bijection
(\ref{natural})
,

\displaystyle\mu=\mu_{r}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}r/2N&\\
&-r/2N\end{array}\right)\in\Omega_{\mu,r}({\bf{Q}}),

we have that
D\equiv r^{2}\bmod 4N
. In this way, we produce a positive norm vector in the quadric

\displaystyle x=x(\mu,m)=x(\mu_{r},-D/4N)

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}r/2N&1/N\\
(D-r^{2})/4N&-r/2N\end{array}\right)\in\Omega_{\mu,m}({\bf{Q}}).

Conversely, given integers
D<0
and
r
such that
D\equiv r^{2}\bmod 4N
, let
m=-D/4N
and
\mu=\mu_{r}
.
Observe that
m\in Q(\mu)+{\bf{Z}}
is positive. As in
[
13
,
\lx@sectionsign
7.1]
, we take this identification for granted,
and note that the corresponding special divisor
Z(\mu,m)=Z(\mu_{r},-D/4N)

defined in
(\ref{special})
above can be identified with a sum of Heegner divisors
P_{D,r}+P_{D,-r}

defined in Gross-Kohnen-Zagier
[
27
, IV.1(1)]
.
We remark that each of these Heegner divisors
P_{D,\pm r}
has degree equal to the Hurwitz class number
H(D)
,

\displaystyle\deg(P_{D,\pm r})

\displaystyle=H(D)=\frac{h(D)}{2w(D)}.

Here,
h(D)
denotes the class number of the imaginary quadratic field
{\bf{Q}}(\sqrt{D})
, equivalently the cardinality
of the class group of positive definite binary quadratic forms of discriminant
D
. We also write
w(D)
to denote the number of
roots of unity in
{\bf{Q}}(\sqrt{D})
, equivalently the cardinality of the unit group of
{\bf{Q}}(\sqrt{D})
.
Hence, we deduce that for a pair
(\mu,m)=(\mu_{r},-D/4N)
corresponding to
(D,r)
in this way, we have the relation

\displaystyle\deg Z(\mu,m)=\deg Z(\mu_{r},-D/4N)=\deg(P_{D,\pm r})=H(D)=\frac{h(D)}{2w(D)}.

Fixing a positive norm vector
x=x(\mu,m)\in\Omega_{\mu,m}({\bf{Q}})
as in
(\ref{x})
above,
we consider the positive and negative definite subspaces defined by

\displaystyle V_{+}

\displaystyle:={\bf{Q}}x,\quad U:=V\cap x^{\perp}

of
(V,Q)=(\operatorname{Mat}_{2\times 2}^{\operatorname{tr=0}}({\bf{Q}}),N\cdot\det(\cdot))
,
as well as the respective positive definite and negative definite lattices

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}:=L\cap V_{+},\quad\mathcal{N}:=L\cap x^{\perp}.

Notice that we can present the negative definite lattice
\mathcal{N}\subset L
more explicitly as

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}

\displaystyle={\bf{Z}}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
-r&1\end{array}\right)\oplus{\bf{Z}}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1/N\\
(r^{2}-D)/4N&0\end{array}\right),

and also that
\mathcal{N}
has determinant
-D
. Writing
t=\gcd(r,2N)
,
the positive definite lattice
\mathcal{P}\subset L
and its dual lattice
\mathcal{P}^{\vee}
can be presented more explicitly as

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}

\displaystyle={\bf{Z}}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}r&2\\
(D-r^{2})/2&-r\end{array}\right)={\bf{Z}}\frac{2N}{t}x,\quad(L_{0}^{\perp})^{\vee}=\mathcal{P}^{\vee}={\bf{Z}}\frac{t}{D}x.

Let us now consider the ideal
\mathfrak{n}=[N,(r+\sqrt{D})/2]
of
{\bf{Z}}[(D+\sqrt{D})/2]
.
This ideal has norm
N
, and we can associate with it the quadratic form given by the corresponding norm form

\displaystyle Q_{\mathfrak{n}}(z)

\displaystyle:=\frac{z\overline{z}}{N}=\frac{{\bf{N}}(z)}{{\bf{N}}\mathfrak{n}}.

As shown in
[
13
, Lemma 7.1]
, if
D
is a fundamental discriminant of the imaginary quadratic field

k={\bf{Q}}(\sqrt{D})
, we have an isomorphism of quadratic lattices

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{n},-Q_{\mathfrak{n}})

\displaystyle\longrightarrow(\mathcal{N},-Q_{\mathfrak{n}}),\quad xN+y\left(\frac{r+\sqrt{D}}{2}\right)\longmapsto\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}x&-y/N\\
-rx-y(r^{2}-D)/4N\end{array}\right).

Both lattices are equivalent to the integral quadratic form defined by

\displaystyle[-N,-r,-(r^{2}-D)/4N]=-Nx^{2}-rxy-(r^{2}-D)/4Ny^{2}.

Now, recall that the spin group
\operatorname{GSpin}(U)=\operatorname{GSpin}(U(x))

can be identified as the multiplicative group
T_{U}=\operatorname{GSpin}(U)\cong k^{\times}
,
with
K_{T}=K\cap T\cong\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{k}^{\times}
maximal.
According to
[
13
, Proposition 7.2]
, if the fundamental discriminant

D
is coprime to
N
, then we have an identification of zero cycles
Z(U)=Z(m,\mu)
.
More precisely, writing
F_{\mu,m}(\tau)=F^{L}_{\mu,m}(\tau,3/4)
for the Hejhal Poincaré
series of Definition
4.4
above, we have the identifications of CM cycles

\displaystyle Z(U)=Z(U(x))=Z(\mu,m)=Z(F_{\mu,m})=Z(F_{\mu,m}^{L})

on
Y_{0}(N)({\bf{C}})=X_{K}({\bf{C}})=\operatorname{Sh}_{K}(\operatorname{GSpin}(V),D(V))({\bf{C}})
.

A.2.2.
Ideal class representatives

Let
k
be any quadratic field (real or imaginary) of discriminant
d_{k}
and class group
C(\mathcal{O}_{k})
.
In the subsections of this appendix, we shall take
k
to be an imaginary quadratic field of
discriminant
d_{k}=D
, though we consider the more general situation for future reference.
Let
\mathcal{Q}_{d_{k}}
denote the class group of binary quadratic forms
q_{a,b,c}(x,y)=ax^{2}+bx+c

of discriminant
d_{k}=b^{2}-4a
. Write
[a,b,c]=[q_{a,b,c}]\in\mathcal{Q}_{d_{k}}
to denote the class
represented by a binary quadratic form
q_{a,b,c}(x,y)
of discriminant
d_{k}=b^{2}-4ac
.
A classical theorem shows that we have an isomorphism of class groups
\mathcal{Q}_{d_{k}}\cong C(\mathcal{O}_{k})
.
For instance (see e.g.
[
19
, Theorem 7.7]
), we have the explicit isomorphism

\displaystyle\varphi:\mathcal{Q}_{d_{k}}\cong C(\mathcal{O}_{k}),\quad\quad[a,b,c]

\displaystyle\longmapsto[a,(-b+\sqrt{d_{k}})/2].

Recall that for each class
A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})
we fix an integral ideal representative
\mathfrak{a}\subset\mathcal{O}_{k}
of
A
.
Let us then consider the sublattice
L_{A}\subset L
defined by

\displaystyle L_{A}

\displaystyle=\left\{\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}b&-a/N\\
c&-b\end{array}\right):a,b,c\in{\bf{Z}},\quad N\det\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}b&-a/N\\
c&-b\end{array}\right)\Bigg|_{L_{A,U}}\equiv-q_{a,b,c}\text{ for }\varphi([a,b,c])=A\right\},

with dual lattice

\displaystyle L_{A}^{\vee}

\displaystyle=\left\{\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}b/2N&-a/N\\
c&-b/2N\end{array}\right):a,b,c\in{\bf{Z}},\quad N\det\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}b&-a/N\\
c&-b\end{array}\right)\Bigg|_{L_{A,U}}\equiv-q_{a,b,c}\text{ for }\varphi([a,b,c])=A\right\}.

Again, we have a natural identification of the corresponding discriminant group

\displaystyle({\bf{Z}}/N{\bf{Z}})\cong L_{A}^{\vee}/L_{A},\quad r\longmapsto\mu_{r}:=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}r/2N&\\
&-r/2N\end{array}\right),

and the quadratic form on
L_{A}^{\vee}/L_{A}
can again be identified with
x\mapsto-x^{2}
on
{\bf{Z}}/4N{\bf{Z}}
.
The corresponding compact open subgroup
K_{A}=K_{L_{A}}\subset\operatorname{GSpin}(V)({\bf{A}}_{f})\cong\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\bf{A}}_{f})

is given by
K_{A}=\prod_{p}K_{A,p}
, with each
K_{A,p}=K_{p}
for
K_{p}\subset\operatorname{GSpin}(V)({\bf{Z}}_{p})\cong\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\bf{Z}}_{p})

as defined above. That is, we have identified the corresponding compact open subgroups
K=K_{A}
for each
A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})
.

Let us now assume
k
is imaginary quadratic with discriminant
d_{k}=D
.
Adapting the discussion above, we consider for each
m\in{\bf{Q}}_{>0}
and
\mu\in L_{A}^{\vee}/L_{A}

with
Q(\mu)\equiv m\bmod L_{A}
the corresponding quadric

\displaystyle\Omega_{A,\mu,m}({\bf{Q}})

\displaystyle=\left\{x\in\mu+L_{A}:Q(x)=m\right\}.

Here, we see that
\Omega_{A,\mu,m}({\bf{Q}})
is empty unless
Q(\mu)\equiv m\bmod L_{A}
.
Given
m\in{\bf{Q}}_{>0}
, let
\mu\in L_{A}^{\vee}/L_{A}
be such that
Q(\mu)\equiv m\bmod 1
.
Hence,
D=-4Nm\in{\bf{Z}}
is again a negative discriminant. If
r\in{\bf{Z}}
with
\mu=m_{r}\bmod L_{A}
,
then again
D\equiv r^{2}\bmod N
, and we have a positive norm vector

\displaystyle x_{A}=x_{A}(\mu,m)=x_{A}(\mu_{r},-D/4N)

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}r/2N&1/N\\
(D-r^{2})/4N&-r/2N\end{array}\right)\in\Omega_{A,\mu,m}({\bf{Q}}).

Conversely, given
D<0
and
r
with
D\equiv r^{2}\bmod 4N
, put
m=-D/4N
and
\mu=\mu_{r}
.
Then,
m\in Q(\mu)+{\bf{Z}}
is positive. The corresponding special divisor

\displaystyle Z_{A}(\mu,m)=Z_{A}(\mu_{r},-D/4N)

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{0}(N)\Big\backslash\coprod\limits_{x\in\mu+L_{A}\atop Q(x)=m}D(V)_{x}=P_{D,r}^{A}+P_{D,-r}^{A}

corresponds to the Heegner divisor
P_{D,r}^{A}+P_{D,-r}^{A}
, where each point
P_{D,\pm r}\in X_{0}(N)(k[1])
in the moduli
description is represented by a triple
(E,E^{\prime},\varphi)
with
E({\bf{C}})\cong{\bf{C}}/\mathfrak{a}
and
E^{\prime}({\bf{C}})\cong{\bf{C}}/\mathfrak{n}^{-1}\mathfrak{a}

and kernel
\ker(\varphi)
of the isogeny
\varphi:E\rightarrow E^{\prime}
annihilated by the primitive ideal
\mathfrak{n}
(see e.g.
[
26
,
\lx@sectionsign
II.1]
).
In this way, we see that each class
A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})

has a representative special (Heegner) divisor
Z_{A}(\mu,m)=P_{D,r}^{A}+P_{D,-r}^{A}
,
as well as a representative positive norm vector

x_{A}=x_{A}(\mu,m)=x_{A}(\mu_{r},-D/4N)\in\Omega_{A,\mu,m}({\bf{Q}})
.
Let us henceforth fix this set of representative special (Heegner) divisors and positive norm vectors

(87)

\displaystyle\left\{Z_{A}(\mu,m)=Z_{A}(\mu_{r},-D/4N)\right\}_{A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})},\quad\left\{x_{A}(\mu,m)=x_{A}(\mu_{r},-D/4N)\right\}_{A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})}

\displaystyle\longleftrightarrow A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k}).

Fixing such a set of representatives
(\ref{representatives})
, we consider the positive and negative definite subspaces

\displaystyle V_{A,+}=V_{A,+}(x_{A})

\displaystyle:={\bf{Q}}x_{A},\quad U_{A}=U_{A}(x_{A}):=V\cap x_{A}^{\perp}

of
(V,Q)=(\operatorname{Mat}_{2\times 2}^{\operatorname{tr=0}}({\bf{Q}}),N\cdot\det(\cdot))
,
with corresponding positive and negative definite lattices

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{A}=\mathcal{P}_{A}(x_{A}):=L_{A}\cap V_{A,+},\quad\mathcal{N}_{A}=\mathcal{N}_{A}(x_{A})=L_{A}\cap U_{A}=L_{A}\cap x_{A}^{\perp}.

In what follows, we shall apply the results of Theorems
5.12
and
7.5
to these negative definite subspaces

V_{A,+}=\mathcal{N}_{A}\otimes_{\bf{Z}}{\bf{Q}}\subset V
for each class
A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})
.

A.3.
Cuspidal eigenforms from vector-valued Shimura lifts

We now describe the harmonic weak Maass forms
f\in H_{1-n/2}(\omega_{L})=H_{1/2}(\omega_{L})
that appear.
Let
L\subset V
be the lattice described in
(\ref{Ln=1})

corresponding to the compact open subgroup
K_{0}(N)\subset\operatorname{GL}_{2}(\widehat{\bf{Z}})
.
Let
S_{3/2}(\omega_{L})
denote the space of holomorphic cuspidal modular forms of weight
3/2

and representation
\omega_{L}
. A theorem of Eichler-Zagier
[
23
, Theorem 5.1]
shows that we have the identification

(88)

\displaystyle S_{3/2}(\omega_{L})\cong J_{2,N}^{\operatorname{cusp}}

with the space
J_{2,N}^{\operatorname{cusp}}
of Jacobi cusp forms of weight
2
and index
N
.
There is a theory of Hecke operators and newforms for the space of Jacobi cusp forms
J_{2,N}^{\operatorname{cusp}}
.
We write
J_{2,N}^{\operatorname{new,cusp}}\subset J_{2,N}^{\operatorname{cusp}}
to denote its subspace of newforms,
with
S_{3/2}^{\operatorname{new}}(\omega_{L})\subset S_{3/2}(\omega_{L})
the induced subspace of vector-valued newforms.

To describe this in a more exact way, we again consider the space
S_{2}(\Gamma_{0}(N))
of scalar-valued
holomorphic cusp forms of weight
2
on
X_{0}(N)
with trivial nebentype character.
Let
S_{2}^{\star}(\Gamma_{0}(N))\subset S_{2}(\Gamma_{0}(N))
denote the subspace of holomorphic cusp forms
which are invariant under the Fricke involution
W_{N}
.
Note that a cusp form
\phi\in S_{2}(\Gamma_{0}(N))
is invariant under the Fricke involution if and only if its corresponding standard

L
-function
14
14

14

Which we normalize here to have central value at
s=1/2
, as in the discussion above,
but distinct from the classical normalizations used by
[
13
,
\lx@sectionsign
7.3]
,
[
27
]
, and
[
26
]
.

\Lambda(s,\phi)=L_{\infty}(s,\phi)L(s,\phi)

has an odd, symmetric functional equation
\Lambda(s,\phi)=-\Lambda(1-s,\phi)
.
Let
S_{2}^{\operatorname{new}}(\Gamma_{0}(N))\subset S_{2}(\Gamma_{0}(N))
denote the subspace of newforms, and

\displaystyle S_{2}^{\operatorname{new},\star}(\Gamma_{0}(N))=S_{2}^{\star}(\Gamma_{0}(N))\cap S_{2}^{\operatorname{new}}(\Gamma_{0}(N))\subset S_{2}(\Gamma_{0}(N))

the subspace of Fricke-invariant newforms. The theorem of Skoruppa-Zagier
[
50
]

shows that the Shimura correspondence can be realized explicitly as an isomorphism
of
J_{2,N}^{\operatorname{new,cusp}}
-Hecke modules

(89)

\displaystyle S_{2}^{\operatorname{new},\star}(\Gamma_{0}(N))

\displaystyle\cong J_{2,N}^{\operatorname{new,cusp}}.

Explicitly, fix a positive rational
m_{0}\in{\bf{Q}}_{>_{0}}
and a coset
\mu_{0}\in L^{\vee}/L
such that
m_{0}\equiv Q(\mu_{0})\bmod 1
.
Suppose that
D_{0}:=-4Nm_{0}\in{\bf{Z}}
determines a fundamental discriminant,
with
x\in\Omega_{\mu_{0},m_{0}}({\bf{Q}})
the corresponding positive norm vector defined in
(\ref{x})
,
and
U=U(x)=V\cap x^{\perp}
the corresponding negative definite space.
Consider the space
S_{3/2}(\omega_{L})\subset M_{3/2}(\omega_{L})\subset M_{3/2}^{!}(\omega_{L})\subset H_{3/2}(\omega_{L})

of holomorphic cuspidal forms of weight
3/2
and representation
\omega_{L}
.
We have for each such pair
(\mu_{0},m_{0})
a linear map

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\mu_{0},m_{0}}:S_{3/2}(\omega_{L})\longrightarrow S_{2}(\Gamma_{0}(N)),\quad g\longmapsto\mathcal{S}_{m_{0},\mu_{0}}(g)

defined on Fourier series expansions

\displaystyle g(\tau)=\sum\limits_{\mu\in L^{\vee}/L}\sum\limits_{m\in{\bf{Q}}_{>0}}c_{g}(\mu,m)e(m\tau){\bf{1}}_{\mu}\in S_{3/2}(\omega_{L})

by the rule

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\mu_{0},m_{0}}(g)(\tau)

\displaystyle:=\sum\limits_{n\geq 1}\left(\sum\limits_{d\mid n}\left(\frac{D_{0}}{d}\right)c_{g}\left(\mu_{0}\cdot\frac{n}{d},m_{0}\cdot\frac{n^{2}}{d^{2}}\right)\right)e(n\tau).

Here, we shall also write the Fourier series expansion of

\mathcal{S}_{\mu_{0},m_{0}}(g)(\tau)\in S_{2}(\Gamma_{0}(N))
with the simpler notations

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\mu_{0},m_{0}}(g)(\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{n\geq 1}c_{\mathcal{S}_{\mu_{0},m_{0}}(g)}(n)e(n\tau),\quad c_{\mathcal{S}_{\mu_{0},m_{0}}(g)}(n):=\sum\limits_{d\mid n}\left(\frac{D_{0}}{d}\right)c_{g}\left(\mu_{0}\cdot\frac{n}{d},m_{0}\cdot\frac{n^{2}}{d^{2}}\right),

as well as the normalized Fourier series expansion

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\mu_{0},m_{0}}(g)(\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{n\geq 1}n^{\frac{1}{2}}a_{\mathcal{S}_{\mu_{0},m_{0}}(g)}(n)e(n\tau),\quad a_{\mathcal{S}_{\mu_{0},m_{0}}(g)}(n)=c_{\mathcal{S}_{\mu_{0},m_{0}}(g)}(n)n^{-\frac{1}{2}}.

Hence, the standard
L
-function

\Lambda(s,\mathcal{S}_{\mu_{0},m_{0}}(g),s)=L_{\infty}(s,\mathcal{S}_{\mu_{0},m_{0}}(g))L(s,\mathcal{S}_{\mu_{0},m_{0}}(g))

has Dirichlet series expansion for
\Re(s)>1
given by

\displaystyle L(s,\mathcal{S}_{\mu_{0},m_{0}}(g))=\sum\limits_{n\geq 1}a_{\mathcal{S}_{\mu_{0},m_{0}}(g)}(n)n^{-s}=\sum\limits_{n\geq 1}c_{\mathcal{S}_{\mu_{0},m_{0}}(g)}(n)n^{-(s+\frac{1}{2})}.

Writing
\eta_{D_{0}}(\cdot)=\left(\frac{D_{0}}{\cdot}\right)
for the quadratic Dirichlet character of discriminant
D_{0}
, this can also be written as

(90)

\displaystyle L(s-1/2,\mathcal{S}_{\mu_{0},m_{0}}(g))

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{n\geq 1}c_{\mathcal{S}_{\mu_{0},m_{0}}(g)}(n)n^{-s}=L(s,\eta_{D_{0}})\sum\limits_{n\geq 1}c_{g}(\mu_{0}n,m_{0}n^{2})n^{-s}.

Each of the linear maps
\mathcal{S}_{\mu_{0},m_{0}}:S_{3/2}(\omega_{L})\longrightarrow S_{2}(\Gamma_{0}(N))
is Hecke-equivariant,
and some linear combination of them supplies the isomorphism

S_{3/2}^{\operatorname{new}}(\omega_{L})\cong S_{2}^{\operatorname{new},\star}(\Gamma_{0}(N))

implicit in the combination of
(\ref{Shimura})
and
(\ref{EZ})
.

Observe from the Dirichlet series expansion of
(\ref{DSESL})
that if

g\in S_{3/2}^{\operatorname{new}}(\omega_{L})
is related via Shimura correspondence to a scalar-valued
cusp form
\phi=\phi_{g}\in S_{2}^{\operatorname{new},\star}(\Gamma_{0}(N))
, then we have the relation of
L
-series

(91)

\displaystyle L(s,\mathcal{S}_{\mu_{0},m_{0}}(g))

\displaystyle=c_{g}(\mu_{0},m_{0})\cdot L(s,\phi),

and hence the relation of central derivative values

\displaystyle L^{\prime}(1/2,\mathcal{S}_{\mu_{0},m_{0}}(g))=c_{g}(\mu_{0},m_{0})\cdot L^{\prime}(1/2,\phi).

Lemma A.1
.

Fix
m_{0}\in{\bf{Q}}_{>0}
and
\mu_{0}\in L^{\vee}/L
with
m_{0}\equiv Q(\mu_{0})\bmod 1
.
Consider a fundamental discriminant
D_{0}=-4Nm_{0}\in{\bf{Z}}
, with corresponding positive norm vector

x_{0}\in\Omega_{\mu_{0},m_{0}}({\bf{Q}})
and negative definite space
U=U(x_{0})=V\cap x_{0}^{\perp}
