as described above.
Given any vector-valued cuspidal form
g\in S_{3/2}(\overline{\omega}_{L})
whose
(\mu_{0},m_{0})

Fourier coefficient
c_{g}(\mu_{0},m_{0})
does not vanish, we have the identification of
L
-series

\displaystyle L(s,g,U)

\displaystyle=(4\pi m_{0})^{-\left(\frac{s+1}{2}\right)}\Gamma\left(\frac{s+1}{2}\right)\frac{L(s+1/2,\mathcal{S}_{\mu_{0},m_{0}}(g))}{L(s+1,\eta_{D_{0}})}.

In particular, if
g\in S_{3/2}(\overline{\omega}_{L})
and
\phi\in S_{2}^{\operatorname{new},\star}(\Gamma_{0}(N))

are linked by the Shimura correspondence
(\ref{Shimura})
via
(\ref{RLS})
, then we have the relation of
L
-series

\displaystyle L(s,g,U)

\displaystyle=(4\pi m_{0})^{-\left(\frac{s+1}{2}\right)}\Gamma\left(\frac{s+1}{2}\right)\frac{c_{g}(\mu_{0},m_{0})\cdot L(s+1/2,\phi)}{L(s+1,\eta_{D_{0}})},

from which we can derive the identification of central derivative values

\displaystyle L^{\prime}(0,g,U)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{N}}{4\pi}\left(\frac{c_{g}(\mu_{0},m_{0})}{\deg Z(\mu_{0},m_{0})}\right)\cdot L^{\prime}(1/2,\phi).

Proof.

See
[
13
, Lemma 7.3]
with
[
13
, (4.24)]
, which we state here in the unitary normalization for the standard
L
-function
of
\phi\in S_{2}^{\operatorname{new},\star}(\Gamma_{0}(N))
. Since we obtain a slightly distinct identification for the central
derivative value (by a factor of
2^{-4}
), we provide details. To be clear, we have from definitions (first for
\Re(s)>1
) that

\displaystyle L(s,g,U)

\displaystyle=(4\pi)^{-\left(\frac{s+1}{2}\right)}\Gamma\left(\frac{s+1}{2}\right)\sum\limits_{m\geq 1}\sum\limits_{\mu\in\mathcal{P}^{\vee}/\mathcal{P}}r_{\mathcal{P}}(\mu,m)c_{g}(\mu,m)m^{-\left(\frac{s+1}{2}\right)}.

Viewing
g\in S_{3/2}(\overline{\omega}_{L})
as a form of weight
3/2
and representation
\omega_{\mathcal{P}\oplus\mathcal{N}}

via
[
13
, Lemma 3.1]
, we argue as in
[
13
, Lemma 7.3]
that
c_{g}((\lambda),\lambda)=0
for all
\lambda\in\mathcal{P}^{\vee}

unless
\lambda\in\mathcal{P}^{\vee}\cap L^{\vee}={\bf{Z}}x
to deduce that

\displaystyle L(s,g,U)

\displaystyle=(4\pi)^{-\left(\frac{s+1}{2}\right)}\Gamma\left(\frac{s+1}{2}\right)\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\mathcal{P}^{\vee}}c_{g}(\lambda,Q(\lambda))Q(\lambda)^{-\left(\frac{s+1}{2}\right)}.

On the other hand, we deduce from
(\ref{DSESL})
that we have the relation

\displaystyle L(s+1/2,\mathcal{S}_{\mu_{0},m_{0}}(g))

\displaystyle=L(s+1,\eta_{D_{0}})\sum\limits_{m\geq 1}c_{g}(\mu_{0}m,m_{0}m^{2})m^{-(s+1)}

\displaystyle=L(s+1,\eta_{D_{0}})\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\mathcal{P}^{\vee}}c_{g}(\mu_{0}\lambda,m_{0}Q(\lambda))\cdot\left(m_{0}Q(\lambda)\right)^{-\left(\frac{s+1}{2}\right)}

\displaystyle=L(s+1,\eta_{D_{0}})\cdot m_{0}^{-\left(\frac{s+1}{2}\right)}\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\mathcal{P}^{\vee}}c_{g}(\lambda,Q(\lambda))Q(\lambda)^{-\left(\frac{s+1}{2}\right)}

and hence

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\mathcal{P}^{\vee}}c_{g}(\lambda,Q(\lambda))Q(\lambda)^{-\left(\frac{s+1}{2}\right)}

\displaystyle=m_{0}^{-\left(\frac{s+1}{2}\right)}\cdot\frac{L(s+1/2,\mathcal{S}_{\mu_{0},m_{0}}(g))}{L(s+1,\eta_{D_{0}})},

so that

\displaystyle L(s,g,U)

\displaystyle=(4\pi m_{0})^{-\left(\frac{s+1}{2}\right)}\Gamma\left(\frac{s+1}{2}\right)\frac{L(s+1/2,\mathcal{S}_{\mu_{0},m_{0}}(g))}{L(s+1,\eta_{D_{0}})}.

which by
(\ref{RLS})
gives the desired relation of
L
-series

\displaystyle L(s,g,U)

\displaystyle=(4\pi m_{0})^{-\left(\frac{s+1}{2}\right)}\Gamma\left(\frac{s+1}{2}\right)\frac{c_{g}(\mu_{0},m_{0})\cdot L(s+1/2,\phi)}{L(s+1,\eta_{D_{0}})}.

Using that
L(1,\phi)=0
as
\phi
is invariant under the Fricke involution, we deduce via the product rule that

(92)

\displaystyle L^{\prime}(0,g,U)

\displaystyle=(4\pi m_{0})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)\frac{c_{g}(\mu_{0},m_{0})\cdot L^{\prime}(1/2,\phi)}{L(1,\eta_{D_{0}})}=\frac{c_{g}(\mu_{0},m_{0})\cdot L^{\prime}(1/2,\phi)}{2\sqrt{m_{0}}\cdot L(1,\eta_{D_{0}})}.

Using the Dirichlet class number formula
(\ref{Dirichlet})
for the imaginary quadratic field
k={\bf{Q}}(\sqrt{D_{0}})
, we evaluate

\displaystyle L(1,\eta_{D_{0}})

\displaystyle=\frac{2\pi h_{k}}{w_{k}\sqrt{|D_{0}|}}=\frac{4\pi}{\sqrt{|D_{0}|}}\cdot\frac{h_{k}}{2w_{k}}=\frac{4\pi}{\sqrt{|D_{0}|}}\cdot H(D_{0})=\frac{2\pi}{\sqrt{Nm_{0}}}\cdot\deg Z(\mu_{0},m_{0}).

Again,
H(D_{0})=h_{k}/2w_{k}
denotes the Hurwitz class number, and we have
H(D_{0})=\deg Z(\mu_{0},m_{0})
. Hence,

(93)

\displaystyle L^{\prime}(0,g,U)

\displaystyle=\frac{c_{g}(\mu_{0},m_{0})\cdot L^{\prime}(1/2,\phi)}{2\sqrt{m_{0}}\cdot L(1,\eta_{D_{0}})}=\frac{\sqrt{Nm_{0}}\cdot c_{g}(\mu_{0},m_{0})\cdot L^{\prime}(1/2,\phi)}{2\sqrt{m_{0}}\cdot 2\pi\cdot\deg Z(\mu_{0},m_{0})}=\frac{N^{\frac{1}{2}}}{4\pi}\cdot\frac{c_{g}(\mu_{0},m_{0})\cdot L^{\prime}(1/2,\phi)}{\deg Z(\mu_{0},m_{0})}.

∎

To relate this to the discussion of Theorems
5.12
and
7.5
above, we choose the harmonic weak
Maass form
f=f^{+}+f^{-}\in H_{1/2}(\overline{\omega}_{L})
according to the following result.

Lemma A.2
.

Fix any cuspidal form
g\in S_{3/2}^{\operatorname{new}}(\overline{\omega}_{L})
,
and let
\phi\in S_{2}^{\operatorname{new},\star}(\Gamma_{0}(N))
denotes its image under the Shimura correspondence via
(\ref{Shimura})
.
There exists a harmonic weak Maass form
f\in H_{1/2}(\overline{\omega}_{L})
with Fourier coefficients
c_{f}^{\pm}(m,\mu)
as above such that:

(i)

We have the relation
\xi_{1/2}(f)=g/||g||^{2}
.

(ii)

The Fourier coefficients
c_{f}^{+}(\mu,m)
of the principal part
P_{f}
of
f
lie in the Hecke field
{\bf{Q}}(\phi)
obtained by adjoining to
{\bf{Q}}

the Fourier coefficients of the cuspidal newform
\phi\in S_{2}^{\operatorname{new},\star}(\Gamma_{0}(N))
.

(iii)

The constant Fourier coefficient
c_{f}^{+}(0,0)
of
f
vanishes.

Proof.

See
[
13
, Lemma 7.4]
or
[
12
, Lemma 7.3]
. ∎

We also have the following result, to ensure the nonvanishing of coefficients
c_{g}(\mu_{0},m_{0})
in Lemma
A.1
.

Lemma A.3
.

Fix a newform

\displaystyle g(\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\mu\in L^{\vee}/L}\sum\limits_{m>0}c_{g}(\mu,m)e(m\tau){\bf{1}}_{\mu}\in S_{3/2}^{\operatorname{new}}(\omega_{L}).

There exist infinitely many fundamental discriminants
D<0
such that

(i)

Each prime divisor
q\mid N
splits in the imaginary quadratic extension
{\bf{Q}}(\sqrt{D})
.

(ii)

The coefficient
c_{g}(\mu,m)
does not vanish for
m=-\frac{D}{4N}
and any
\mu\in L^{\vee}/L
for which
m\equiv Q(\mu)\bmod 1
.

Proof.

See
[
13
, Lemma 7.5]
. This is deduced from the nonvanishing theorem of
Bump-Friedberg-Hoffstein
[
14
]
together with the Waldspurger formula
shown in
[
27
,
\lx@sectionsign
II.4 Corollary 1]
and
[
50
]
. ∎

A.4.
Relation to heights

We now consider the moduli stack
\mathcal{Y}_{0}(N)
over
{\bf{Z}}
of cyclic isogenies of degree
N
of elliptic curves

\pi:E\longrightarrow E^{\prime}
for which
\ker(\pi)
meets each irreducible component of each geometric fibre. We also consider
the moduli stack
\mathcal{X}_{0}(N)
over
{\bf{Z}}
of cyclic isogenies of degree
N
of generalized elliptic curves

\pi:E\longrightarrow E^{\prime}
for which
\ker(\pi)
meets each irreducible component of each geometric fibre.
Hence, we have the relation
\mathcal{X}_{0}(N)({\bf{C}})=X_{0}(N)=X_{K}^{\star}({\bf{C}})
, and

\mathcal{X}_{0}(N)
is smooth over
{\bf{Z}}[1/N]
, regular away from supersingular points
\underline{x}
in characteristic
p
for
p\mid N
any prime divisor.

Recall that each of the special divisors
Z(\mu,m)
has an extension
\mathcal{Z}(\mu,m)
to the integral model
\mathcal{X}=\mathcal{Y}_{0}(N)
.
More precisely, we can view each
\mathcal{Z}(\mu,m)
as a Deligne-Mumford stack which assigns to a base scheme
S
over
{\bf{Z}}

a set of pairs
(\pi:E\longrightarrow E^{\prime},\iota)
consisting of

•

A cyclic isogeny
\pi:E\longrightarrow E^{\prime}
of elliptic curves
E,E^{\prime}
over
S
of degree
N

•

An action
\iota:\mathcal{O}_{{\bf{Q}}(\sqrt{D})}\hookrightarrow\operatorname{End}(\pi)=\left\{\alpha\in\operatorname{End}(E):\pi\alpha\pi^{-1}\in\operatorname{End}(E^{\prime})\right\}

of
\mathcal{O}_{{\bf{Q}}(\sqrt{D})}
on
\pi
for which
\iota(\mathfrak{n})\ker(\pi)=0
.

Again, we take
\mathfrak{n}
to be the ideal
\mathfrak{n}=[N,(r+\sqrt{d})/2]
in
k={\bf{Q}}(\sqrt{D})
, with

\displaystyle D=-4Nm\quad\text{and}\quad\mu=\mu_{r}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\frac{r}{2N}&\\
&-\frac{r}{2N}\end{array}\right).

Remark A.4
.

Although
\mathcal{X}_{0}(N)
is not regular, we may use intersection theory for the special divisors

\mathcal{Z}(\mu,m)
and for cuspidal divisors on
\mathcal{X}_{0}(N)
. To justify this, we consider the corresponding forgetful maps

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(\mu,m)\longrightarrow\mathcal{Y}_{0}(N),\quad(\pi:E\rightarrow E^{\prime},\iota)\longmapsto(\pi:E\rightarrow E^{\prime}),

each of which is finite étale, and generally
2
to
1
.
The image of each of these forgetful maps consists of the flat closure of
\mathcal{Z}(\mu,m)
in
\mathcal{X}_{0}(N)
,
which does not intersect the boundary
\mathcal{X}_{0}(N)\backslash\mathcal{Y}_{0}(N)
,
and which moreover lies in the regular locus of
\mathcal{X}_{0}(N)
.

Let us now fix a harmonic weak Maass form
f(\tau)=f^{+}(\tau)+f^{-}(\tau)\in H_{1/2}(\overline{\omega}_{L})
as in Lemma
A.2
above.
Hence, the principal part
P_{f}(\tau)
has Fourier coefficients contained in the Hecke field
{\bf{Q}}(\phi)
, and
c_{f}^{+}(0,0)=0
.
Note that if
\phi(\tau)\in S_{2}^{\operatorname{new},\star}(\Gamma_{0}(N))

is the eigenform parametrizing a modular elliptic curve
E
over
{\bf{Q}}
of conductor
N
,
then we can deduce that the Fourier coefficients of
P_{f}(\tau)
are in fact rational integers.
Now, recall that we have associated to this harmonic weak Maass form
f
a divisor

\displaystyle Z(f)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\mu\in L^{\vee}/L}\sum\limits_{m\in{\bf{Q}}\atop m>0}c_{f}^{+}(\mu,-m)Z(\mu,m)\in\operatorname{Div}(Y_{0}(N)),

and that the corresponding reguarized theta lift
\Phi(f;\cdot)=\Phi(f;z,h)
can be identified as the automorphic Green’s function

G_{Z(f)}(\cdot)=G_{Z(f)}(z,h)
with logarithmic singularity along this divisor
Z(f)
.
As explained in
[
13
,
\lx@sectionsign
7.3]
, there exists a divisor
C(f)
on
X_{0}(N)
supported on the cusps for which the divisor

\displaystyle Z^{c}(f)

\displaystyle:=Z(f)+C(f)

has degree zero on
X_{0}(N)
. Moreover, the regularized theta lift
\Phi(f,\cdot)

can be viewed as the automorphic Green’s function
G_{Z^{c}(f)}(\cdot)
for this divisor
Z^{c}(f)
on the compactification
X_{0}(N)=X_{K}
.
We write

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{c}(f)=\mathcal{Z}(f)+\mathcal{C}(f)

to denote its flat closure in
\mathcal{X}_{0}(N)
,
and consider the corresponding arithmetic divisor

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{c}(f)

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{Z}^{c}(f),\Phi(f,\cdot))=(\mathcal{Z}^{c}(f),G_{Z^{c}(f)}(\cdot))\in\widehat{\operatorname{Ch}}^{1}(\mathcal{X}_{0}(N))_{{\bf{Q}}(\phi)}.

Given a rational number
m\in{\bf{Q}}_{>0}
and a coset
\mu\in L^{\vee}/L
, we consider this divisor on
X_{0}(N)
given by

\displaystyle y(\mu,m):=Z(\mu,m)-\frac{\deg(Z(\mu,m))}{2}\left((\infty)+(0)\right).

Note that this divisor
y(\mu,m)
has degree zero, and is invariant under the Fricke involution.
Let
\mathcal{Y}(\mu,m)
denote its flat closure in
\mathcal{X}_{0}(N)
. As explained in
[
13
,
\lx@sectionsign
7.3]
,
for each prime
p
not dividing the discriminant
D=-4Nm
, this latter divisor
\mathcal{Y}(\mu,m)
has
zero intersection with each fibre component of
\mathcal{X}_{0}(N)
over
{\bf{F}}_{p}
. We also consider the divisor defined by

\displaystyle y(f):=\sum\limits_{\mu\in L^{\vee}/L}\sum\limits_{m\in{\bf{Q}}\atop m>0}c_{f}^{+}(\mu,-m)y(\mu,m)\in\operatorname{Div}(X_{0}(N))=\operatorname{Div}(X^{\star}_{K}),

and write
\mathcal{Y}(f)
to denote its flat closure in
\mathcal{X}_{0}(N)
.

Let
J_{0}(N)
denote the Jacobian of
X_{0}(N)
, with
J_{0}(N)(F)
the
F
-rational points for some number field
F
.
Hence, elements of
J_{0}(N)(F)
correspond to divisor classes of degree zero on
X_{0}(N)
which are rational over
F
.
Now, observe that
y(f)
is a divisor of degree zero on
X_{0}(N)
which differs from the
Z^{c}(f)
by a divisor of degree
zero supported at the cusps. We deduce from the Manin-Drinfeld theorem that
y(f)
and
Z^{c}(f)
represent the same
point in
J_{0}(N)\otimes_{\bf{Z}}\overline{{\bf{Q}}}
.
Keeping with the setup of Lemmas
A.1
,
A.2
, and
A.3
above, let us now consider the generating series

\displaystyle{\mbox{{Zh}}}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\mu\in L^{\vee}/L}\sum\limits_{m\in{\bf{Q}}\atop m>0}y(\mu,m)e(m\tau){\bf{1}}_{\mu}.

By the theorem
15
15

15

This was reproven later by Borcherds using Borcherds products for weakly holomorphic forms in the
space
M_{1/2}^{!}(\omega_{L})
.
of Gross-Kohnen-Zagier
[
27
]
,
this generating series
{\mbox{{Zh}}}(\tau)
can be viewed as a modular form taking values
J_{0}(N)({\bf{Q}})\otimes_{\bf{Z}}{\bf{Q}}
.
Given a normalized newform
\phi\in S^{\operatorname{new},\star}(\Gamma_{0}(N))
as above, we can consider the corresponding
projection
{\mbox{{Zh}}}^{\phi}(\tau)
of
{\mbox{{Zh}}}(\tau)
to the
\phi
-isotypical component. The coefficients of this projection
{\mbox{{Zh}}}^{\phi}(\tau)

consist of the projections
y^{\phi}(\mu,m)
of each of the divisors
y(\mu,m)
to the
\phi
-isotypical component,

\displaystyle{\mbox{{Zh}}}^{\phi}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\mu\in L^{\vee}/L}\sum\limits_{m\in{\bf{Q}}\atop m>0}y^{\phi}(\mu,m)e(m\tau){\bf{1}}_{\mu}.

Theorem A.5
.

Let us retain the setups of Lemmas
A.1
,
A.2
, and
A.3
above, so that
g=\xi_{1/2}(f)\in S_{3/2}(\omega_{L})
is the vector-valued
Shimura lift of the Fricke-invariant newform
\phi\in S_{2}^{\operatorname{new},\star}(\Gamma_{0}(N))
. We have the identity

\displaystyle{\mbox{{Zh}}}^{\phi}(\tau)

\displaystyle=g(\tau)\otimes y(f)\in S_{3/2}(\overline{\omega}_{L})\otimes J_{0}(N)({\bf{Q}}).

In particular, the divisor
y(f)
factors through the
\phi
-isotypical component of the Jacobian
J_{0}(N)({\bf{Q}})\otimes_{\bf{Z}}\overline{\bf{Q}}
.

Proof.

See
[
13
, Theorem 7.6]
, which explains how to deduce this from
[
27
]
and
[
12
, Theorem 7.7]
. ∎

Theorem A.6
.

Let us retain the setups of Lemmas
A.1
,
A.2
, and
A.3
above,
so that
g=\xi_{1/2}(f)\in S_{3/2}(\omega_{L})
is the vector-valued Shimura lift
of the Fricke-invariant newform
\phi\in S_{2}^{\operatorname{new},\star}(\Gamma_{0}(N))
.
The Néron-Tate height
\left[y(f),y(f)\right]_{\operatorname{NT}}
of the divisor
y(f)
is given by the preliminary Gross-Zagier formula

\displaystyle\left[y(f),y(f)\right]_{\operatorname{NT}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{N}}{8\pi||g||^{2}}\cdot L^{\prime}(1/2,\phi).

Proof.

See
[
13
, Theorem 7.7]
; we modify the proof via Lemma
A.1
and Theorem
5.12
above as follows.
Observe that Theorem
A.5
implies the identification of Fourier coefficient divisors

c_{g}(\mu,m)y(f)=y^{\phi}(\mu,m)
for each of the pairs
(\mu,m)
we consider.
Using this identification together with the Manin-Drinfeld theorem, we deduce that

(94)

\displaystyle\left[y(f),y(f)\right]_{\operatorname{NT}}\cdot c_{g}(\mu,m)

\displaystyle=\left[y(f),y^{\phi}(\mu,m)\right]_{\operatorname{NT}}=\left[y(f),y(\mu,m)\right]_{\operatorname{NT}}=\left[Z^{c}(f),y(\mu,m)\right]_{\operatorname{NT}}

for each pair
(\mu,m)
contributing to the principal part
P_{f}(\tau)
of
f\in H_{1/2}(\overline{\omega}_{L})
.

Let us now fix two distinct pairs
(\mu_{0},m_{0})
and
(\mu_{1},m_{1})
, and for simplicity write

\displaystyle d(\mu_{j},m_{j})

\displaystyle=\deg Z(\mu_{j},m_{j})\quad\quad\text{for $j=0,1$}.

Define the constant

\displaystyle c

\displaystyle=c(\mu_{0},\mu_{1},m_{0},m_{1}):=d(\mu_{1},m_{1})c_{g}(\mu_{0},m_{0})-d(\mu_{0},m_{0})c_{g}(\mu_{1},m_{1}).

Consider the divisor of degree zero on
X_{0}(N)
defined by

\displaystyle Z

\displaystyle=d(\mu_{1},m_{1})y(\mu_{0},m_{0})-d(\mu_{0},m_{0})y(\mu_{1},m_{1})=d(\mu_{1},m_{1})Z(\mu_{0},m_{0})-d(\mu_{0},m_{0})Z(\mu_{1},m_{1}).

We also write
\mathcal{Z}
to denote its flat closure in
\mathcal{X}_{0}(N)
. Observe that
Z
is supported outside of the cusps of
X_{0}(N)
.
Let
M
denote the least common multiple of all the discriminants of the special divisors
Z(\mu,m)
in the support of
Z(f)
.
Assume that for each
j=0,1
the discriminant
D_{j}=-4Nm_{j}
is coprime to
MN
. This ensures that the divisors
Z
and
Z^{c}(f)
are coprime.
It also allows ensures for each prime
p
that
\mathcal{Z}
and
\mathcal{Z}^{c}(f)
have zero intersection with each fibral component of

\mathcal{X}_{0}(N)
over
{\bf{F}}_{p}
. Via
(\ref{observation})
, we compute

\displaystyle c\cdot\left[y(f),y(f)\right]_{\operatorname{NT}}

\displaystyle=\left[Z^{c}(f),d(\mu_{1},m_{1})Z(\mu_{0},m_{0})-d(\mu_{0},m_{0})Z(\mu_{1},m_{1})\right]_{\operatorname{NT}}

\displaystyle=-d(\mu_{1},m_{1})\left[\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{c}(f),\mathcal{Z}(\mu_{0},m_{0})\right]_{\operatorname{Fal}}+d(\mu_{0},m_{0})\left[\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{c}(f),\mathcal{Z}(\mu_{1},m_{1})\right]_{\operatorname{Fal}}.

Note that the cuspidal divisor
\mathcal{C}(f)
does not intersect with any of the special divisors
\mathcal{Z}(\mu,m)
.
We now apply the arithmetic height formula
(\ref{AHF})
shown
16
16

16

Here, we could also use the original
argument of Bruinier-Yang
[
13
, Theorem 7.7]
, replacing their substitution of the formula
[
13
, Theorem 4.7]

with the slightly modified version we derive in Theorem
5.12
above to obtain the same result,
i.e. without any condition on the parity of the discriminant
d_{k}
.
in Theorem
7.5

for each of the negative definite spaces
U_{j}=V\cap x(\mu_{j},m_{j})^{\perp}
and lattices

\mathcal{N}_{j}=U_{j}\cap L
and
\mathcal{P}_{j}=\mathcal{N}_{j}^{\perp}\subset L

with Lemma
A.1
(cf.
[
13
, Lemma 7.3]
) and the identification of central derivative
L
-values
(\ref{HurwitzID})

and Lemma
A.2
(i) and (iii) for the cuspidal form
f\in H_{1/2}(\omega_{L})
to each index
j=0,1
to obtain the arithmetic height formulae

\displaystyle\left[\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{c}(f),\mathcal{Z}(\mu_{j},m_{j})\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\Phi(f,Z(\mu_{j},m_{j}))+\left[\mathcal{Z}(f),\mathcal{Z}(\mu_{j},m_{j})\right]_{\operatorname{fin}}+\left[\mathcal{C}(f),\mathcal{Z}(\mu_{j},m_{j})\right]_{\operatorname{fin}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{d(\mu_{j},m_{j})}{2}\left(L^{\prime}(0,\xi_{1/2}(f),U_{j})+c_{f}^{+}(0,0)\cdot\kappa_{\mathcal{N}_{j}}(0,0)\right)+\left[\mathcal{C}(f),\mathcal{Z}(\mu_{j},m_{j})\right]_{\operatorname{fin}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{d(\mu_{j},m_{j})}{2}\cdot L^{\prime}(0,\xi_{1/2}(f),U_{j})

\displaystyle=-\frac{d(\mu_{j},m_{j})}{2||g||^{2}}\cdot\frac{N^{\frac{1}{2}}}{4\pi}\cdot\frac{c_{g}(\mu_{j},m_{j})L^{\prime}(1/2,\phi)}{\deg Z(\mu_{j},m_{j})}

\displaystyle=-\frac{N^{\frac{1}{2}}}{8\pi||g||^{2}}\cdot c_{g}(\mu_{j},m_{j})\cdot L^{\prime}(1/2,\phi)

so that

(95)

\displaystyle

\displaystyle c\cdot\left[y(f),y(f)\right]_{\operatorname{NT}}=-d(\mu_{1},m_{1})\left[\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{c}(f),\mathcal{Z}(\mu_{0},m_{0})\right]_{\operatorname{Fal}}+d(\mu_{0},m_{0})\left[\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{c}(f),\mathcal{Z}(\mu_{1},m_{1})\right]_{\operatorname{Fal}}

\displaystyle=d(\mu_{1},m_{1})\cdot\frac{N^{\frac{1}{2}}}{8\pi||g||^{2}}\cdot c_{g}(\mu_{0},m_{0})\cdot L^{\prime}(1/2,\phi)-d(\mu_{0},m_{0})\cdot\frac{N^{\frac{1}{2}}}{8\pi||8||^{2}}\cdot c_{g}(\mu_{1},m_{1})\cdot L^{\prime}(1/2,\phi).

Now, it is not hard to show that we can choose the pairs
(\mu_{0},m_{0})
and
(\mu_{1},m_{1})
in such a way that the constant

c=c(\mu_{0},\mu_{1},m_{0},m_{1})
does not vanish. We can then deduce from the calculation
(\ref{hc})
that

\displaystyle c\cdot\left[y(f),y(f)\right]_{\operatorname{NT}}

\displaystyle=c\cdot\frac{\sqrt{N}}{8\pi||g||^{2}}\cdot L^{\prime}(1/2,\phi),

so that the claimed formula follows after dividing out by the nonzero constant
c
. ∎

Corollary A.7
.

For any coset
\mu\in L^{\vee}/L
and positive integer
m\in Q(\mu)+{\bf{Z}}
, we have for
D:=-4Nm
that

\displaystyle\left[y^{\phi}(\mu,m),y^{\phi}(\mu,m)\right]_{\operatorname{NT}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{|D|}}{8\pi^{2}||\phi||^{2}}\cdot L(1/2,\phi\otimes\eta_{D})\cdot L^{\prime}(1/2,\phi).

Proof.

Cf.
[
13
, Corollary 7.8]
. We deduce this from Theorem
A.6

using the relation
y^{\phi}(\mu,m)=c_{g}(\mu,m)\cdot y(f)
with the Waldspurger-like
formula theorem shown shown in Gross-Kohnen-Zagier
[
27
, II,
\lx@sectionsign
4 Corollary 1]
:

\displaystyle\frac{c_{j_{g}}(\mu,m)^{2}}{\langle j_{g},j_{g}\rangle}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{|D|}}{2\pi}\cdot\frac{L(1/2,\phi\otimes\eta_{D})}{\langle\phi,\phi\rangle},

where
j_{g}\in J_{2,N}^{\operatorname{new},\operatorname{cusp}}
denotes the Jacobi form corresponding to
g
.
Using that the Petersson norm
||g||
is equal to
N^{\frac{1}{4}}||j_{g}||
,
by Eichler-Zagier
[
23
, Theorem 5.3]
, we derive from this the coefficient formula

(96)

\displaystyle c_{g}(\mu,m)^{2}=c_{j_{g}}(\mu,m)^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{||j_{g}||^{2}}{2\pi||\phi||^{2}}\cdot|D|^{\frac{1}{2}}\cdot L(1/2,\phi\otimes\eta_{D})=\frac{||g||^{2}}{2\pi N^{\frac{1}{2}}||\phi||^{2}}\cdot|D|^{\frac{1}{2}}\cdot L(1/2,\phi\otimes\eta_{D})

to get

\displaystyle\left[y^{\phi}(\mu,m),y^{\phi}(\mu,m)\right]_{\operatorname{NT}}=\left[c_{g}(\mu,m)y(f),c_{g}(\mu,m)y(f)\right]_{\operatorname{NT}}=c_{g}(\mu,m)^{2}\cdot\left[y(f),y(f)\right]_{\operatorname{NT}}

\displaystyle=\frac{|D|^{\frac{1}{2}}||g||^{2}}{2\pi N^{\frac{1}{2}}||\phi||^{2}}\cdot L(1/2,\phi\otimes\eta_{D})\cdot\frac{N^{\frac{1}{2}}}{4\pi||g||^{2}}\cdot L^{\prime}(1/2,\phi)=\frac{|D|^{\frac{1}{2}}}{8\pi^{2}||\phi||^{2}}\cdot L(1/2,\phi\otimes\eta_{D})\cdot L^{\prime}(1/2,\phi).

∎

A.5.
Class group twists

We now explain how to adapt Theorem
A.6
and Corollary
A.9
to derive the full Gross-Zagier formula
[
26
, I Theorem (6.3)]
,
which applies to twists by any character
\chi
of the ideal class group
C(\mathcal{O}_{k})
for
k={\bf{Q}}(\sqrt{D})
as we consider above.
Note that this general form of the Gross-Zagier formula is not derived in
[
13
]
.
We take for granted all of the discussion above leading to Corollary
A.9
,
and fix a set of representatives as in
(\ref{representatives})
above.
Hence, we choose for each class
A
a point
x_{A}\in\Omega_{A,\mu,m}({\bf{Q}})

which gives rise to a negative definite space
U_{A}=V\cap x_{A}^{\perp}
.
Note that each space
U_{A}
corresponds to a fractional ideal representative of the class
A
of
C(\mathcal{O}_{k})
.
We also obtain a negative definite lattice
\mathcal{N}_{A}=U_{A}\cap L_{A}

and a positive definite lattice
\mathcal{P}_{A}=\mathcal{N}_{A}^{\perp}\subset L_{A}
.
Recall that in
(\ref{representatives})
, we also fix for each class
A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})

a divisor
Z_{A}(\mu,m)=Z_{A}(\mu_{r},-D/4N)
on
X_{K_{A}}\cong Y_{0}(N)
.
Fixing
f\in H_{1/2}(\omega_{L})
as in Lemma
A.2
, and taking the restriction
f_{A}
to the sublattice

L_{A}\subset L
as in Lemma
5.7
and
(\ref{IPrelations})
we then define the corresponding divisor

\displaystyle Z_{A}(f_{A})

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\mu\in L_{A}^{\vee}/L_{A}}\sum\limits_{m\in{\bf{Q}}\atop m>0}c_{f_{A}}^{+}(\mu,-m)Z_{A}(\mu,m)\in\operatorname{Div}(Y_{0}(N)).

There exists for each
A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})
a divisor
C_{A}(f)
supported on the cusps
X_{0}(N)\backslash Y_{0}(N)
for which the divisor

Z_{A}^{c}(f)=Z_{A}(f)+C_{A}(f)
on
X_{0}(N)
has degree zero. Again, the regularized theta lift
\Phi(f_{A},\cdot)
determines the automorphic
Green’s function for this divisor
Z_{A}^{c}(f_{A})\in\operatorname{Div}(X_{0}(N))
. Writing

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{A}^{c}(f_{A})=\mathcal{Z}_{A}(f_{A})+\mathcal{C}_{A}(f_{A})

for the extension to the flat closure in
\mathcal{X}_{0}(N)
of this divisor
Z_{A}^{c}(f_{A})
, we obtain an arithmetic divisor

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}^{c}_{A}(f_{A})

\displaystyle:=(\mathcal{Z}_{A}^{c}(f_{A}),\Phi(f_{A},\cdot))=(\mathcal{Z}_{A}^{c}(f_{A}),G_{Z_{A}^{c}(f_{A})}(\cdot))\in\widehat{\operatorname{Ch}}^{1}(\mathcal{X}_{0}(N)).

We also consider for each class
A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})
the Fricke-invariant divisor of degree zero on
X_{0}(N)
defined by

\displaystyle y_{A}(\mu,m)

\displaystyle=Z_{A}(\mu,m)-\frac{\deg Z_{A}(\mu,m))}{2}\left((\infty)+(0)\right),

with
\mathcal{Y}_{A}(\mu,m)
its flat closure in
\mathcal{X}_{0}(N)
. We also consider

\displaystyle y_{A}(f_{A})

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\mu\in L_{A}^{\vee}/L_{A}}\sum\limits_{m\in{\bf{Q}}\atop m>0}c_{f_{A}}^{+}(\mu,-m)y_{A}(\mu,m)\in\operatorname{Div}(X_{0}(N)),

with
\mathcal{Y}_{A}(f)
its flat closure in
\mathcal{X}_{0}(N)
.

A.5.1.
Decompositions of basechange
L
-functions

For each class, a minor variation of the argument of Lemma
A.1
allows us to make the identification

\displaystyle L(s,g,U_{A})

\displaystyle=(4\pi m)^{-\left(\frac{s+1}{2}\right)}\Gamma\left(\frac{s+1}{2}\right)\sum\limits_{m\geq 1}\sum\limits_{\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{A}^{\vee}/\mathcal{P}_{A}}r_{\mathcal{P}_{A}}(\mu,m)c_{g_{A}}(\mu,m)m^{-\left(\frac{s+1}{2}\right)}

\displaystyle=(4\pi)^{-\left(\frac{s+1}{2}\right)}\Gamma\left(\frac{s+1}{2}\right)\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\mathcal{P}_{A}^{\vee}}c_{g_{A}}(\lambda,Q(\lambda))Q(\lambda)^{-\left(\frac{s+1}{2}\right)}

\displaystyle=(4\pi m)^{-\left(\frac{s+1}{2}\right)}\Gamma\left(\frac{s+1}{2}\right)\frac{L_{A}(s+1/2,\phi)}{L(s+1,\eta_{D})}.

Here, we view
g=g_{A}\in S_{3/2}(\overline{\omega}_{L_{A}})
as a form of weight
3/2
and representation
\omega_{\mathcal{P}_{A}\oplus\mathcal{N}_{A}}

via Lemma
5.7
and
(\ref{IPrelations})
(cf.
[
13
, Lemma 3.1]
),
and we argue as in
[
13
, Lemma 7.3]
that
c_{g_{A}}((\lambda),\lambda)=0
for all
\lambda\in\mathcal{P}_{A}^{\vee}

unless
\lambda\in\mathcal{P}_{A}^{\vee}\cap L_{A}^{\vee}={\bf{Z}}x_{A}
. We then define
L_{A}(s,\phi)
by the corresponding relation

\displaystyle L(s,\mathcal{S}_{\mu,m}(g_{A}))=c_{g_{A}}(\mu,m)\cdot L_{A}(s,\phi).

Using the same argument as for
(\ref{HurwitzID})
, we compute

\displaystyle L^{\prime}(0,g_{A},U_{A})

\displaystyle=(4\pi m)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)\frac{L_{A}^{\prime}(1/2,\phi)}{L(1,\eta_{D})}=\frac{\sqrt{Nm}}{2\sqrt{m}}\frac{c_{g_{A}}(\mu,m)L^{\prime}_{A}(1/2,\phi)}{2\pi H(D)}=\frac{N^{\frac{1}{2}}}{4\pi}\cdot\frac{c_{g_{A}}(\mu,m)L^{\prime}_{A}(1/2,\phi)}{\deg Z_{A}(\mu,m)},

which via Lemma
A.2
(i) and (iii) is the same as

(97)

\displaystyle L^{\prime}(0,g_{A},U_{A})

\displaystyle=\frac{N^{\frac{1}{2}}}{4\pi||g_{A}||^{2}}\cdot\frac{c_{g_{A}}(\mu,m)L^{\prime}_{A}(1/2,\phi)}{\deg Z_{A}(\mu,m)}.

We can intepret
L_{A}(s,\phi)
as the partial/class basechange
L
-function of
\phi
to
k
with our unitary normalizations for
the standard
L
-function (cf.
[
26
]
), so that in this setup we have the identifications of
L
-functions

\displaystyle L(s,\phi\times\theta(\chi))

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})}\chi(A)L_{A}(s,\phi)L_{A}(s,\phi\otimes\eta_{k})=\sum\limits_{A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})}\chi(A)L(s,\phi\times\theta_{A}).

To be clear, we define each
L_{A}(s,\phi)
according to the partition of the lattice

L=\bigoplus_{A\in C(O_{k})\cong\mathcal{Q}_{D}}L_{A}
so that

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})}L_{A}(s,\phi)

\displaystyle=L(s,\phi)

is the finite part
L(s,\phi)
of the standard
L
-function
\Lambda(s,\phi)=L_{\infty}(s,\phi)L(s,\phi)
.
We can then define
L_{A}(s,\phi\otimes\eta)
simply as the quadratic twist.
Writing
\Pi=\operatorname{BC}_{k/{\bf{Q}}}(\pi(\phi))
to denote the quadratic basechange lifting of the cuspidal automorphic representation

\pi(\phi)
of
\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\bf{A}})
associated to
\phi
to
\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\bf{A}}_{k})
,with standard
L
-function

\displaystyle\Lambda(s,\Pi)=L_{\infty}(s,\Pi)L(s,\Pi)=\Lambda(s,\phi)\Lambda(s,\phi\otimes\eta_{k})=L_{\infty}(s,\phi)L(s,\pi)L_{\infty}(s,\phi\otimes\eta_{k})L(s,\phi\otimes\eta_{k}),

we have an identification of
L
-functions

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})}L_{A}(s,\phi)L_{A}(s,\phi\otimes\eta_{k})

\displaystyle=L(s,\phi)L(s,\phi\otimes\eta_{k})=L(s,\Pi).

Moreover, we have for any character
\chi\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})^{\vee}
the equivalence of
L
-functions

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})}\chi(A)L_{A}(s,\phi)L_{A}(s,\phi\otimes\eta_{k})

\displaystyle=L(s,\Pi\otimes\chi).

Note that we can justify this latter identification after opening up Dirichlet series expansions for
\Re(s)>1
to see that both sides
describe the Dirichlet series expansion of the basechange
L
-function
L(s,\Pi\otimes\chi)
over nonzero ideals

\mathfrak{a}\subset\mathcal{O}_{k}
corresponding to sums over partial basechange
L
-functions (first for
\Re(s)>1
)

\displaystyle L_{A}(s,\Pi\otimes\chi)=\chi(A)L_{A}(s,\Pi)

\displaystyle=\chi(A)\sum\limits_{\mathfrak{a}\subset\mathcal{O}_{k}\backslash\{0\}\atop[\mathfrak{a}]=A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})}\frac{a_{\phi}({\bf{N}}\mathfrak{a})}{{\bf{N}}\mathfrak{a}^{s}}=\chi(A)\sum\limits_{\mathfrak{a}\subset\mathcal{O}_{k}\backslash\{0\}\atop[\mathfrak{a}]=A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})}\frac{a_{\Pi}(\mathfrak{a})}{{\bf{N}}\mathfrak{a}^{s}}

determined by sums over ideals
\mathfrak{a}\in A
in each ideal class
A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})
.
That is, we have for each class
A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})
the identification of partial/class basechange
L
-functions (first for
\Re(s)>1
)

\displaystyle L_{A}(s,\phi)L_{A}(s,\phi\otimes\eta)

\displaystyle=L_{A}(s,\Pi)=\sum\limits_{\mathfrak{a}\subset\mathcal{O}_{k}\backslash\{0\}\atop[\mathfrak{a}]=A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})}\frac{a_{\phi}({\bf{N}}\mathfrak{a})}{{\bf{N}}\mathfrak{a}^{s}}=\sum\limits_{\mathfrak{a}\subset\mathcal{O}_{k}\backslash\{0\}\atop[\mathfrak{a}]=A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})}\frac{a_{\Pi}(\mathfrak{a})}{{\bf{N}}\mathfrak{a}^{s}}.

Hence, we have the identifications of quadratic basechange and Rankin-Selberg
L
-functions

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})}\chi(A)L_{A}(s,\phi)L_{A}(s,\phi\otimes\eta_{k})

\displaystyle=L(s,\Pi\otimes\chi):=\sum\limits_{A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})}L_{A}(s,\Pi\otimes\chi)=\sum\limits_{A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})}\chi(A)L_{A}(s,\Pi)

\displaystyle=L(s,\phi\times\theta(\chi)):=\sum\limits_{A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})}\chi(A)L(s,\phi\times\theta_{A}).

Remark A.8
.

Note that this realization of the Rankin-Selberg
L
-function

L(s,\phi\times\theta(\chi))
is distinct from that considered in
Gross-Zagier
[
26
]
, where the corresponding
L_{\mathcal{A}}(s,f)
for
\mathcal{A}\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})
denotes to the
partial Rankin-Selberg
L
-function
L(s-1/2,\phi\times\theta_{A})
in our description above. In particular, the
L_{A}(s,\phi)
here
forms a summand of the
\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\bf{A}})
-automorphic
L
-function
L(s,\phi)
as described in Lemma
A.1
.
In other words, we are working with some explicit form of the basechange
equivalence
L(s,\Pi\otimes\chi)=L(s,\phi\times\theta(\chi))
in this setup.

A.5.2.
Relation to arithmetic heights of Heegner divisors

We argue as in the proof of Theorem
A.6
that when
D
is prime to
2N
,
we have for each class
A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})
the corresponding arithmetic height formulae

\displaystyle\left[\widehat{\mathcal{Z}}_{A}^{c}(f_{A}),\mathcal{Z}_{A}(\mu,m)\right]_{\operatorname{Fal}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{N^{\frac{1}{2}}}{8\pi||g_{A}||^{2}}\cdot c_{A}(\mu,m)\cdot L^{\prime}_{A}(1/2,\phi)

and

\displaystyle\left[y_{A}(f_{A}),y_{A}(f_{A})\right]_{\operatorname{NT}}

\displaystyle=\frac{N^{\frac{1}{2}}}{8\pi||g_{A}||}\cdot L_{A}^{\prime}(1/2,\phi).

We then argue as in Corollary
A.6
that we can use the Waldspurger-like formula
(\ref{GKZ})
to derive the formula

(98)

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\left[y_{A}^{\phi}(\mu,m),y_{A}^{\phi}(\mu,m)\right]_{\operatorname{NT}}=\left[c_{g_{A}}(\mu,m)y_{A}(f_{A}),c_{A}(\mu,m)y_{A}(f_{A})\right]_{\operatorname{NT}}=c_{g_{A}}(\mu,m)^{2}\cdot\left[y_{A}(f_{A}),y_{A}(f_{A})\right]_{\operatorname{NT}}

\displaystyle=\frac{|D|^{\frac{1}{2}}||g_{A}||^{2}}{2\pi N^{\frac{1}{2}}||\phi||^{2}}\cdot L(1/2,\phi\otimes\eta_{D})\cdot\frac{N^{\frac{1}{2}}}{4\pi||g_{A}||^{2}}\cdot L_{A}^{\prime}(1/2,\phi)=\frac{|D|^{\frac{1}{2}}}{8\pi^{2}||\phi||^{2}}\cdot L(1/2,\phi\otimes\eta_{D})\cdot L_{A}^{\prime}(1/2,\phi)

for the
\phi
-isotypical components. Taking the
\chi
-twisted linear combination for any
\chi\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})^{\vee}
, we obtain

(99)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{h_{k}}\cdot\widehat{h}_{k}(y_{\chi}^{\phi})=\left[y_{\chi}^{\phi},y_{\chi}^{\phi}\right]_{\operatorname{NT}}

\displaystyle:=\sum\limits_{A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})}\chi(A)\left[y_{A}^{\phi}(\mu,m),y_{A}^{\phi}(\mu,m)\right]_{\operatorname{NT}}

\displaystyle=\frac{|D|^{\frac{1}{2}}}{8\pi^{2}||\phi||^{2}}\cdot L(1/2,\phi\otimes\eta_{D})\sum\limits_{A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})}\chi(A)L_{A}^{\prime}(1/2,\phi)

\displaystyle=\frac{|D|^{\frac{1}{2}}}{8\pi^{2}||\phi||^{2}}\cdot L^{\prime}(1/2,\Pi\otimes\chi)=\frac{|D|^{\frac{1}{2}}}{8\pi^{2}||\phi||^{2}}\cdot L^{\prime}(1/2,\phi\times\theta(\chi)).

This is the same as the formula of Gross-Zagier
[
26
, Theorem I (6.3)]
when
|D|>4
:

Theorem A.9

(Gross-Zagier)
.

Assume the fundamental discriminant
D
is coprime to
2N
, and that
|D|>4
.
Let
f\in H_{1/2}(\overline{\omega}_{L})
as described in Lemma
A.2
with
g/||g||^{2}=\xi_{1/2}(f)\in S_{3/2}(\overline{\omega}_{L})

and
\phi\in S_{2}^{\operatorname{new},\star}(\Gamma_{0}(N))
the corresponding Shimura lift.
For
\chi
any character of the ideal class group
C(\mathcal{O}_{k})
of
k={\bf{Q}}(\sqrt{D})
, we have that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{h_{k}}\cdot\widehat{h}_{k}(y_{\chi}^{\phi})=\left[y_{\chi}^{\phi},y_{\chi}^{\phi}\right]_{\operatorname{NT}}:=\sum\limits_{A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})}\chi(A)\left[y_{A}^{\phi}(\mu,m),y_{A}^{\phi}(\mu,m)\right]_{\operatorname{NT}}

\displaystyle=\frac{|D|^{\frac{1}{2}}}{8\pi^{2}||\phi||^{2}}\cdot L^{\prime}(1/2,\phi\times\theta(\chi)).

Proof.

See
[
26
, Theorem I (6.3)]
, as presented in Theorem
1.1
,
which gives a
distinct
proof, e.g. without using the Shimura correspondence.
This approach is also distinct from the re-proofs and generalizations established by Zhang
[
65
]
,
[
66
]
,
[
63
]
,
[
64
]

and Yuan-Zhang-Zhang
[
60
]
for Shimura curves over totally real fields. ∎

Phrasing the result in terms of completed
L
-functions

\displaystyle\Lambda^{\prime}(1/2,\phi\times\theta(\chi))

\displaystyle=\Lambda(1,\eta_{k})L^{\prime}(1/2,\phi\times\theta(\chi))=\frac{2\pi h_{k}}{w_{k}}\cdot L^{\prime}(1/2,\phi\times\theta(\chi))

and comparing with Theorem
7.8
and Corollary
7.9
, we obtain the following relations.

Corollary A.10
.

We have the arithmetic height formula

\displaystyle 2\pi\cdot\widehat{h}_{k}(y_{\chi}^{\phi})

\displaystyle=\frac{|D|^{\frac{1}{2}}}{8\pi^{2}||\phi||^{2}}\cdot\Lambda^{\prime}(1/2,\phi\times\theta(\chi))=-2\pi\sum\limits_{A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})}\chi(A)\left[\widehat{Z}^{c}(f_{0,A}):\mathcal{Z}(V_{A,0})\right],

where the twisted sum of special arithmetic divisors on the right-hand side represents the arithmetic Hirzebruch-Zagier
divisors on
X_{0}(N)\times X_{0}(N)
described in Remark
7.7
, Theorem
7.8
, and Corollary
7.9
above.

Appendix B
Relation to metaplectic Fourier coefficients

We now use the connection to the regularized theta lifts
\Phi(f_{1/2},z)=G_{Z(f_{1/2})}(z)\in L^{2}(X_{0}(N))
of Theorem
A.9

and
\Phi(f_{0},z)=G_{Z(f_{0})}(z)\in L^{2}(X_{0}(N)\times X_{0}(N))
of Theorem
7.8
to relate the central derivative values

\Lambda^{\prime}(1/2,\phi\times\theta(\chi))
to Fourier coefficients of half-integral weight forms; cf. the works
[
12
]
,
[
35
]
, and
[
11
]
.

B.1.
The setting of signature
(1,2)
with
\Phi(f_{1/2},z)\in L^{1+\varepsilon}(X_{0}(N))

Let us first recall the harmonic weak Maass form described in Lemma
A.2
, adapted to class group representatives as in Theorem
A.9
above.
Hence, we retain all of the setup of the previous section, with
(V,Q)=(\operatorname{Mat}_{2\times 2}^{\operatorname{tr=0}}({\bf{Q}}),N\det(\cdot))
,
the lattice
L\subset V
giving rise to the congruence subgroup
\Gamma_{0}(N)\subseteq\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\bf{Z}})
,
and the sublattices
L_{A}\subset V
corresponding to ideal classes
A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})
described in
(\ref{LAn=1})
.
To be clear, we fix a cuspidal newform
\phi\in S_{2}^{\operatorname{new},\star}(\Gamma_{0}(N))
which is invariant under the Fricke involution
w_{N}
.

B.1.1.
Quadratic sublattices

If
k
is an imaginary quadratic field of discriminant
d_{k}<0
, we fix a set of lattice representatives as in
(\ref{LAn=1})
above, together with positive-norm
vectors
x\in\Omega_{A,\mu,m}({\bf{Q}})
and Heegner divisors
Z_{A}(\mu,m)\in Y_{0}(N)
as in
(\ref{representatives})
.
If
k
is a real quadratic field of discriminant
d_{k}>0
, we fix for each class
A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})

an integer ideal representative
\mathfrak{a}\subset\mathcal{O}_{k}
.
We then fix a Witt decomposition

\displaystyle V_{A}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{K}_{A}\oplus{\bf{Q}}e_{A,1}\oplus{\bf{Q}}e_{A,2},

so that the orthogonal complement
W_{A}=\mathcal{K}_{A}^{\perp}\subset V
of the Lorentzian
subspace
\mathcal{K}_{A}
of signature
(1,0)
is isomorphic to the signature
(1,1)
subspace determined by

(\mathfrak{a}_{\bf{Q}},Q_{\mathfrak{a}})=(\mathfrak{a}_{\bf{Q}},{\bf{N}}_{k/{\bf{Q}}}(\cdot)/{\bf{N}}\mathfrak{a})
.
Here, we write
e_{A,j}\in V
to denote the corresponding nonzero isotropic basis vectors with

(e_{A,j},e_{A,j})=0
for
j=1,2
and
(e_{A,1},e_{A,2})=1
. We then write
L_{A,W}=L\cap W_{A}

for the corresponding lattice as in the discussion above, leading to Theorem
5.14
(for the case of
n=1
).
Note that
(L_{A,W},Q|_{W_{A}})
corresponds to the quadratic lattice
(\mathfrak{a},Q_{\mathfrak{a}}(\cdot))
.
Note as well that this quadratic lattice
L_{A,W}
is isomorphic to the sublattice
L_{A}\subset V
defined in
(\ref{LAn=1})
above.
Hence, for either case on of the quadratic field
k
, we have for each class
A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})
a corresponding
sublattice
L_{A}\subset L
corresponding to an integral ideal representative
\mathfrak{a}\subset\mathcal{O}_{k}
.

B.1.2.
Vector-valued Shimura lifts

Fix a quadratic field
k
, real or imaginary, with discriminant
d_{k}
, and character
\eta_{k}(\cdot)=(\frac{d_{k}}{\cdot})
.
For each ideal class
A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})
, we let
g_{A}\in S_{3/2}^{\operatorname{new}}(\omega_{L_{A}})
denote the
holomorphic vector-valued cusp form of weight
3/2
and (Weil) representation
\omega_{L_{A}}
associated to
\phi

by the Shimura correspondence via the isomorphism
(\ref{Shimura})
. By Lemma
A.2
(cf.
[
13
, Lemma 7.4]
,

[
12
, Lemma 7.3]
), there exists for each class
A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})
a harmonic weak Maass form

f_{1/2,A}\in H_{1/2}(\omega_{L_{A}})
such that

(i)

We have the relation
\xi_{1/2}(f_{1/2,A})=g_{A}/||g_{A}||^{2}
.

(ii)

The Fourier coefficients
c_{f_{1/2,A}}^{+}(\mu,m)
of the principal/holomorphic part
f^{+}_{1/2,A}
lie in
{\bf{Q}}(\phi)
.

(iii)

The constant coefficient
c_{f_{1/2,A}}^{+}(0,0)
vanishes.

Proposition B.1
.

For each class
A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{K})
, consider the corresponding regularized theta lift

\displaystyle\Phi(f_{1/2,A},z)=\Phi(f_{1/2,A},z,1)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{F}}^{\star}\langle\langle f_{1/2,A}(\tau),\theta_{L_{A}}(\tau,z,1)\rangle\rangle d\mu(\tau)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{CT}_{s=0}\left(\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\int\limits_{\mathcal{F}_{T}}\langle\langle f_{1/2,A}(\tau),\theta_{L_{A}}(\tau,z,1)\rangle\rangle v^{-s}d\mu(\tau)\right)

as a function of the variable
z\in D(V)=D^{\pm}(V)\cong\mathfrak{H}
. The following assertions are true.

(i)

\Phi(f_{1/2,A},z)
determines a (weight zero) modular function on
X_{0}(N)
with Laplacian eigenvalue
0
.

(ii)

\Phi(f_{1/2,A},z)
is the automorphic Green’s function for the special divisor
\mathcal{Z}^{c}(f_{1/2,A})
on
\mathcal{X}_{0}(N)
.

(iii)

\Phi(f_{1/2,A},z)\in L^{1+\varepsilon}(X_{0}(N))
.

Proof.

All three assertions follow from Theorem
4.2
, using the identification
X_{K_{A}}\cong Y_{0}(N)
and extending to the cusps,
as well as that
c_{f_{1/2,A}}^{+}(0,0)=0
. ∎

Remark B.2
.

Note that for
A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})
with
k
real quadratic, the Fourier series expansions of these modular functions
\Phi(f_{1/2,A},z)

can be calculated according to
[
12
, Theorem 5.3, cf. (5.10)]
. More precisely, let
r\in{\bf{Z}}
be any integer such that
d_{k}\equiv r^{2}\bmod 4N
.
Let us for each lattice
L_{A}
as defined in
(\ref{LAn=1})
above fix a primitive isotropic vector
l_{A}\in L_{A}
and
l_{A}^{\prime}\in L_{A}^{\vee}

so that
(l_{A},l_{A}^{\prime})=1
. We then have the corresponding positive definite subspace defined by
\mathcal{K}_{A}=L_{A}\cap l_{A}^{\perp}\cap l_{A}^{\prime}
.
We choose these vectors so that

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{A}={\bf{Z}}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
0&-1\end{array}\right).

Given a vector
\lambda\in\mathcal{K}_{A}\otimes{\bf{R}}
, we write
\lambda>0
if it is a positive multiple of

\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
0&-1\end{array}\right)
. We then have

\displaystyle\Phi(f_{1/2,A},z)

\displaystyle=-4\sum\limits_{\lambda\in\mathcal{K}_{A}\atop\lambda>0}\sum\limits_{b\bmod d_{k}}\eta_{k}(b)c_{f_{1/2,A}}^{+}\left(\frac{d_{k}\lambda^{2}}{2},r\lambda\right)\log\left|1-e\left((\lambda,z)+\frac{b}{d_{k}}\right)\right|.

B.1.3.
Relation to metaplectic Fourier coefficients

If we knew the functions
\Phi(f_{1/2,A},z)\in L^{1+\varepsilon}(X_{0}(N))
were square integrable,
then a straightforward generalization of the theorem of Katok-Sarnak
[
35
]
would relate the sums over CM cycles
and geodesic sets we compute in Theorems
5.12
and
5.14
for the case of
n=1
to twisted sums over the half-integal weight
forms
F_{j}
related to
\Phi(f_{1/2,A},z)
by the Shimura correspondence
\operatorname{Shim}(F_{j})=\Phi(f_{1/2,A},\cdot)
of the Fourier
coefficients
c_{F_{j}}(d_{k})\overline{c_{F_{j}}(1)}
. On the other hand, we have the following more precise result in this direction. To describe it,
we first need to describe the following trace coefficients
\operatorname{tr}_{\mu,m}(\Phi(f_{1/2,A}))
for each case on the quadratic field
k
.

When
d_{k}<0
so that
k
is imaginary quadratic, define for each
m\in{\bf{Q}}_{>0}
and
\mu\in L_{A}^{\vee}/L_{A}
the trace function

\displaystyle\operatorname{tr}_{\mu,m}\left(\Phi(f_{1/2,A})\right)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{x\in\Gamma_{0}(N)\backslash\Omega_{A,\mu,m}({\bf{Q}})}\frac{1}{\#\operatorname{Stab}_{\Gamma_{0}(N)}(x)}\cdot\Phi\left(f_{1/2,A},\iota(D(V)_{x})\right).

Here, we write
\iota
to denote the identification
\iota:D(V)\cong\mathfrak{H}
. We also write

\iota(D(V)_{x})
to denote the image of
D(V)_{x}
in the modular curve
X_{0}(N)
, so that the special
(Heegner) divisors
Z_{A}(\mu,m)
we consider above is

\displaystyle Z_{A}(\mu,m)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{x\in\Gamma_{0}(N)\backslash\Omega_{A,\mu,m}({\bf{Q}})}\iota(D(V)_{x}).

When
d_{k}>0
so that
k
is real quadratic, we fix a vector
x\in V({\bf{Q}})
of positive length
m\in{\bf{Q}}
, and consider
the corresponding geodesic in
D(V)\cong\mathfrak{H}
defined by
\gamma_{x}=D(V)_{x}
. Here, we fix the following orientation:

\displaystyle x=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
0&-1\end{array}\right)\quad\implies\quad\gamma_{x}=\pm(0,i\infty)\quad\text{is the imaginary axis with the orientation $\pm$}.

The orientation-preserving action of
\operatorname{SL}_{2}({\bf{R}})
then induces an orientation on each geodesic
\gamma_{x}
. We also define

\displaystyle dz_{x}

\displaystyle=\pm dz/\sqrt{m}z\quad\text{for}\quad x=\pm\sqrt{\frac{m}{N}}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
0&-1\end{array}\right).

Let
\alpha(x)=\operatorname{Stab}_{\Gamma_{0}(N)}(x)\backslash\gamma_{x}
, as well as its image in
X_{0}(N)
. Note that when
the stabilizer
\operatorname{Stab}_{\Gamma_{0}(N)}(x)
is infinite,
\alpha(x)
determines a closed geodesic in
X_{0}(N)
.
We then define for each
m\in{\bf{Q}}_{>0}
and
\mu\in L_{A}^{\vee}/L_{A}
the corresponding trace function

\displaystyle\operatorname{tr}_{\mu,m}\left(\Phi(f_{1/2,A})\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\sum\limits_{x\in\Gamma_{0}(N)\backslash\Omega_{A,\mu,m}({\bf{Q}})}~~\int\limits_{\alpha(x)}\Phi(f_{1/2,A},z)dz_{x}.

Here, each
\alpha(x)
is a closed geodesic, equivalently,
x^{\perp}
is nonsplit over
{\bf{Q}}
, and we again write

\displaystyle\Omega_{A,\mu,m}({\bf{Q}})

\displaystyle=\left\{x\in\mu+L_{A}:Q(x)=m\right\}

for the corresponding quadric
17
17

17

In the remaining case where
\alpha(x)
is an infinite geodesic, equivalently
when
\operatorname{Stab}_{\Gamma_{0}(N)}(x)
is trivial and the complement
x^{\perp}\subset V

determines an isotropic quadratic space – which only happens if
Q(x)\in{\bf{N}}({\bf{Q}}^{\times})^{2}
– then the trace
is defined according to the regularization procedure described in
[
11
,
\lx@sectionsign
3.3]
, and the corresponding complementary trace in
[
11
,
\lx@sectionsign
3.3.2]
.
However, as the orthogonal complement
x^{\perp}\subset V
always determines an anisotropic subspace

(W_{A},Q|_{W_{A}})\cong(\mathfrak{a}_{\bf{Q}},Q_{\mathfrak{a}})
in the setup we consider above, we do not need to consider these variations of the trace here.
.

Theorem B.3

(Bruinier-Funke-Imamoglu)
.

Fix any class
A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})
. The generating series defined by

\displaystyle I_{1/2,\mu}(\Phi(f_{1/2,A},\cdot),\tau)

\displaystyle=-2\sqrt{v}\operatorname{tr}_{\mu,0}(\Phi(f_{1/2,A}))+\sum\limits_{m<0}\operatorname{tr}_{\mu,m}(\Phi(f_{1/2,A}))\frac{\operatorname{erf}(2\sqrt{\pi|m|v})}{2\sqrt{|m|}}e(m\tau)+\sum\limits_{m>0}\operatorname{tr}_{\mu,m}(\Phi(f_{1/2,A}))e(m\tau)

determines the
\mu
part of a harmonic weak Maass form in
H_{1/2}(\omega_{L_{A}})
,

\displaystyle I_{1/2}(\Phi(f_{1/2,A}),\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\mu\in L_{A}^{\vee}/L_{A}}I_{1/2,\mu}(\Phi(f_{1/2,A}),\tau)\in H_{1/2}(\omega_{L_{A}}).

Proof.

This is special case of
[
11
, Theorem 4.1]
detailed for the setup we consider above. ∎

Now, we identify these traces with the CM cycles
Z(U_{A})=Z(L_{A,0})
and geodesic sets
\mathfrak{G}(W_{A})
described for
Theorems
5.12
and
5.14
above (for the special case of
n=1
).

Proposition B.4
.

Let
k
be any quadratic field of discriminant
d_{k}
prime to the level
N
.
We have the following identifications of the traces defined above in terms of the sums
(\ref{Asums})

of the regularized theta lift
\Phi(f_{1/2,A},z)
along the CM cycles
Z(U_{A})
defined in
(\ref{CMcycle})

and the geodesic sets
\mathfrak{G}(W_{A})
defined in
(\ref{geodesic})
.

(i)

If
A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})
for
k
an imaginary quadratic field of discriminant
d_{k}<0
, then for each positive norm vector

x\in\Omega_{A,\mu,m}({\bf{Q}})
with orthogonal complement
U_{A}=U_{A}(x):=x^{\perp}\subset V
, we have that

\displaystyle\operatorname{tr}_{\mu,m}(\Phi(f_{1/2,A}))

\displaystyle=\Phi(f_{1/2,A},Z(U_{A})).

(ii)

If
A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})
for
k
a real quadratic field of discriminant
d_{k}>0
, then for each positive norm vector

x\in\Omega_{A,\mu,m}({\bf{Q}})\subset\mathcal{K}_{A}
with orthogonal complement
W_{A}=W_{A}(x):=x^{\perp}\subset V
, we have that

\displaystyle\operatorname{tr}_{\mu,m}(\Phi(f_{1/2,A}))

\displaystyle=\Phi(f_{1/2,A},\mathfrak{G}(W_{A})).

Proof.

Cf.
[
13
, Proposition 7.2]
. We use that
\Omega_{A,\mu,m}({\bf{A}}_{f})=K_{A}x=K_{L_{A}}x
in either case.
Hence for (i), we see from the corresponding definition
(\ref{special})
of
Z_{A}(\mu,m)
that
Z_{A}(\mu,m)=Z(U_{A})
.
The result then follows from the definitions. Similarly, for (ii), we see from the corresponding definition

(\ref{geodesic})
of the geodesic
\mathfrak{G}(W_{A})
with the natural identification
D(V)_{x}\cong D(W_{A})
that the identification
follows from the definitions. ∎

We obtain the following via Theorem
5.12
, Theorem
5.14
, and Lemma
A.2
(cf.
[
13
, Lemma 7.4]
).

Theorem B.5
.

We have via Theorem
5.12
and Theorem
5.14
for the quadratic space
(V,Q)
of signature
(1,2)
described above
the following identification of central derivative values of
L
-functions as Fourier coefficients of half-integral weight forms.

(i)

Let
k
be an imaginary quadratic field of discriminant
D=d_{k}<0
.
Assume as in Lemma
A.1
that
m=-D/4N
for
D=-4Nm
with
D\equiv r^{2}\bmod 4N
, and take
\mu=\mu_{r}
.
Then, for
\chi
and character of the ideal class group
C(\mathcal{O}_{k})
, we have the relation

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})}\chi(A)\cdot c_{g_{A}}(\mu,m)\cdot\operatorname{tr}_{\mu,m}\left(\Phi(f_{A,1/2})\right)

\displaystyle=-\frac{|D|^{\frac{1}{2}}}{16\pi^{2}||\phi||^{2}}\cdot L^{\prime}(1/2,\phi\times\theta(\chi)).

(ii)

Let
k
be a real quadratic field of discriminant
d_{k}>0
,
and
x\in\Omega_{A,\mu,m}({\bf{Q}})\subset\mathcal{K}_{A}
a positive norm vector
with orthogonal complement
W_{A}=W_{A}(x):=x^{\perp}\subset V
as in Proposition
B.4
(ii).
We have for each class
A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})
the relation

\displaystyle\operatorname{tr}_{\mu,m}(\Phi(f_{1/2,A}))

\displaystyle=-\frac{4}{\operatorname{vol}(K_{W_{A}})}\cdot L^{\prime}(0,g_{A}\times\theta_{L_{A,W}^{\perp}}).

Proof.

For (i), we combine Proposition
B.4
, Theorem
5.12
, Lemma
A.1
(cf.
(\ref{HurwitzA})
) and Lemma
A.2
to find

(100)

\displaystyle\operatorname{tr}_{\mu,m}(\Phi(f_{1/2,A}))

\displaystyle=\Phi(f_{1/2,A},Z(U_{A}))=-\frac{\deg(Z(U_{A}))}{2}\cdot L^{\prime}(0,\xi_{1/2}(f_{1/2,A})\times\theta_{L_{A}^{\perp}})

\displaystyle=-\frac{\deg(Z(U_{A}))}{2}\cdot\frac{N^{\frac{1}{2}}}{4\pi||g_{A}||^{2}}\cdot\frac{c_{g_{A}}(\mu,m)L^{\prime}_{A}(1/2,\phi)}{\deg Z_{A}(\mu,m)}

\displaystyle=-\frac{N^{\frac{1}{2}}\cdot c_{g_{A}}(\mu,m)}{8\pi||g_{A}||^{2}}\cdot L^{\prime}_{A}(1/2,\phi).

A variation of the formula
(\ref{GKZ})
implied by
[
27
,
\lx@sectionsign
II.4, Corollary 1]
gives us the corresponding relation

(101)

\displaystyle c_{g_{A}}(\mu,m)^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{||g_{A}||^{2}|D|^{\frac{1}{2}}}{2\pi N^{\frac{1}{2}}||\phi||^{2}}\cdot L_{A}(1/2,\phi\otimes\eta_{D}).

Multiplying both sides of
(\ref{metaCM})
by the Fourier coefficient
c_{g_{A}}(\mu,m)
and applying
(\ref{GKZA})
, we find that

\displaystyle c_{g_{A}}(\mu,m)\cdot\operatorname{tr}_{\mu,m}\left(\Phi(f_{A,1/2})\right)

\displaystyle=-\frac{N^{\frac{1}{2}}\cdot c_{g_{A}}(\mu,m)}{8\pi||g_{A}||^{2}}\cdot L^{\prime}_{A}(1/2,\phi)

\displaystyle=-\frac{|D|^{\frac{1}{2}}}{16\pi^{2}||\phi||^{2}}\cdot L_{A}(1/2,\phi\otimes\eta_{D})\cdot L_{A}^{\prime}(1/2,\phi).

Taking the twisted linear combination for any class group character
\chi\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})^{\vee}
, we then find that

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})}\chi(A)\cdot c_{g_{A}}(\mu,m)\cdot\operatorname{tr}_{\mu,m}\left(\Phi(f_{A,1/2})\right)

\displaystyle=-\frac{|D|^{\frac{1}{2}}}{16\pi^{2}||\phi||^{2}}\sum\limits_{A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})}\chi(A)L_{A}(1/2,\phi\otimes\eta_{D})L_{A}^{\prime}(1/2,\phi)

\displaystyle=-\frac{|D|^{\frac{1}{2}}}{16\pi^{2}||\phi||^{2}}\cdot L^{\prime}(1/2,\phi\times\theta(\chi)).

For (ii), we put together Proposition
B.4
, Theorem
5.14
, and Lemma
5.8
to get the claimed relation

\displaystyle\operatorname{tr}_{\mu,m}(\Phi(f_{1/2,A}))

\displaystyle=\Phi(f_{1/2,A},\mathfrak{G}(W_{A}))=-\frac{4}{\operatorname{vol}(K_{W_{A}})}\cdot L^{\prime}(0,\xi_{1/2}(f_{1/2,A})\times\theta_{L_{A,W}^{\perp}}).

∎

Remark B.6
.

In the setting of Theorem
B.5
(ii), we expect there to be an analogue of the adjoint map of
[
27
,
\lx@sectionsign
II.4]
,
to give a precise relation between
L(s,g_{A})
and
L(s,\phi)
.
This would imply an analogue of
[
27
,
\lx@sectionsign
II.4, Corollary 1]
, giving a precise relation between the squares
c_{g_{A}}(\mu,m)^{2}
and the central values
L(1/2,\phi\otimes\eta_{D})
.
Some relation between these values is suggested by the works of Waldspurger
[
57
]
,
[
56
]
and Kohnen-Zagier
[
39
]
.
We hope to make this more explicit this in a subsequent work, and to clarify the conjecture implied by Bruinier-Ono
[
12
, Theorem 1.1 (2)]
.

B.2.
The setting of signature
(2,2)
with
\Phi(f_{0},z)\in L^{1+\varepsilon}(X_{0}(N)\times X_{0}(N))

We now return to the setup of Theorem
7.8
and Corollary
7.9
.
Hence, for
k
a real or imaginary quadratic field of discriminant
d_{k}
prime to
N
and
quadratic Dirichlet character
\eta_{k}(\cdot)=(\frac{d_{k}}{\cdot})
,
we fix for each ideal class
A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})

an integer ideal representative
\mathfrak{a}\subset\mathcal{O}_{k}

with norm form
Q_{\mathfrak{a}}(\cdot)={\bf{N}}_{k/{\bf{Q}}}(\cdot)/{\bf{N}}\mathfrak{a}
. We then
consider the quadratic space
(V_{A},Q_{A})
of signature
(2,2)
defined by

(V_{A},Q_{A})=(\mathfrak{a}_{\bf{Q}}\oplus\mathfrak{a}_{\bf{Q}},Q_{\mathfrak{a}}-Q_{\mathfrak{a}})
.
We fix
L_{A}=N^{-1}\mathfrak{a}+N^{-1}\mathfrak{a}\subset V_{A}
to be the lattice whose adelization
corresponds to the compact open subgroup
K_{A}=K_{L_{A}}=K_{0}(N)\oplus K_{0}(N)
, as described in Corollary
2.4
.
We fix a cuspidal newform
\phi\in S_{2}^{\operatorname{new}}(\Gamma_{0}(N))
.
We then argue following Corollary
6.4
that there exists a vector-valued form
g_{\phi,A}\in S_{2}(\overline{\omega}_{L_{A}})

lifting
\phi
. For each ideal class
A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})
, we then take
f_{0,A}\in H_{0}(\omega_{L_{A}})
to be
any cuspidal harmonic weak Maass form of weight zero and representation
\omega_{L_{A}}

whose image under the differential operator
\xi_{1/2}
equals
g_{\phi,A}
.

Proposition B.7
.

For each class
A\in C(\mathcal{O}_{K})
, consider the corresponding regularized theta lift

\displaystyle\Phi(f_{0,A},z)=\Phi(f_{0,A},z,1)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{F}}^{\star}\langle\langle f_{0,A}(\tau),\theta_{L_{A}}(\tau,z,1)\rangle\rangle d\mu(\tau)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{CT}_{s=0}\left(\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\int\limits_{\mathcal{F}_{T}}\langle\langle f_{0,A}(\tau),\theta_{L_{A}}(\tau,z,1)\rangle\rangle v^{-s}d\mu(\tau)\right)

as a function of the variable
z\in D(V_{A})=D^{\pm}(V_{A})\cong\mathfrak{H}^{2}
. The following assertions are true.

(i)

\Phi(f_{0,A},z)
determines a (parallel weight
0
) modular function on
X_{0}(N)^{2}
with Laplacian eigenvalue
0
.

(ii)

\Phi(f_{0,A},z)
is the automorphic Green’s function for the special divisor
\mathcal{Z}^{c}(f_{0,A})
on
\mathcal{X}_{0}(N)^{2}
.

(iii)

\Phi(f_{0,A},z)\in L^{1+\varepsilon}(X_{0}(N)^{2})
.

Proof.

Again, all three assertions follow from Theorem
4.2
, using the identification
X_{K_{A}}\cong Y_{0}(N)^{2}

and extending to the cusps, as well as that
c_{f_{0,A}}^{+}(0,0)=0
. ∎

Again, we note that if these regularized theta lifts
\Phi(f_{0,A},z)\in L^{1+\varepsilon}(X_{0}(N)^{2})
were known to be square integrable,
then a straightforward generalization of the main theorem of Katok-Sarnak
[
35
]
would allow us to relate the twisted linear combinations of
Theorem
6.7
and Corollary
6.8
to twisted linear combinations of metaplectic Hilbert modular forms (on
X_{0}(4N)^{2}
) of parallel
weight
1/2
. Here, we expect there to be some version of Theorem
B.3
that generalizes the theorems of Waldspurger
[
57
]
and Gross-Kohnen-Zagier

[
27
,
\lx@sectionsign
II.4, Corollary 1]
relating central values of quadratic twists of
\operatorname{GL}_{2}({\bf{A}})
-automorphic
L
-functions to Fourier coefficients
of half-integral weight forms to central derivative values.

Conjecture B.8
.

Retain the setups of Theorem
5.12
and
5.14
, with
k
the corresponding quadratic field.
Through the connection to the sums
\Phi(f_{0,A},V_{A,0})
and
\Phi(f_{0,A},\mathfrak{G}(W_{A}))
of the automorphic Green’s functions

\Phi(f_{0,A})
along CM cycles
V_{A,0}\subset V_{A}
or geodesic sets respectively via Theorem
6.7
and Corollary
6.8
,
we have for any class group character
\chi\in C(\mathcal{O}_{k})
a relation between the central derivative value
L^{\prime}(1/2,\phi\times\theta(\chi))

and a twisted linear combination of Fourier coefficients of some Hilbert modular form of parallel weight
3/2
on the Hilbert modular surface
X_{0}(4N)\times X_{0}(4N)
,
as well as a relation to a twisted linear combintation of Fourier coefficient of a harmonic weak Maass form of parallel weight
1/2
and representations
\omega_{L_{A}}
.

That is, we expect to have an analogue of the main theorem of Bruinier-Funke-Imamoglu
[
11
]
for this setup of type with
rational quadratic spaces
(V_{A},Q_{A})
of signature
(2,2)
, linking to Fourier coefficients of harmonic weak Hilbert Maass forms of parallel weight
1/2
.
We also expect to have an analogue of the theorems of Waldspurger
[
57
]
,
[
56
]
, Kohnen-Zagier
[
39
]
,
and Gross-Kohnen-Zagier
[
27
,
\lx@sectionsign
II.4, Corollary 1]
to express the central derivative values
L^{\prime}(1/2,\phi\times\theta(\chi))
– at least for the principal
class group character
\chi=\chi_{0}
where
L^{\prime}(1/2,\phi\times\theta(\chi_{0}))=L^{\prime}(1/2,\phi)L(1/2,\phi\otimes\eta_{k})
– relating to squares of Fourier
coefficients of Hilbert modular forms of parallel weight
3/2
. Although we do not pursue the idea here, we use this method of proof of the Gross-Zagier
formula via regularized theta lifts to illustrate the natural connection for some future work.
